# Detailed curriculum: 2.1 Machine learning and 3.1 Research papers

Blueprint work file, Stage 2 Phase 1. Draft 1, 6 October 2026.

**Source of truth.** `docs/curriculum.md` for stages, topics, hours and exit tests. This file expands item 2.1 Machine learning (200 h) and item 3.1 Research papers (150 h) into courses, levels, lessons, homework, topic exams, the rebuilt exit tests, reading and verification, and writes one lesson in full. The two items share one file because 3.1 is where 2.1's discipline is spent: a replication is a prediction problem with a published answer, and every trap a learner meets in 2.1 (choosing with the test set, leakage, selection among many trials, overlapping labels) is a reason a published effect shrinks when someone else rebuilds it.

> **Revised by the `docs/PLAN.md` review, 7 October 2026.** These lines win over the rest of this file where they differ; content agents are briefed from them, and CI enforces them on the lesson files.
>
> | Where | Was | Now |
> |---|---|---|
> | Runtime tags (`micropython-ok`, `ulab-ok`, `web`), MicroPython CI runs, the `zqrandom` port | Drills tagged for MicroPython in the Artifact and web-only otherwise | **Void.** Pyodide 314.0.7 runs on both targets (measured on the owner's iPhone, `spikes/README.md`). Every drill declares `needs`: read `micropython-ok` as `needs: []`, `ulab-ok` as `needs: [numpy]`, and `web` as the packages the drill actually imports (NumPy, pandas, SciPy, statsmodels, scikit-learn); where it runs is decided at run time from `needs` against the build's load groups (`docs/PLAN.md` 5.7). CI runs every drill on CPython 3.12 and Pyodide only. Seeded randomness uses `random.Random(seed)` or the `zq_rng` drill helper (`docs/WIDGETS.md` 5.1.1), not a port |
> | Handed-off screens | A Parsons fallback built from the reference solution | **No Parsons fallback** on a handed-off screen: it shows the web-app link and accepts the result code. A Parsons ordering would credit ordering, not writing, and would be a second graded artefact per drill to author and verify. The small-screen Parsons mode of `code-cell` (`docs/WIDGETS.md` 5.1.1) is unaffected |
> | Sample lesson headers | `introduces` lists of 3 or 4 skills | At most 2 skills a lesson, at level grain; finer distinctions become step tags with no card (`docs/ASSESSMENT.md` 4.1) |
> | Decisions M7 and M10 | Two neural-network levels (TN5, TN6) and single lessons on attention and initialisation; deep learning left to canon-gap P14 | Unchanged here, but `docs/PLAN.md` 12 decision 2 recommends a core item 2.1b Deep learning (120 h: Prince chapters 1 to 12, Karpathy's *Zero to Hero* in NumPy on Pyodide, MIT 6.7960 problem sets) and a toy double-descent replication as a 3.1 option; without it the project's ML-lab aim is not met |

**Built on.** `docs/design-research.md` sections 5, 8, 9 and 10; `docs/blueprint/work/widgets-ml-research.md` (widget ids, modes, generators, misconception register; its section 1 outline is the working draft this file replaces); `docs/CONTENT-FORMAT.md` (ids, screen types, checks, drills, verification); `docs/ASSESSMENT.md` (paper kinds, the rows `2.1.exit` and `3.1.exit`, project records); `docs/AI-TUTOR.md` (Bertie's modes and gate); `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` (decisions 15 and 16: Pyodide on both targets); `docs/blueprint/work/canon-gap.md` (the resource canon for 2.1 and 3.1, proposals P1, P9, P10, P11, P12 and P14); the sibling files `stage1-calculus.md` (gradients, gradient descent, backpropagation on a graph), `stage1-linear-algebra.md` (least squares, ridge, SVD, PCA, conditioning), `stage1-probability.md` (Chernoff, Jensen, Poisson processes, Brownian motion preview, Kelly preview), `stage1-statistics-ts.md` (estimators, likelihood, testing, regression, Bayes, time series) and `stage1-finance-projects.md` (markets, the Research Method course RM, the alpha course AR), whose conventions this file follows; the owner's lesson-creator, maths-teaching, code-craft and project-forge skills.

**Tags.** Decisions sit in tables with the rejected alternative and the reason. [check] marks a resource detail (a section, lecture, chapter or exercise number, a URL path) that I recall but could not confirm from this environment (statlearning.com, ocw.mit.edu and most publisher pages were blocked by the network proxy on 6 October 2026). [searched] marks a fact confirmed by web search on 6 October 2026: the Stanford CS229 Autumn 2018 lecture list, the Caltech Learning From Data lecture list, MIT 15.097 (Spring 2012) on OpenCourseWare, and the chapter list of Géron's PyTorch edition. [recalled, unverified] marks a figure quoted from a paper from memory; such figures appear only as context and never as an answer key (widget catalogue decision 9). [verified] marks a number recomputed on 6 October 2026 with Python 3.11.15, NumPy 2.4.6, SciPy 1.17.1, statsmodels 0.15.0, scikit-learn 1.9.1, SymPy 1.14.0 and pandas 3.0.6. Every numeric answer in sections 2 (hooks), 3, 4 and 7 is [verified] unless tagged otherwise; the tag is not repeated on each. [inferred] marks a deduction; [our choice] a decision without outside evidence.

| # | Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Reason |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Unit of lesson time | **15 minutes per lesson, quiz included**, as in every sibling file | 10 or 12 minutes | One unit across the app keeps hours tables comparable. ML screens carry fits, curves and tables that take longer to read than a tap |

| 3 | What a trained model may be graded on | **Widget catalogue decision 3**: (a) a prediction made before training, (b) an exact computation on fixed small data shown on screen, or (c) a deterministic run on the item's fixed seed whose threshold sits a stated margin below what the reference settings reach, proved in CI | A tolerance on a resampled metric | A seed-sensitive metric fails honest learners on unlucky seeds. Sample screen 5 grades "which degree has the lowest test error on these ten points", an exact computation, never a simulated average |

The exit projects use real data in the learner's own repository |

PAC and VC theory beyond one lesson belong to canon-gap P14 |
| 8 | Transfer screens | **A quant or ML-lab context wherever an honest one exists**, at least one per level | A finance story forced into every lesson | As the siblings. ML has honest finance contexts almost everywhere: return prediction, default and fill models, execution schedules, signal shrinkage |
| 9 | Notation | **ISLP's in 2.1**: $n$ rows, $p$ predictors **not counting the intercept**, $\mathbf X$ the $n\times p$ design, $f$ the truth and $\hat f$ the fit, $\lambda$ the penalty in "RSS + $\lambda\cdot$penalty" (scikit-learn's `alpha` and its $\frac{1}{2n}$ scaling are named wherever code appears), log loss in nats with $\ln$, entropy in bits with $\log_2$. **3.1** follows each paper: $J$ and $K$ for JT's windows, $b, s, h$ for FF loadings, $\gamma, k, A, \sigma, q$ for Avellaneda and Stoikov | 1.6's convention that $p$ counts the intercept | ISLP is the spine and its labs print its notation. Where a 2.1 formula counts all fitted coefficients it writes $p + 1$ or $d$ (degrees of freedom), and the first such lesson (10) says so once |
| 10 | Sample lesson | **2.1 lesson 1, "Judged on data it has not seen"** (`lsn.learning.unseen-data`), section 7 | Lesson 7 (the bias-variance decomposition); 3.1 lesson 6 (the replication ledger) | It is the root of both items: every validation scheme, penalty, leakage rule and replication is a way of measuring or protecting test error. It carries the field's most expensive trap (a curve close to the points you have) and the first derivation of optimism, $\frac{2\sigma^2}{n}$ for the simplest model, which lesson 10 generalises to $\frac{2(p+1)\sigma^2}{n}$ |
| 11 | Tracks | **Every track takes all lessons of both items** (as `stage1-finance-projects.md` decision 12). The track decides the capstone's problem and the replication's paper, not which lessons exist | Track-specific lesson cuts | Researcher interviews test all of 2.1; trader and developer interviews ask about overfitting, leakage and backtests, and 3.1's market-making course is the trader track's paper |
| 12 | Bertie | Built in from the first lesson: every screen carries `tutor.focus`, `tutor.notes` and `tutor.avoid` (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.10); away during `exm.ml` and `exm.papers`; reviews a capstone note or replication `PLAN.md` only on request and never writes project code. ML-specific rule: Bertie never calls a training score "accuracy" without saying "on the training data", and never states a test score for a model before its screen's Check | | The capstone and the replication are the learner's evidence |

### 1.3 2.1 Purpose in a quant or ML career

- **Most models in use are linear, regularised or tree-based, and the hard part is validation** (the curriculum's own reason for making ISLP core). A desk does not lose money because its model was too simple; it loses money because a backtest flattered a model that had seen the future or been chosen from hundreds.
- **Finance is the low signal-to-noise regime.** A monthly return model with an out-of-sample $R^2$ of 0.5% is valuable (Campbell and Thompson 2008 [recalled]); 40 features on 120 months give an in-sample $R^2$ of about 0.34 from pure noise (lesson 5). Every 2.1 habit (hold-outs, shrinkage, honest folds) exists because the noise is louder than the signal.
- **Interviews test it directly.** Research interviews ask for the bias-variance decomposition, why the lasso gives zeros, how a random forest reduces variance, what AUC measures, and how to cross-validate on time series without leaking. The exit's "explain leakage, regularisation and the bias-variance trade-off without notes" is a typical first-round question.
- **ML labs run on the same ideas at scale.** Cross-entropy is maximum likelihood (lesson 15); weight decay is ridge (49, 114); early stopping is regularisation (98); benchmark leaderboards overfit exactly as backtests do (145); a reusable hold-out (146) is how labs keep a test set honest across many experiments.
- **It makes 2.3 and 3.1 possible.** Project 2's alpha research (AR7) uses boosted trees with purged folds; Gu, Kelly and Xiu (2020) in 3.1 is an ML paper; every replication is an exercise in not leaking.

### 1.4 2.1 Learning outcomes

| # | Outcome | Measured by |
|---|---|---|
| ML-O1 | Distinguish training, validation and test error; derive the optimism of the training error ($\frac{2\sigma^2}{n}$ for the mean, $\frac{2(p+1)\sigma^2}{n}$ for least squares); explain the curse of dimensionality and the low-signal regime with numbers | 85% on fresh difficulty-3 items; exam Q1, Q2 |
| ML-O2 | Derive the bias-variance decomposition at a point, compute bias and variance for k-NN and least squares in closed form, and read learning curves | Homework LD2 long-form at 7 of 10; exam Q20 (exit slot) |
| ML-O3 | Choose a loss for a goal (mean, median, quantile, probability), compute entropies, cross-entropies and KL divergences, prove KL $\ge 0$, and show minimising log loss is maximum likelihood | Exam Q3; homework LD3 |
| ML-O4 | Derive gradients in matrix form (least squares, logistic, softmax, a two-layer network), check them by finite differences, and choose step sizes from curvature | Homework LD4 and TN6 long-form; exam Q4 |
| ML-O5 | Estimate test error by validation sets, k-fold, LOOCV and the bootstrap with standard errors; apply the one-standard-error rule; run nested CV; never select outside the folds | Exam Q5, Q6; homework LD5, LD6 |
| ML-O6 | Fit and interpret ridge, lasso, elastic net, PCR and splines; derive ridge's SVD form and the lasso's soft threshold; choose $\lambda$ by CV; explain ridge as a Gaussian prior and the lasso as a Laplace prior | Exam Q7, Q8, Q19 (exit slot); homework LS3, LS4 |
| ML-O7 | Fit logistic, LDA, QDA, naive Bayes and SVM classifiers, derive their boundaries, and evaluate them by confusion counts, precision, recall, ROC, AUC, PR curves, cost-based thresholds, calibration and proper scores | Exam Q9 to Q11; homework CP1 to CP5 |
| ML-O8 | Grow, prune and interpret trees; derive the variance of a bagged average; tune forests and gradient boosting; compute XGBoost gains and Shapley values; recognise importance traps | Exam Q12, Q13; homework TN1 to TN4 |
| ML-O9 | Explain what hidden units, ReLU sums and depth buy; derive backpropagation; choose initialisation and optimiser settings; regularise networks; read attention as a weighted average | Exam Q14; homework TN5, TN6 |
| ML-O10 | Validate through time with walk-forward, purged and embargoed k-fold and CPCV; build triple-barrier and meta-labels with uniqueness weights; audit a study for every class of leakage | Exam Q15 to Q17, Q18 (exit slot); homework FD1 to FD3 |
| ML-O11 | Build features from market data (bars, fractional differences, cross-sectional ranks), cluster assets and features, and fit PCA factor models | Homework FD4, FD5 |
| ML-O12 | Do one tabular prediction problem end to end with time-ordered cross-validation, a locked hold-out, a trial ledger and a results note | `2.1.exit`: `prj.ml.exit` audits and `exm.ml` slots |

### 1.5 2.1 Prerequisites

- **1.1** NumPy, pandas, seeded simulation (level A3), returns and look-ahead (lessons 93, 94), log-sum-exp (the `np.exp(1000)` lesson in A2). **1.2** Git and pytest: the capstone lives in the learner's repository.
- **1.3** lessons 33 (Newton's method), 50 (convex functions), 116 (backpropagation on a graph), 117 (gradient descent in two dimensions), 125 to 128 (Lagrange multipliers, maximum entropy).
- **1.4** lessons 39 to 51 (projections, least squares, ridge, QR), 79 (covariance matrices), 82 (gradients of quadratic forms), 83 to 88 (SVD, pseudoinverse, conditioning), 89 to 94 (PCA), 95 (covariance shrinkage), 96 (gradient descent and conditioning).
- **1.5** conditional probability and Bayes' rule, the named distributions, the bivariate normal, the LLN and CLT, lessons 112 to 114 (Markov, Chebyshev, Chernoff, Jensen), 154 (Kelly preview).
- **1.6** lessons 6 (bias, variance, MSE), 11 to 15 (likelihood), 25 (bootstrap), 38 to 42 (multiple testing, backtest overfitting), 46 (AIC and BIC), 52 (prediction intervals), 58 (hat values), 63 to 65 (dummies, model selection, logistic regression), 77 and 78 (empirical Bayes, ridge as a prior).
- **1.7** returns, excess returns, the order book; **2.3 is not required** (it comes after 2.1 on the researcher and trader tracks; on the developer track 2.3 comes first and FD1 becomes a recap of RM5).

External problems 14.0 h: the ISLP Python labs of chapters 2 to 10 and 12 (9.0 h); ISLP conceptual exercises (5.0 h) | 46.0 | 23.0% |

Solving time (everything except the 32 hours of watching and reading) is 168 hours, 84%, above the curriculum's 60% floor (rule 2).

**Pace.** About 18 weeks at the curriculum's 15-hour week: the main block (9 h a week) carries lessons, practice, homework, reading and exams (160 h, 18 weeks at 9 h); the Build block (3 h) carries the ML Lab and the capstone (40 h, 13 weeks), with 2.4 contests sharing it (`ASSESSMENT.md` 8.3). The main block sets the pace: about 18 weeks, or four months.

### 1.7 3.1 Research papers: scope, boundaries and decisions

| # | Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Reason |
|---|---|---|---|---|

Momentum is the simplest replication (one sort, one table), so the method of reading and replicating is taught on it, in its first two levels. Harvey, Liu and Zhu is the natural home of "how much survived", so the exit project's final level closes course WS |
| R3 | Order | **Momentum, factors, pairs, market making, multiple testing** | The curriculum's listing order (which puts pairs before market making and HLZ last, the same) | JT is a sort; FF adds time-series regressions and the GRS test on sorts; HLZ tests the factor zoo FF started, so it must follow FF, and it closes the item because its question ("is it real?") is the exit's. Pairs and market making are time-series and microstructure papers that need neither |
| R4 | What 3.1 assumes from 2.3 | **RM8 (sorts, Fama-MacBeth, clustered errors, event studies, spanning), RM3 to RM5 (Sharpe inference, selection, validation), AR (signals, IC, factor models) and 1.7 MK (order book, spreads)** are recapped in hook screens and never re-taught | Re-teaching them | Every learner takes all of 2.3's lessons (that file's decision 12). 3.1 goes further on each paper: Shanken's correction, GRS, overlapping-return errors, the HJB sketch |
| R5 | Canon-gap P11 (financial econometrics, 80 h) and P12 (microstructure, 50 h) | **3.1 teaches what the five replications need**: Newey-West and Hansen-Hodrick errors for overlapping returns (16), errors-in-variables and Shanken (29, 30), GRS (39, 40), spanning (43), the OU spread and a Kalman preview (61, 63), Glosten-Milgrom and Kyle (85, 86). GMM, panel methods and full state-space models wait for P11; queue dynamics and execution wait for P12 | Waiting for the proposals | The replication exit cannot be passed honestly without overlapping-return errors and a joint alpha test (catalogue finding 0.2) |
| R6 | Stochastic control | **The Avellaneda-Stoikov quotes are derived from indifference (the reservation price) and from a sketched HJB with the paper's ansatz** (lesson 75), and say so | Full stochastic control | 3.2 is optional and uncounted; the paper's closed forms are reachable from exponential utility and a first-order expansion, which lessons 71 to 77 do |
| R7 | The data route (catalogue 0.3, an owner decision) | **Default exit route: Fama and French (1993) rebuilt from Ken French's data library and extended from 1994 to the present**; momentum at decile level from the same library (its "10 portfolios formed on prior return" files and the momentum factor [recalled]) is the second route; stock-level replications on free prices must state their survivorship bias; Avellaneda and Stoikov qualifies only with an empirical extension (lesson 104) | WRDS-only replications | The learner can download Ken French's files without a licence; CRSP needs institutional access. Every route is rehearsed first on `panel-sim`, where the truth is known |

If declined, the paper runs as an ungated practice paper (as `stage1-finance-projects.md` decision 18) |

### 1.8 3.1 Purpose in a quant or ML career

- **Research is the job, and most of it is replication.** A quant researcher's first months at a fund are spent rebuilding published or internal results before extending them. The skill is not reading quickly; it is finding the twelve unstated choices that move a number by a third.
- **Published effects shrink.** McLean and Pontiff (2016) report that the returns of 97 published predictors are 26% lower out of sample and 58% lower after publication [searched]; Hou, Xue and Zhang (2020) report that most of 452 anomalies fail with value weights and NYSE breakpoints [recalled, unverified]. A researcher who has measured the shrinkage once, on their own replication, treats every backtest differently.
- **The five papers are the canon interviewers know.** Momentum, the three factors, pairs trading, the Avellaneda-Stoikov quotes and the $t > 3$ hurdle come up by name in research and trading interviews; the market-making paper is the theory behind 3.3's market-making games.
- **ML labs need the same reading.** Gu, Kelly and Xiu (2020) is where 2.1 meets 3.1; the reading, ledger and referee-report lessons transfer to any arXiv paper.

### 1.9 3.1 Learning outcomes

| # | Outcome | Measured by |
|---|---|---|
| RP-O1 | Read an empirical paper in three passes, extract its claim, sample, method and identification, and read its tables (coefficients, $t$-statistics, standard errors, stars) | Exam Q1, Q2; homework MP1 |
| RP-O2 | Write a replication ledger, pre-register an extension, and define "how much survived" as a ratio with a delta-method interval | Exam Q3; homework MP2; `3.1.exit` |
| RP-O3 | Build JT momentum portfolios with formation, skip and overlapping holding windows; compute the $J\times K$ table with correct standard errors for overlapping returns; derive JT's profit decomposition; explain crashes | Exam Q4 to Q6; homework MP3, MP4 |
| RP-O4 | Build SMB and HML with NYSE breakpoints and the June timing convention; run FF time-series regressions; compute and interpret GRS; run Fama-MacBeth with Shanken's correction; judge new factors by spanning | Exam Q7 to Q10; homework FF1 to FF4 |
| RP-O5 | Form pairs by the distance method, trade them by GGR's rule with the one-day wait, compute returns on committed and employed capital, and fit an OU model to a spread | Exam Q11, Q12; homework PS1 to PS3 |
| RP-O6 | Derive the Avellaneda-Stoikov reservation price and spread, simulate the inventory and symmetric strategies, and explain what the model leaves out | Exam Q13, Q14; homework MM1 to MM4 |
| RP-O7 | Apply Bonferroni, Holm, BH and BHY to factor $t$-statistics, compute HLZ hurdles and haircut Sharpe ratios, and summarise the replication evidence | Exam Q15, Q16; homework WS1, WS2 |
| RP-O8 | Read an ML-in-finance paper critically (out-of-sample $R^2$ against zero, leakage, selection) and write a referee report | Exam Q18; homework WS3 |
| RP-O9 | Replicate one paper's main table, extend it to data after publication, and write a plain note on how much survived | `3.1.exit` |

### 1.10 3.1 Prerequisites

- **2.3** all lessons, and specifically RM3 to RM8 (lessons 13 to 47), AR1 to AR4, BT3 (costs). **2.2** lessons 11 (AR(1) and half-life), 37 to 42 (cointegration, error correction, pairs without look-ahead). **2.1** lessons 117 to 128 (time-ordered validation, leakage), 144 to 147 (overfitting by search), 45 to 49 (shrinkage, for lesson 96) and 15 (log loss, for lesson 100).
- **1.6** lessons 38 to 42 (multiple testing), 55 and 56 (HC and HAC errors), 50 and 51 ($t$ and $F$), 19 (Sharpe ratio standard error). **1.7** MK1 and MK3 (order book, spreads), EQ2 (CAPM). **1.5** lessons 74 (Poisson processes) and 140 (Brownian motion preview). **1.3** exponential and logarithmic approximations, partial derivatives. **1.4** covariance matrices and their inverses (GRS).

### 1.11 3.1 Hours budget (150 h)

| Block | What | Hours | Share |
|---|---|---|---|
| In-app lessons | 108 lessons at 15 minutes, quiz included | 27.0 | 18.0% |
| In-app practice and redo | 22 level reviews at about 15 minutes (5.5 h); generated practice and the Redo queue (4.5 h) | 10.0 | 6.7% |
| Homework | 22 sets at about 1.75 hours (8 items; the papers' reading moves to the external block) | 38.5 | 25.7% |
| Exams | One practice paper (1.5 h), `exm.papers` (1.5 h), reviewing both (1.0 h) | 4.0 | 2.7% |
| External reading and lectures | Reading 18.0 h: the five papers in three passes (about 2.5 h each, 12.5 h); method and extension papers (5.5 h: Shanken 1992, Daniel and Moskowitz 2016, Do and Faff 2010, Guéant, Lehalle and Fernandez-Tapia 2013, McLean and Pontiff 2016, Gu, Kelly and Xiu 2020, in part). Watching 4.0 h: selected sessions of Cochrane's online Asset Pricing class on time-series and cross-sectional tests [check] | 22.0 | 14.7% |
| Projects and drills | Replication Lab, 5 rehearsal replications of 1.5 h (7.5 h); exit `prj.papers.replication` (41.0 h: choose and pre-register 3, data and pipeline 10, the main table 10, the out-of-sample extension 8, results note with the survival section 5, repository, tests and README 3, defence 2) | 48.5 | 32.3% |
| **Total** | | **150.0** | 100% |

Solving time (everything except 22 hours of reading and watching) is 128 hours, 85%.

**Pace.** About 12 weeks at 15 hours a week: the main block carries lessons, practice, homework, reading and exams (101.5 h, 11.3 weeks); the Build block carries the Replication Lab and the exit project (48.5 h at 3 h a week is 16 weeks, so the project starts after level MP2, in week 2, and runs to the end; `ASSESSMENT.md` 8.2 puts the replication in Stage 3's Build block).

---

## 2. Courses

### 2.0 Conventions for every lesson below

**Ids** follow `CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 4.1: `crs.<course>`, `lvl.<course>.<level>`, `lsn.<course>.<slug>`, `set.<course>.<level>` for homework, `exm.ml` and `exm.papers` for the topic exams, `prj.ml.exit` and `prj.papers.replication` for the exit projects. Course namespaces: 2.1 `learning` (LD), `shrinkage` (LS), `classify` (CP), `ensembles` (TN), `finml` (FD); 3.1 `momentum` (MP), `factors` (FF), `pairs` (PS), `making` (MM), `survival` (WS). Libraries use the topic namespaces `ml` and `papers`: `skl.ml.*`, `mc.ml.*`, `gen.ml.*`, `drl.ml.*`, and the same with `papers`. Misconception ids are the widget catalogue's register (its section 10) under the topic namespace (`lower-train-error-is-better` becomes `mc.ml.lower-train-error-is-better`); new ids are marked [new]. Generators are the catalogue's section 9 families under `gen.ml.*` and `gen.papers.*`. The `#` column is the lesson's number within its item (2.1: 1 to 152; 3.1: 1 to 108); display numbers are computed from position and never stored.

**Every lesson** runs the design research 8.3 sequence: a hook on a canvas, free manipulation, the name for what the learner just did, a ladder of 2 to 4 screens adding one constraint each, the trap, the rule stated as a description of the learner's own steps, a transfer screen where a true quant or ML context exists, the summary card, then a quiz of 3 to 5 items. From the owner's skills, every lesson also carries the "why this exists" frame, one common trap, at least one edge case, one worked example with a decision point where the naive route fails, at least one line the learner derives (a `derive` screen; `leap: naive, fails, pointsTo` wherever the step is creative), and concreteness fades: pictures and fixed small data first, symbols last.

In machine learning and empirical finance these take particular forms:

- **Manipulation before notation** means dragging a curve through points and then meeting tomorrow's points, sliding a flexibility or penalty control and watching two error curves part, dragging a threshold across two score distributions, cutting the plane with a split, tapping the training labels a purge must remove, setting formation and holding windows on a timeline, or dragging an inventory slider until the quotes lean, before any formula appears. The prediction made before a run is what is graded (decision 3).
- **The worked example's decision point** is usually a choice between two defensible procedures that differ in what they let the model see: shuffled or blocked folds, a scaler fitted inside or outside the folds, $\lambda_{\min}$ or $\lambda_{1\text{se}}$, accuracy or a cost-weighted threshold, impurity or permutation importance, plain or Newey-West errors, committed or employed capital, the paper's number or the out-of-sample one.
- **Derive** means completing or ordering the lines of a decomposition (bias-variance, optimism, MSE), a gradient in matrix form, a threshold function from subgradients, a variance of an average, a split gain from a Taylor expansion, a purge rule from interval overlap, a profit decomposition, a reservation price from indifference.

**Widgets** use the ids of `widgets-ml-research.md` section 12 and its naming table 0.4 (`plot-2d`, `marks`, `drag-point`, `slider`, `stepper`, `region-select`, `value-table`, `live-formula`, `choice`, `numeric-input`, `expression-input`, `derivation-steps`, `code-cell`, `sim-runner`, `card-sort`, `free-response`, `span-track`): ML composites `fit-explorer`, `loss-lab`, `error-curves`, `bias-variance-lab`, `logistic-lab`, `classifier-plane`, `threshold-lab`, `calibration-lab`, `feature-builder`, `basis-lab`, `cv-lab`, `tuning-lab`, `regularisation-path`, `penalty-geometry`, `split-scorer`, `tree-builder`, `ensemble-lab`, `boosting-lab`, `importance-lab`, `cluster-lab`, `nn-playground`, `relu-composer`, `sequence-filter`, `time-cv-lab`, `leakage-detective`, `labelling-lab`, `bar-sampler`, `fracdiff-lab`, `sharpe-lab`, `overfit-lab`, `ml-workbench`, `viva`; research composites `portfolio-sort`, `signal-lab`, `factor-model-lab`, `pairs-lab`, `market-maker-sim`, `replication-workbench`; and from the sibling catalogues `regression-lab`, `summary-table`, `multiple-testing`, `coint-lab`, `contingency-table`, `icon-array`, `sampling-lab`, `posterior-lab`, `dist-explorer`, `mass-editor`, `descent-lab`, `dependency-graph`, `pca-lab`, `surface-explorer`, `matrix`, `sde-stepper`, `pipeline-flow`, `predict-output`, `backtest-lab`, `results-note`, `game-lab`. A mode the catalogue does not yet list is marked [mode to add]; section 1.12 collects them.

**Bertie** is built in from the first lesson (decision 12). In widget screens he reads the widget's `describe()` and, in lesson and explore modes only, its `secret()` block (planted truth, exact answers) under the never-reveal instruction. He never changes answer state; he may offer a "Try this" chip that sets a slider, which the learner taps.

**Quiz item types.** The letters of `CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.4 as the siblings use them: N numeric, X expression, C single choice, M multi-select, O order the steps of a derivation or proof, S sort or match, I interval, P point or handle on a canvas, T predict the output of code, K code with tests, D a distribution or set of bars. Long-form items appear in homework and exams, never in quizzes. Single and multiple choice (C and M) stay at or below 40% of each course's quiz items (design research 10); section 2.11 counts them.

**Runtime tags.** Lesson screens run on both targets. `code` screens and drills declare `needs` (decision 5): `stdlib` (also `micropython-ok`), `numpy`, `pandas`, `sklearn`, `scipy`, `statsmodels`, or `local-torch`. In the Artifact a screen whose `needs` the build lacks shows the handoff link and a Parsons fallback built from the reference solution.

**Level review.** Every level ends with a Level review of 8 items at difficulty 2 to 4, interleaved, with up to 30% drawn from earlier levels of the same course (`ASSESSMENT.md` 2.4). Homework follows (section 3).

**Columns.** Objective is one measurable sentence. Hook is the puzzle the lesson opens with, with its answer where it has one. Trap is the misconception the trap screen is built around; Edge is the fringe case the lesson must show. Quiz lists the item types in order.

### 2.1 Course LD: Learning from Data (`crs.learning`, 6 levels, 33 lessons)

What learning is and how it is measured. The course starts from the one fact everything else serves (a model is judged on data it has not seen), takes test error apart into noise, bias and variance, chooses the loss that says what "good" means (and the information theory behind log loss), fits by gradients in matrix form, and ends with the tools that estimate test error honestly: validation, cross-validation, nested CV, the bootstrap and a first generalisation bound. Spine: ISLP chapters 2 and 5; ESL chapters 2 and 7; CS229 lectures 2, 8 and 9 [searched]; Caltech Learning From Data lectures 1, 2, 5, 8 and 13 [searched].

**Level LD1: The learning problem** (`lvl.learning.problem`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `lsn.learning.unseen-data` Judged on data it has not seen (section 7) | Distinguish training error from test error, compute both from fixed data, and derive that the mean model's expected training error is $\frac{n-1}{n}\sigma^2$ against an expected test error of $\frac{n+1}{n}\sigma^2$ | A share's trading volume at ten times of day forms a U. Three curves: a straight line, a gentle U, and a curve through every point. Which will sit closest to tomorrow's ten points? (The gentle U: test MSE 0.239, against 0.963 for the line and 4.05 for the curve through every point) | `fit-explorer` (sketch, fixed data), `error-curves` (predict, fixed data), `derivation-steps`, `value-table` | Trap: the curve closest to the points you have is best (`mc.ml.lower-train-error-is-better`); the curve through every point predicts negative volume at 0.92. Edge: with noise-free data, or a rule fixed before the data were seen, training and test error have the same expectation | C, N, S, N, C |
| 2 | `lsn.learning.flexibility` Flexibility: training error falls, test error turns up | Predict the shapes of training and test error against flexibility, explain why nested models' training error never rises, and locate the test minimum from a table | Before sweeping polynomial degree from 0 to 12 on 30 points, sketch both error curves. Which one can never rise? (Training error: each degree contains the one before) | `fit-explorer` (flex), `error-curves` (predict) | Trap: more flexibility keeps improving test error (`mc.ml.test-error-falls-forever`). Edge: for non-nested families (k-NN as $k$ changes, trees grown from different seeds) training error need not be monotone; test error can dip twice (lesson 58) | P, M, N, C |
| 3 | `lsn.learning.knn` k-nearest neighbours and flexibility as $n/k$ | Compute k-NN predictions by hand with a stated tie rule, and relate $k$ to flexibility through the effective number of parameters $n/k$ | Points $x = 1, 2, 3, 5, 8$ with $y = 2, 4, 3, 7, 9$. Predict at $x_0 = 4$ with $k = 3$ ($\frac{3 + 7 + 4}{3} = 4.67$), then with $k = 1$ (two points tie at distance 1) | `fit-explorer` (k-NN), `classifier-plane` (k-NN vote) | Trap: $k = 1$ uses the most local information, so it is best (`mc.ml.k1-is-best`). Edge: ties need a rule (lowest training index here); $k = n$ predicts the mean, 5; rescaling one feature changes every neighbour | N, N, C, S |
| 4 | `lsn.learning.curse-of-dimensionality` The curse of dimensionality | Compute the edge $r^{1/p}$ of the sub-cube holding a fraction $r$ of uniform data and the median nearest-neighbour distance $\left(1 - 2^{-1/n}\right)^{1/p}$ in the unit ball, and explain why local methods fail in high dimensions | To capture 10% of uniform data in 10 dimensions, how wide must a neighbourhood be along each axis? ($0.1^{1/10} = 0.794$ of the range; in 2 dimensions 0.316) | screen from primitives (catalogue 8.1), `plot-2d` | Trap: more features always help a nearest-neighbour model. Edge: with 500 points in the 10-dimensional unit ball the median nearest neighbour of the centre is 0.52 away, over halfway to the edge; real data near a low-dimensional surface escape the worst of it | N, N, C, M |
| 5 | `lsn.learning.low-signal` Low signal: what finance looks like | Compute the expected in-sample $R^2$ of pure noise, $\frac{p}{n-1}$, compare a reported fit with it, and state the best possible out-of-sample $R^2$ when the truth explains 1% | A model with 30 features on 100 months reports $R^2 = 0.31$. What does pure noise give on average? ($30/99 = 0.30$; 2,000 simulated noise fits average 0.302) | `fit-explorer` (snr), `sim-runner` (predict first) | Trap: a good return model explains much of the variance (`mc.ml.high-r2-expected-in-finance`). Edge: out-of-sample $R^2$ is negative for most fitted noise models; adjusted $R^2$ removes the null's mean but not the effect of having chosen the features | N, N, C, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 1 forecasts the intraday volume curve that a VWAP execution schedule follows (section 7, screen 11); lesson 5 reads a return model's in-sample $R^2$ against the noise benchmark.

**Level LD2: Bias and variance** (`lvl.learning.bias-variance`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 6 | `lsn.learning.bias-variance-pointwise` Bias and variance at a point | Estimate bias and variance at $x_0$ from a table of fits over 20 training sets, and rank models by each | Fifty training sets drawn from one truth; a line and a degree-8 polynomial fitted to each. At $x_0 = 0.9$, which fan of fits is wider, and which misses the truth on average? | `bias-variance-lab` (pointwise) | Trap: variance is the noise in $y$ (`mc.ml.variance-is-the-noise`); bias means unfairness (`mc.ml.bias-means-unfair`). Edge: a model can be unbiased at one $x_0$ and biased at another; averaging many fits removes variance, never bias | O, N, N, C |
| 7 | `lsn.learning.bias-variance-derivation` The decomposition derived | Derive $E\left[(Y_0 - \hat f(x_0))^2\right] = \sigma^2 + \text{bias}^2 + \text{variance}$, naming where the independence of the new noise from the training set is used | At $x_0$ the noise variance is 1, the fits' bias 0.5 and their variance 0.5. Expected squared error on a new point? ($1 + 0.25 + 0.5 = 1.75$) | `derivation-steps` (leap), `bias-variance-lab` (integrated), `error-curves` | Trap: adding the bias unsquared, or forgetting the noise floor. Edge: the decomposition is exact for squared loss only; for 0-1 loss a variance can help or hurt depending on which side of one half the average fit sits | X, O, N, C |
| 8 | `lsn.learning.knn-tradeoff` k-NN's trade-off in closed form | Compute bias and variance of k-NN at a point for a fixed design, $\text{Var} = \sigma^2/k$ and $\text{bias} = f(x_0) - \frac1k\sum_i f(x_{(i)})$, and choose $k$ | $f(x) = x^2$, points at the integers, $\sigma^2 = 4$, predict at 0. Two neighbours ($\pm 1$) or four ($\pm 1, \pm 2$)? (Expected errors $4 + 1 + 2 = 7$ against $4 + 6.25 + 1 = 11.25$: two) | `bias-variance-lab` (k-NN), `fit-explorer` | Trap: the best $k$ does not depend on the noise (with $\sigma^2 = 100$, four neighbours win: 131.25 against 151). Edge: at the edge of the data the neighbours all lie on one side, so bias grows there first | N, N, C, N |
| 9 | `lsn.learning.learning-curves` Learning curves: what more data buys | Predict training and test error against $n$ for unbiased least squares, $\frac{n - (p+1)}{n}\sigma^2$ and $\frac{n + (p+1)}{n}\sigma^2$, and tell high bias from high variance | A model with 4 features and an intercept, $\sigma^2 = 1$: training and test error at $n = 10$ and at $n = 100$? (0.5 and 1.5; 0.95 and 1.05) | `error-curves` (learning curve), `fit-explorer` | Trap: more data lower training error (`mc.ml.train-error-falls-with-n`); more data cure bias (`mc.ml.more-data-cures-bias`). Edge: two curves meeting above the noise floor mean bias, so the remedy is features, not rows | N, N, M, C |
| 10 | `lsn.learning.optimism` The optimism of the training error | Derive that least squares with $p + 1$ coefficients has expected optimism $\frac{2(p+1)\sigma^2}{n}$ on a fixed design, compute Mallows' $C_p$, and generalise to effective degrees of freedom $\operatorname{tr}(S)$ | 10 coefficients on 100 points with $\sigma^2 = 1$: expected training error 0.90, expected error on new $y$ at the same $x$ 1.10. Where do the 0.20 come from? ($2\times 10\times 1/100$) | `derivation-steps`, `regression-lab` (hat values) | Trap: optimism depends only on how many features were used, not on how they were chosen (a selected subset is more optimistic). Edge: k-NN has $\operatorname{tr}(S) = n/k$; a smoother's degrees of freedom need not be whole. This lesson states once that 2.1 counts the intercept separately from $p$ (decision 9) | X, N, O, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 8 picks the number of past days a nearest-neighbour volatility forecast averages; lesson 9 reads a fund's learning curve (more history or better features?); lesson 10 explains why a strategy with 10 tuned settings on 100 months is expected to lose a fifth of a noise variance out of sample.

**Level LD3: Loss, likelihood and information** (`lvl.learning.information`, 6 lessons)

Canon-gap P9's core (decision M6).

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 11 | `lsn.learning.loss-functions` Loss functions and what they make you predict | Show that squared loss is minimised by the mean, absolute loss by the median and pinball loss $\rho_\tau$ by the $\tau$-quantile, and choose a loss for a goal | Daily fills of 1, 2, 3, 4 and 100 lots. One constant forecast: which value minimises squared loss, and which absolute loss? (22 and 3) | `loss-lab` (constant, pinball) | Trap: squared loss treats every point fairly (`mc.ml.squared-loss-is-neutral`); any sensible loss is minimised at the mean (`mc.ml.mean-minimises-absolute-loss`). Edge: the pinball minimiser can be a whole interval (for $\tau = 0.8$ here, every value from 4 to 100), and NumPy's default quantile picks 23.2 inside it | P, N, C, S |
| 12 | `lsn.learning.surrogate-losses` Empirical risk and surrogate losses | Write empirical risk minimisation, explain why 0-1 loss cannot be descended, and compare hinge, log and exponential losses as convex upper bounds on it | Rotate a boundary through 20 points and watch the error count: a staircase with flat treads. Which way should you turn? | `loss-lab` (margin), `classifier-plane` | Trap: minimise the error count directly (`mc.ml.zero-one-is-trainable`). Edge: exponential loss punishes a large negative margin enormously, which makes AdaBoost fragile to mislabelled points (lesson 100) | C, S, M, N |
| 13 | `lsn.learning.entropy` Entropy: average surprise | Compute entropy in bits, read it as the expected number of yes-or-no questions with the best questioning, and find its maximum | Eight equally likely outcomes need 3 questions. Four outcomes with chances $\frac12, \frac14, \frac18, \frac18$ need how many on average? (1.75) | `mass-editor` (entropy readout [mode to add]), `card-sort` | Trap: entropy measures spread in value (it ignores what the outcomes are). Edge: $0\log 0 = 0$; certainty has entropy 0; the uniform distribution has the most; bits and nats differ by a factor $\ln 2$ | N, N, C, M |
| 14 | `lsn.learning.cross-entropy-kl` Cross-entropy and KL divergence | Compute $H(p, q) = H(p) + D_{KL}(p\Vert q)$, show KL is not symmetric, and prove $D_{KL}\ge 0$ by Jensen (1.5 lesson 114) | Code those four outcomes as if they were equally likely: 2 bits each. How many bits per outcome are wasted? ($D_{KL} = 0.25$) | `mass-editor` (two distributions), `derivation-steps` | Trap: KL is a distance (for $(0.9, 0.1)$ against $(0.5, 0.5)$ it is 0.531 bits one way and 0.737 the other). Edge: $q_i = 0$ where $p_i > 0$ gives an infinite divergence, which is why models clip probabilities | N, X, O, C |
| 15 | `lsn.learning.log-loss` Log loss is negative log-likelihood | Show that average log loss of a probabilistic classifier is its negative log-likelihood per point and the cross-entropy between labels and forecasts (recapping 1.6 lesson 65) | A forecaster says 90% and the event does not happen. Log loss? ($-\ln 0.1 = 2.30$ nats, against 0.105 had it happened) | `logistic-lab` (loss bars), `loss-lab` | Trap: fit probabilities by squared error (`mc.ml.squared-loss-for-probabilities`). Edge: a confident wrong forecast of exactly 0 or 1 costs infinity; libraries clip near $10^{-15}$, which caps one point's loss at about 34.5 nats | N, N, C, X |
| 16 | `lsn.learning.mutual-information` Mutual information: dependence that correlation misses | Compute $I(X; Y) = H(Y) - H(Y\mid X)$ from a small table, and exhibit dependence with zero correlation | $X$ is $-1$, 0 or 1 with equal chance and $Y = X^2$. Correlation? (0.) Mutual information? ($H(Y) = 0.918$ bits, because $Y$ is a function of $X$) | `contingency-table` (MI readout [mode to add]), `mass-editor` | Trap: zero correlation means independence. Edge: MI is unchanged by one-to-one transforms of $X$; plug-in MI estimates from few rows are biased upwards, so an MI feature screen needs a permutation null (1.6 lesson 35) | N, C, M, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 11 forecasts a 90% quantile of daily volume for a capacity limit; lesson 15 scores two default-probability models by log loss; lesson 16 screens a feature whose effect on returns is U-shaped.

**Level LD4: Fitting by optimisation** (`lvl.learning.optimisation`, 6 lessons)

Canon-gap P10's exit lives in lessons 20 to 22 and 111 (decision M5).

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 17 | `lsn.learning.gradient-descent` Gradient descent and the step size | Derive the update factor $1 - \eta\lambda$ along each curvature direction of a quadratic loss and the step size above which descent diverges, $\frac{2}{\lambda_{\max}}$ (recapping 1.4 lesson 96 and 1.3 lesson 117) | Minimise $5w^2$ from $w = 1$ with step 0.25. What happens? (Each step multiplies $w$ by $-1.5$: it diverges; any step below 0.2 converges, and 0.1 lands in one step) | `descent-lab` | Trap: a bigger step reaches the bottom sooner (`mc.ml.high-learning-rate-trains-faster`). Edge: with curvatures $\lambda_{\max}$ and $\lambda_{\min}$ the best fixed step is $\frac{2}{\lambda_{\max} + \lambda_{\min}}$, and the error shrinks by $\frac{\kappa - 1}{\kappa + 1}$ per step | N, N, C, P |
| 18 | `lsn.learning.convexity` Convex losses have no bad valleys | Check convexity of squared, log and hinge losses in linear models by second derivatives or Hessians (1.3 lesson 50), and explain why a local minimum of a convex loss is global | Two descents start on opposite sides of a log-loss bowl, and two on a small network's loss. Which pair must end at the same value? (The bowl's) | `surface-explorer`, `descent-lab` | Trap: convex means a unique minimiser (collinear features give a flat valley of equal minima; a ridge penalty makes the loss strictly convex). Edge: a network's loss is not convex, yet many of its minima are about equally good | C, S, X, C |
| 19 | `lsn.learning.sgd` Stochastic gradients and minibatches | Show that a minibatch gradient is unbiased with standard error falling like $1/\sqrt b$, and explain why constant-step SGD hovers near the minimum | A full gradient over a million rows costs a second; a batch of 32 costs 32 microseconds. How noisy is it? (Its standard error is $1/\sqrt{32} = 0.18$ of one row's) | `descent-lab` (stochastic [mode to add]), `sampling-lab` | Trap: SGD with a constant step converges to the exact minimum. Edge: a decaying step (Robbins and Monro) does converge; data sorted by date or class must be shuffled each epoch, unless the order is the point (lesson 117) | N, C, M, C |
| 20 | `lsn.learning.matrix-gradients` Gradients in matrix form | Derive $\nabla_{\mathbf w}\Vert\mathbf y - \mathbf X\mathbf w\Vert^2 = -2\mathbf X^\top(\mathbf y - \mathbf X\mathbf w)$ by the matrix chain rule, write Jacobians with their shapes, and check by finite differences (extending 1.4 lesson 82) | $\mathbf X$ has rows $(1, 0), (0, 1), (1, 1)$ and $\mathbf y = (1, 2, 2)$. The gradient at $\mathbf w = \mathbf 0$? ($(-6, -8)$) | `derivation-steps`, `matrix`, `code-cell` (`numpy`) | Trap: shapes that do not match ($\mathbf X\mathbf r$ written for $\mathbf X^\top\mathbf r$). Edge: the Jacobian of a map from $\mathbb R^n$ to $\mathbb R^m$ is $m\times n$; a gradient is the transpose of a one-row Jacobian; a central difference has error of order $h^2$ (1.3 lesson 27) | N, X, O, K |
| 21 | `lsn.learning.logistic-softmax-gradients` The logistic and softmax gradients | Derive $\nabla\ell = \mathbf X^\top(\mathbf p - \mathbf y)$ for logistic loss and $\mathbf p - \mathbf e_y$ for softmax cross-entropy, through the softmax Jacobian $\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf p) - \mathbf p\mathbf p^\top$ | Logits $(2, 1, 0)$ and the true class is the first. The gradient of the loss with respect to the logits? ($(-0.335, 0.245, 0.090)$) | `derivation-steps`, `logistic-lab` (softmax) | Trap: differentiating softmax entry by entry and missing the off-diagonal terms. Edge: the gradient's entries sum to zero, because adding a constant to every logit changes nothing | X, N, O, C |
| 22 | `lsn.learning.trace-logdet` Trace and log-determinant gradients: the Gaussian MLE | Use $\nabla_X\operatorname{tr}(AX) = A^\top$ and $\nabla_X\ln\det X = X^{-\top}$ to derive $\hat{\boldsymbol\Sigma} = \frac1n\sum_i(\mathbf x_i - \bar{\mathbf x})(\mathbf x_i - \bar{\mathbf x})^\top$ (canon-gap P10's last identities) | Three return vectors $(1, 2), (3, 4), (2, 0)$. Their maximum-likelihood covariance? ($\begin{pmatrix} 0.667 & 0.667\\ 0.667 & 2.667\end{pmatrix}$) | `derivation-steps`, `matrix` | Trap: divisor $n - 1$ (the unbiased estimator, not the MLE). Edge: with $n\le p$ the MLE is singular, so LDA (lesson 67) on many assets needs shrinkage (1.4 lesson 95); work with the precision $\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1}$ to make the log-likelihood concave | X, N, O, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 17 chooses a learning rate for a factor model fitted by gradient descent; lesson 19 sizes a minibatch for a model retrained every night on a year of ticks; lesson 22 fits the covariance a minimum-variance portfolio uses.
