| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 23 | `lsn.learning.validation-set` The validation set and its wobble | Split data into training and validation sets, and show the validation estimate varies with the split and is biased upwards because the model trains on fewer rows | Re-split the same 100 rows five times: one model's validation error reads 1.9, 2.6, 2.1, 3.0 and 2.2. Which is its error? | `cv-lab` (split) | Trap: one split gives the answer (`mc.ml.validation-split-is-stable`). Edge: with time-ordered data the split must respect time (lesson 117) | C, N, M, S |
| 24 | `lsn.learning.k-fold` k-fold cross-validation | Assign rows to folds, compute the CV estimate and its standard error from fold errors, and say why that standard error is optimistic | Five fold errors 1.2, 0.9, 1.1, 1.4, 0.9: the estimate and its standard error? (1.10 and $0.212/\sqrt5 = 0.095$) | `cv-lab` (k-fold) | Trap: treating the fold standard error as exact (folds share training rows, so their errors correlate). Edge: stratify folds for rare classes; repeating CV with new splits reduces the split's variance, not the estimate's bias | N, N, S, C |
| 25 | `lsn.learning.loocv` Leave-one-out and its shortcut | Compute a leave-one-out residual by refitting and by $\frac{y_i - \hat y_i}{1 - h_{ii}}$, and explain why the shortcut holds for least squares (1.6 lesson 58's hat values) | Points $(1, 1), (2, 3), (3, 2), (4, 5), (5, 4)$. The fitted line misses the last point by $-0.6$ and $h_{55} = 0.6$. Its leave-one-out residual? ($-1.5$; refitting on four points agrees. The LOOCV MSE is 1.81) | `cv-lab` (loocv-shortcut [mode to add]), `regression-lab` (hat values) | Trap: leave-one-out uses the most data, so it is best (`mc.ml.loocv-is-always-best`). Edge: the shortcut fails for the lasso and any tuned model; a point with $h_{ii}$ near 1 has an enormous leave-one-out residual | N, N, C, X |
| 26 | `lsn.learning.cv-choice-of-k` Bias and variance of the CV estimate | Explain how $k$ trades the estimate's bias (training on $\frac{k - 1}{k}n$ rows) against its variance, and default to 5 or 10 | Slide $k$ from 2 to $n$ and watch the estimate's centre and spread over 200 resplits. Where is the spread smallest? | `cv-lab` (k sweep) | Trap: larger $k$ is always better. Edge: CV estimates the error of the procedure trained on that many rows, not of the final model refitted on all of them | C, M, S, C |
| 27 | `lsn.learning.one-se-rule` The one-standard-error rule | Choose the simplest model whose CV error is within one standard error of the minimum | Ridge CV errors by $\lambda$: the minimum 0.82 (standard error 0.05) at $\lambda = 1$; 0.86 at $\lambda = 10$; 0.90 at $\lambda = 30$. Which $\lambda$ does the rule pick? (10: $0.86\le 0.87$) | `error-curves` (one-se) | Trap: always take the lowest CV point (`mc.ml.pick-the-minimum-always`). Edge: "simplest" must be fixed before looking (larger $\lambda$, fewer leaves, lower degree) | P, N, C, S |
| 28 | `lsn.learning.selection-outside-folds` The wrong way to cross-validate | Show that screening features on all rows before cross-validating gives near-perfect accuracy on pure noise, and put every data-driven step inside the folds (ESL 7.10.2) | 50 rows, 5,000 noise features, balanced labels. Keep the 100 features most correlated with the labels, then 5-fold CV a 1-NN classifier. Predict the CV error before running (about 2% over 50 runs; the right way gives about 52%, and the truth is 50%) | `cv-lab` (selection-outside), `leakage-detective` (pipeline) | Trap: select features first, then cross-validate (`mc.ml.select-features-then-cv`). Edge: label-free steps (centring, PCA without $y$) leak far less, but still use test rows' feature values | C, N, M, O |

Transfer screens: lesson 24 reports a default model's CV log loss with its standard error; lesson 28 audits a "100 alpha signals screened, then cross-validated" research note.

**Level LD6: Model selection and the bootstrap** (`lvl.learning.selection`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 29 | `lsn.learning.nested-cv` Selection inflates the score: nested CV | Show that the best of $m$ CV scores is optimistic by about $\text{SE}\times E[\max_m Z]$, and estimate the selected model's error by nested CV | Twenty equally good models each have true accuracy 0.70 and CV standard error 0.02. What does the best look like, on average? ($0.70 + 0.02\times 1.87 = 0.737$) | `cv-lab` (nested), `tuning-lab` | Trap: the tuned model's CV score is its expected score (`mc.ml.cv-score-of-tuned-model-is-unbiased`). Edge: nested CV estimates the tuning procedure, so the final model is retuned on all rows, and its score is the nested estimate, not the inner one | N, O, C, M |
| 30 | `lsn.learning.random-search` Grid against random search | Show that with one important hyperparameter, random search tries more distinct values of it than a grid, and compute how many trials give a 95% chance of landing in the best 5% (Bergstra and Bengio 2012) | Nine evaluations: a $3\times 3$ grid tries 3 values of the axis that matters, random search 9. How many random trials give a 95% chance of one in the top 5%? (59, since $0.95^{59} < 0.05$) | `tuning-lab` | Trap: a grid covers the space (`mc.ml.grid-search-is-thorough`). Edge: scale parameters ($\lambda$, learning rates) are sampled log-uniformly; the "top 5%" is of the space, not of achievable scores | N, C, N, S |
| 31 | `lsn.learning.information-criteria` Subset selection and information criteria | Compute $C_p$, AIC, BIC and adjusted $R^2$ along a stepwise path, compare them with CV, and explain why BIC picks smaller models (recapping 1.6 lessons 46 and 64) | $n = 100$. Model A has 3 coefficients and RSS 120; B has 8 and RSS 110. Which does AIC $= n\ln(\text{RSS}/n) + 2d$ prefer? (A: 24.23 against 25.53. BIC prefers A by more: 32.05 against 46.37) | `regularisation-path` (stepwise) | Trap: $p$-values after stepwise selection mean what they say (`mc.ml.stepwise-p-values-are-valid`). Edge: best subset over 40 features is $2^{40}$ models; forward stepwise can miss the best pair | N, N, C, M |
| 32 | `lsn.learning.bootstrap-632` The bootstrap and the 0.632 rule | Derive $P(\text{a row is never drawn}) = \left(1 - \frac1n\right)^n\to e^{-1}$, and explain why training on a bootstrap sample and scoring on the original rows is optimistic (recapping 1.6 lesson 25) | Resample 10 rows with replacement. The chance a given row is never drawn? ($0.9^{10} = 0.349$; for large $n$, $e^{-1} = 0.368$) | `sampling-lab` (bootstrap), screen from primitives (catalogue 8.2) | Trap: the bootstrap error on the original rows estimates test error (63% of the scored rows were trained on). Edge: the 0.632 estimator mixes out-of-bag and training error, and is itself optimistic for models that interpolate, which is why 0.632+ exists (Efron and Tibshirani 1997) | N, X, C, M |
| 33 | `lsn.learning.generalisation-bound` A first generalisation bound | Combine Hoeffding's inequality (a Chernoff bound, 1.5 lesson 113) with the union bound to show the best of $M$ rules on $n$ independent samples has true accuracy within $\sqrt{\ln(2M/\delta)/(2n)}$ of its observed accuracy with probability at least $1 - \delta$ | 1,000 trading rules tested on 500 independent days. How far can the best rule's hit rate sit above its true rate, at 95% confidence? (Up to 0.103: about 10 percentage points) | `plot-2d`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: the bound says the best rule is probably good (it bounds the gap, not the level). Edge: the bound is loose and needs independence; overlapping returns shrink the effective $n$; for infinite families the VC dimension replaces $\ln M$ (Caltech lecture 7 [searched]) | N, O, C, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 29 reports a tuned boosting model's nested-CV score to a portfolio manager; lesson 33 turns "we tried a million rules" into the number of days needed for the best to mean anything (homework LD6).

### 2.2 Course LS: Linear Models and Shrinkage (`crs.shrinkage`, 5 levels, 26 lessons)

The models a quant uses most, and the penalties that make them predict. The course treats regression as a forecaster, builds features (encodings, interactions, splines, kernels), then shrinks: ridge from three directions (path, geometry, SVD, prior), the lasso from its corners and its threshold, the elastic net for correlated features, and finally the regime where features outnumber rows. Spine: ISLP chapters 3, 6 and 7; ESL chapters 3 and 5; CS229 lectures 2 to 4 [searched]; Tibshirani (1996); Zou and Hastie (2005); Friedman, Hastie and Tibshirani (2010).

**Level LS1: Linear regression as a predictor** (`lvl.shrinkage.predictor`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 34 | `lsn.shrinkage.prediction-interval` Regression as a predictor | Compute a prediction interval $\hat y_0\pm t\sqrt{\hat\sigma^2 + \text{SE}(\hat y_0)^2}$, contrast it with the interval for the mean (recapping 1.6 lesson 52), and see why more data shrink only one of them | At $x_0$, $\text{SE}(\hat y_0) = 0.5$ and $\hat\sigma = 2$. Half-widths of the 95% intervals for the mean and for a new $y$, using 1.96? (0.98 and 4.04) | `regression-lab` (prediction), `plot-2d` | Trap: the confidence band read as the forecast's uncertainty. Edge: far from $\bar x$ both bands widen; with noise that fans out the constant-width prediction band is wrong at both ends | N, N, C, P |
| 35 | `lsn.shrinkage.categorical-encoding` Encoding categories and the dummy trap | One-hot encode a categorical feature against a reference level, explain the rank deficiency of one column per level plus an intercept, and handle levels unseen in training | Three sectors, one column each, plus an intercept: the rank of the 4-column design? (3) | `feature-builder` | Trap: one column per level plus an intercept (`mc.ml.dummy-for-every-level-with-intercept`). Edge: a penalised model can keep every level, because the penalty breaks the tie; a level that first appears at prediction time needs a stated rule (an "other" level, or an error) | N, S, C, O |
| 36 | `lsn.shrinkage.interactions-transforms` Interactions, transforms and hierarchy | Add interaction and transformed features, read two slopes from one model, and keep main effects whenever an interaction is in | Momentum predicts returns more strongly among small firms. Which one column lets a linear model say so? (Momentum times size) | `feature-builder`, `regression-lab` | Trap: a linear model cannot curve (`mc.ml.linear-model-cannot-curve`). Edge: an interaction without its main effects changes meaning when either variable's origin moves | S, C, O, N |
| 37 | `lsn.shrinkage.scaling-pipeline` Scaling inside the pipeline | Standardise with statistics from training rows only, and build the scaler into a pipeline that CV refits in every fold | A scaler fitted on all 1,000 rows, then 5-fold CV. Which statistics saw the test rows? (Every feature's mean and SD) | `feature-builder`, `pipeline-flow` (sklearn) | Trap: fit the scaler once on everything (`mc.ml.scale-on-all-data`). Edge: in a time series even a training-window scaler must not reach forward; rolling statistics are features with their own timestamps (lesson 124) | C, O, S, T |
| 38 | `lsn.shrinkage.missing-data` Missing values | Impute inside the folds, add missingness indicators, and explain why values that are missing not at random carry information | Analysts' earnings forecasts are missing more often for small, distressed firms. Fill with the mean and move on? | `feature-builder` (impute), `value-table` | Trap: imputing with full-sample statistics. Edge: trees can route missing values directly (lesson 91); low-rank matrix completion fills a panel's gaps from its factors (ISLP 12.3) | C, M, S, O |
| 39 | `lsn.shrinkage.glm` Generalised linear models: counts and the exponential family | Fit a Poisson regression with a log link, read coefficients as rate ratios $e^{\beta}$, and place linear, logistic and Poisson regression in one exponential-family pattern (CS229 lecture 4 [searched]; ISLP 4.6) | Trades per minute follow $\ln\lambda = \beta_0 + 0.2x$. One unit more of $x$ multiplies the expected count by? ($e^{0.2} = 1.22$) | `regression-lab` (glm [mode to add]), `dist-explorer` (Poisson) | Trap: least squares on counts (it predicts negative counts; $\ln(y + 1)$ biases). Edge: variance above the mean (overdispersion) makes Poisson standard errors too small; the negative binomial or quasi-likelihood fixes it | N, N, C, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 34 gives a slippage forecast with its prediction interval; lesson 35 encodes exchange and sector for a fill-probability model; lesson 39 models trade counts per minute for an execution schedule.

**Level LS2: Beyond linearity** (`lvl.shrinkage.nonlinear`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 40 | `lsn.shrinkage.polynomial-edges` Polynomials and their wild edges | Fit polynomials of rising degree and show interpolation error exploding near the ends of the range (Runge's phenomenon) | Interpolate $\frac{1}{1 + 25x^2}$ at 11 equally spaced points with a degree-10 polynomial. The largest error on $[-1, 1]$? (1.92, near $x = \pm 0.94$; a cubic spline through the same points errs by at most 0.022) | `basis-lab` (polynomial), `fit-explorer` | Trap: a high-degree polynomial extrapolates calmly (`mc.ml.polynomials-behave-at-edges`). Edge: Chebyshev nodes cut the same polynomial's error to 0.109; outside the data any polynomial is a guess | N, C, P, M |
| 41 | `lsn.shrinkage.splines` Step functions and regression splines | Build piecewise-constant and cubic regression splines from a truncated power basis, and count degrees of freedom ($K + 4$ for a cubic spline with $K$ knots, $K$ for a natural cubic spline, intercepts included) | A cubic spline with 3 knots: how many coefficients, intercept included? (7) | `basis-lab` (knots) | Trap: a spline is one polynomial (`mc.ml.spline-is-one-polynomial`); more knots always fit better out of sample (`mc.ml.more-knots-always-better`). Edge: a natural spline is linear beyond its boundary knots, which calms the edges of lesson 40 | N, N, C, S |
| 42 | `lsn.shrinkage.smoothing-splines` Smoothing splines and effective degrees of freedom | Minimise $\sum_i(y_i - g(x_i))^2 + \lambda\int g''(t)^2\,dt$, read effective degrees of freedom $\operatorname{tr}(S_\lambda)$ between 2 and $n$, and choose $\lambda$ by LOOCV | Slide $\lambda$ from 0 to very large. What do the two ends fit? (A curve through every point; the least-squares line) | `basis-lab` (smoothing), `cv-lab` | Trap: $\lambda$ counts knots. Edge: lesson 25's LOOCV shortcut holds for smoothing splines because they are linear smoothers | C, N, M, P |
| 43 | `lsn.shrinkage.local-regression-gam` Local regression and additive models | Fit local linear regression with kernel weights, and combine one-dimensional smooths into a generalised additive model by backfitting | Volume depends on time of day (a U) and on volatility (rising). One additive model, or a two-dimensional smooth? | `basis-lab` (gam [mode to add]), `fit-explorer` | Trap: additive models capture interactions (they do not unless one is added). Edge: a locally constant fit is biased at the boundary; a locally linear one is not | C, O, S, C |
| 44 | `lsn.shrinkage.kernel-ridge` Kernels: features you never compute | Write kernel ridge regression, $\hat f(\mathbf x) = \mathbf k(\mathbf x)^\top(\mathbf K + \lambda I)^{-1}\mathbf y$, and explain the kernel trick and the representer theorem | A degree-2 polynomial feature map of 100 inputs has how many features, the constant included? (5,151.) The kernel $(1 + \mathbf x^\top\mathbf z)^2$ computes their inner product without building them | `basis-lab` (kernel [mode to add]), `matrix` | Trap: a kernel method is a different model from ridge (it is ridge in feature space). Edge: cost grows like $n^3$ in rows, not in features; an RBF kernel of tiny width memorises; a Gaussian process gives the same mean with a variance (preview) | N, X, C, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 41 fits the intraday volume U by a natural spline for a VWAP schedule; lesson 43 builds an additive model of bid-ask spread on time of day and volatility.

**Level LS3: Ridge** (`lvl.shrinkage.ridge`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 45 | `lsn.shrinkage.uniform-shrink` Shrinking can predict better | Show that multiplying an unbiased estimate by $c < 1$ lowers MSE when its variance is large, with $c^* = \frac{\beta^2}{\beta^2 + v}$ (recapping 1.6 lesson 6) | An OLS coefficient is unbiased for $\beta = 0.5$ with variance 0.25, so its MSE is 0.25. Halve it: MSE? (0.125) | `regularisation-path` (uniform shrink) | Trap: unbiased is best. Edge: $c^*$ needs the unknown $\beta$; ridge chooses its shrinkage from the data through CV | N, P, C, X |
| 46 | `lsn.shrinkage.ridge-path` Ridge and its path | Fit ridge, $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} = (\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X + \lambda I)^{-1}\mathbf X^\top\mathbf y$, trace coefficients against $\lambda$, and compute the one-feature case (extending 1.4 lesson 47) | One standardised feature with $\sum x_iy_i = 10$, $\sum x_i^2 = 20$ and $\lambda = 5$. The ridge slope? (0.4, against OLS 0.5) | `regularisation-path` (ridge) | Trap: ridge removes features (`mc.ml.ridge-sets-coefficients-to-zero`). Edge: a coefficient can change sign along the path when features correlate; the intercept is never penalised | N, P, C, M |
| 47 | `lsn.shrinkage.ridge-geometry` Constraint and penalty | Show that ridge solves least squares inside a disc $\sum_j\beta_j^2\le t$, find where the error ellipse first touches the disc, and link $t$ and $\lambda$ by a Lagrange multiplier (1.3 lesson 125) | Shrink the budget disc until it touches the error ellipses. Drag your guess of the contact point | `penalty-geometry` (L2) | Trap: a budget and a penalty are different methods (`mc.ml.constraint-and-penalty-differ`). Edge: when the OLS point lies inside the disc the constraint is slack and $\lambda = 0$ | P, C, X, S |
| 48 | `lsn.shrinkage.ridge-svd` Ridge through the SVD | Write ridge's fit as $\sum_j\mathbf u_j\frac{d_j^2}{d_j^2 + \lambda}\mathbf u_j^\top\mathbf y$, see the low-variance directions shrink most, and compute $\text{df}(\lambda) = \sum_j\frac{d_j^2}{d_j^2 + \lambda}$ | Squared singular values 10, 1 and 0.1 with $\lambda = 1$. Effective degrees of freedom? ($\frac{10}{11} + \frac12 + \frac{0.1}{1.1} = 1.5$) | `regularisation-path` (SVD panel), `pca-lab` | Trap: ridge shrinks every coefficient by one factor (true only in an orthonormal design). Edge: ridge never needs $\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X$ to be invertible; as $\lambda\to 0$ it tends to the minimum-norm solution (lesson 57) | N, X, C, O |
| 49 | `lsn.shrinkage.ridge-bayes` Regularisation as a prior | Show that ridge is the posterior mode under a Gaussian prior with $\lambda = \sigma^2/\tau^2$ and the lasso under a Laplace prior (recapping 1.6 lesson 78) | Noise variance 4 and a prior variance of 0.5 on each coefficient: which $\lambda$? (8) | `posterior-lab` (MAP), `penalty-geometry` | Trap: the lasso's zeros mean the posterior puts mass at zero (a Laplace posterior has no point mass; its mode is zero, its mean is not). Edge: a flat prior gives OLS; weight decay in networks is this prior (lesson 114) | X, N, C, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 45 shrinks a noisy stock beta towards 1 (the Blume and Vasicek adjustments as a uniform shrink); lesson 48 reads ridge on 30 highly correlated factor exposures through their principal directions.

**Level LS4: The lasso and the elastic net** (`lvl.shrinkage.lasso`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 50 | `lsn.shrinkage.lasso-corners` The lasso: corners make zeros | Show that the $L_1$ ball's corners make exact zeros likely, and fit the lasso path | Swap the disc for a diamond. Where does the error ellipse first touch it? (On a corner, so one coefficient is exactly zero) | `penalty-geometry` (L1), `regularisation-path` (lasso) | Trap: the lasso shrinks proportionally (`mc.ml.lasso-shrinks-proportionally`). Edge: with $p > n$ the lasso selects at most $n$ features | P, C, M, S |
| 51 | `lsn.shrinkage.soft-threshold` Hard, soft and proportional thresholds | Derive by subgradients, in an orthonormal design with objective $\frac12\Vert\mathbf y - \mathbf X\boldsymbol\beta\Vert^2 + \lambda\Vert\boldsymbol\beta\Vert_1$, the soft threshold $\operatorname{sign}(z)(\lvert z\rvert - \lambda)_+$, and match ridge, lasso and best subset to their threshold functions | OLS estimate 0.8 and $\lambda = 0.5$. The lasso estimate? (0.3.) Ridge with the same $\lambda$? ($0.8/1.5 = 0.533$) | `penalty-geometry` (1D), `card-sort` | Trap: mixing conventions: with ISLP's "RSS $+\lambda\sum_j\lvert\beta_j\rvert$" the threshold is $\frac{\lambda}{2}$. Edge: best subset's hard threshold keeps 0.8 whole or drops it to 0; it never shrinks | N, S, X, C |
| 52 | `lsn.shrinkage.coordinate-descent` Coordinate descent: one coefficient at a time | Implement cyclic coordinate descent with soft-thresholding on standardised features, and find $\lambda_{\max} = \max_j\lvert\mathbf x_j^\top\mathbf y\rvert/n$, the smallest penalty that zeroes every coefficient in scikit-learn's scaling | With $\frac1n\mathbf X^\top\mathbf y = (0.3, -0.5, 0.1)$ and $\frac1n\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X = I$: $\lambda_{\max}$? (0.5.) The fit at $\lambda = 0.35$? ($(0, -0.15, 0)$) | `regularisation-path` (coordinate trace), `code-cell` (`numpy`) | Trap: forgetting to update the residual after each coordinate. Edge: warm starts down a decreasing path of $\lambda$ make the whole path cheap (glmnet's strategy); highly correlated features slow convergence | N, N, K, T |
| 53 | `lsn.shrinkage.standardise-first` Standardise first | Show that penalties depend on units, standardise before penalising, and report coefficients on the original scale | Rescale one feature from pounds to pence and refit the lasso. What happens to it? (Its coefficient must shrink 100 times to do the same work, so it now costs almost nothing to keep; a feature measured in thousands of pounds would be dropped instead) | `regularisation-path` (standardise toggle) | Trap: penalties ignore units (`mc.ml.no-need-to-standardise`). Edge: scaling dummies like continuous features changes how hard each is penalised; leaving dummies unscaled is a choice to state | C, O, S, C |
| 54 | `lsn.shrinkage.choose-lambda` Choosing $\lambda$, and what not to claim afterwards | Choose $\lambda_{\min}$ and $\lambda_{1\text{se}}$ from a CV curve, and explain why ordinary $p$-values for lasso-selected features are invalid | The CV minimum sits at $\lambda = 0.02$ with 14 features; within one standard error, $\lambda = 0.08$ keeps 5. Which do you report, and may you quote $p$-values for the 5? (The 5, as a sparser model of equal accuracy; no) | `regularisation-path`, `error-curves` (one-se) | Trap: choose $\lambda$ on the test set (`mc.ml.lambda-from-test-set`). Edge: the selected set changes from fold to fold; report how stable it is, not one list | P, C, M, S |
| 55 | `lsn.shrinkage.elastic-net` Correlated features and the elastic net | Show that the lasso picks one of two twin features arbitrarily and that an added $L_2$ part keeps them together (Zou and Hastie 2005) | Two features correlate at 0.98. Resample the rows 20 times: how often does the lasso keep each? | `penalty-geometry` (elastic net), `regularisation-path` (stability) | Trap: the lasso finds the true one of two twins (`mc.ml.lasso-picks-the-true-feature`). Edge: stability selection reports how often each feature survives resampling | C, M, N, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 52 implements the lasso that selects 8 of 100 candidate return predictors; lesson 55 shows why two value measures (book-to-market and earnings yield) swap in and out of a lasso alpha model.

**Level LS5: High dimensions** (`lvl.shrinkage.high-dimensions`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 56 | `lsn.shrinkage.pcr-pls` Principal components and partial least squares | Regress on the first $m$ principal components, compare with ridge's smooth shrinkage, and contrast PLS, which uses $\mathbf y$ to choose its directions | Keep 1, 2 or 3 components of correlated factor exposures. Which directions does ridge shrink most? (The smallest-variance ones) | `pca-lab`, `regularisation-path` (PCR) | Trap: the top components are the ones that predict $\mathbf y$ (variance in $\mathbf X$ is not relevance to $\mathbf y$). Edge: PCR with every component is OLS | C, N, S, C |
| 57 | `lsn.shrinkage.p-greater-than-n` More features than rows | Show that with $p > n$ least squares has infinitely many exact fits, find the minimum-norm one $\mathbf X^\top(\mathbf X\mathbf X^\top)^{-1}\mathbf y$, and see ridge tend to it as $\lambda\to 0$ (1.4 lesson 87) | 200 candidate signals and 120 months. How many exact fits to the training returns are there? (Infinitely many, if the rows are linearly independent) | `regularisation-path`, `matrix` | Trap: a perfect training fit means something when $p > n$. Edge: the minimum-norm interpolator can still predict tolerably when many small features each carry a little signal (lesson 58) | C, X, M, N |
| 58 | `lsn.shrinkage.double-descent` Double descent | Predict where test error peaks as random features pass $p = n$, and explain the second descent through the minimum-norm solution (ISLP 10.8; Belkin, Hsu, Ma and Mandal 2019) | Fit 50 points with 10, 30, 50, 70 and 200 random ReLU features. Where does test error spike? (At 50, where $p = n$) | `fit-explorer` (random features), `error-curves` (double descent) | Trap: the U-curve is the whole story. Edge: with a tuned ridge penalty the spike disappears, which is why practitioners rarely see it | P, C, S, C |
| 59 | `lsn.shrinkage.many-signals` Transfer: shrinking many noisy signals | Shrink 50 noisy signal estimates towards zero by the James-Stein factor $1 - \frac{k - 2}{\sum_i z_i^2}$ (recapping 1.6 lesson 77), and check next year's performance | 50 signals whose $z$-scores have squares summing to 75. The shrinkage factor? ($1 - 48/75 = 0.36$) | `regularisation-path`, `posterior-lab` | Trap: trust the largest $z$-score most (it is the one most inflated by selection). Edge: shrink towards the group mean when signals share a common premium; when $\sum z^2 < k - 2$ the positive-part rule sets every estimate to zero | N, C, M, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 57 explains why a 200-signal regression on 10 years of monthly data fits perfectly and predicts nothing without a penalty; lesson 59 is the whole lesson.

### 2.3 Course CP: Classification and Probabilities (`crs.classify`, 5 levels, 26 lessons)

Classifiers and the probabilities they emit. The course fits logistic regression by hand and by Newton's method, derives the generative classifiers (Bayes, LDA, QDA, naive Bayes) from class densities, measures classifiers honestly (base rates, precision, recall, ROC, AUC, PR curves, costs), makes their probabilities fit to bet on (calibration, proper scores, Kelly sizing), and closes with margins and kernels. Spine: ISLP chapters 4 and 9; ESL chapters 4 and 12; CS229 lectures 3 to 7 [searched]; Niculescu-Mizil and Caruana (2005) on calibration; López de Prado chapter 10 on bet sizing.

**Level CP1: Logistic regression** (`lvl.classify.logistic`, 6 lessons)

1.6 lesson 65 met logistic regression as maximum likelihood. This level makes it a predictor: probabilities, odds, the fitting algorithm, its failure under separation, and its many-class form.

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 60 | `lsn.classify.why-not-linear` Why not a straight line for a probability | Show that least squares on 0 and 1 labels predicts probabilities outside $[0, 1]$ and lets distant points drag the boundary | A straight line fitted to default flags against a debt ratio reads $\hat p = 0.1 + 0.3x$. What does it say for the safest firm, at $x = -1$? ($-0.2$) | `logistic-lab` (1D), `regression-lab` | Trap: each unit of $x$ adds the same probability (`mc.ml.probability-linear-in-x`). Edge: the linear probability model still estimates an average slope and is used with HC errors in economics; for ranking alone it can do | N, C, S, O |
| 61 | `lsn.classify.sigmoid-by-hand` Fitting the S-curve by hand | Write $p(x) = \sigma(wx + b)$, fit it by dragging midpoint and steepness to raise the log-likelihood, and compute a probability | Fills (1) and misses (0) of limit orders against quote distance. Drag the curve. With $w = 2$ and $b = -1$, the fill probability at $x = 1$? ($\sigma(1) = 0.731$) | `logistic-lab` (fit-by-hand) | Trap: the steepest curve is the best fit (until one misclassified point's log loss explodes). Edge: the midpoint $-b/w$ is where $p = \frac12$; with $w < 0$ the S-curve falls | N, P, C, N |
| 62 | `lsn.classify.log-odds` Log-odds and odds ratios | Read a coefficient as additive in log-odds and multiplicative in odds, $e^w$, and show the change in probability depends on where you start | $w = 0.7$ per unit: odds multiply by $e^{0.7} = 2.01$. From $p = 0.2$, one unit more gives? (0.335.) From $p = 0.5$? (0.668) | `logistic-lab` (log-odds axis) | Trap: the coefficient is a change in probability (`mc.ml.probability-linear-in-x`). Edge: a coefficient's size depends on the feature's units, so compare standardised coefficients; a dummy's $e^w$ is an odds ratio between groups | N, N, C, X |
| 63 | `lsn.classify.newton-irls` Fitting logistic regression: convexity and Newton's method | Show log loss has Hessian $\mathbf X^\top\mathbf W\mathbf X$ with $\mathbf W = \operatorname{diag}(p_i(1 - p_i))$, so it is convex, and fit by Newton's method (iteratively reweighted least squares, 1.3 lesson 33) | Newton's method reaches the optimum in 5 steps; gradient descent with a safe step needs hundreds. What does Newton know that gradient descent does not? (The curvature) | `descent-lab` (Newton), `logistic-lab` | Trap: logistic regression has a closed form like OLS. Edge: when every $p_i(1 - p_i)$ is tiny (near separation) the Hessian is nearly singular and the steps blow up | O, C, X, N |
| 64 | `lsn.classify.separation` Perfect separation | Show that with linearly separable classes the likelihood rises for ever as $w$ grows, so the MLE does not exist, and that a ridge penalty restores a finite fit | Five defaults, every one with a debt ratio above 0.6; no default below it. Fit logistic regression: what happens to the slope? (It grows without bound; software stops with a warning) | `logistic-lab` (separation) | Trap: separated classes give the best fit (`mc.ml.separable-is-easy`). Edge: quasi-separation inside one category (no defaults in one sector) sends that dummy's coefficient to $-\infty$ alone | C, S, N, O |
| 65 | `lsn.classify.softmax` Many classes: softmax and temperature | Extend logistic regression to $K$ classes with softmax, show adding a constant to every logit changes nothing, compute it stably by subtracting the maximum, and use temperature | Logits $(2, 1, 0)$ give probabilities $(0.665, 0.245, 0.090)$. Halve the temperature (logits $(4, 2, 0)$): the probabilities? ($(0.867, 0.117, 0.016)$) | `logistic-lab` (softmax), `mass-editor` (read-only) | Trap: softmax picks the largest logit (`mc.ml.softmax-is-argmax`). Edge: logits $(1000, 999, 998)$ overflow $e^{1000}$; subtracting 1000 first gives the same probabilities (1.1's log-sum-exp lesson) | N, N, C, T |

Transfer screens: lesson 61 fits a limit order's fill probability against its distance from the mid; lesson 62 reads a default model's odds ratio for a downgrade; lesson 65 classifies next-day moves into down, flat and up.

**Level CP2: Generative classifiers** (`lvl.classify.generative`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 66 | `lsn.classify.bayes-classifier` The Bayes classifier and the Bayes error | Derive the Bayes classifier from class densities and priors, and compute the Bayes error rate for two normal classes | Two equally likely classes, $N(0, 1)$ and $N(2, 1)$. The best boundary, and the error no classifier can beat? ($x = 1$; $\Phi(-1) = 0.159$) | `classifier-plane` (Bayes), `dist-explorer` | Trap: the boundary passes near the points (`mc.ml.boundary-follows-the-points`). Edge: unequal priors move the boundary towards the rarer class's mean; with equal variances the boundary is a single point | N, N, C, S |
| 67 | `lsn.classify.lda` Linear discriminant analysis | Derive LDA's linear discriminant from Gaussian classes with a shared covariance (the quadratic terms cancel), estimate it with a pooled covariance, and shift the boundary with priors | The same classes, with prior 0.8 on the first. Where does the boundary move? ($1 + \frac{\ln 4}{2} = 1.693$) | `classifier-plane` (LDA), `derivation-steps` | Trap: LDA is useless unless the features are normal (it often classifies well anyway). Edge: with $p$ near $n$ the pooled covariance is singular; shrink it (lesson 22's edge) | X, N, O, C |
| 68 | `lsn.classify.qda` Quadratic discriminant analysis | Compare QDA's per-class covariances (quadratic boundaries) with LDA by counting parameters and by bias against variance | 10 features, 2 classes: covariance parameters for LDA 55, for QDA 110. Which do you trust with 60 rows per class? (Usually LDA) | `classifier-plane` (QDA against LDA) | Trap: the more flexible model always wins. Edge: classes with equal means and different variances (calm against volatile days) defeat LDA entirely and QDA separates them | N, C, M, P |
| 69 | `lsn.classify.naive-bayes` Naive Bayes | Fit naive Bayes, show that its independence assumption double-counts correlated evidence, and explain why it still ranks well | A word that moves a stock appears in 80% of moving headlines and 30% of others; its constant companion word always appears with it. True chance of a move given both, with prior $\frac12$? (0.727.) Naive Bayes says? (0.877) | `contingency-table`, `classifier-plane` (naive Bayes) | Trap: naive Bayes probabilities are calibrated. Edge: a word never seen with one class gives probability 0 unless counts are smoothed (Laplace) | N, N, C, M |
| 70 | `lsn.classify.which-classifier` Which classifier where | Predict which of logistic regression, LDA, QDA, naive Bayes and k-NN wins on four data shapes (ISLP 4.5), then check by CV | Four scenes: a linear boundary with normal classes, heavy-tailed classes, a curved boundary, a very irregular boundary. Predict the winner of each | `classifier-plane` (predict mode), `cv-lab` | Trap: one classifier is best everywhere. Edge: with few rows the rigid model wins even when the truth curves (lesson 9's bias against variance) | S, C, M, P |

Transfer screens: lesson 67 classifies days into risk-on and risk-off from two factor returns; lesson 68 separates calm and volatile regimes by their variances.

**Level CP3: Evaluating classifiers** (`lvl.classify.evaluation`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 71 | `lsn.classify.confusion-base-rate` The confusion matrix and the base rate | Build a confusion matrix at a threshold and show accuracy misleads when the base rate is 1% | 1% of a desk's orders carry a keying error. A model that flags none scores? (99% accuracy) | `threshold-lab`, `contingency-table` (confusion), `icon-array` | Trap: 99% accuracy is excellent (`mc.ml.accuracy-is-enough`). Edge: balanced accuracy and Cohen's kappa correct for the base rate | N, C, M, N |
| 72 | `lsn.classify.precision-recall` Precision, recall and $F_1$ | Compute precision, recall, specificity and $F_1$ from counts, and say which errors each ignores | 40 true positives, 60 false positives, 10 false negatives, 890 true negatives. Precision, recall, $F_1$, accuracy? (0.40, 0.80, 0.533, 0.93) | `threshold-lab`, `contingency-table` | Trap: $F_1$ rewards true negatives (it ignores them). Edge: precision is undefined when nothing is flagged; report it with the count flagged | N, N, S, C |
| 73 | `lsn.classify.roc-auc` ROC and AUC | Trace an ROC curve by moving the threshold, and compute AUC as the chance a random positive outscores a random negative, ties counted half | Positives score 0.9, 0.7, 0.6, 0.3; negatives 0.8, 0.5, 0.4, 0.2. AUC by counting pairs? ($\frac{11}{16} = 0.6875$) | `threshold-lab` (ROC, pair count) | Trap: AUC measures calibration (`mc.ml.auc-measures-calibration`). Edge: any increasing transform of the scores leaves AUC unchanged; AUC says nothing about the threshold you will use | N, P, C, M |
| 74 | `lsn.classify.pr-imbalance` Precision-recall under imbalance | Show the ROC point is unchanged by the base rate while precision collapses, and use PR curves for rare events | True-positive rate 0.8, false-positive rate 0.1. Precision at a 10% base rate, and at 1%? (0.471; 0.075) | `threshold-lab` (ROC and PR) | Trap: an unchanged ROC means unchanged usefulness (`mc.ml.roc-hides-base-rate-so-its-fine`). Edge: the PR curve's baseline is the base rate, not one half | N, N, C, M |
| 75 | `lsn.classify.cost-threshold` Thresholds from costs | Derive the cost-minimising threshold $p^* = \frac{c_{FP}}{c_{FP} + c_{FN}}$ for calibrated probabilities, and apply it | Missing a default costs 9 times a false alarm. Classify as "default" above which probability? (0.1) | `threshold-lab` (cost mode) | Trap: classify at one half (`mc.ml.threshold-must-be-half`). Edge: the rule needs calibrated probabilities; with an uncalibrated model choose the threshold on validation data | N, X, C, P |
| 76 | `lsn.classify.imbalance` Class imbalance and resampling | Correct probabilities after training on a balanced resample by the prior shift, and compare reweighting with resampling | Trained on a 50/50 resample; the true base rate is 2%. The model says 0.8: the probability on the real population? (Odds $4\times\frac{0.02}{0.98} = 0.082$, so 0.075) | `threshold-lab` (imbalance), `calibration-lab` | Trap: oversampling the rare class adds information. Edge: synthetic oversampling (SMOTE) done before the split puts near-copies of test rows into training | N, C, M, O |

Transfer screens: lesson 71 scores an order-error detector; lesson 75 sets the threshold of a margin-call model from the costs of each error; lesson 76 corrects a fraud model trained on a balanced sample.

**Level CP4: Probabilities you can bet on** (`lvl.classify.calibration`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 77 | `lsn.classify.calibration` Calibration and reliability diagrams | Read a reliability diagram, and tell over-confident from under-confident models | A model says 0.9 on 100 cases and 70 happen. Over- or under-confident? (Over) | `calibration-lab` | Trap: a good ranker gives honest probabilities (`mc.ml.high-auc-means-calibrated`). Edge: calibration on average can hide miscalibration inside groups (by sector, by volatility regime) | C, P, M, N |
| 78 | `lsn.classify.proper-scores` Proper scoring rules | Compute Brier score and log loss, show both are proper (expected score is best at the true probability), and show accuracy is not | Forecasts 0.9, 0.7, 0.2, 0.6, 0.1; outcomes 1, 0, 0, 1, 0. Brier score? (0.142; log loss 0.430) | `calibration-lab`, `loss-lab` | Trap: the Brier score counts right answers (`mc.ml.brier-is-accuracy`). Edge: always forecasting the base rate is perfectly calibrated and useless; Murphy's decomposition splits Brier into reliability, resolution and uncertainty | N, N, C, X |
| 79 | `lsn.classify.recalibration` Recalibration: Platt and isotonic | Recalibrate scores with Platt scaling (a logistic fit on the scores) or isotonic regression (pool adjacent violators), fitted on data the model never trained on | A boosted model's scores bunch near 0 and 1. On which rows do you fit the recalibration? (A held-out split) | `calibration-lab` (recalibrate) | Trap: recalibrate on the test set (`mc.ml.isotonic-on-test-data`). Edge: isotonic regression is a step function and overfits small sets; Platt assumes an S-shaped distortion | C, O, M, N |
| 80 | `lsn.classify.bet-sizing` From probability to position | Size an even-money bet by Kelly, $f^* = 2p - 1$ (extending 1.5 lesson 154), and show the cost of acting on an over-confident probability | The model says 0.6; the truth is 0.55. Betting the model's Kelly fraction 0.2 instead of the true 0.1: growth per bet? (About $-0.0001$, against $+0.0050$) | `calibration-lab` (sizing), `game-lab` (Kelly) | Trap: bet more when unsure (`mc.ml.bet-more-when-unsure`). Edge: betting twice the Kelly fraction grows at about zero; half Kelly gives up a quarter of the growth for much smaller drawdowns; López de Prado sizes from a test statistic of $p$ | N, N, C, M |
| 81 | `lsn.classify.sign-prediction` Transfer: predicting the sign of next month's return | Compute expected profit from hit rate and payoff sizes, and choose a confidence threshold that maximises it on validation data | A sign model is right 52% of the time, but its wins average 0.8% and its losses 1.0%. Expected profit per bet? ($0.52\times 0.8 - 0.48\times 1.0 = -0.064\%$) | `threshold-lab` (cost mode) | Trap: accuracy above 50% means profit. Edge: the profit-maximising threshold is itself chosen on data, so it is optimistic (lesson 29) | N, P, C, M |

Transfer screens: the whole level is the bridge to López de Prado's bet sizing (chapter 10) and to 3.3's betting games.

**Level CP5: Margins and kernels** (`lvl.classify.kernels`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 82 | `lsn.classify.maximal-margin` The maximal-margin classifier | Find the maximal-margin hyperplane, compute its width $\frac{2}{\Vert\mathbf w\Vert}$, and identify the support vectors | Tap the points that hold the boundary in place, then move any other point: nothing changes. With $\mathbf w = (3, 4)$, the margin's width? (0.4) | `classifier-plane` (SVM) | Trap: every point moves the boundary (`mc.ml.svm-uses-all-points`). Edge: when no separating hyperplane exists the problem has no solution, which is why the soft margin exists | N, M, C, P |
| 83 | `lsn.classify.soft-margin-hinge` The soft margin and the hinge loss | Write the soft-margin SVM as hinge loss plus an $L_2$ penalty, compute hinge losses, and choose $C$ by CV | Points with margins $y f(x)$ of 2, 0.5 and $-1$. Their hinge losses? (0, 0.5 and 2) | `loss-lab` (margin), `classifier-plane` | Trap: a larger $C$ regularises more (a smaller $C$ does). Edge: a point exactly on the margin costs nothing yet is a support vector | N, C, M, S |
| 84 | `lsn.classify.kernel-trick` The kernel trick | Show the polynomial kernel equals an inner product of explicit features, and draw curved boundaries with an RBF kernel | $\mathbf x = (1, 2)$, $\mathbf z = (3, 1)$: $(1 + \mathbf x^\top\mathbf z)^2 = 36$. Check it with the six-feature map $(1, \sqrt2 x_1, \sqrt2 x_2, x_1^2, x_2^2, \sqrt2 x_1x_2)$ (36) | `classifier-plane` (RBF), `matrix` | Trap: kernels belong to SVMs (lesson 44 used one in ridge). Edge: a large RBF $\gamma$ memorises the training set; a kernel matrix must be positive semi-definite | N, X, C, P |
| 85 | `lsn.classify.svm-vs-logistic` SVM against logistic regression | Compare hinge and log loss as functions of the margin, explain why SVM outputs are not probabilities, and choose between the two | Two models draw the same boundary; one also says how sure it is. Which can size a bet? (Logistic regression) | `loss-lab` (compare), `calibration-lab` | Trap: SVM decision values are probabilities. Edge: Platt scaling on held-out data converts SVM scores into probabilities (lesson 79) | C, S, O, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 84 separates two volatility regimes in a curved region of two features; lesson 85 decides which model drives position size in lesson 80.

### 2.4 Course TN: Trees, Ensembles and Networks (`crs.ensembles`, 6 levels, 31 lessons)

The non-linear learners and how to read them. Trees from impurity and pruning; bagging and forests from the variance of an average; boosting as gradient descent in function space, down to XGBoost's second-order gain; how to interpret any model without fooling yourself; then neural networks at an intuitive but derived level: what hidden units and depth buy, backpropagation in matrix form, and the practice of training. Spine: ISLP chapters 8 and 10; ESL chapters 9, 10, 15 and 16; CS229 lectures 10 to 12 [searched]; Breiman (2001); Friedman (2001); Chen and Guestrin (2016); Goodfellow, Bengio and Courville chapters 6 to 8; 3Blue1Brown's neural-network series.

**Level TN1: Decision trees** (`lvl.ensembles.trees`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 86 | `lsn.ensembles.tree-by-hand` Grow a tree by hand | Grow a classification tree by axis-aligned splits, and read it as a set of boxes each with one prediction | Cut the plane at most three times until each box is nearly one colour; the tree grows beside the plot | `tree-builder` | Trap: a tree can cut at any angle (`mc.ml.tree-splits-diagonally`). Edge: a diagonal boundary needs a staircase of many splits | P, C, S, N |
| 87 | `lsn.ensembles.impurity` Gini, entropy and misclassification | Compute Gini, entropy and misclassification for a node and the weighted impurity of a split, and show why Gini and entropy prefer pure children | Parent $(400, 400)$. Split A gives $(300, 100)$ and $(100, 300)$; split B gives $(200, 400)$ and $(200, 0)$. Both misclassify 200 points. Which does Gini prefer? (B: weighted Gini 0.333 against 0.375) | `split-scorer` (chord [mode to add]) | Trap: split to reduce the error count (`mc.ml.misclassification-error-for-splitting`); Gini is the error rate (`mc.ml.gini-is-error-rate`). Edge: the Gini of $(6, 4)$ is 0.48, the chance two draws with replacement disagree; strict concavity is why it rewards pure nodes | N, N, C, S |
| 88 | `lsn.ensembles.regression-trees` Regression trees | Split a one-dimensional scatter to minimise RSS, put thresholds midway between neighbours, and predict leaf means | $y = 1, 2, 1, 7, 8, 7$ at $x = 1, \dots, 6$. Best single split? (Between 3 and 4, at 3.5: RSS 1.33) | `split-scorer` (RSS), `tree-builder` (1D) | Trap: thresholds sit on data values (`mc.ml.split-at-a-data-value`). Edge: every split candidate between two neighbours gives the same training fit, so the midpoint is a convention | N, P, N, C |
| 89 | `lsn.ensembles.pruning` Cost-complexity pruning | Grow a deep tree and prune it by cost-complexity, computing each internal node's weakest-link value $\frac{R(t) - R(T_t)}{\lvert T_t\rvert - 1}$ | A subtree with 4 leaves has error 0.10; collapsed to a leaf it has 0.16. Above which $\alpha$ does pruning it pay? ($\frac{0.16 - 0.10}{4 - 1} = 0.02$) | `tree-builder` (prune), `error-curves` | Trap: grow until every leaf is pure (`mc.ml.deeper-tree-is-better`). Edge: the pruned trees form a nested sequence, so CV chooses among a handful of trees, not every subtree | N, O, C, S |
| 90 | `lsn.ensembles.tree-instability` Trees are unstable | Show small changes to the data reshape a tree, and measure its variance by resampling | Resample 200 rows ten times: how often is the root split the same? | `tree-builder` (resample), `bias-variance-lab` (tree) | Trap: similar data give similar trees (`mc.ml.trees-are-stable`). Edge: the instability is exactly what bagging averages away (lesson 92) | C, O, N, S |
| 91 | `lsn.ensembles.trees-vs-linear` What trees can and cannot do | Say what trees do well (interactions, invariance to monotone transforms of features, missing values) and badly (additive linear structure, smooth boundaries, extrapolation) | A tree trained on $x$ from 0 to 10 with $y = 2x$. Its prediction at $x = 20$? (Its last leaf's mean: never above 20) | `fit-explorer` (tree against linear) | Trap: trees need standardised features (they do not). Edge: invariance holds for transforms of the features, not of the target | C, O, N, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 88 splits a fill-rate series by quote distance; lesson 91 shows why a tree on price levels cannot forecast a new all-time high.

**Level TN2: Bagging and random forests** (`lvl.ensembles.forests`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 92 | `lsn.ensembles.bagging` Bagging and the variance of an average | Derive $\operatorname{Var}\left(\frac1B\sum_b\hat f_b\right) = \rho\sigma^2 + \frac{1 - \rho}{B}\sigma^2$ for identically distributed predictions with pairwise correlation $\rho$, and show bagging cuts variance, not bias | $\rho = 0.5$, $\sigma^2 = 1$, $B = 100$: the average's variance? (0.505, and never below 0.5 however many trees) | `ensemble-lab` (bagging), `derivation-steps` | Trap: averaging trees fixes their bias (`mc.ml.bagging-reduces-bias`). Edge: bagging a stable model (least squares) changes almost nothing | X, N, C, O |
| 93 | `lsn.ensembles.oob` Out-of-bag error | Compute out-of-bag predictions and error, and explain why they need no separate hold-out | 500 trees. In how many is a given row out of bag, on average? (About 184, from $e^{-1}$) | `ensemble-lab` (oob) | Trap: OOB error needs separate data (`mc.ml.oob-needs-a-holdout`). Edge: on overlapping time-series labels OOB rows have near-copies in the bag, so OOB error flatters (lesson 117) | N, C, S, N |
| 94 | `lsn.ensembles.random-forest` Random forests decorrelate | Show that trying $m$ of $p$ features per split lowers the correlation between trees and the forest's variance; default $m = \sqrt p$ for classification | Feature sampling cuts $\rho$ from 0.6 to 0.2 while each tree's variance rises from 1.0 to 1.3. With 500 trees, the forest's variance before and after? (0.601; 0.262) | `ensemble-lab` (forest) | Trap: weaker trees make a weaker forest. Edge: with many irrelevant features a small $m$ offers mostly junk at each split | N, C, M, P |
| 95 | `lsn.ensembles.more-trees` More trees do not overfit; what does | Show test error flattens rather than rises as trees are added, and find what does overfit a forest on noisy data (deep trees, tiny leaves) | 50, 500 and 5,000 trees on a low-signal return set. Sketch the test error against the number of trees | `ensemble-lab`, `error-curves` | Trap: too many trees overfit (`mc.ml.more-trees-overfit`). Edge: on low-signal data the minimum leaf size moves test error far more than the tree count | C, P, M, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 95 tunes a forest on 10 years of monthly stock features, where a minimum leaf of 1 memorises noise.

**Level TN3: Boosting** (`lvl.ensembles.boosting`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 96 | `lsn.ensembles.boosting-residuals` Boosting: fit what is left | Fit stumps to the current residuals stage by stage with shrinkage $\nu$, and watch the fit climb | Target 10, start at 0, each round's stump predicts the current residual exactly, $\nu = 0.1$. Prediction after 10 rounds? ($10(1 - 0.9^{10}) = 6.51$) | `boosting-lab` (predict mode) | Trap: boosting averages independent trees (`mc.ml.boosting-averages-like-bagging`). Edge: with $\nu = 1$ and deep trees boosting memorises in a round or two | N, P, C, M |
| 97 | `lsn.ensembles.gradient-boosting` Gradient boosting is gradient descent on functions | Derive pseudo-residuals as the negative gradient of the loss ($y - F$ for squared loss, $\operatorname{sign}(y - F)$ for absolute, $y - p$ for log loss), and see boosting as gradient descent in function space (Friedman 2001) | Log loss, $y = 1$, current probability 0.3. The pseudo-residual? (0.7) | `boosting-lab` (loss switch), `derivation-steps` | Trap: the residual is always $y - \hat y$ in the target's units. Edge: under log loss the leaf value needs a Newton step, not the mean pseudo-residual | X, N, O, C |
| 98 | `lsn.ensembles.early-stopping` Learning rate, rounds and early stopping | Trade learning rate against number of rounds, and stop at the validation minimum | $\nu = 0.3$ for 200 rounds or $\nu = 0.03$ for 2,000: which usually generalises better? (The slow one) | `boosting-lab`, `error-curves` (rounds) | Trap: boosting keeps improving (`mc.ml.boosting-rounds-cannot-overfit`); large steps learn better (`mc.ml.bigger-learning-rate-is-better`). Edge: stopping on the test set is tuning on the test set | P, C, M, S |
| 99 | `lsn.ensembles.xgboost-gain` Second-order gain: XGBoost and LightGBM | Derive the leaf weight $-\frac{G}{H + \lambda}$ and the split gain $\frac12\left[\frac{G_L^2}{H_L + \lambda} + \frac{G_R^2}{H_R + \lambda} - \frac{G^2}{H + \lambda}\right] - \gamma$ from a second-order Taylor expansion of the loss (Chen and Guestrin 2016) | $G_L = -4$, $H_L = 3$, $G_R = 2$, $H_R = 2$, $\lambda = 1$, $\gamma = 0.5$. The gain, and the left leaf's weight? (1.83; 1) | `split-scorer` (gain), `derivation-steps` | Trap: $\lambda$ and $\gamma$ do the same job ($\lambda$ shrinks leaf weights; $\gamma$ charges for each split). Edge: minimum child weight bounds $H$ per leaf; histogram split-finding (LightGBM, Ke and others 2017) trades exact thresholds for speed | N, N, X, C |
| 100 | `lsn.ensembles.adaboost` AdaBoost and exponential loss | Run AdaBoost.M1 by hand and show it minimises exponential loss stagewise (ESL 10.4) | Ten equally weighted points, two misclassified: error 0.2, so $\alpha = \ln 4 = 1.386$. New weights? (0.25 for each misclassified point, 0.0625 for each correct one; the old classifier's weighted error is now exactly 0.5) | `boosting-lab` (AdaBoost) | Trap: AdaBoost gives up on misclassified points. Edge: exponential loss makes it chase mislabelled points (lesson 12) | N, N, C, O |
| 101 | `lsn.ensembles.boosting-in-practice` Tuning boosting in practice | Tune depth, learning rate, subsampling, minimum child weight and monotone constraints, and order their effects | Rank five changes to a default model by their effect on validation log loss | `tuning-lab`, `boosting-lab` | Trap: tune everything at once on one split. Edge: monotone constraints write economics into the model (default risk never falls as the debt ratio rises) | S, C, M, P |

Transfer screens: lesson 99 scores a split of a default model's debt-ratio feature by hand; lesson 101 sets monotone constraints for a credit model a regulator will read.

**Level TN4: Interpreting models** (`lvl.ensembles.interpret`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 102 | `lsn.ensembles.permutation-importance` Permutation importance | Compute permutation importance on held-out data, and read it as the loss from breaking one feature's link to the target | Shuffle one column of the test set: accuracy falls from 0.80 to 0.71. Shuffle another: 0.80 stays 0.80. What does each say? | `importance-lab` | Trap: low permutation importance means no signal (`mc.ml.low-mda-means-useless`). Edge: computed on training rows it rewards memorised features | N, C, M, S |
| 103 | `lsn.ensembles.impurity-importance-traps` The traps of impurity importance | Show impurity importance (MDI) favours many-valued features and splits credit between correlated twins (Strobl and others 2007) | Add a random ID column with 1,000 distinct values: MDI ranks it first | `importance-lab` (id-trap, twin) | Trap: impurity importance is trustworthy (`mc.ml.mdi-is-reliable`). Edge: drop-column importance (refit without the feature) avoids both traps at the cost of one refit per feature | C, O, S, C |
| 104 | `lsn.ensembles.partial-dependence` Partial dependence and ICE | Compute partial dependence and ICE curves, and spot when correlated features make them extrapolate | Sweep the debt ratio while every other feature keeps each row's value. What can the average curve hide? (Opposite ICE curves that cancel) | `importance-lab` (PDP, ICE) | Trap: a flat partial dependence means no effect. Edge: sweeping one of two correlated features creates rows that never occur | P, C, M, S |
| 105 | `lsn.ensembles.shapley` Shapley values | Compute Shapley values by enumerating coalitions for two and three features, and check efficiency, symmetry and the dummy property (Lundberg and Lee 2017) | $v(\varnothing) = 0$, $v(\{1\}) = 3$, $v(\{2\}) = 1$, $v(\{1, 2\}) = 6$. The two Shapley values? (4 and 2) | `importance-lab` (Shapley) | Trap: a feature's value is its contribution alone (that would give 3, not 4). Edge: interventional and conditional value functions disagree when features correlate | N, N, O, M |
| 106 | `lsn.ensembles.importance-not-causation` Importance is not causation | Separate "the model uses this feature" from "this feature causes the outcome", and list what an importance ranking can support | Ice-cream sales top a drowning-risk model's importance ranking. Ban ice cream? | `importance-lab`, `card-sort` | Trap: an important feature causes the outcome (`mc.ml.importance-means-causation`). Edge: a causal feature can show zero importance when its twin carries the signal | C, S, O, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 103 explains why a stock ticker column topped an alpha model's importance chart; lesson 105 splits one default prediction into feature contributions for a credit committee.

**Level TN5: Neural networks: representation** (`lvl.ensembles.nn-representation`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 107 | `lsn.ensembles.neuron` A neuron is logistic regression | Redraw logistic regression as a neuron and compute a neuron's output | $\mathbf w = (1, -2)$, $b = 0.5$, $\mathbf x = (2, 1)$. Output with a sigmoid? ($\sigma(0.5) = 0.622$) | `logistic-lab` (neuron), `nn-playground` (one unit) | Trap: a neuron is a new kind of model. Edge: with a linear activation it is linear regression | N, C, S, N |
| 108 | `lsn.ensembles.hidden-units-xor` Hidden units are learned features | Solve XOR with a 2-2-1 ReLU network by hand-set weights, train it, and show that linear layers alone collapse into one | No line separates XOR. Set $h_1 = \operatorname{ReLU}(x_1 + x_2)$, $h_2 = \operatorname{ReLU}(x_1 + x_2 - 1)$, output $h_1 - 2h_2$. Outputs at $(0,0), (1,0), (0,1), (1,1)$? (0, 1, 1, 0) | `nn-playground` (hand-set, train) | Trap: more linear layers add power (`mc.ml.linear-activations-add-power`). Edge: starting every weight at zero keeps the two hidden units identical for ever (`mc.ml.zero-initialisation-is-fine`) | N, M, C, P |
| 109 | `lsn.ensembles.relu-sums` Sums of ReLUs approximate any curve | Build a curve as a sum of ReLU ramps, count its kinks (at most $m$ for $m$ units), and state universal approximation informally | Make $\lvert x\rvert$ from two ReLUs, then a smooth bump from six | `relu-composer` | Trap: ReLU is just a line (`mc.ml.relu-is-linear`). Edge: approximation can need very many units, and says nothing about learning the weights or generalising | N, P, C, M |
| 110 | `lsn.ensembles.depth` Depth multiplies pieces | Show that composing a two-unit tent map $k$ times gives $2^k$ linear pieces, while one hidden layer needs $2^k - 1$ units for the same function (Telgarsky 2016) | Compose the tent map three times. How many pieces? (8) | `relu-composer` (depth), `nn-playground` (regions) | Trap: units count the same however they are arranged (`mc.ml.width-equals-depth`). Edge: depth helps representation, not necessarily training (lesson 113) | N, N, C, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 108 builds an "either factor but not both" feature for a regime signal.

**Level TN6: Neural networks: training** (`lvl.ensembles.nn-training`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 111 | `lsn.ensembles.backprop-matrix` Backpropagation in matrix form | Derive the gradients of a two-layer ReLU network in matrix form ($\delta_2 = \hat y - y$, $\nabla_{\mathbf W_2} = \delta_2\mathbf h^\top$, $\boldsymbol\delta_1 = (\mathbf W_2^\top\delta_2)\odot\mathbf 1[\mathbf z_1 > 0]$, $\nabla_{\mathbf W_1} = \boldsymbol\delta_1\mathbf x^\top$), extending 1.3 lesson 116, and check one by finite differences | $\mathbf x = (1, 2)$, $\mathbf W_1$ rows $(1, -1)$ and $(0.5, 0.5)$, $\mathbf W_2 = (2, -1)$, $b_2 = 0.5$, squared loss $\frac12(\hat y - y)^2$ with $y = 1$. Forward: $\mathbf h = (0, 1.5)$, $\hat y = -1$. Then $\nabla_{\mathbf W_2}$? ($(0, -3)$.) $\nabla_{\mathbf W_1}$? (rows $(0, 0)$ and $(2, 4)$; the dead unit passes nothing) | `dependency-graph` (backprop), `derivation-steps` | Trap: take the gradient along one path (`mc.ml.gradient-from-one-path`). Edge: a dead ReLU passes zero gradient for every input on which it is off; backpropagation costs a small multiple of one forward pass | N, X, O, K |
| 112 | `lsn.ensembles.optimisers` Momentum, RMSProp and Adam | Race SGD, momentum, RMSProp and Adam on a narrow valley, and show Adam's first step has size $\eta$ whatever the gradient's scale (Kingma and Ba 2015) | Adam's first step with gradient 0.001, or with gradient 1,000, at $\eta = 0.01$: how big is it? (0.01 either way, after bias correction) | `descent-lab` (optimisers) | Trap: bigger steps reach the bottom sooner (`mc.ml.high-learning-rate-trains-faster`). Edge: weight decay inside Adam is not $L_2$ regularisation; AdamW separates them | N, C, M, S |
| 113 | `lsn.ensembles.initialisation` Initialisation and vanishing signals | Derive the weight variance $\frac{2}{n_{\text{in}}}$ that keeps ReLU activations' second moment constant through layers (He and others 2015), and show vanishing and exploding signals otherwise | Weights with variance $\frac{1}{n_{\text{in}}}$ through 10 ReLU layers: the second moment of the activations is multiplied by? ($0.5^{10} = 0.001$.) With $\frac{3}{n_{\text{in}}}$? ($1.5^{10} = 57.7$) | `nn-playground` (depth statistics) | Trap: start every weight at zero (`mc.ml.zero-initialisation-is-fine`). Edge: Glorot's $\frac{1}{n_{\text{in}}}$ suits tanh; residual connections and normalisation layers relax the constraint | N, X, C, M |
| 114 | `lsn.ensembles.nn-regularisation` Overfitting and its remedies | Regularise a large network on few points with weight decay, dropout and early stopping, and name each (ridge; an averaged ensemble; a limit on the path) | 50 points and 1,000 parameters: training loss falls to 0. Which of three remedies moves test error most here? | `nn-playground`, `error-curves` (epochs) | Trap: bigger networks are always better (`mc.ml.more-units-always-better`). Edge: inverted dropout with $p = 0.5$ doubles the kept units' outputs in training, so nothing changes at prediction | C, M, N, S |
| 115 | `lsn.ensembles.sequences` Sequences: filters, recurrence and attention | Write a convolution, an EWMA recurrence and attention as weighted averages of past inputs, compute their weights, and spot a filter that reads the future | EWMA with $\alpha = 0.9$: the weight on the input 10 steps back? ($0.1\times 0.9^{10} = 0.035$.) Attention scores $(2, 1, 0)$ give weights? ($(0.665, 0.245, 0.090)$) | `sequence-filter` | Trap: a recurrent state remembers equally (`mc.ml.rnn-has-no-memory-decay`); attention is not an average (`mc.ml.attention-is-magic`); a centred window is a smoother average (`mc.ml.centred-window-is-fine`). Edge: a causal mask is what stops attention reading tomorrow | N, N, C, M |
| 116 | `lsn.ensembles.networks-in-quant` Where networks fit in quant work | Run linear, forest, boosting and a small network on one noisy panel with time-ordered folds, and explain why trees usually win on tabular data (Grinsztajn, Oyallon and Varoquaux 2022) while networks help with raw, high-dimensional inputs (Gu, Kelly and Xiu 2020) | Predict the ranking of four models on a low-signal monthly return panel, then run them | `ml-workbench` (arena) | Trap: deeper is better on tabular data. Edge: networks pay off on order-book images, text and many weak, smooth interactions; the comparison must use the same folds and the same trial budget | S, C, O, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 115 builds a causal attention filter over a return series; lesson 116 is the transfer.

### 2.5 Course FD: Machine Learning on Financial Data (`crs.finml`, 7 levels, 36 lessons)

What changes when the rows are dates. Validation through time (walk-forward, purging, embargo, CPCV), every class of leakage and how to detect it, labels that respect paths and overlaps (triple barrier, meta-labels, uniqueness weights, the sequential bootstrap), features built from market data, unsupervised structure in returns, overfitting by model search, and the capstone that is the exit. Spine: López de Prado chapters 2 to 8, 10 and 12; Bergmeir, Hyndman and Koo (2018); Kaufman, Rosset and Perlich (2012); ISLP chapter 12 for FD5; 2.3 course RM cited for backtest statistics (decision M8).

**Level FD1: Validation through time** (`lvl.finml.time-validation`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 117 | `lsn.finml.shuffled-kfold` Shuffled folds flatter time series | Show shuffled k-fold flatters a model on overlapping time-series labels, and state when ordinary k-fold is still valid (an autoregression with serially uncorrelated errors, Bergmeir, Hyndman and Koo 2018) | 20-day forward-return labels sampled daily. Predict which scheme reports higher accuracy, shuffled folds or blocked folds, then run both | `time-cv-lab` (compare) | Trap: shuffled folds work for any data (`mc.ml.shuffled-kfold-is-fine-for-time-series`). Edge: k-fold is valid for a pure AR model whose errors are uncorrelated; add one overlapping label and it is not (2.2 lesson 21 is the time-series half) | C, O, N, S |
| 118 | `lsn.finml.walk-forward` Walk-forward: expanding and rolling | Lay out expanding and rolling walk-forward splits and count their out-of-sample observations | 8 years of daily data, first training window 2 years, test 6 months, step 6 months. How many splits? (12) | `time-cv-lab` (walk-forward) | Trap: tuning on the walk-forward and still calling it out of sample. Edge: an expanding window adapts slowly to a regime change; a rolling one forgets old data on purpose | N, S, C, O |
| 119 | `lsn.finml.overlapping-labels` Overlapping labels share returns | Draw labels as spans $[t_0, t_1]$, count how many cover each day, and explain why overlapping labels are not independent | 20-day forward returns labelled every day. How many labels cover a given day in the middle of the sample? (20) | `time-cv-lab` (spans), `span-track`, `labelling-lab` (weights) | Trap: each label is a separate observation (`mc.ml.overlapping-labels-are-independent`). Edge: the effective sample size falls roughly by the overlap; 1.6's standard errors need HAC corrections for the same reason | N, C, S, N |
| 120 | `lsn.finml.purging` Purging | Compute the exact purge set for a test fold from label spans, on both sides of the fold (López de Prado 7.4.1) | Labels span $[t, t + 10]$ in days. The test fold holds labels starting on days 500 to 599. Which training labels must go? (Starts 490 to 499, and 600 to 609) | `time-cv-lab` (purge task) | Trap: purge only after the test fold (`mc.ml.purge-only-after-test`); training on the past needs no purge (`mc.ml.walk-forward-needs-no-purge`). Edge: with closed spans the label starting on day 490 shares only the price on day 500 with the fold; a return-sharing rule would keep it (9 a side, not 10). López de Prado's closed rule is the default because it is the safer of the two | N, I, C, S |
| 121 | `lsn.finml.embargo` Embargo | Add an embargo after each test fold for features with serial correlation, and size it (about 1% of $T$, López de Prado 7.4.2) | $T = 1{,}000$ days: how long an embargo? (About 10 days after the purge) | `time-cv-lab` (embargo) | Trap: purging is enough (`mc.ml.embargo-is-optional`). Edge: no embargo is needed before the test fold, because features there do not see test labels | N, C, O, S |
| 122 | `lsn.finml.cpcv` Combinatorial purged cross-validation | Count CPCV's splits $\binom{N}{k}$ and backtest paths $\frac{k}{N}\binom{N}{k}$, and assemble one path from the split matrix (López de Prado chapter 12; recapped in 2.3 RM5) | $N = 10$ groups, $k = 2$ held out at a time. How many splits, and how many complete paths? (45; 9) | `time-cv-lab` (CPCV) | Trap: the paths are independent samples. Edge: the distribution of path Sharpe ratios still comes from one history (2.3 lesson 28) | N, N, P, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 117 re-scores a published-looking 58% hit rate with blocked folds; lesson 120 purges the folds of the capstone (lesson 149).

**Level FD2: Leakage** (`lvl.finml.leakage`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 123 | `lsn.finml.leakage-taxonomy` A taxonomy of leakage | Classify leaks (target proxies, future-dated features, preprocessing on test rows, sample selection, duplicated rows, test-set reuse) in a described study (Kaufman, Rosset and Perlich 2012; Kapoor and Narayanan 2023) | Audit a one-paragraph study and tap every flaw | `leakage-detective` (audit) | Trap: leakage is only about the target. Edge: the same trade reported by two venues lands in training and test as two rows | N, S, C, O |
| 124 | `lsn.finml.known-at` When is each value known? | Place each feature's "known at" time against the decision time, including publication lags, revisions and time zones | Fourth-quarter earnings are released on 3 February. May a 31 January decision use them? (No) | `leakage-detective` (timeline) | Trap: use today's close to trade today (`mc.ml.close-price-is-known-at-the-close`); database values are what you knew (`mc.ml.restated-data-is-what-you-knew`). Edge: an Asian close is known before New York opens; a London close is not, because London closes after New York opens | S, C, N, O |
| 125 | `lsn.finml.pipeline-leakage` Preprocessing leakage and pipelines | Move scaling, imputation, feature selection and tuning inside the CV loop with a pipeline, and check which rows each step was fitted on | Three pipelines: which one's scaler saw the test rows? | `leakage-detective` (pipeline), `pipeline-flow` (sklearn) | Trap: scaling is too small to leak (`mc.ml.scaler-on-full-data-is-harmless`). Edge: hyperparameter tuning is a pipeline step too, which is why lesson 29 nests it | O, C, S, K |
| 126 | `lsn.finml.target-leakage` Target leakage and target encoding | Detect target proxies and target-encoding leaks, and replace them with out-of-fold encodings | One category's four rows have labels 1, 1, 0, 0. Leave-one-out target encoding gives the 1s 0.333 and the 0s 0.667. What can a tree now do? (Read every label from its encoding) | `feature-builder` (target encoding) | Trap: encode a category by its mean target (`mc.ml.target-encoding-is-free`). Edge: the leave-one-out repair leaks in reverse, as the hook shows; only encodings from other folds are safe | N, C, O, S |
| 127 | `lsn.finml.survivorship` Survivorship and universe leakage | Measure survivorship bias by training and testing on today's survivors against a point-in-time universe with delisting returns (recapping 2.3 lesson 4 where taken) | An ML stock selector trained on today's index members beats the market by 3% a year in a 20-year backtest. Why might it? | `leakage-detective` (universe), `portfolio-sort` | Trap: today's stocks are a fair sample of the past (`mc.ml.survivors-represent-the-market`). Edge: missing delisting returns are not zero; for performance delistings they are often large and negative | N, C, O, S |
| 128 | `lsn.finml.leak-detection` Detecting leaks | Detect leaks with checks: a result too good to be true, a shuffled-label run, a feature-time audit, and adversarial validation | Your model reaches AUC 0.95 on next-day returns. What do you do first? (Look for a leak: a shuffled-label run, a feature-time audit) | `leakage-detective`, `ml-workbench` (audit) | Trap: a great score is cause to celebrate. Edge: adversarial validation (classify training rows against test rows) finds drift, which is not always a leak | O, C, N, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 124 builds a feature timeline for an earnings-drift model; lesson 128 audits a "next-day direction at 70% accuracy" claim.

**Level FD3: Labels and sample weights** (`lvl.finml.labels`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 129 | `lsn.finml.fixed-horizon` Fixed-horizon labels and their flaws | Label events by the sign of the $h$-day return, and list the flaws: the path is ignored, and a fixed threshold means different things in calm and volatile markets | A path falls 8% on its way to finishing up 1%. Its fixed-horizon label? (+1, a win) | `labelling-lab` (fixed) | Trap: the return at the horizon tells the story (`mc.ml.horizon-return-tells-the-story`). Edge: a threshold fixed in percent labels most calm days 0 and most volatile days $\pm 1$ | N, C, O, S |
| 130 | `lsn.finml.triple-barrier` The triple-barrier method | Apply profit-taking, stop-loss and time barriers scaled by volatility, and find each path's first touch (López de Prado 3.4) | Drag the barriers and predict which one each of six paths touches first | `labelling-lab` (triple barrier) | Trap: a tight stop only removes losses (`mc.ml.stop-loss-is-free`). Edge: with close-only data a gap through both barriers is labelled by the close that crossed; the vertical barrier can label by sign or as 0 | P, C, N, S |
| 131 | `lsn.finml.meta-labelling` Meta-labelling | Train a second model that decides whether to act on a primary rule's signal, and compute precision and recall before and after (López de Prado 3.6) | A rule gives 1,000 signals, 550 of them right. A meta-model keeps 400, of which 260 are right. Precision before and after, and recall? (0.55 and 0.65; 0.473) | `labelling-lab` (meta), `threshold-lab` | Trap: higher precision means more profit (there are fewer trades). Edge: the meta-model's probability also sizes the bet (lesson 80) | N, N, C, S |
| 132 | `lsn.finml.uniqueness-weights` Concurrency, uniqueness and sample weights | Compute concurrency $c_t$ and each label's average uniqueness $\frac{1}{\lvert T_i\rvert}\sum_{t\in T_i}\frac{1}{c_t}$, and weight samples by it (López de Prado 4.3, 4.4) | Labels A (days 1 to 3), B (2 to 4) and C (4 and 5). Their average uniqueness? ($\frac23$, $\frac12$ and $\frac34$) | `labelling-lab` (weights), `span-track` | Trap: overlapping labels count as independent rows (`mc.ml.overlapping-labels-are-independent`). Edge: López de Prado also weights by attributed absolute return (4.6), which lets large moves count more | N, N, S, C |
| 133 | `lsn.finml.sequential-bootstrap` The sequential bootstrap | Draw a sequential bootstrap that favours unique labels, and compute the draw probabilities after one draw (López de Prado 4.5) | After A is drawn, the chances that the next draw is A, B or C? (0.231, 0.308 and 0.462) | `labelling-lab` (bootstrap) | Trap: an ordinary bootstrap suits overlapping labels. Edge: with no overlap the sequential bootstrap is the ordinary one | N, O, C, M |

Transfer screens: the level labels a mean-reversion rule's trades for the capstone.

**Level FD4: Features from market data** (`lvl.finml.features`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |

| 134 | `lsn.finml.bars-and-events` Bars and events | Build time, tick, volume and dollar bars from one trade list, and sample events with a symmetric CUSUM filter (López de Prado 2.3 and 2.5) | Ten trades worth 20,000, 15,075, 30,300, 10,080, 25,050, 39,920, 12,012, 18,108, 26,312 and 9,090 dollars. Dollar bars of 50,000: how many complete bars? (3, with 9,090 left over) | `bar-sampler` | Trap: clock time is the natural sampling (`mc.ml.time-bars-are-neutral`); every volume bar lasts as long as the others (`mc.ml.volume-bar-has-fixed-duration`). Edge: dollar bars adjust for price-level changes that tick and volume bars ignore | N, N, C, M |
| 135 | `lsn.finml.fracdiff` Fractional differentiation | Compute fractional-difference weights $w_k = -w_{k-1}\frac{d - k + 1}{k}$, and find the smallest $d$ that passes an ADF test while keeping memory (López de Prado chapter 5; the ADF test as a stated tool, with 2.2 lesson 33 to come) | $d = 0.5$: the first four weights? ($1, -0.5, -0.125, -0.0625$) | `fracdiff-lab` | Trap: returns ($d = 1$) are the right transform (`mc.ml.fracdiff-d-one-is-best`); stationary series forget the past (`mc.ml.stationary-means-no-memory`). Edge: at $d = 0.4$ a weight threshold of $10^{-4}$ keeps 282 weights, so the fixed window needs a long warm-up | N, P, C, M |
| 136 | `lsn.finml.cross-sectional-features` Cross-sectional features | Normalise features across stocks on each date (ranks, $z$-scores, winsorising), and explain why that removes market-wide level shifts (recapping 2.3 AR2 where taken) | A price-to-earnings feature drifts upwards for a decade; the model learns the decade. Fix it without leaking | `feature-builder` (cross-sectional), `signal-lab` | Trap: normalise over the whole sample (which uses future dates). Edge: ranks drop magnitude information; $z$-scores are pulled by outliers | C, M, S, O |
| 137 | `lsn.finml.clustered-importance` Feature importance in finance | Compare MDI, MDA and single-feature importance when features correlate, and cluster features before measuring importance (López de Prado chapter 8) | Two value measures split their importance between them; cluster them and the cluster ranks first | `importance-lab` (finance), `cluster-lab` (feature clusters) | Trap: low permutation importance means no signal (`mc.ml.low-mda-means-useless`). Edge: single-feature importance has no substitution effect but misses interactions | C, O, S, C |
| 138 | `lsn.finml.target-choice` Choosing the target | Match the target (raw, excess or residual return, rank, sign, volatility) to the decision, and show how the choice changes what is learnable | Predicting raw returns, the model mostly learns the market. Predicting residual returns, what does it learn? (What a market-neutral book can use) | `ml-workbench` (frame), `signal-lab` | Trap: raw returns are the natural target. Edge: volatility is far more predictable than returns (2.2 course VL), so a volatility model may be the more useful one | S, C, O, C |
