ISLP chapter 12 taught on returns (decision M4).

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 139 | `lsn.finml.kmeans` k-means | Run Lloyd's assignment and update steps by hand, show the objective never rises, and seed with k-means++ | Points $(1,1), (1,2), (4,4), (5,4), (2,1)$ with centroids $(1,1)$ and $(5,4)$. The new centroids after one step? ($(1.33, 1.33)$ and $(4.5, 4)$) | `cluster-lab` | Trap: k-means finds the true clusters (`mc.ml.kmeans-finds-the-true-clusters`); the elbow is objective (`mc.ml.elbow-is-objective`). Edge: a cluster can empty (keep its old centroid or reseed); rescaling features changes the clusters | N, S, C, M |
| 140 | `lsn.finml.hierarchical` Hierarchical clustering | Build dendrograms with single, complete, average and Ward linkage, and cut them into clusters | Four assets' distances. Merge them by single linkage, then by complete linkage: where do the two trees differ? | `cluster-lab` (dendrogram) | Trap: neighbouring leaves on a dendrogram are similar (the leaf order is arbitrary up to flips). Edge: single linkage chains through intermediate points | O, N, C, M |
| 141 | `lsn.finml.correlation-clustering` Clustering assets by correlation | Turn correlations into distances $\sqrt{\frac12(1 - \rho)}$, cluster assets, and reorder the correlation matrix (the quasi-diagonalisation of López de Prado 2016) | Correlation 0.6: distance? (0.447.) Correlation $-1$? (1) | `cluster-lab` (correlation), `cell-grid` | Trap: correlation is a distance (`mc.ml.correlation-is-a-distance`). Edge: correlations from short samples cluster noise (lesson 143); this distance treats $\rho = -1$ as farthest, which a long-short book may not want | N, N, C, O |
| 142 | `lsn.finml.gmm-em` Gaussian mixtures and EM | Fit a two-component Gaussian mixture by EM, computing responsibilities and the M step (CS229 lectures 14 and 15 [searched]) | $x = 1$ with components $N(0, 1)$ and $N(3, 1)$ of equal weight. The first component's responsibility? (0.818) | `cluster-lab` (gmm [mode to add]), `dist-explorer` | Trap: EM finds the global maximum. Edge: a component collapsing onto one point sends the likelihood to infinity; calm and volatile return regimes are the finance use | N, O, C, M |
| 143 | `lsn.finml.pca-factors` PCA as a statistical factor model | Fit PCA to a return panel, read the first component as the market, compute the equicorrelated top eigenvalue $1 + (N - 1)\rho$, and compare eigenvalues with the Marchenko-Pastur noise edge $\left(1 + \sqrt{N/T}\right)^2$ (recapping 1.4 lessons 89 to 93) | 100 stocks with pairwise correlation 0.3. The first component's share of total variance? (30.7%) | `pca-lab`, `cluster-lab` | Trap: every component is a factor. Edge: with $N = 100$ and $T = 500$, pure noise produces eigenvalues up to 2.09; components below that edge are noise | N, N, C, M |

The ML half of research overfitting; backtest statistics belong to 2.3 RM3 and RM4 (decision M8).

| 144 | `lsn.finml.best-of-n-models` The best of $N$ models | Compute the expected best of $N$ equally good models as $\text{SE}\times E[\max_N Z]$ (recapping 1.6 lesson 42 and 2.3 lesson 20), and apply it to a model search | 30 configurations, each scored with a standard error of 1.5 accuracy points, none truly better than another. How much better does the best look? (About $2.04\times 1.5 = 3.1$ points) | `overfit-lab` (zoo), `tuning-lab` | Trap: the top strategy of many has an edge (`mc.ml.best-backtest-is-skill`); more trials make the winner more trustworthy (`mc.ml.more-trials-more-confidence`). Edge: correlated configurations inflate less; count effective trials (2.3 lesson 19) | N, C, M, S |
| 145 | `lsn.finml.leaderboards` Leaderboards overfit too | Show that a public leaderboard overfits under repeated submission, and compute the expected best public score of random submissions (Blum and Hardt 2015) | 100 random submissions scored on 500 public labels. The best public accuracy? (About 0.556; the private score is 0.5) | `overfit-lab` (leaderboard) | Trap: the public score measures generalisation (`mc.ml.leaderboard-is-the-test-set`). Edge: the standard error of an accuracy near 0.8 on 900 rows is 0.013, so leaderboard gaps below that are noise | N, C, S, C |

What is the hold-out now? (Validation data) | `ml-workbench` (ledger), `time-cv-lab` | Trap: a quick look costs nothing (`mc.ml.holdout-peeking-is-harmless`). Edge: thresholdout answers queries with noise added, so a hold-out survives many adaptive questions | C, M, O, S |
| 147 | `lsn.finml.research-protocol` A research protocol | Write a protocol that fixes the hypothesis, target, universe and metric first, logs every trial, locks the hold-out and reports the trial count (Arnott, Harvey and Markowitz 2019) | Order eight research steps so that no later step can leak into an earlier one | `ml-workbench` (ledger), `card-sort` | Trap: unlogged trials do not count (`mc.ml.unlogged-trials-do-not-count`). Edge: exploration is allowed when labelled as such and kept off the hold-out | O, M, S, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 145 reads a Kaggle-style competition's public and private ranks; lesson 147 writes the capstone's protocol.

These lessons drive `prj.ml.exit` in `ml-workbench`; their homework is the capstone's milestone pack (section 3.5, FD7).

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 148 | `lsn.finml.frame` Frame the problem | Choose the target, horizon, universe, features with their known-at times, metric and decision for the capstone, before touching the data | "Predict returns" is not a problem. Turn it into one a desk could act on | `ml-workbench` (frame) | Trap: choosing the metric after seeing results. Edge: a model that forecasts volatility well may be the useful one (lesson 138) | S, C, M, O |

Find them | `ml-workbench` (pipeline), `time-cv-lab` | Trap: a green audit proves there is no leak (it checks known leak classes only). Edge: the audit's shuffled-future test catches features that read forward | O, M, C, S |

Convincing? ($t = 0.002/(0.004/\sqrt{10}) = 1.58$: no) | `ml-workbench` (arena) | Trap: compare the best single fold. Edge: fold differences correlate, so even this $t$ overstates the evidence | N, C, M, S |
| 151 | `lsn.finml.write-up` Write it up | Write the capstone's results note: question, data, method, results with uncertainty, what failed, limitations and the trial count (2.3 RM7's note) | Two notes on one capstone: one leads with "AUC 0.61"; the other with "0.53 out of sample after 40 trials, two variants failed". Which passes? | `results-note` | Trap: hiding failures. Edge: an honest null result passes the exit | O, M, C, S |
| 152 | `lsn.finml.explain-without-notes` Explain it without notes | Explain leakage, regularisation and the bias-variance trade-off without notes, under two follow-up probes from Bertie, marked against the exam rubric | "In two minutes, with no notes: why might your cross-validated accuracy be wrong?" | `viva`, `free-response` (timed, no notes) | Trap: reciting definitions without a mechanism or an example. Edge: Bertie's mark is advisory and the learner confirms it; `exm.ml`'s three slots are the gate | viva rubric |

### 2.6 Course MP: Reading Papers and Replicating Momentum (`crs.momentum`, 5 levels, 25 lessons)

How to read an empirical paper and how to replicate one, taught on the simplest of the five: Jegadeesh and Titman (1993). The first two levels are method (anatomy, tables, claims, the ledger, data, rehearsal on known truth, pre-registration, the survival ratio); the last three are the paper (windows, deciles, overlapping cohorts, the $J\times K$ table and its standard errors; risk, the profit decomposition, behavioural stories, crashes, momentum elsewhere; then the replication and its note). Spine: Jegadeesh and Titman (JF 1993); Keshav, "How to Read a Paper" (2007); Bali, Engle and Murray chapters 5 (portfolio analysis) and 11 (the momentum effect) [searched]; Daniel and Moskowitz (2016); Moskowitz, Ooi and Pedersen (2012).

**Level MP1: How to read an empirical paper** (`lvl.momentum.reading`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `lsn.momentum.paper-anatomy` Anatomy of an empirical paper | Map an empirical paper's sections to the questions they answer, and read it in three passes: the skim, the argument, the details (Keshav 2007) | Six questions ("what sample?", "what is the main claim?", "how were the portfolios formed?") and seven sections. Match them before reading a word | `replication-workbench` (read), `card-sort` | Trap: reading front to back in one pass. Edge: an abstract can claim more than the tables show; data filters often live in footnotes | S, O, C, N |
| 2 | `lsn.momentum.finding-papers` Where papers live | Find papers on SSRN, arXiv q-fin, NBER and journal sites, read WorldQuant BRAIN's formulaic alphas as small testable claims, and tell working papers from published versions | One paper exists as a 1991 working paper and a 1993 journal article with different tables. Which do you replicate? (The published version, noting what changed) | `card-sort`, `replication-workbench` (read) | Trap: SSRN download counts measure quality. Edge: arXiv q-fin papers are not refereed; a BRAIN alpha is a formula with a delay and a universe, not a paper, and its backtest is the platform's | C, O, S, N |
| 3 | `lsn.momentum.reading-tables` Reading a results table | Read coefficients, standard errors or $t$-statistics in parentheses, stars and sample sizes, and recover each from the others | A cell reads "0.42 (2.10)" and the table note says $t$-statistics in parentheses. The standard error and two-sided $p$? (0.20; 0.036) | `replication-workbench` (table), `summary-table` | Trap: reading parentheses as standard errors when they are $t$-statistics. Edge: stars hide how many cells were tested (WS1); a $t$ from overlapping returns may need correcting (lesson 16) | N, C, N, S |
| 4 | `lsn.momentum.claims-and-evidence` Claims, identification and threats | State a paper's claim, the comparison that identifies it, and the threats that would make it wrong, as a referee would | "Momentum profits are not explained by risk." Which table must show what for this to hold? | `free-response`, `card-sort` | Trap: a significant coefficient proves the story told about it. Edge: a paper can be right about the fact and wrong about its cause (level MP4) | S, N, C, O |
| 5 | `lsn.momentum.replication-vs-reproduction` Reproduction, replication and extension | Distinguish reproducing with the authors' code and data, replicating from the method with fresh choices, and extending to new data or periods | You run the authors' posted code and get their table. Have you replicated the paper? (No: reproduced it) | `card-sort`, `replication-workbench` | Trap: rerunning their code is replication (`mc.papers.replication-equals-reproduction`). Edge: a "failed" replication may reflect a different, defensible choice, which the ledger (lesson 6) records | S, C, O, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 2 finds the five papers on their free sources; lesson 3 reads a table from a broker's research note with the same eye.

**Level MP2: The replication method** (`lvl.momentum.method`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 6 | `lsn.momentum.ledger` The replication ledger | List every choice that could move a result (universe, filters, dates, weights, skip, rebalancing, return definition, delistings), and mark the ones the paper leaves unstated | The text says "stocks on the NYSE and AMEX". What else must you decide before you can sort? (Share types, price filters, delisting returns, the skip, weights, the first formation date) | `replication-workbench` (ledger) | Trap: unstated details are minor (`mc.papers.unstated-choices-do-not-matter`). Edge: two readings of one line can move a cell by a third (lesson 23) | N, S, C, O |
| 7 | `lsn.momentum.data-for-replication` Data for replication | Name CRSP-style conventions (share codes, exchange codes, delisting returns, point-in-time listings) and the biases of free substitutes (current constituents, adjusted prices, missing delistings) | Free daily prices for today's S&P 500 members back to 1990. Which bias is built in? (Survivorship) | `leakage-detective` (universe), `card-sort` | Trap: today's index members are a sample of the past (`mc.ml.survivors-represent-the-market`). Edge: Ken French's library offers portfolio returns built from CRSP, which avoids survivorship at the cost of fixed sorts | C, O, S, N |
| 8 | `lsn.momentum.rehearsal` Replicate where the truth is known | Run a planted-premium sort on `panel-sim` and recover the premium within its standard error before touching real data | The simulator plants a 0.8% monthly winners-minus-losers premium. Your sort finds 0.71% with standard error 0.18%. Success? (Yes: half a standard error away) | `portfolio-sort`, `replication-workbench` (compare) | Trap: the goal is to match the paper's digits (`mc.papers.published-number-is-the-truth`). Edge: a bug that doubles returns is caught here and nowhere else | N, C, I, S |
| 9 | `lsn.momentum.pre-registration` Pre-register the extension | Write the extension's hypothesis, sample, test and decision rule before downloading post-publication data (recapping 2.3 lesson 2) | You will extend momentum past 1989. Write the one sentence that decides "it survived" before you look | `free-response`, `token-tray` (hypothesis builder) | Trap: deciding what counts as survival after seeing the number. Edge: pre-registration does not forbid exploration; it labels it | O, S, N, C |
| 10 | `lsn.momentum.how-much-survived` How much survived | Define survival as the ratio of out-of-sample to in-sample mean return, and attach a delta-method interval (1.6 lesson 19's method) | In sample 1.0% a month (SE 0.25%), out of sample 0.4% (SE 0.3%), independent. The ratio and its standard error? (0.40; 0.32) | `replication-workbench` (decay), `taylor-builder` (delta method) | Trap: the paper's number holds after it (`mc.papers.in-sample-equals-out-of-sample`). Edge: when the in-sample mean's $t$ is small the delta method misleads, and Fieller's interval is needed | N, N, C, I |

Transfer screens: lesson 6 writes the ledger the exit project starts from; lesson 10 defines the number the exit's `survival-ratio` predicate checks.

**Level MP3: Momentum: the strategy** (`lvl.momentum.strategy`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 11 | `lsn.momentum.winners-keep-winning` Winners keep winning, for a while | Sort stocks on past six-month returns and predict which decile does best over the next six months | Sort 50 simulated stocks into deciles by their last six months. Which decile do you expect to lead next? | `portfolio-sort` (single, one date) | Trap: momentum means buying last month's winners (`mc.papers.momentum-means-buying-last-month`). Edge: at horizons of a week to a month returns reverse; at three to five years they reverse again | C, S, P, O |
| 12 | `lsn.momentum.windows` Formation, skip and holding windows | Set JT's $J$-month formation, skip and $K$-month holding windows on a timeline, and count the history needed | JT's 6/6 strategy with a one-week skip, formed at the start of July 1990. Which returns rank the stocks, and when is the portfolio held? (January to June 1990; from the second week of July for six months) | `portfolio-sort` (timeline), `span-track` | Trap: the skip is a detail (`mc.papers.skip-month-is-optional`). Edge: "12-1" momentum ranks on months $t - 12$ to $t - 2$, 11 monthly returns | S, N, C, P |
| 13 | `lsn.momentum.deciles-wml` Deciles and winners minus losers | Compute decile returns and the winners-minus-losers (WML) return with equal and value weights | The loser decile earns 0.6% next month and the winner decile 1.7%. WML? (1.1%) | `portfolio-sort` | Trap: equal and value weights agree (`mc.papers.equal-and-value-weights-agree`). Edge: equal weights lean on small, illiquid stocks where costs are highest | N, C, M, N |
| 14 | `lsn.momentum.overlapping-portfolios` Overlapping portfolios | Hold $K$ cohorts at once, replace one each month, and average them into one monthly return series (JT's method) | $K = 6$. In any month, how many cohorts are held, and with what weight each? (6; one sixth) | `portfolio-sort` (overlap) | Trap: one cohort's 6-month return sampled monthly is a monthly series. Edge: the first $K - 1$ months of a sample hold fewer cohorts | N, C, O, M |
| 15 | `lsn.momentum.jk-table` The $J\times K$ table | Build and read JT's grid of 16 strategies (32 with the skip) and find its pattern | Scan the grid: which strategies earn most, and does the skip help? | `portfolio-sort` (grid) | Trap: the best cell is the strategy to trade (it is the best of 32). Edge: the cells share most of their stocks, so they are far from 32 independent tests | C, P, M, S |
| 16 | `lsn.momentum.overlap-t-stats` $t$-statistics with overlapping returns | Show that overlapping $K$-month returns sampled monthly inflate a naive $t$ by about $\sqrt K$, correct it with Hansen-Hodrick or Newey-West, and see why JT's cohort averaging avoids the problem | A naive $t$ of 4.0 from overlapping 6-month returns. Corrected by Hansen-Hodrick's uniform weights at 5 lags? ($4/\sqrt6 = 1.63$.) By Newey-West's Bartlett weights at 5 lags? (1.99) | `regression-lab` (HAC), `portfolio-sort` (hansen-hodrick [mode to add]) | Trap: plain standard errors are fine for overlapping returns (`mc.papers.overlapping-returns-plain-t`). Edge: the overlap makes an MA(5) with autocorrelations $\frac{6 - j}{6}$; Bartlett weights shrink them, so Newey-West at 5 lags estimates a variance factor of 4.06 where the truth is 6 | N, N, C, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 16 re-reads a strategy deck that reports $t$-statistics from overlapping annual returns.

**Level MP4: What explains momentum** (`lvl.momentum.explanations`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 17 | `lsn.momentum.risk-adjustment` Is momentum just risk? | Regress WML on the market and on the three Fama-French factors and read the alphas | WML's CAPM beta is near zero and its alpha close to its raw return. What does that rule out? (Market risk as the explanation) | `factor-model-lab` (single) | Trap: three factors explain momentum (`mc.papers.ff3-explains-momentum`). Edge: a near-zero average beta hides a beta that swings with the market's past (lesson 20) | C, N, S, C |
| 18 | `lsn.momentum.decomposition` Where the profit comes from | Derive JT's decomposition of a weighted relative-strength strategy's expected profit into $\sigma_\mu^2$ (cross-sectional variance of expected returns), $\sigma_b^2\operatorname{Cov}(f_t, f_{t-1})$ and the idiosyncratic autocovariance term (after Lo and MacKinlay 1990) | If every stock had the same expected return and returns were serially uncorrelated, what would the strategy earn on average? (Zero) | `derivation-steps`, `portfolio-sort` | Trap: momentum profits prove returns are predictable through time (the $\sigma_\mu^2$ term earns with no serial correlation at all). Edge: JT argue the factor term is small and point to the idiosyncratic term [recalled, unverified] | X, O, C, M |
| 19 | `lsn.momentum.behavioural` Behavioural stories and event time | Trace winners' and losers' event-time returns for 60 months after formation, and match the pattern to underreaction, delayed overreaction and risk stories (Jegadeesh and Titman 2001; Hong and Stein 1999) | After the first year, do winners keep beating losers, flatten, or reverse? Predict, then plot | `portfolio-sort` (event time), `plot-2d` | Trap: profits that persist prove underreaction. Edge: reversal in years 2 to 5 is the evidence offered for overreaction stories [recalled, unverified] | C, P, M, S |
| 20 | `lsn.momentum.crashes` Momentum crashes | Show that after bear markets losers carry high betas, so WML's beta turns sharply negative and a market rebound crashes it (Daniel and Moskowitz 2016) | Loser beta 1.8, winner beta 0.7, and the market rebounds 15% in a month. WML's return from beta alone? ($-1.1\times 15\% = -16.5\%$) | `portfolio-sort` (crash view), `sharpe-lab` (skew) | Trap: a long-short strategy has no market risk. Edge: hedging the time-varying beta or scaling by forecast volatility removes much of the crash risk [recalled, unverified] | N, C, M, P |
| 21 | `lsn.momentum.everywhere` Momentum elsewhere | Compare cross-sectional momentum with time-series momentum (Moskowitz, Ooi and Pedersen 2012), industry momentum (Moskowitz and Grinblatt 1999) and momentum across asset classes (Asness, Moskowitz and Pedersen 2013) | A futures fund goes long every market whose past-year return is positive and short the rest. Is that JT's strategy? (No: it bets on each market's own trend, not on ranks) | `signal-lab`, `card-sort` | Trap: all momentum is one strategy. Edge: time-series momentum is long the market in up-trends, so part of it is market timing | S, C, O, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 20 sizes a momentum book's crash risk after a bear market; lesson 21 reads a trend-following fund's factsheet.

**Level MP5: Replicating Jegadeesh and Titman** (`lvl.momentum.replication`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 22 | `lsn.momentum.replicate-table` Rebuild the main table | Rebuild JT's $J\times K$ table on rehearsal data, then from the learner's own data (Ken French's momentum deciles or a stock file), and compare cell by cell | Your 6/6 cell is 0.82% a month where the paper reports about 0.95% [recalled, unverified]. Close enough? | `replication-workbench` (compare), `code-cell` (`pandas`) | Trap: match the published number exactly (`mc.papers.published-number-is-the-truth`). Edge: monthly data cannot reproduce a one-week skip; the ledger says so | N, C, M, K |
| 23 | `lsn.momentum.unstated-choices` What moves the number | Vary the unstated choices one at a time (price filter, exchanges, weighting, skip) and rank their effects | Dropping stocks below 5 dollars cuts WML by a third. Which reading of the paper is right? (Report both and why) | `replication-workbench` (ledger, sensitivity) | Trap: unstated details are minor (`mc.papers.unstated-choices-do-not-matter`). Edge: a choice that flips the sign is a finding in itself | S, C, M, N |
| 24 | `lsn.momentum.extend-oos` Extend out of sample | Run the pre-registered extension past the paper's sample end, report survival with its interval, and check sub-periods and January | After 1989 the 6/6 WML averages 0.5% a month against 1.0% before, with standard errors 0.3% and 0.25%. How much survived? (Half, with a wide interval) | `replication-workbench` (decay), `sharpe-lab` | Trap: one decade decides. Edge: losers rebound in January in many samples, so momentum's January return is often negative [recalled, unverified] | N, I, C, M |
| 25 | `lsn.momentum.results-note` The momentum note | Write the replication's results note: ledger, table, extension, survival and what failed (recapping 2.3 RM7) | Two notes: "Momentum replicated (Sharpe 0.9)", or "6/6 replicated at 85% of the published return; after 1989 half survived; the skip mattered". Which is the note? | `results-note` | Trap: hiding failed variants. Edge: a replication that finds less is a success if it finds why | O, M, C, S |

Transfer screens: the level is the Replication Lab's first rehearsal (`drl.papers.momentum-rehearsal`, sections 3.6 and 3.11).

### 2.7 Course FF: Factor Models: Fama and French (`crs.factors`, 5 levels, 24 lessons)

Why the CAPM needed help, how SMB and HML are built, how a factor model is tested (time-series regressions, the GRS test, Fama-MacBeth with Shanken's correction), what came after (momentum, profitability and investment, spanning, characteristics), and the default exit replication. Spine: Fama and French (JFE 1993); Gibbons, Ross and Shanken (1989); Cochrane, *Asset Pricing* chapter 12 [check]; Bali, Engle and Murray chapters 5 to 10 (portfolio analysis, Fama-MacBeth regressions, the CRSP sample and market factor, beta, size, value) [searched]; Ken French's data library.

**Level FF1: The CAPM and its failure** (`lvl.factors.capm`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 26 | `lsn.factors.capm-test` What the CAPM predicts | State the CAPM's prediction $E[R_i] - r_f = \beta_i(E[R_m] - r_f)$, test it in time series as $\alpha_i = 0$, and compute an alpha (recapping 2.3 lesson 12) | A portfolio earns 1.2% a month above cash with beta 1.1; the market earns 0.6% above cash. Alpha? (0.54%) | `factor-model-lab` (SML), `regression-lab` | Trap: return above the index is alpha. Edge: one sample's alpha is an estimate with a standard error (homework FF1) | N, N, C, S |
| 27 | `lsn.factors.sml-too-flat` The security market line is too flat | Plot portfolio betas against average returns, see the line flatter than the CAPM's, and connect it to Black, Jensen and Scholes (1972) and Fama and French (1992) | Ten beta-sorted portfolios: does their line pass through cash at beta 0 and the market at beta 1? (It starts higher and rises too slowly) | `factor-model-lab` (SML) | Trap: a flat line means beta is not risk (it means beta does not earn its predicted premium). Edge: borrowing constraints are one explanation (Frazzini and Pedersen's betting against beta, 2014) | C, P, O, S |
| 28 | `lsn.factors.anomalies` Size and value as anomalies | Describe the size and book-to-market patterns that motivated FF (Banz 1981; Fama and French 1992), and why they are anomalies for the CAPM | Sort into size and book-to-market quintiles. Which corner earns most relative to its beta? (Small value) | `portfolio-sort` (double) | Trap: an anomaly is an investor mistake (it may be a missing risk factor). Edge: the size effect is concentrated among the smallest stocks [recalled, unverified] | C, O, S, N |
| 29 | `lsn.factors.errors-in-variables` Estimated betas and errors in variables | Show that estimation error in betas shrinks a second-pass slope by $\frac{\operatorname{Var}(\beta)}{\operatorname{Var}(\beta) + \operatorname{Var}(u)}$, and why portfolios help (Fama and MacBeth 1973) | True betas vary with variance 0.09 and each stock's estimate has error variance 0.09. The slope is shrunk by? (Half.) With 50-stock portfolios? (To 0.98 of the truth) | `factor-model-lab`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: noisy betas only add noise (they bias the slope towards zero). Edge: portfolios cut estimation error but also the spread of betas, so the sort must keep the spread | N, X, C, S |
| 30 | `lsn.factors.fama-macbeth` Fama-MacBeth with Shanken's correction | Run monthly cross-sectional regressions, average the slopes with their standard error (recapping 2.3 lesson 43), and inflate it by Shanken's factor $\sqrt{1 + \lambda^2/\sigma_f^2}$ for estimated betas (Shanken 1992) | A market premium of 0.6% a month and a factor SD of 4.5%. Shanken's inflation factor? (1.009: small for the market, larger for high-Sharpe factors) | `factor-model-lab` (Fama-MacBeth) | Trap: the Fama-MacBeth $t$ needs no correction (`mc.papers.fm-standard-errors-ignore-estimation`). Edge: autocorrelated slopes need Newey-West on the slope series | N, N, C, O |

Transfer screens: lesson 26 tests a fund's alpha claim; lesson 29 explains why stock-level beta regressions find "no premium".

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 31 | `lsn.factors.book-equity` Book equity and the June convention | Apply FF's timing: portfolios formed at the end of June of year $t$ use book equity for fiscal years ending in calendar $t - 1$, December $t - 1$ market equity for B/M, and June $t$ market equity for size | Which book equity and which market equities form the portfolios held from July 1993 to June 1994? (Fiscal-year 1992 book equity; December 1992 market equity for B/M; June 1993 for size) | `span-track`, `leakage-detective` (timeline) | Trap: use the latest book equity in the database (it was not public at formation). Edge: the gap of at least six months between fiscal year-end and June allows for reporting lags | S, C, O, N |
| 32 | `lsn.factors.nyse-breakpoints` NYSE breakpoints | Set size and B/M breakpoints from NYSE stocks only and apply them to every stock | Breakpoints from all stocks would put the size median among thousands of tiny Nasdaq firms. What happens to the "big" portfolio? (It holds nearly all the market's value, and "small" holds microcaps) | `portfolio-sort` (breakpoints) | Trap: breakpoints use every stock (`mc.papers.breakpoints-from-all-stocks`). Edge: NYSE breakpoints leave the small portfolios with many more stocks than the big ones | C, O, S, N |
| 33 | `lsn.factors.six-portfolios` Six portfolios | Form the 2 by 3 size and B/M portfolios with value weights, rebalanced each June | Drag the size median and the 30th and 70th B/M percentiles; read the six portfolios' stock counts and weights | `portfolio-sort` (double, factor) | Trap: equal weights within portfolios (FF value-weight). Edge: firms with negative book equity are left out | P, N, C, S |
| 34 | `lsn.factors.smb-hml` SMB and HML | Combine the six into $SMB = \frac13(SL + SM + SH) - \frac13(BL + BM + BH)$ and $HML = \frac12(SH + BH) - \frac12(SL + BL)$ | Six portfolios return SL 1.2, SM 1.0, SH 1.5, BL 0.8, BM 0.9 and BH 1.1 (% a month). SMB and HML? (0.3 and 0.3) | `portfolio-sort` (factor), `numeric-input` | Trap: HML averages all three B/M groups (it uses only the extremes). Edge: SMB averages across B/M groups and HML across size groups, so each is roughly neutral to the other | N, N, C, S |
| 35 | `lsn.factors.twenty-five` The 25 test portfolios and the bond factors | Form the 5 by 5 size and B/M portfolios that FF set out to explain, and meet TERM and DEF, the bond factors of the 1993 paper | Quintile sorts need how many NYSE breakpoints per variable? (4) | `portfolio-sort` (double) | Trap: test assets are chosen neutrally (sorting on the model's own characteristics flatters it, Lewellen, Nagel and Shanken 2010). Edge: for stocks the bond factors add little once the three stock factors are in [recalled, unverified] | N, C, O, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 31 audits a value backtest that used same-year book equity; lesson 34 rebuilds a factor from a provider's six portfolio series.

**Level FF3: Time-series regressions and the GRS test** (`lvl.factors.grs`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 36 | `lsn.factors.time-series-regression` Time-series regressions | Regress a portfolio's excess return on Mkt-RF, SMB and HML, read loadings, $R^2$ and alpha, and compute alpha from means | Mean excess return 1.1%; loadings 1.0, 0.8 and 0.5 on factors averaging 0.6, 0.2 and 0.4. Alpha? (0.14%) | `factor-model-lab` (single) | Trap: a loading is a characteristic (`mc.papers.loading-is-a-characteristic`). Edge: alpha from means equals the regression intercept exactly, because least squares passes through the means | N, P, C, S |
| 37 | `lsn.factors.reading-ff-tables` Reading FF's regression tables | Read a grid of loadings and $R^2$ across the 25 portfolios and find its patterns | In which direction should the SMB loading move from small to big portfolios if SMB does its job? (Down) | `factor-model-lab` (grid) | Trap: a high $R^2$ means the model prices the assets (pricing is about alphas, not fit). Edge: diversified portfolios often show $R^2$ above 0.9 [recalled, unverified] | C, O, S, C |
| 38 | `lsn.factors.alpha-grid` The alpha grid | Compare the grid of CAPM alphas with three-factor alphas and find the portfolios still mispriced | Switch from the CAPM to three factors and watch the alphas fade except in one corner. Which? (Small growth) | `factor-model-lab` (grid) | Trap: a positive alpha proves skill whatever the model (`mc.papers.alpha-is-skill-whatever-the-model`). Edge: 25 alphas tested at 5% will show one or two by chance | P, C, M, N |
| 39 | `lsn.factors.grs` The GRS test | Compute $GRS = \frac{T}{N}\cdot\frac{T - N - L}{T - L - 1}\cdot\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol\alpha}^\top\hat{\boldsymbol\Sigma}^{-1}\hat{\boldsymbol\alpha}}{1 + \hat{\boldsymbol\mu}^\top\hat{\boldsymbol\Omega}^{-1}\hat{\boldsymbol\mu}}$ and test it against $F(N, T - N - L)$ (Gibbons, Ross and Shanken 1989) | $T = 360$, $N = 25$, $L = 3$, $\hat{\boldsymbol\alpha}^\top\hat{\boldsymbol\Sigma}^{-1}\hat{\boldsymbol\alpha} = 0.05$ and the factors' squared Sharpe ratio 0.04. GRS and the 5% critical value? (0.646 against 1.54: not rejected) | `factor-model-lab` (GRS), `dist-explorer` ($F$) | Trap: test each alpha separately and count the rejections. Edge: references differ on the covariance divisor ($T$ or $T - L - 1$); state the one used (catalogue open question 9) | N, N, C, O |
| 40 | `lsn.factors.grs-geometry` GRS as a Sharpe-ratio comparison | Show that $\boldsymbol\alpha^\top\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1}\boldsymbol\alpha = SR^2(\text{factors and assets}) - SR^2(\text{factors})$, so GRS asks whether the test assets raise the best attainable Sharpe ratio | The factors' best monthly Sharpe ratio is 0.2; adding the 25 portfolios lifts it to 0.3. What is $\boldsymbol\alpha^\top\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1}\boldsymbol\alpha$? (0.05) | `factor-model-lab` (frontier), `derivation-steps` | Trap: a model with small alphas is the true model. Edge: in-sample maximum Sharpe ratios are optimistic, which the $F$ distribution allows for | N, X, C, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 39 tests whether a vendor's factor model prices a set of sector portfolios.

Its four-factor alpha, other loadings unchanged? (About 0.02%) | `factor-model-lab` | Trap: alpha against three factors is skill. Edge: a fund that loads on momentum inherits its crash risk (lesson 20) | N, C, M, S |

What intercept would make HML redundant? (Zero) | `factor-model-lab` (spanning) | Trap: more factors make a better model. Edge: FF5 leaves momentum out by choice [recalled, unverified] | C, N, M, S |
| 43 | `lsn.factors.q-factors-spanning` Spanning and model comparison | Judge a factor by the intercept of its regression on the others, and compare models by squared Sharpe ratios (recapping 2.3 lesson 46; the q-factor model of Hou, Xue and Zhang 2015) | Factor A has Sharpe ratio 0.3, factor B 0.2, correlation 0.5. Their best combined Sharpe ratio? (0.306: B adds almost nothing) | `factor-model-lab` (spanning) | Trap: judge a factor by its own Sharpe ratio. Edge: B's alpha on A in Sharpe units is $\frac{0.2 - 0.5\times 0.3}{\sqrt{1 - 0.25}} = 0.058$, and its square is exactly the gain in squared Sharpe ratio | N, N, C, M |

If returns follow the characteristic, which earns more? (Neither) | `factor-model-lab`, `portfolio-sort` | Trap: a loading and a characteristic are the same thing (`mc.papers.loading-is-a-characteristic`). Edge: the answer can change with the sample period; the debate is open | C, O, S, C |

Death of value or a bad decade? (Compute the decade's standard error first) | `replication-workbench` (decay), `sharpe-lab` | Trap: one decade decides. Edge: with a monthly SD of 3%, a decade's mean has standard error $3/\sqrt{120} = 0.27\%$, too wide to tell 0.4% from 0 | N, C, M, I |

**Level FF5: Replicating Fama and French** (`lvl.factors.replication`, 4 lessons)

The default exit route (decision R7).

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 46 | `lsn.factors.replicate-smb-hml` Rebuild SMB and HML | Rebuild SMB and HML on rehearsal data, then compare a rebuild with Ken French's published factors by correlation and mean | A rebuilt HML correlates 0.97 with the library's and differs in mean by 0.05% a month [illustrative]. Replicated? | `portfolio-sort` (factor), `replication-workbench` (compare), `code-cell` (`pandas`) | Trap: any difference means a bug. Edge: the library's factors are revised as the underlying databases are corrected, so a download today differs from one in 2015 | N, C, M, K |
| 47 | `lsn.factors.replicate-regressions` Replicate the 25-portfolio regressions | Run FF's time-series regressions on the library's 25 portfolios over the paper's sample, and compare loadings, $R^2$ and alphas cell by cell | Your small-growth alpha is $-0.3\%$ a month with $t = -2.4$ [illustrative]. What does the paper's table say, and what explains a gap? | `factor-model-lab` (grid), `code-cell` (`statsmodels`) | Trap: one mismatched cell means the replication failed. Edge: the library's portfolios come from today's corrected data, so 1963 to 1991 is not the paper's exact input | N, C, M, K |

What survived, and how sure are you? | `replication-workbench` (decay), `factor-model-lab` | Trap: an insignificant post-sample mean means the premium is zero (it may be too noisy to tell). Edge: the library's developed-market factors are a second out-of-sample test | N, I, C, M |
| 49 | `lsn.factors.note` The factor note | Write the replication note for the default route, with its survival section | Order the note's sections. Which one must carry a number with an interval? (How much survived) | `results-note` | Trap: burying the out-of-sample result. Edge: "three factors still explain the 25 portfolios' variation, and price their means less well after 1993" is a precise, valid conclusion | O, M, C, S |

### 2.8 Course PS: Pairs Trading and Statistical Arbitrage (`crs.pairs`, 4 levels, 19 lessons)

Gatev, Goetzmann and Rouwenhorst's distance method rebuilt step by step, what explains its profits and their decay, the models that extend it (the OU spread, bands, time-varying hedge ratios, factor residuals), and its replication. Spine: Gatev, Goetzmann and Rouwenhorst (RFS 2006); Do and Faff (FAJ 2010); Avellaneda and Lee (QF 2010); 2.2 lessons 37 to 42 (cointegration and pairs without look-ahead) recapped.

**Level PS1: The distance method** (`lvl.pairs.distance`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 50 | `lsn.pairs.twins` Trade the gap between twins | Watch two normalised prices drift apart and back, and decide when to open and close a long-short position | Two railway stocks' normalised prices separate by 6%. Which do you buy, and which sell? (Buy the laggard, sell the leader) | `pairs-lab` (single pair) | Trap: the gap must close. Edge: some gaps widen for good reasons (a takeover, a default) | C, P, S, O |
| 51 | `lsn.pairs.normalised-prices` Normalised prices | Build cumulative total-return indices starting at 1 for each stock in the formation window, dividends included | A stock pays a 2% dividend mid-window. Price index or total-return index? (Total return: the price drops on the ex-date with no loss to the holder) | `pairs-lab`, `value-table` | Trap: compare raw prices (a 10-dollar and a 100-dollar stock can still be a pair). Edge: splits and dividends must be in the index, or the gap jumps on corporate actions | C, N, O, S |
| 52 | `lsn.pairs.ssd-formation` Formation by distance | Compute the sum of squared deviations (SSD) between normalised prices, rank all pairs, and count the candidates | Normalised A: 1, 1.02, 1.01, 1.03; B: 1, 1.01, 1.03, 1.02. SSD? (0.0006.) How many pairs among 500 stocks? (124,750) | `pairs-lab` (formation) | Trap: close pairs share a long-run link (`mc.papers.ssd-pairs-are-cointegrated`). Edge: the smallest SSDs pick pairs whose spread barely moves, which caps the profit per trade (Do and Faff 2010) | N, N, C, S |
| 53 | `lsn.pairs.trading-rule` The trading rule | Open a pair when the normalised spread exceeds two formation-period standard deviations, close at the next crossing, and mark the trades | The formation SD of the spread is 0.025. At what spread do you open? (0.05) | `pairs-lab` (trading) | Trap: use the trading period's SD (look-ahead). Edge: a pair still open at the end of the trading period is closed at its last price, often at a loss | N, P, C, S |
| 54 | `lsn.pairs.returns-on-capital` Committed and employed capital | Compute a trade's payoff on one dollar long and one dollar short, and the portfolio's return on committed capital (all pairs) and on employed capital (pairs that traded) | 20 pairs with a dollar each; 12 traded and their payoffs sum to 0.30. Return on committed and on employed capital? (1.5%; 2.5%) | `pairs-lab` (portfolio) | Trap: return is return (`mc.papers.committed-equals-employed`). Edge: a dollar long and a dollar short needs margin, not two dollars, so the capital basis is a convention to state | N, N, C, S |
| 55 | `lsn.pairs.one-day-wait` The one-day wait | Execute a day after the signal, watch the profit fall, and attribute the fall to bid-ask bounce | The leader's last trade was at the ask and the laggard's at the bid on the day the pair opens. What happens to the measured gap the next day? (Part of it closes by itself) | `pairs-lab` (wait toggle) | Trap: trade at the signal's price (`mc.papers.same-day-execution`). Edge: the wait also guards against stale prices | C, O, S, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 52 screens share classes and dual listings, where the distance method's assumptions hold best.

**Level PS2: What the paper found, and why** (`lvl.pairs.findings`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 56 | `lsn.pairs.risk-exposure` Is pairs trading a risk premium? | Regress pairs returns on market, size, value, momentum and short-term reversal factors and read the alpha | Pairs returns load on a short-term reversal factor. What does that suggest? (Part of the profit is reversal) | `factor-model-lab` | Trap: a low market beta means no risk. Edge: GGR report profits that these factors do not fully explain [recalled, unverified] | C, O, N, S |
| 57 | `lsn.pairs.common-factor` A common factor in pairs returns | Show that the profits of pairs from different industries move together, and interpret a common latent factor | Bank pairs and utility pairs lose in the same months. What does that say about diversification? | `pairs-lab` (portfolio), `pca-lab` | Trap: breadth is the number of pairs (`mc.papers.breadth-is-number-of-stocks`). Edge: restricting pairs to one industry changes the exposure, not its existence | C, O, S, N |
| 58 | `lsn.pairs.decay` Decay and crowding | Run the strategy across decades and read the fall in profits since the 1990s (Do and Faff 2010) | Profits per pair fall decade by decade. Crowding, lower costs elsewhere, or fewer good pairs? | `pairs-lab`, `replication-workbench` (decay) | Trap: the backtest's average is the future. Edge: profits that hold up in crises suggest the strategy is paid for providing liquidity [recalled, unverified] | C, O, S, C |
| 59 | `lsn.pairs.limits-to-arbitrage` Limits to arbitrage | Explain divergence risk, short-sale constraints, financing and investor withdrawals, and why a convergence trade can fail | A pair keeps diverging after you open it, and your investors withdraw. What happened to "the gap must close"? | `pairs-lab` (divergence), `free-response` | Trap: a good pair stays a pair (`mc.papers.pairs-never-break`). Edge: the borrowing fee on the short leg can exceed the expected convergence | C, M, S, O |
| 60 | `lsn.pairs.distance-vs-cointegration` Distance or cointegration? | Select pairs by SSD and by an Engle-Granger test (recapping 2.2 lessons 39 and 42), and compare their out-of-sample spreads | The two methods pick different pairs. Which pair broke the next year, and was it predictable? | `pairs-lab` (compare), `coint-lab` (trading) | Trap: SSD pairs are cointegrated. Edge: testing thousands of pairs for cointegration needs a multiple-testing correction (WS1) | C, O, S, C |

**Level PS3: Statistical arbitrage models** (`lvl.pairs.models`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 61 | `lsn.pairs.ou-spread` The spread as an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process | Fit an AR(1) to a spread, convert to OU parameters, and compute the half-life and equilibrium SD (recapping 2.2 lessons 11 and 40) | A daily AR(1) coefficient of 0.95 on a spread with residual SD 0.01. Half-life and equilibrium SD? (13.5 days; 0.032) | `sde-stepper`, `coint-lab` (ECM) | Trap: the half-life is $\frac{1}{1 - b}$ (it is $\frac{\ln 0.5}{\ln b}$). Edge: a coefficient near 1 gives a long and very uncertain half-life | N, N, C, X |
| 62 | `lsn.pairs.optimal-bands` Entry and exit bands | Simulate an OU spread and trade band width against trade frequency and profit per trade | Wider bands: more profit per trade, fewer trades. Predict the profit-maximising band for a given half-life, then simulate | `pairs-lab` (bands), `sim-runner` | Trap: two standard deviations is optimal. Edge: costs push the best band outwards | P, C, M, S |
| 63 | `lsn.pairs.kalman-hedge` Time-varying hedge ratios | Update a hedge ratio with a one-dimensional Kalman filter preview (the ratio as a random walk), and compare it with a fixed formation ratio (canon-gap P11 holds the full treatment) | The true ratio drifts from 1.0 to 1.3 during the trading period. What does a fixed ratio do to the spread? (It trends) | `sde-stepper`, `pairs-lab` | Trap: the formation window fixes the hedge ratio. Edge: a fast-adapting filter can hide a real divergence | C, N, M, S |
| 64 | `lsn.pairs.pca-stat-arb` Statistical arbitrage on factor residuals | Trade residuals of returns on PCA factors by their s-scores (Avellaneda and Lee 2010) | Each stock's residual on 15 PCA factors behaves like an OU process. Why open on a $z$-score of its equilibrium distribution rather than of its raw level? | `pca-lab`, `pairs-lab` | Trap: pairs are the only relative-value trade. Edge: PCA factors estimated over the trading window leak the future | C, M, N, S |

| 65 | `lsn.pairs.replicate` Replicate the top-20 portfolio | Rebuild GGR's 12-month formation and 6-month trading portfolios on rehearsal data, started each month and averaged, and compare excess returns | Your top-20 portfolio earns 0.6% a month on committed capital [illustrative]. What do you check before comparing with the paper? (Survivorship) | `results-note` | Trap: hiding the universe's bias. Edge: an honest bound on the bias is worth more than silence | O, M, C, S |

### 2.9 Course MM: Market Making: Avellaneda and Stoikov (`crs.making`, 4 levels, 20 lessons)

The market maker's problem (spread income against inventory risk), the Avellaneda-Stoikov solution derived from indifference and a sketched HJB equation, the paper's simulation replicated, and what the model leaves out. Spine: Avellaneda and Stoikov (QF 2008); Guéant, Lehalle and Fernandez-Tapia (2013); Ho and Stoll (1981); Glosten and Milgrom (1985); Kyle (1985); Cartea, Jaimungal and Penalva, *Algorithmic and High-Frequency Trading* (CUP 2015) chapters on market making [check]. Stochastic control is sketched and the sketch is labelled (decision R6).

**Level MM1: The market maker's problem** (`lvl.making.problem`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 69 | `lsn.making.earn-spread` Earn the spread, carry the inventory | Quote symmetrically around the mid, watch fills accumulate inventory, and separate spread income from inventory P&L (recapping 1.7 MK1) | Quote 99.9 and 100.1 around a mid of 100 for one simulated session. Where does your inventory end, and what did it cost? | `market-maker-sim` (live, symmetric) | Trap: inventory is just a number (`mc.papers.inventory-is-free`). Edge: inventory under symmetric quoting wanders like a random walk, growing like the square root of the number of fills | C, N, O, S |
| 70 | `lsn.making.fill-intensity` Fill intensity and the best one-sided quote | Use $\lambda(\delta) = Ae^{-k\delta}$, and show that one side's expected profit rate $\delta Ae^{-k\delta}$ peaks at $\delta = \frac1k$ | $A = 140$ and $k = 1.5$: the best distance, and the profit rate there? (0.667; $\frac{A}{ke} = 34.3$ per unit time) | `market-maker-sim` (geometry) | Trap: wider spreads are always safer (`mc.papers.wider-spread-is-always-safer`). Edge: this ignores inventory; the full solution moves and widens the quotes | N, N, P, C |
| 71 | `lsn.making.utility` Risk aversion and certainty equivalents | Compute the certainty equivalent of a normal gamble under exponential utility, $\mu - \frac\gamma2\sigma^2$, and read $\gamma$ as a price of variance | A gamble with mean 1 and variance 4, and $\gamma = 0.1$. Certainty equivalent? (0.8) | `derivation-steps`, `game-lab` | Trap: risk aversion is about losses only. Edge: exponential utility makes decisions independent of wealth, which is why the paper uses it | N, X, C, S |
| 72 | `lsn.making.inventory-risk` Inventory risk | Model the mid as arithmetic Brownian motion and compute the variance of the inventory's value over the time left, $q^2\sigma^2(T - t)$ | Inventory 5, $\sigma = 2$, half the session left. The variance of the inventory's value at the close? (50) | `market-maker-sim` (geometry), `sde-stepper` | Trap: inventory risk is the same all session. Edge: at the close the remaining risk is zero, which is why the paper's quotes converge on the mid | N, N, C, S |
| 73 | `lsn.making.where-spreads-come-from` Where spreads come from | Separate a spread's components (order processing, inventory, adverse selection) and place Ho and Stoll (1981), Avellaneda and Stoikov (2008) and Glosten and Milgrom (1985) among them (recapping 1.7 MK3) | Which component does the Avellaneda-Stoikov model contain? (Inventory) | `card-sort` | Trap: fills are random (`mc.papers.no-informed-traders`). Edge: the model's spread stays positive even as risk aversion vanishes, from the fill intensity alone (lesson 77) | S, C, O, N |

**Level MM2: The Avellaneda-Stoikov solution** (`lvl.making.solution`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 74 | `lsn.making.reservation-price` The reservation price | Derive $r = s - q\gamma\sigma^2(T - t)$ as the price at which a holder of inventory $q$ is indifferent to one more unit, from the frozen-inventory value function | Mid 100, inventory 3, $\gamma = 0.1$, $\sigma = 2$, half the session left. Reservation price? (99.4) | `market-maker-sim` (geometry), `derivation-steps` | Trap: centre the quotes on the mid (`mc.papers.quote-around-the-mid`). Edge: a short inventory lifts the reservation price above the mid | N, X, C, P |
| 75 | `lsn.making.hjb-sketch` The HJB equation and the ansatz | Sketch the value function's HJB equation, the paper's ansatz and the first-order expansion in inventory that gives closed-form quotes (stochastic control left to 3.2) | Why does the optimal quote depend on the value function at $q + 1$ and $q - 1$? (A fill changes inventory by one) | `derivation-steps` | Trap: the closed forms are exact (they come from an expansion). Edge: the expansion is poor for large inventories, which motivates lesson 78 | O, C, N, S |
| 76 | `lsn.making.optimal-spread` The optimal spread | Compute $\delta^a + \delta^b = \gamma\sigma^2(T - t) + \frac2\gamma\ln\left(1 + \frac\gamma k\right)$, and place the bid and ask around $r$ | $\gamma = 0.1$, $\sigma = 2$, $T - t = 0.5$, $k = 1.5$. The spread? (1.491) | `market-maker-sim` (geometry) | Trap: the spread depends on inventory (in this approximation only the centre does). Edge: as $T - t\to 0$ the spread falls to $\frac2\gamma\ln\left(1 + \frac\gamma k\right) = 1.291$ | N, N, C, S |
| 77 | `lsn.making.limits` Limits of the solution | Take $\gamma\to 0$ and large $\gamma$ in the quotes, and interpret each | As $\gamma\to 0$, what does the spread tend to? ($\frac2k = 1.333$) | `market-maker-sim` (geometry), `live-formula` | Trap: a risk-neutral maker quotes at the mid. Edge: for high $\gamma$ the $\gamma\sigma^2(T - t)$ term dominates early in the session | X, N, C, M |
| 78 | `lsn.making.inventory-bounds` Inventory bounds and stationary quotes | Read Guéant, Lehalle and Fernandez-Tapia's (2013) stationary quotes with inventory limits, and compare their spread with Avellaneda and Stoikov's [formula recalled, check] | With the same parameters and $A = 140$, the stationary approximation's spread is 1.342 against 1.491 from AS at half a session left. Why can they differ? (The stationary quotes have no closing time; AS's inventory term decays to the close) | `market-maker-sim` (glft [mode to add]) | Trap: the AS formula holds at any horizon. Edge: at an inventory limit the maker stops quoting on one side | C, N, M, S |
| 79 | `lsn.making.sensitivity` Sensitivity | Order the effects of $\gamma$, $k$, $\sigma$ and $T - t$ on the spread and on the skew | Raise $k$, so fills fall off faster with distance. The spread? (Narrower) | `market-maker-sim` | Trap: volatility always widens the spread a lot (only through $\gamma\sigma^2(T - t)$). Edge: $A$ appears in neither AS formula; it enters the stationary quotes | S, C, O, C |

**Level MM3: Simulating the paper** (`lvl.making.simulation`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 80 | `lsn.making.discrete-sim` The paper's simulation | Simulate the paper's discrete scheme: the mid moves by $\pm\sigma\sqrt{dt}$, each side fills with probability $\lambda(\delta)dt$, and inventory and cash update | $A = 140$, $k = 1.5$, a quote 0.75 from the mid and $dt = 0.005$. The fill probability per step? (0.227) | `market-maker-sim` (batch), `code-cell` (`numpy`) | Trap: $\lambda\,dt$ is always a probability (it can exceed 1; the widget clamps at 0.5 and says so, catalogue decision 14). Edge: both sides can fill in one step | N, C, M, K |
| 81 | `lsn.making.inventory-vs-symmetric` Inventory strategy against symmetric | Predict, then run 1,000 sessions, how the inventory strategy and a symmetric strategy with the same spread differ in mean and spread of final profit and inventory | Predict: which strategy has the narrower distribution of profit? | `market-maker-sim` (batch) | Trap: the inventory strategy must earn more on average. Edge: the comparison is about the whole distribution; a higher mean with a much wider spread is a worse strategy for a risk-averse maker | C, P, M, S |
| 82 | `lsn.making.replicate-table` Replicate the comparison table | Reproduce the paper's table of mean and SD of profit and final inventory for several $\gamma$, with fixed seeds | Set $s = 100$, $T = 1$, $\sigma = 2$, $dt = 0.005$, $A = 140$, $k = 1.5$ and $\gamma = 0.1$ [paper's parameters recalled, unverified], and run 1,000 sessions | `market-maker-sim`, `replication-workbench`, `code-cell` (`numpy`) | Trap: matching digits (another seed gives other digits). Edge: the paper's table is itself a simulation, so replication means agreement within Monte Carlo error | N, C, K, M |
| 83 | `lsn.making.pnl-attribution` P&L attribution | Split a session's profit into spread capture and inventory P&L, $\sum_t q_t\Delta s_t$ | 40 fills at an average 0.7 from the mid, and inventory P&L of $-6$. Spread capture and total? (28; 22) | `market-maker-sim` (attribution) | Trap: all profit is spread. Edge: inventory P&L has mean zero when the mid is a martingale, but it drives the variance | N, N, C, S |
| 84 | `lsn.making.what-is-left-out` What the model leaves out | List what the model omits (informed flow, queue position, tick size, latency, fees and rebates, jumps), and switch on informed flow | Turn on traders who know the next move. What happens to the maker's profit? | `market-maker-sim` (adverse selection, limits) | Trap: fills are random (`mc.papers.no-informed-traders`). Edge: optimal execution (Almgren and Chriss) and queue models belong to canon-gap P12 | M, C, S, O |

**Level MM4: Beyond the paper** (`lvl.making.beyond`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 85 | `lsn.making.adverse-selection` Glosten-Milgrom in one picture | Compute bid and ask as the expected value given a sell or a buy when a fraction $\mu$ of traders is informed (Glosten and Milgrom 1985) | Value is 90 or 110, equally likely; 20% of traders are informed. The ask and the bid? (102 and 98) | `market-maker-sim` (adverse selection), `odds-ledger` | Trap: the spread is the maker's profit (with adverse selection alone the maker breaks even). Edge: after each trade the maker updates beliefs, so prices learn from order flow | N, N, C, M |
| 86 | `lsn.making.kyle-impact` Informed trading and price impact | Compute the one-period Kyle (1985) price impact $\lambda = \frac{\sigma_v}{2\sigma_u}$ and the impact of an order | Value SD 2 and noise-trader flow SD 1,000 shares. Impact per share, and of a 10,000-share order? (0.001; 10) | `market-maker-sim`, `live-formula` | Trap: impact is a fixed cost per share (it scales with the information in the flow relative to the noise). Edge: empirical impact is closer to a square root of size; P12 | N, N, C, M |

(1.51; 140.5) | `market-maker-sim` (calibrate), `regression-lab` | Trap: one distance identifies both parameters. Edge: fill rates vary with volatility and time of day, so one $A$ is an average | N, N, C, S |

Can you beat the formula? | `market-maker-sim` (play) | Trap: one good session proves a good strategy. Edge: this mode feeds 3.3's market-making games | C, M, S |

### 2.10 Course WS: What Survives: Multiple Testing and Replication (`crs.survival`, 4 levels, 20 lessons)

Harvey, Liu and Zhu's question (with hundreds of factors tested, what $t$ should a new one clear?), the evidence on replication and decay, machine learning in the cross-section as the bridge back to 2.1, and the exit project's final level. Spine: Harvey, Liu and Zhu (RFS 2016); Harvey and Liu (2015); Benjamini and Yekutieli (2001); McLean and Pontiff (2016); Hou, Xue and Zhang (2020); Jensen, Kelly and Pedersen (2023); Chen and Zimmermann (2022); Gu, Kelly and Xiu (2020).

**Level WS1: The factor zoo** (`lvl.survival.zoo`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 89 | `lsn.survival.zoo` The factor zoo | Count published factors by year and compute the expected number of false discoveries among null factors tested at 5% | 300 factors, all noise, each tested at 5%. How many look significant? (15 on average) | `multiple-testing` (zoo timeline) | Trap: $t > 2$ makes a factor real (`mc.papers.t-two-is-enough-for-a-new-factor`). Edge: HLZ review 316 published factors [searched] | N, C, S, P |
| 90 | `lsn.survival.t-hurdles` Hurdles under Bonferroni and Holm | Compute the hurdle $\Phi^{-1}\left(1 - \frac{0.025}{M}\right)$ for $M$ two-sided tests and run Holm's step-down (recapping 1.6 lesson 39) | Bonferroni hurdles for 1, 10, 100 and 300 tests? (1.96, 2.81, 3.48, 3.76) | `multiple-testing` ($t$ axis) | Trap: Bonferroni is exact for correlated factors (it is conservative). Edge: Holm never rejects fewer than Bonferroni | N, N, C, O |
| 91 | `lsn.survival.bhy` False discovery rates under dependence: BH and BHY | Apply BH and BHY (thresholds divided by $c(M) = \sum_{i=1}^M\frac1i$) to a list of factor $p$-values (recapping 1.6 lesson 40; Benjamini and Yekutieli 2001) | For 300 tests, by how much does BHY divide BH's thresholds? ($c(300) = 6.28$) | `multiple-testing` (BHY) | Trap: BH controls the false discovery rate under any dependence (BHY does). Edge: FDR control allows some false discoveries, which suits screening, not a single headline claim | N, O, C, S |
| 92 | `lsn.survival.hurdle-over-time` The hurdle rises with time | Show the hurdle climbing as tests accumulate, and read HLZ's recommendation that a new factor clear a $t$ of about 3.0 [searched] | Slide the year from 1980 to 2012 and the hurdle climbs. What sets its pace? (The number of factors tested so far) | `multiple-testing` (timeline) | Trap: a factor significant when published stays significant. Edge: the hurdle is a heuristic; a strong economic prior can justify a lower bar, and HLZ say so | P, C, O, S |
| 93 | `lsn.survival.unobserved-tests` Tests you never see | Show how unpublished failures raise the true number of tests and tighten the adjustment | For every published factor, three failed versions were never published. What is $M$ now? (Four times the published count) | `multiple-testing` (unobserved tests) | Trap: count only published tests (`mc.papers.ignore-unpublished-tests`). Edge: HLZ model the missing tests explicitly [recalled, unverified] | N, C, O, S |
| 94 | `lsn.survival.haircut` Haircut Sharpe ratios | Convert a Sharpe ratio to a $t$-statistic, adjust its $p$-value for $M$ tests, and convert back (Harvey and Liu 2015; recapping 2.3 lesson 23) | Annual Sharpe 1.0 over 10 years: $t$? (3.16.) Bonferroni-adjusted for 100 strategies, the haircut Sharpe? (0.45, a 55% haircut) | `sharpe-lab` (haircut) | Trap: haircuts are proportional (marginal results lose far more). Edge: Holm and BHY haircuts are smaller than Bonferroni's | N, N, C, S |

**Level WS2: The replication evidence** (`lvl.survival.evidence`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 95 | `lsn.survival.mclean-pontiff` Decay after publication | Read McLean and Pontiff's (2016) design (in sample, after the sample, after publication) and estimate decay with period dummies on a rehearsal panel | A planted anomaly earns 0.8% a month in sample; your regression finds 0.35% after publication. Decay? (56%) | `replication-workbench` (decay, many), `regression-lab` | Trap: the paper's number holds after it (`mc.papers.in-sample-equals-out-of-sample`). Edge: decay after publication is evidence that an effect was real and traded away, not only of data mining | N, C, O, S |
| 96 | `lsn.survival.hxz` Replicating anomalies with honest choices | Show how NYSE breakpoints and value weights remove anomalies that live in microcaps (Hou, Xue and Zhang 2020) | A signal's equal-weighted spread is 1.2% a month and its value-weighted spread 0.3%. Where did the difference come from? (Microcaps) | `portfolio-sort` (weights, breakpoints) | Trap: equal and value weights agree (`mc.papers.equal-and-value-weights-agree`). Edge: microcap anomalies may be real and still untradable after costs | C, N, O, S |
| 97 | `lsn.survival.jkp` Is there a replication crisis? | Read Jensen, Kelly and Pedersen's (2023) argument that most factors replicate once estimated with a hierarchical prior over clusters, and set it beside HXZ | Two papers, two verdicts. What differs in the questions they ask? | `card-sort`, `posterior-lab` | Trap: the papers contradict each other. Edge: shrinkage (2.1 lesson 59) changes the question from "is each factor real?" to "how large is each, given the others?" | C, O, S, N |
| 98 | `lsn.survival.open-source-ap` Open data for replication | Use Open Source Asset Pricing (Chen and Zimmermann 2022) to find a predictor's code and portfolio returns, and say which claims it supports | You want the long-short returns of 200 published predictors. Where are they, and what can you claim from them? | `card-sort`, `replication-workbench` (read) | Trap: an open dataset makes replication unnecessary. Edge: the signals are built from licensed data; the portfolio return files can be downloaded without it [check] | C, O, S, C |
| 99 | `lsn.survival.forking-paths` Forking paths in finance research | Count specifications in a specification curve (Simonsohn, Simmons and Nelson 2020), and read the spread of results across research teams (Menkveld and others 2024) | Four start dates, three weightings, two breakpoint rules and three skip choices. How many specifications? (72) | `replication-workbench` (sensitivity), `multiple-testing` | Trap: one specification is the result. Edge: the spread of estimates across many teams answering one question can rival a standard error [recalled, unverified] | N, C, M, S |

**Level WS3: Machine learning meets the cross-section** (`lvl.survival.ml`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 100 | `lsn.survival.gkx` Machine learning in the cross-section | Read Gu, Kelly and Xiu (2020): models, inputs, the validation scheme, and the long-short portfolio of predictions | Which 2.1 lessons does the paper's validation scheme rely on? (Rolling training and validation windows: lessons 118 and 29) | `ml-workbench` (arena), `card-sort` | Trap: an ML paper's headline Sharpe ratio is out of sample in every sense. Edge: hyperparameters tuned on the validation window are part of the search (2.1 lesson 144) | C, M, S, O |
| 101 | `lsn.survival.oos-r2` Out-of-sample $R^2$ against zero | Compute out-of-sample $R^2$ against a zero forecast and against the historical mean, and translate a small $R^2$ into a Sharpe-ratio gain (Campbell and Thompson 2008) | A monthly $R^2$ of 0.5% for an investor whose buy-and-hold Sharpe ratio is 0.1 a month. The timing strategy's Sharpe ratio? ($\sqrt{(0.01 + 0.005)/0.995} = 0.123$ a month: 0.43 a year against 0.35) | `signal-lab`, `sharpe-lab` | Trap: an $R^2$ of 0.5% is worthless. Edge: the historical mean is a harder benchmark than zero, and is itself estimated | N, N, C, M |
| 102 | `lsn.survival.ml-paper-audit` Auditing an ML finance paper | Audit a described ML paper for leakage, selection and costs with 2.1's checklists (lessons 123 to 128 and 144 to 147) | The paper tunes on the first year of its test period. Leak or not? (Leak) | `leakage-detective` (audit), `card-sort` | Trap: a long out-of-sample period guarantees honesty. Edge: survivorship and look-ahead in the feature set are the commonest flaws | M, S, C, O |
| 103 | `lsn.survival.referee-report` Writing a referee report | Write a referee report: summary, main concerns, minor points, requested analyses, recommendation (Cochrane's writing tips; Berk, Harvey and Hirshleifer, JEP 2017 [searched]) | Order a report's five parts. Which concern goes first? (The one that could overturn the main result) | `free-response`, `card-sort` | Trap: list the typos first. Edge: a good report asks for tests the authors can run, not for a different paper | O, S, M, C |

**Level WS4: The exit: your replication** (`lvl.survival.exit`, 5 lessons)

These lessons drive `prj.papers.replication`; their homework is its milestone pack (section 3.10, WS4).

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 104 | `lsn.survival.choose-paper` Choose the paper and the data route | Choose the paper and data route (decision R7), naming the bias each route carries | Ken French's library, free stock prices, or WRDS: which route suits which paper, and which bias does each carry? | `card-sort`, `replication-workbench` | Trap: choose the paper with the biggest published effect. Edge: Avellaneda and Stoikov qualifies only with an empirical extension (calibrate $A$ and $k$ from public trade data, lesson 87, and replay the quotes) | S, C, O, C |
| 105 | `lsn.survival.replication-plan` The replication plan | Write the replication's `PLAN.md` (project-forge Phase 1) with the ledger, pre-registration, data manifest and tests | Which section must be written before any post-publication data is downloaded? (The pre-registration) | `setup-checklist` (project), `free-response` | Trap: writing the plan after the results. Edge: the threat class is A (public data, no secrets) | O, M, C, S |
| 106 | `lsn.survival.survival-note` Writing "how much survived" | Write the survival section: the ratio with its interval, sub-periods and costs | In sample 0.9% a month (SE 0.2%); out of sample 0.3% (SE 0.25%). The ratio and its 95% interval? (0.33; $-0.23$ to 0.90) | `replication-workbench` (decay), `results-note` | Trap: a point estimate without an interval. Edge: an interval covering both 0 and 1 means "cannot tell", a valid finding | N, I, C, M |
| 107 | `lsn.survival.limitations` Limitations and threats | List the replication's threats (survivorship, vendor differences, period choice, costs, the variants tried) and the direction each pushes | Which threats inflate your survival ratio, and which deflate it? | `card-sort`, `results-note` | Trap: vague limitations. Edge: one limitation with a bound is worth more than three without | S, M, C, O |
| 108 | `lsn.survival.defence` Defending the replication | Answer the standard follow-ups under two probes from Bertie (`viva`): what would make this wrong, how much is in sample, what failed | "Your replication finds less than the paper. Whose number do you believe, and why?" | `viva`, `free-response` | Trap: defending the paper rather than the evidence. Edge: "I cannot tell, and this is what would decide it" scores well | viva rubric |

|---|---|
| Statistical learning, flexibility, test error (ISLP ch. 2) | 1 to 10 |

| Labelling (López de Prado) | 129 to 133 |

| Videos (the curriculum's CampusX, Ng's Machine Learning Specialization, StatQuest) | StatQuest per level in section 5; Ng's Specialization as the English alternative to the CS229 lectures for LD to TN (section 5.0); CampusX left out, since the curriculum flags it as taught largely in Hindi |

The top-programme layer of decision M7 adds 27 lessons beyond ISLP. ISLP chapter 11 (survival analysis) is deliberately out (decision M11).

**The 3.1 cover.** Where: SSRN, arXiv q-fin, WorldQuant BRAIN (lesson 2). Method: replicate before reading widely, reproduce the main table, extend after publication (lessons 5 to 10, 22 to 25, 46 to 49, 65 to 68, 80 to 82, 104 to 108). The five papers: Jegadeesh and Titman (11 to 25), Fama and French (26 to 49), Gatev, Goetzmann and Rouwenhorst (50 to 68), Avellaneda and Stoikov (69 to 88), Harvey, Liu and Zhu (89 to 94). Exit: lessons 104 to 108 and `prj.papers.replication`. Added by the top-programme standard: overlapping-return errors (16), Shanken and GRS (30, 39, 40), Carhart, FF5, the q-factors and spanning (41 to 43), crashes and time-series momentum (20, 21), OU and factor-residual statistical arbitrage (61 to 64), Glosten-Milgrom, Kyle and stationary quotes (78, 85, 86), the replication evidence (95 to 99), ML in the cross-section (100 to 102) and the referee report (103).

**Quiz formats.** Choice items (C and M) as a share of each course's quiz items, counted from the tables above by script: LD 38.3%, LS 38.5%, CP 38.5%, TN 37.9%, FD 37.9%; MP 38.0%, FF 37.5%, PS 38.2%, MM 38.0%, WS 38.2%. Every course is under the 40% ceiling (design research 10). Sort-and-match items (S) carry most of the conceptual checks that a multi-select would otherwise hold, because sorting statements into true and false bins also grades each statement.

**Counts.** 2.1: 152 lessons in 29 levels (levels of 4 to 6 lessons); 3.1: 108 lessons in 22 levels (4 to 6). Both at 15 minutes give the lesson hours of sections 1.6 and 1.11.

---

## 3. Homework blueprint per level

### 3.0 Rules for every set

- **Issue, due and marking** follow `ASSESSMENT.md` 2.5: issued when the level's last lesson is done, due 7 days later, no feedback before submission; code items run their visible examples freely and their hidden tests at submission; long-form items are self-marked against the rubric with Bertie's suggested mark and evidence quote beside the learner's (`AI-TUTOR.md` 2.7).
- **Size and mix, 2.1.** Levels of 4 or 5 lessons get **8 items: 3 at difficulty 3, 3 at difficulty 4, 2 at difficulty 5**. Levels of 6 lessons get **10 items: 4 at difficulty 3, 4 at difficulty 4, 2 at difficulty 5**. 2.1: 29 sets, 256 items (17 sets of 8, 12 of 10), about 2 hours each.
- **Size and mix, 3.1.** Every set has **8 items: 3 at difficulty 3, 3 at difficulty 4, 2 at difficulty 5**, because the papers' reading sits in the external block (section 1.11). 3.1: 22 sets, 176 items, about 1.75 hours each.
- **Inside every set:** exactly one long-form item (a proof, derivation or written explanation with a 10-point rubric, difficulty 4 unless stated) and, where the level has code to test, exactly one code drill; at least half the items are generator slots, so a redo is a fresh variant; at most two choice items.
- **Long-form marking.** Every rubric has 4 to 10 points totalling 10 marks, and each point names the model-answer lines that evidence it (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.4). 7 of 10 is the bar wherever an outcome cites a rubric.
- **Code drills** list their hidden tests here so CI can check that the reference passes and that each known-wrong solution fails its named test (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.9). `needs` follows decision 5: `stdlib` drills also carry `micropython-ok`; `numpy` and `pandas` drills run on both targets; `sklearn`, `scipy` and `statsmodels` drills hand off where the Artifact build lacks them; `local-torch` drills run on the learner's machine with paste-back.
- **Generators** named per level are `gen.ml.*` and `gen.papers.*` families built on the widget catalogue's section 9, with tidy answers by construction: planted coefficients projected so fits are round, counts chosen so Gini values, precisions and AUCs are simple fractions, label spans generated tie-free, and panels whose planted premia sit at least 4 standard errors from zero.

Each level below gives its set id, item mix, long-form item, code drill, generators, and two example problems in full: **example A** is the long-form item with its model answer (numbered lines) and rubric; **example B** is a difficulty-5 item with its worked answer and its decision point.

### 3.1 Course LD (2.1)

**LD1 The learning problem** (`set.learning.problem`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the median nearest-neighbour distance in high dimensions. Code drill `drl.ml.knn` (d3, `stdlib`): `knn_predict(xs, ys, x0, k)` for one- or many-dimensional inputs with Euclidean distance, ties broken by the lower training index. Hidden tests: the hook's data with $k = 3$ gives $\frac{14}{3}$ to $10^{-12}$; $k = 1$ gives 3 (the tie between $x = 3$ and $x = 5$ goes to index 2); $k = 5$ gives 5.0; $k = 6$ raises `ValueError`; a two-dimensional case matches a hand-computed value; the inputs are not mutated. Known-wrong: ties broken by the higher index (fails test 2); neighbours chosen by $\lvert y - y_0\rvert$ (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.ml.knn-predict`, `gen.ml.curse-edge`, `gen.ml.snr-ceiling`, `gen.ml.pick-the-winner`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* $N$ points are drawn uniformly in the unit ball in $p$ dimensions. (a) Derive the median distance from the centre to its nearest neighbour, $d(p, N) = \left(1 - 2^{-1/N}\right)^{1/p}$. (b) Evaluate it for $N = 500$ with $p = 2$ and $p = 10$. (c) Explain what it means for a 10-nearest-neighbour model with 10 features.
  *Model answer.* (1) For one uniform point, $P(\text{distance} \le r) = r^p$, because the volume of a ball of radius $r$ scales like $r^p$ and the unit ball's volume cancels. (2) So $P(\text{distance} > r) = 1 - r^p$. (3) The nearest of $N$ independent points is farther than $r$ only if all $N$ are: $P(\min > r) = (1 - r^p)^N$. (4) The median $d$ solves $(1 - d^p)^N = \frac12$, so $1 - d^p = 2^{-1/N}$ and $d = \left(1 - 2^{-1/N}\right)^{1/p}$. (5) For $N = 500$: $p = 2$ gives 0.037; $p = 10$ gives 0.518. (6) In 10 dimensions the nearest of 500 points is typically over halfway to the boundary, so "local" neighbours are not local: a 10-NN average mixes points from far apart and the fit's bias grows, which is the curse of dimensionality. (7) The leap: the minimum of many distances is hard to handle directly, but its survival function is a product of the individual ones because the points are independent, which turns the problem into one line of algebra.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the single-point distribution from volume scaling (line 1). 1: the survival function (line 2). 2: the product for the minimum, with independence named (line 3). 2: solving for the median (line 4). 1: both numbers (line 5). 2: the interpretation for k-NN (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A researcher regresses next-month returns on 40 candidate features over 120 months and reports $R^2 = 0.36$. (a) Under the null that no feature matters (Gaussian errors), $R^2\sim\text{Beta}\left(\frac k2, \frac{n - k - 1}{2}\right)$. What is its mean? (b) Compute the adjusted $R^2$. (c) What should the researcher do next?
  *Answer.* (a) The mean of $\text{Beta}(a, b)$ is $\frac{a}{a + b} = \frac{k}{n - 1} = \frac{40}{119} = 0.336$; 2,000 simulated noise fits average 0.336. (b) $\bar R^2 = 1 - (1 - 0.36)\frac{119}{79} = 0.036$. (c) Decision point: the naive reading is "the features explain a third of returns". Under the null $R^2$ has SD about 0.06 here, so 0.36 sits less than half a standard deviation above what noise gives: no evidence at all. The researcher should evaluate on held-out months (time-ordered, FD1), cut the features or shrink them (LS3, LS4), and expect an out-of-sample $R^2$ near zero or negative.

**LD2 Bias and variance** (`set.learning.bias-variance`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the bias-variance decomposition at a point. Code drill `drl.ml.bias-variance-exact` (d4, `numpy`): `ols_pointwise(X, x0, sigma2)` returns the variance $\sigma^2\mathbf x_0^\top(\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X)^{-1}\mathbf x_0$ of a least-squares prediction; `knn_pointwise(f, xs, x0, k, sigma2)` returns $(\text{bias}^2, \text{variance})$ for a fixed design. Hidden tests: the hook's $f(x) = x^2$ case gives $(1, 2)$ for $k = 2$ and $(6.25, 1)$ for $k = 4$ at $\sigma^2 = 4$; with $\sigma^2 = 100$ the totals are 151 and 131.25; an intercept-only design gives $\frac{\sigma^2}{n}$; inputs are not mutated. Known-wrong: variance $\sigma^2/k^2$ (fails test 1); bias unsquared (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.ml.rank-variance`, `gen.ml.table-compute`, `gen.ml.decompose`, `gen.ml.learning-curve`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $Y_0 = f(x_0) + \varepsilon_0$ with $E[\varepsilon_0] = 0$, $\operatorname{Var}(\varepsilon_0) = \sigma^2$, and $\varepsilon_0$ independent of the training set that produced $\hat f$. Prove $E\left[(Y_0 - \hat f(x_0))^2\right] = \sigma^2 + \left(f(x_0) - E\hat f(x_0)\right)^2 + \operatorname{Var}(\hat f(x_0))$, and say what each term means and which one more data cannot reduce.
  *Model answer.* (1) Write $Y_0 - \hat f = \varepsilon_0 + (f - E\hat f) + (E\hat f - \hat f)$, all at $x_0$. (2) The leap: adding and subtracting $E\hat f$ splits the error into a fixed part and a part with mean zero, which is what makes the cross terms vanish. (3) Square and take expectations over both $\varepsilon_0$ and the training set. (4) $E[\varepsilon_0(f - E\hat f)] = 0$ because $E\varepsilon_0 = 0$ and $f - E\hat f$ is a constant. (5) $E[\varepsilon_0(E\hat f - \hat f)] = E[\varepsilon_0]E[E\hat f - \hat f] = 0$ by independence of the new noise and the training set. (6) $E[(f - E\hat f)(E\hat f - \hat f)] = (f - E\hat f)\cdot 0 = 0$. (7) What remains is $E\varepsilon_0^2 + (f - E\hat f)^2 + E(E\hat f - \hat f)^2 = \sigma^2 + \text{bias}^2 + \text{variance}$. (8) $\sigma^2$ is the noise floor no model can beat; bias is how far the average fit misses the truth (a property of the model family); variance is how much fits from different training sets disagree. More data shrinks variance and leaves the noise floor and the bias of a fixed family untouched.
