  *Rubric (10).* 2: the split with the motivation for adding and subtracting $E\hat f$ (lines 1, 2). 1: expectation over both sources of randomness (line 3). 3: the three cross terms, one mark each, with independence named in line 5. 1: the result (line 7). 3: the meaning of each term and which one data cannot reduce (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* $f(x) = x^2$, training points at every integer, and k-NN predicts at $x_0 = 0$ using the $k$ nearest points (ties at $\pm j$ taken in pairs). For $\sigma^2 = 4$ and for $\sigma^2 = 100$, find the expected test error for $k = 2, 4, 6$ and the best $k$ in each case.
  *Answer.* Neighbours $\pm 1$; $\pm 1, \pm 2$; $\pm 1, \pm 2, \pm 3$ give mean $f$ values 1, 2.5 and $\frac{28}{6}$, so squared biases 1, 6.25 and 21.78 (since $f(0) = 0$). Variances are $\frac{\sigma^2}{k}$. At $\sigma^2 = 4$: totals 7, 11.25 and 26.44, so $k = 2$. At $\sigma^2 = 100$: 151, 131.25 and 138.44, so $k = 4$. Decision point: the naive rule "pick the $k$ that worked last time" fails because the best flexibility depends on the noise level; noisier data (most finance data) favour smoother models.

**LD3 Loss, likelihood and information** (`set.learning.information`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Gibbs' inequality and log loss as likelihood. Code drill `drl.ml.entropy` (d3, `stdlib`): `entropy(p, base=2)`, `cross_entropy(p, q, base=2)`, `kl(p, q, base=2)`, with $0\log 0 = 0$, infinity when $q_i = 0 < p_i$, and `ValueError` when a vector does not sum to 1 within $10^{-9}$. Hidden tests: $H\left(\frac12, \frac14, \frac18, \frac18\right) = 1.75$; cross-entropy against uniform 2.0; KL 0.25; $D_{KL}((0.9, 0.1)\Vert(0.5, 0.5)) = 0.5310$ and the reverse 0.7370; $q = (1, 0)$ against $p = (0.5, 0.5)$ gives `inf`. Known-wrong: natural logarithm by default (fails test 1); arguments of KL swapped (fails test 4). Generators: `gen.ml.minimiser`, `gen.ml.pinball`, `gen.ml.entropy`, `gen.ml.kl`, `gen.ml.log-loss`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Prove $D_{KL}(p\Vert q)\ge 0$ with equality only when $p = q$. (b) Show that for a model $q_\theta$ and data drawn from $p$, minimising expected log loss over $\theta$ is the same as minimising $D_{KL}(p\Vert q_\theta)$, and that on a sample it is maximum likelihood. (c) Conclude that log loss is a proper scoring rule.
  *Model answer.* (1) $-D_{KL}(p\Vert q) = \sum_i p_i\ln\frac{q_i}{p_i}$, summing over $p_i > 0$. (2) The leap: a sum of $p$-weighted logs is an expectation of a concave function, so Jensen's inequality (1.5 lesson 114) applies: $E[\ln Z]\le\ln E[Z]$ with $Z = q_i/p_i$. (3) So $-D_{KL}\le\ln\sum_i p_i\frac{q_i}{p_i} = \ln\sum_{i: p_i > 0}q_i\le\ln 1 = 0$. (4) Equality needs $Z$ constant where $p > 0$ and all of $q$'s mass on $p$'s support, so $q = p$. (5) Expected log loss is $-\sum_i p_i\ln q_{\theta, i} = H(p) + D_{KL}(p\Vert q_\theta)$; $H(p)$ does not depend on $\theta$, so minimising one minimises the other. (6) Replacing $p$ by the empirical distribution of the sample gives $-\frac1n\sum_j\ln q_\theta(y_j)$, the negative log-likelihood per point, so the minimiser is the MLE. (7) Taking $q_\theta$ to be any forecast, the expected log loss is smallest exactly at $q = p$ by line 4: telling the truth is optimal, which is the definition of a proper scoring rule.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the rewrite (line 1). 2: Jensen named and applied in the right direction (lines 2, 3). 1: the equality case (line 4). 2: the decomposition into entropy and KL (line 5). 2: the empirical step to likelihood (line 6). 2: properness stated and justified (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Daily fills were 1, 2, 3, 4 and 100 lots. (a) Find every constant $c$ that minimises the pinball loss $\sum_i\rho_{0.8}(y_i - c)$, where $\rho_\tau(u) = \tau u$ for $u\ge 0$ and $(\tau - 1)u$ for $u < 0$. (b) What does NumPy's default `np.quantile(y, 0.8)` return? (c) Which number would you give a risk manager as the 80% quantile?
  *Answer.* (a) The loss is piecewise linear in $c$ with slope $-\tau\cdot\#\{y_i > c\} + (1 - \tau)\cdot\#\{y_i < c\}$. Between 4 and 100 the slope is $-0.8 + 4\times 0.2 = 0$, so the loss is flat at its minimum, 78.0, on the whole interval $[4, 100]$. (b) 23.2: linear interpolation at position $0.8\times 4 = 3.2$ between the 4th and 5th values. (c) Decision point: the naive answer quotes 23.2 as "the" quantile. With five points the 80% quantile is not identified anywhere between 4 and 100; any single number is a convention. Report the interval, the method, and that the sample is far too small for a tail quantile.

**LD4 Fitting by optimisation** (`set.learning.optimisation`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the logistic loss's gradient, Hessian and convexity in matrix form. Code drill `drl.ml.logistic-grad` (d4, `numpy`): `loss(w, X, y)` (summed, not averaged), `grad`, `hess` and `check_grad(f, g, w, h=1e-6)` by central differences. Hidden tests: on a fixed $8\times 3$ design the analytic gradient matches central differences with relative error below $10^{-6}$; the Hessian is symmetric and its smallest eigenvalue is non-negative; at $\mathbf w = \mathbf 0$ the gradient is $\mathbf X^\top(\frac12\mathbf 1 - \mathbf y)$; `loss` stays finite at $\mathbf w = 1000\cdot\mathbf 1$ (computed with `logaddexp`). Known-wrong: gradient $\mathbf X^\top(\mathbf y - \mathbf p)$ (fails test 1); `log(1 + exp(z))` computed directly (fails test 4). Generators: `gen.ml.gd-factor`, `gen.ml.matrix-gradient`, `gen.ml.softmax-gradient`, `gen.ml.sgd-se`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For labels $y_i\in\{0, 1\}$, $p_i = \sigma(\mathbf x_i^\top\mathbf w)$ and loss $\ell(\mathbf w) = -\sum_i[y_i\ln p_i + (1 - y_i)\ln(1 - p_i)]$, derive $\nabla\ell = \mathbf X^\top(\mathbf p - \mathbf y)$ and $\nabla^2\ell = \mathbf X^\top\mathbf W\mathbf X$ with $\mathbf W = \operatorname{diag}(p_i(1 - p_i))$, and prove $\ell$ is convex.
  *Model answer.* (1) With $z_i = \mathbf x_i^\top\mathbf w$: $\sigma'(z) = \sigma(z)(1 - \sigma(z))$, from $\sigma(z) = (1 + e^{-z})^{-1}$. (2) Per point, $\frac{\partial\ell_i}{\partial z_i} = -y_i\frac{p_i(1 - p_i)}{p_i} + (1 - y_i)\frac{p_i(1 - p_i)}{1 - p_i} = p_i - y_i$. (3) Chain rule in matrix form: $\frac{\partial z_i}{\partial\mathbf w} = \mathbf x_i$, so $\nabla\ell = \sum_i(p_i - y_i)\mathbf x_i = \mathbf X^\top(\mathbf p - \mathbf y)$; shapes: $(p\times n)(n\times 1)$. (4) Differentiate again: $\frac{\partial p_i}{\partial\mathbf w^\top} = p_i(1 - p_i)\mathbf x_i^\top$, so $\nabla^2\ell = \sum_i p_i(1 - p_i)\mathbf x_i\mathbf x_i^\top = \mathbf X^\top\mathbf W\mathbf X$. (5) For any $\mathbf v$: $\mathbf v^\top\mathbf X^\top\mathbf W\mathbf X\mathbf v = \sum_i p_i(1 - p_i)(\mathbf x_i^\top\mathbf v)^2\ge 0$, so the Hessian is positive semi-definite everywhere and $\ell$ is convex. (6) It is strictly convex when $\mathbf X$ has full column rank, because every weight $p_i(1 - p_i)$ is positive. (7) The insight: the gradient has the same "residual times feature" form as least squares, $\mathbf X^\top(\hat{\mathbf y} - \mathbf y)$, and Newton's method with this Hessian is weighted least squares repeated (lesson 63).
  *Rubric (10).* 1: $\sigma'$ (line 1). 2: the per-point derivative simplified (line 2). 2: the matrix gradient with shapes (line 3). 2: the Hessian (line 4). 2: the quadratic-form argument for convexity (line 5). 1: strict convexity or the link to least squares (lines 6, 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Least squares $f(\mathbf w) = \Vert\mathbf y - \mathbf X\mathbf w\Vert^2$ where $\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X$ has eigenvalues 10 and 1. (a) For which fixed steps does gradient descent converge? (b) Which step is fastest, and by what factor does the error shrink per step? (c) How many steps to cut the error by $10^{-6}$? (d) What would you change?
  *Answer.* The Hessian is $2\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X$, with eigenvalues 20 and 2. Along each eigenvector the error is multiplied by $1 - \eta\lambda$. (a) Converges when $\lvert 1 - 20\eta\rvert < 1$, so $0 < \eta < 0.1$. (b) The best fixed step equalises the two factors: $\eta^* = \frac{2}{20 + 2} = \frac{1}{11}$, giving $\pm\frac{9}{11} = \pm 0.818$, which is $\frac{\kappa - 1}{\kappa + 1}$ with $\kappa = 10$. (c) $\frac{\ln 10^{-6}}{\ln(9/11)} = 68.8$, so 69 steps. (d) Decision point: a naive learner raises the step to go faster and diverges past 0.1. The fix is to change the conditioning: standardise the features, use a preconditioner or Newton's method (one step for a quadratic), or momentum.

**LD5 Validation and cross-validation** (`set.learning.validation`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the leave-one-out shortcut for least squares. Code drill `drl.ml.kfold` (d3, `stdlib`): `kfold_indices(n, k)` returns $k$ contiguous folds whose sizes differ by at most one (the first $n \bmod k$ folds one larger); `cv_estimate(errors)` returns the mean and the standard error with divisor $k - 1$. Hidden tests: $n = 10$, $k = 3$ gives sizes $[4, 3, 3]$; the folds partition `range(n)`; $k > n$ raises `ValueError`; $k = n$ gives singletons; the hook's fold errors give $(1.10, 0.0949)$ to $10^{-4}$. Known-wrong: divisor $k$ in the SD (gives 0.0849, fails test 5); folds of size $\lfloor n/k\rfloor$ dropping the remainder (fails test 2). Generators: `gen.ml.cv-estimate`, `gen.ml.which-rows`, `gen.ml.loocv-shortcut`, `gen.ml.one-se`, `gen.ml.assign-stratified`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For least squares with hat matrix $\mathbf H = \mathbf X(\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X)^{-1}\mathbf X^\top$, prove that the leave-one-out residual is $y_i - \hat y_{(-i)} = \frac{y_i - \hat y_i}{1 - h_{ii}}$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Let $\mathbf A = \mathbf X^\top\mathbf X$ and $\mathbf A_{(-i)} = \mathbf A - \mathbf x_i\mathbf x_i^\top$, the matrix without row $i$. (2) The leap: refitting $n$ times looks unavoidable, but removing one row is a rank-one change, and Sherman-Morrison inverts a rank-one change in closed form: $\mathbf A_{(-i)}^{-1} = \mathbf A^{-1} + \frac{\mathbf A^{-1}\mathbf x_i\mathbf x_i^\top\mathbf A^{-1}}{1 - h_{ii}}$, using $h_{ii} = \mathbf x_i^\top\mathbf A^{-1}\mathbf x_i$. (3) The leave-one-out fit is $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_{(-i)} = \mathbf A_{(-i)}^{-1}(\mathbf X^\top\mathbf y - \mathbf x_iy_i)$. (4) Its prediction at $\mathbf x_i$: $\hat y_{(-i)} = \mathbf x_i^\top\mathbf A_{(-i)}^{-1}(\mathbf X^\top\mathbf y - \mathbf x_iy_i)$. Substituting line 2, $\mathbf x_i^\top\mathbf A_{(-i)}^{-1} = \mathbf x_i^\top\mathbf A^{-1}\left(1 + \frac{h_{ii}}{1 - h_{ii}}\right) = \frac{\mathbf x_i^\top\mathbf A^{-1}}{1 - h_{ii}}$. (5) So $\hat y_{(-i)} = \frac{\hat y_i - h_{ii}y_i}{1 - h_{ii}}$, since $\mathbf x_i^\top\mathbf A^{-1}\mathbf X^\top\mathbf y = \hat y_i$. (6) Then $y_i - \hat y_{(-i)} = \frac{y_i(1 - h_{ii}) - \hat y_i + h_{ii}y_i}{1 - h_{ii}} = \frac{y_i - \hat y_i}{1 - h_{ii}}$. (7) The shortcut holds for any linear smoother whose leave-one-out fit is the full fit with row $i$ removed and renormalised (smoothing splines, ridge with fixed $\lambda$), but not for procedures that choose features or $\lambda$ from the data.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the rank-one setup (line 1). 2: Sherman-Morrison with its motivation (line 2). 1: the leave-one-out fit (line 3). 2: the simplification of $\mathbf x_i^\top\mathbf A_{(-i)}^{-1}$ (line 4). 2: lines 5 and 6. 2: where the shortcut does and does not hold (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A colleague has 200 rows, 1,000 candidate features and binary labels. They keep the 20 features most correlated with the labels on all 200 rows, then report 10-fold CV accuracy of 0.71 for logistic regression, and ask you to sign off. The features are in fact pure noise. (a) What accuracy will the model have on new data? (b) Why did CV not catch it? (c) What do you change, and what would you expect it to report?
  *Answer.* (a) 0.5: the labels are independent of every feature. (b) The screening step saw every row's label, including each fold's test rows, so the 20 features were chosen for their chance agreement with exactly the labels CV later scores. CV estimated the error of a procedure that did not include the selection. (c) Decision point: the naive fix is "use a hold-out", which fails the same way if the screen ran first. Move the screen inside each fold (a pipeline, lesson 125); the CV estimate of accuracy then centres near 0.5 (lesson 28's simulation, with 50 rows and 5,000 features, gives a CV error of 0.52 the right way against 0.02 the wrong way).

**LD6 Model selection and the bootstrap** (`set.learning.selection`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the bootstrap's 0.632 and why bootstrap error is optimistic. Code drill `drl.ml.nested-cv` (d4, `sklearn`): `nested_cv_ridge(X, y, alphas, outer=5, inner=5, seed=0)` returns the outer-fold scores and the chosen alpha per fold. Hidden tests: a spy estimator records the rows each fit sees, and no outer test row is ever seen by its fold's tuning; a fixed seed reproduces the stored scores to $10^{-9}$; with one alpha in the grid, nested CV equals plain CV. Known-wrong: tuning once on all rows, then cross-validating the chosen alpha (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.ml.optimism`, `gen.ml.grid-vs-random`, `gen.ml.criteria`, `gen.ml.boot-out-prob`, `gen.ml.hoeffding-bound`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Show that a given row is absent from a bootstrap sample of size $n$ with probability $\left(1 - \frac1n\right)^n$, and that this tends to $e^{-1}$. (b) Explain why training on a bootstrap sample and scoring on all $n$ original rows underestimates test error. (c) Describe the out-of-bag and 0.632 estimators and say when the latter still fails.
  *Model answer.* (1) Each of the $n$ draws picks the row with probability $\frac1n$, independently. (2) So it is missed by all $n$ draws with probability $\left(1 - \frac1n\right)^n$; for $n = 10$ this is 0.349. (3) $\ln\left(1 - \frac1n\right)^n = n\ln\left(1 - \frac1n\right) = n\left(-\frac1n - \frac{1}{2n^2} - \dots\right)\to -1$, so the limit is $e^{-1} = 0.368$ and about 63.2% of distinct rows appear in each sample. (4) Scoring on all original rows scores about 63% of them on data the model trained on, so the estimate mixes training and test error and is biased low, badly so for flexible models. (5) The out-of-bag estimator scores each row only with models whose sample missed it, which is honest but pessimistic, because each model trained on only about 63% distinct rows. (6) The 0.632 estimator, $0.368\,\overline{\text{err}}_{\text{train}} + 0.632\,\text{err}_{\text{OOB}}$, corrects that pessimism. (7) It fails for models that interpolate (1-NN, deep trees), whose training error is 0, so it is pulled down too far; the 0.632+ estimator (Efron and Tibshirani 1997) adjusts the weight for overfitting.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: one draw (line 1). 1: the product (line 2). 2: the limit with the expansion (line 3). 2: why the naive score is optimistic (line 4). 2: OOB and its pessimism (line 5). 2: 0.632 and its failure case (lines 6, 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A rule-mining system searches $M = 2^{20}$ trading rules for the best hit rate. Treat days as independent. (a) How many days make the best rule's observed hit rate within 0.05 of its true rate with probability 0.95, by Hoeffding and the union bound? (b) How many days would one pre-specified rule need? (c) What would you tell the head of research?
  *Answer.* (a) $n\ge\frac{\ln(2M/\delta)}{2\varepsilon^2} = \frac{\ln(2\cdot 2^{20}/0.05)}{2\times 0.0025} = \frac{17.55}{0.005} = 3{,}510$ days, about 14 years. (b) $\frac{\ln(2/0.05)}{0.005} = 738$ days, about 3 years. (c) Decision point: the naive reading is that the search "only costs a log", so it is cheap. A millionfold search costs nearly five times the data, the bound is a worst case that ignores the rules' correlation (which helps) and returns' dependence (which hurts), and it says nothing about whether the best rule's true rate is any good. Pre-specify, hold out, and count the trials (FD6).

### 3.2 Course LS (2.1)

**LS1 Linear regression as a predictor** (`set.shrinkage.predictor`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the prediction error's variance and the two intervals. Code drill `drl.ml.one-hot` (d3, `stdlib`): `encode(values, levels, reference, unknown="error")` returns rows of 0s and 1s without the reference column; `unknown="zeros"` maps an unseen level to all zeros. Hidden tests: three sectors with reference "Energy" give 2 columns; adding an intercept column gives a full-rank design (rank checked by exact Gaussian elimination on fractions); an unseen level raises `ValueError` by default and returns zeros with the flag; the order of `levels` fixes the column order. Known-wrong: a column for the reference level too (fails the rank test). Generators: `gen.ml.prediction-interval`, `gen.ml.encode-rows`, `gen.ml.count-columns`, `gen.ml.find-leaks`, `gen.ml.poisson-rate`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* In $y = \mathbf x^\top\boldsymbol\beta + \varepsilon$ with independent errors of variance $\sigma^2$, show that $\operatorname{Var}(y_0 - \mathbf x_0^\top\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}) = \sigma^2\left(1 + \mathbf x_0^\top(\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X)^{-1}\mathbf x_0\right)$ for a new point, contrast it with the variance of the fitted mean, and explain which interval a forecaster should quote and what more data do to each.
  *Model answer.* (1) $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} = (\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X)^{-1}\mathbf X^\top\mathbf y$ has covariance $\sigma^2(\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X)^{-1}$ (1.6 lesson 48). (2) So the fitted mean $\mathbf x_0^\top\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$ has variance $\sigma^2\mathbf x_0^\top(\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X)^{-1}\mathbf x_0$. (3) The new $y_0 = \mathbf x_0^\top\boldsymbol\beta + \varepsilon_0$ has its own error $\varepsilon_0$, independent of the training errors, hence of $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$. (4) $y_0 - \mathbf x_0^\top\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} = \varepsilon_0 - \mathbf x_0^\top(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \boldsymbol\beta)$, and the variance of a difference of independent terms is the sum: $\sigma^2 + \sigma^2\mathbf x_0^\top(\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X)^{-1}\mathbf x_0$. (5) The confidence interval for the mean uses line 2; the prediction interval uses line 4, and a forecaster of one outcome must quote the prediction interval. (6) As $n$ grows, $(\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X)^{-1}$ shrinks like $\frac1n$, so the mean's interval shrinks to zero while the prediction interval shrinks only to $\pm 1.96\sigma$, the noise floor. (7) Both widen as $\mathbf x_0$ moves from the centre of the data, because $\mathbf x_0^\top(\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X)^{-1}\mathbf x_0$ is a Mahalanobis-type distance from $\bar{\mathbf x}$ when an intercept is included.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the covariance of $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$ (line 1). 1: the mean's variance (line 2). 2: independence of the new error (line 3). 2: the prediction variance (line 4). 2: which interval and why (line 5). 2: the behaviour in $n$ and in $\mathbf x_0$ (lines 6, 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Trades per minute on a venue follow a Poisson regression $\ln\lambda = 0.5 + 0.2x_1 - 0.1x_2$, where $x_1$ is volatility (in daily %) and $x_2$ the quoted spread (in ticks). (a) Expected trades per minute at $x = (2, 3)$? (b) Interpret the two coefficients. (c) An analyst instead fits least squares to the counts. What goes wrong?
  *Answer.* (a) $e^{0.5 + 0.4 - 0.3} = e^{0.6} = 1.82$ trades a minute. (b) One more point of volatility multiplies the rate by $e^{0.2} = 1.22$ (22% more trades); one more tick of spread multiplies it by $e^{-0.1} = 0.905$ (9.5% fewer). (c) Decision point: least squares on counts is the naive choice. It can predict negative counts for calm, wide-spread minutes, assumes constant variance where a Poisson's variance equals its mean, and fits additive effects where the data multiply. If the counts are overdispersed, the Poisson's standard errors are too small as well, so check the variance against the mean before quoting them.

**LS2 Beyond linearity** (`set.shrinkage.nonlinear`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: counting the degrees of freedom of regression and natural splines. Code drill `drl.ml.spline-basis` (d4, `numpy`): `cubic_spline_basis(x, knots)` (truncated powers: $1, x, x^2, x^3, (x - \xi_k)_+^3$) and `fit_spline(x, y, knots)`. Hidden tests: the basis has shape $(n, K + 4)$; a cubic $y = 1 - 2x + x^3$ is fitted with residuals below $10^{-10}$; the fitted function and its first two derivatives are continuous at each knot (one-sided finite differences agree to $10^{-6}$); the third derivative jumps; with no knots it is cubic regression. Known-wrong: $(x - \xi_k)_+^2$ terms (fails the second-derivative test). Generators: `gen.ml.df-count`, `gen.ml.place-knots`, `gen.ml.match-family`, `gen.ml.runge`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Count the free parameters of a cubic regression spline with $K$ interior knots by counting cubic pieces and continuity constraints. (b) Do the same for a natural cubic spline, which is linear beyond the boundary knots. (c) Explain why the natural spline behaves better at the edges.
  *Model answer.* (1) $K$ knots cut the line into $K + 1$ pieces, each a cubic with 4 coefficients: $4(K + 1)$ parameters. (2) At each knot the function, its first derivative and its second derivative must agree from both sides: 3 constraints per knot, $3K$ in all. (3) Free parameters: $4K + 4 - 3K = K + 4$, matching the truncated power basis $1, x, x^2, x^3$ plus one $(x - \xi_k)_+^3$ per knot. (4) A natural spline adds that the function is linear on the two outer pieces, which removes the quadratic and cubic coefficients there: 2 constraints at each end, 4 in all. (5) So it has $K + 4 - 4 = K$ free parameters when the boundary knots are counted among the $K$ knots, intercept included (ESL 5.2.1's convention [check]). (6) Polynomials grow without bound past the data and their high-degree terms are poorly pinned near the ends, where there are fewest points, so the variance of the fit is largest there (lesson 40). (7) Forcing linearity beyond the boundary knots spends the freed degrees of freedom inside the data and stops the cubic terms from swinging at the edges, at the cost of a little bias if the truth curves there.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: pieces and coefficients (line 1). 2: the three constraints per knot (line 2). 1: $K + 4$ with the basis (line 3). 2: the natural constraints (line 4). 1: $K$, with the counting convention stated (line 5). 3: the edge argument in terms of variance and the trade made (lines 6, 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Interpolate $f(x) = \frac{1}{1 + 25x^2}$ at 11 equally spaced points on $[-1, 1]$. (a) Using a degree-10 polynomial, what is the largest error on $[-1, 1]$ and where? (b) Using Chebyshev nodes instead? (c) Using a cubic spline through the 11 equally spaced points? (d) What does this teach about choosing a basis for a volume curve over the trading day?
  *Answer.* (a) 1.92, near $x = \pm 0.94$. (b) 0.109. (c) 0.022 (natural or not-a-knot end conditions give almost the same). (d) Decision point: the naive move is "more data points, higher degree". A global polynomial's error grows towards the ends of the interval even as degree rises, and the open and close are exactly the ends of a trading day, where volume matters most for execution. Local bases (splines) or node placement fix it; a degree-10 polynomial through ten half-hour buckets is the sample lesson's curve through every point.

**LS3 Ridge** (`set.shrinkage.ridge`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: ridge's solution, its existence and its SVD form. Code drill `drl.ml.ridge-svd` (d3, `numpy`): `ridge(X, y, lam)` with an unpenalised intercept (centre $\mathbf X$ and $\mathbf y$, solve, restore the intercept) via the SVD; `ridge_df(X, lam)`. Hidden tests: equals `np.linalg.solve(X.T @ X + lam * I, X.T @ y)` on centred data to $10^{-10}$; `ridge_df` on singular values with squares 10, 1 and 0.1 at $\lambda = 1$ gives 1.5; $\lambda = 0$ with full rank equals least squares; adding a constant to $\mathbf y$ changes only the intercept. Known-wrong: penalising the intercept (fails test 4). Generators: `gen.ml.ridge-1d`, `gen.ml.ridge-df`, `gen.ml.shrink-mse`, `gen.ml.map-lambda`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Derive the ridge solution $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_\lambda = (\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X + \lambda I)^{-1}\mathbf X^\top\mathbf y$ and show the matrix is invertible for every $\lambda > 0$. (b) Using $\mathbf X = \mathbf U\mathbf D\mathbf V^\top$, show the fit is $\sum_j\mathbf u_j\frac{d_j^2}{d_j^2 + \lambda}\mathbf u_j^\top\mathbf y$, and say which directions shrink most.
  *Model answer.* (1) The objective $\Vert\mathbf y - \mathbf X\boldsymbol\beta\Vert^2 + \lambda\Vert\boldsymbol\beta\Vert^2$ has gradient $-2\mathbf X^\top(\mathbf y - \mathbf X\boldsymbol\beta) + 2\lambda\boldsymbol\beta$ (lesson 20). (2) Setting it to zero: $(\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X + \lambda I)\boldsymbol\beta = \mathbf X^\top\mathbf y$. (3) For any $\mathbf v\ne\mathbf 0$, $\mathbf v^\top(\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X + \lambda I)\mathbf v = \Vert\mathbf X\mathbf v\Vert^2 + \lambda\Vert\mathbf v\Vert^2 > 0$, so the matrix is positive definite, hence invertible, even when $\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X$ is singular; the objective is strictly convex, so this is the unique minimiser. (4) With the thin SVD, $\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X = \mathbf V\mathbf D^2\mathbf V^\top$ and $\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X + \lambda I = \mathbf V(\mathbf D^2 + \lambda I)\mathbf V^\top$ on the row space. (5) So $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_\lambda = \mathbf V(\mathbf D^2 + \lambda I)^{-1}\mathbf D\mathbf U^\top\mathbf y$ and $\mathbf X\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_\lambda = \mathbf U\mathbf D(\mathbf D^2 + \lambda I)^{-1}\mathbf D\mathbf U^\top\mathbf y = \sum_j\mathbf u_j\frac{d_j^2}{d_j^2 + \lambda}\mathbf u_j^\top\mathbf y$. (6) The leap made concrete: in the SVD basis ridge stops being a matrix inverse and becomes one shrinkage factor per direction. (7) The factor is near 1 when $d_j^2\gg\lambda$ and near 0 when $d_j^2\ll\lambda$, so ridge shrinks most along directions of small variance in $\mathbf X$, where least squares is noisiest; OLS is $\lambda = 0$, every factor 1.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the gradient (line 1). 1: the normal equations (line 2). 2: positive definiteness and uniqueness (line 3). 2: the SVD substitution (lines 4, 5). 2: the fit as shrinkage factors (lines 5, 6). 2: which directions shrink and why it helps (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* In an orthonormal design ($\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X = I$) with noise variance $\sigma^2$, the OLS estimate $z$ of one coefficient $\beta$ is unbiased with variance $\sigma^2$, and ridge gives $\frac{z}{1 + \lambda}$. (a) Find the ridge estimate's MSE as a function of $\lambda$. (b) Find the best $\lambda$ and the MSE there. (c) For $\sigma^2 = 1$ and $\beta = 0.5$, compare with OLS. (d) Why can you not simply use this $\lambda$?
  *Answer.* (a) Bias $-\frac{\lambda\beta}{1 + \lambda}$ and variance $\frac{\sigma^2}{(1 + \lambda)^2}$, so $\text{MSE}(\lambda) = \frac{\sigma^2 + \lambda^2\beta^2}{(1 + \lambda)^2}$. (b) The derivative is zero at $\lambda^* = \frac{\sigma^2}{\beta^2}$, where $\text{MSE} = \frac{\sigma^2\beta^2}{\sigma^2 + \beta^2}$. (c) $\lambda^* = 4$ and $\text{MSE} = \frac{0.25}{1.25} = 0.2$, against OLS's 1: five times smaller. (d) Decision point: $\lambda^*$ needs the unknown $\beta$, the same trap as 1.6's shrinkage factor. In practice $\lambda$ is chosen by cross-validation, which estimates the trade-off from data, or set by a prior belief about the size of coefficients (lesson 49); the result also shows why ridge helps most when signals are small relative to noise, as in finance.

**LS4 The lasso and the elastic net** (`set.shrinkage.lasso`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the soft threshold from subgradients. Code drill `drl.ml.lasso-cd` (d4, `numpy`): `lasso_cd(X, y, lam, tol=1e-10, max_iter=10000)` minimising $\frac{1}{2n}\Vert\mathbf y - \mathbf X\boldsymbol\beta\Vert^2 + \lambda\Vert\boldsymbol\beta\Vert_1$ (scikit-learn's scaling) on standardised columns, and `lambda_max(X, y)`. Hidden tests: with $\frac1n\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X = I$ it returns the soft threshold of $\frac1n\mathbf X^\top\mathbf y$ (the hook gives $(0, -0.15, 0)$ at 0.35); $\lambda\ge\lambda_{\max}$ gives exact zeros; the KKT conditions hold to $10^{-6}$ ($\lvert\frac1n\mathbf x_j^\top\mathbf r\rvert\le\lambda$ for zero coefficients, $= \lambda\operatorname{sign}(\beta_j)$ otherwise); a stored scikit-learn `Lasso(alpha=0.1)` fixture matches to $10^{-6}$. Known-wrong: no residual update between coordinates (fails the KKT test); threshold $\lambda/2$ (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.ml.soft-threshold`, `gen.ml.lasso-active-set`, `gen.ml.lambda-1se`, `gen.ml.match-thresholds`, `gen.ml.budget-to-lambda`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* With $\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X = I$ and objective $\frac12\Vert\mathbf y - \mathbf X\boldsymbol\beta\Vert^2 + \lambda\Vert\boldsymbol\beta\Vert_1$, derive $\hat\beta_j = \operatorname{sign}(z_j)(\lvert z_j\rvert - \lambda)_+$ where $z_j = \mathbf x_j^\top\mathbf y$, and explain why the lasso gives exact zeros while ridge never does.
  *Model answer.* (1) Expand: $\frac12\Vert\mathbf y\Vert^2 - \boldsymbol\beta^\top\mathbf X^\top\mathbf y + \frac12\boldsymbol\beta^\top\boldsymbol\beta + \lambda\sum_j\lvert\beta_j\rvert = \text{const} + \sum_j\left(\frac12\beta_j^2 - z_j\beta_j + \lambda\lvert\beta_j\rvert\right)$, so the problem separates into one-dimensional problems. (2) The leap: $\lvert\beta\rvert$ has no derivative at 0, the very point of interest, so use its subgradient: $\partial\lvert\beta\rvert = \{\operatorname{sign}\beta\}$ for $\beta\ne 0$ and the interval $[-1, 1]$ at 0. (3) Optimality: $0\in\beta - z + \lambda\,\partial\lvert\beta\rvert$. (4) If $\beta > 0$: $\beta = z - \lambda$, which needs $z > \lambda$. If $\beta < 0$: $\beta = z + \lambda$, which needs $z < -\lambda$. (5) If $\beta = 0$: $z\in\lambda[-1, 1]$, that is $\lvert z\rvert\le\lambda$. (6) Together: $\hat\beta = \operatorname{sign}(z)(\lvert z\rvert - \lambda)_+$, the soft threshold. (7) Ridge's one-dimensional problem $\frac12\beta^2 - z\beta + \frac\lambda2\beta^2$ is smooth with solution $\frac{z}{1 + \lambda}$, zero only when $z = 0$; the lasso's kink at 0 makes a whole interval of $z$ map to exactly 0. Geometrically, the $L_1$ ball has corners on the axes (lesson 50). (8) With ISLP's objective, "RSS $+\lambda\sum\lvert\beta_j\rvert$", every term doubles except the penalty, so the threshold is $\frac\lambda2$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the separation (line 1). 2: the subgradient with its motivation (line 2). 3: the three cases (lines 3 to 5). 1: the soft threshold (line 6). 2: why ridge never gives zeros, with the scaling convention (lines 7, 8).
- *Example B (d5).* Standardised features with $\frac1n\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X = I$ and $\frac1n\mathbf X^\top\mathbf y = (0.3, -0.5, 0.1)$, objective as in the drill. (a) Find $\lambda_{\max}$. (b) Give the lasso fit at $\lambda = 0.35$ and at $\lambda = 0.2$. (c) Now features 1 and 2 correlate at 0.9 instead. Will the active set at $\lambda = 0.35$ necessarily be the same? (d) What would you report?
  *Answer.* (a) $\lambda_{\max} = \max_j\lvert\frac1n\mathbf x_j^\top\mathbf y\rvert = 0.5$. (b) At 0.35: $(0, -0.15, 0)$. At 0.2: $(0.1, -0.3, 0)$. (c) No. The soft-threshold formula needs orthogonal features; with correlation 0.9 the coordinates interact, the second feature's coefficient absorbs some of the first's role, and which of the two survives can change with a small resample (lesson 55). (d) Decision point: the naive report lists the selected features as "the drivers". Report the fit path and how often each feature is selected across resamples, consider the elastic net to keep correlated features together, and never quote ordinary $p$-values for the selected set (lesson 54).

**LS5 High dimensions** (`set.shrinkage.high-dimensions`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the minimum-norm interpolator and ridge's limit. Code drill `drl.ml.pcr` (d3, `numpy`): `pcr_fit(X, y, m)` on centred columns and `pcr_predict(model, X_new)` using the training means. Hidden tests: $m = p$ equals least squares to $10^{-10}$; the component scores are orthogonal; predicting the training rows reproduces the fitted values; new rows are centred by training means, not their own. Known-wrong: centring new rows by their own mean (fails test 4). Generators: `gen.ml.pcr-vs-ridge`, `gen.ml.min-norm`, `gen.ml.double-descent-peak`, `gen.ml.james-stein`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $\mathbf X$ be $n\times p$ with $p > n$ and full row rank. (a) Show that $\mathbf X\boldsymbol\beta = \mathbf y$ has infinitely many solutions. (b) Show that $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_0 = \mathbf X^\top(\mathbf X\mathbf X^\top)^{-1}\mathbf y$ is the one of smallest norm. (c) Show that ridge tends to it as $\lambda\to 0$.
  *Model answer.* (1) The null space of $\mathbf X$ has dimension $p - n > 0$ (rank-nullity), so if $\boldsymbol\beta$ solves the system, so does $\boldsymbol\beta + \mathbf v$ for any $\mathbf v$ in the null space. (2) $\mathbf X\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_0 = \mathbf X\mathbf X^\top(\mathbf X\mathbf X^\top)^{-1}\mathbf y = \mathbf y$, and $\mathbf X\mathbf X^\top$ is invertible because $\mathbf X$ has full row rank. (3) $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_0 = \mathbf X^\top\mathbf c$ lies in the row space, which is orthogonal to the null space. (4) Any other solution is $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_0 + \mathbf v$ with $\mathbf v$ in the null space, and by Pythagoras $\Vert\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_0 + \mathbf v\Vert^2 = \Vert\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_0\Vert^2 + \Vert\mathbf v\Vert^2$, which is smallest at $\mathbf v = \mathbf 0$. (5) The leap: ridge's formula with $(\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X + \lambda I)^{-1}$ involves a singular matrix as $\lambda\to 0$; the push-through identity $(\mathbf X^\top\mathbf X + \lambda I)^{-1}\mathbf X^\top = \mathbf X^\top(\mathbf X\mathbf X^\top + \lambda I)^{-1}$ moves the inverse to the invertible side. (6) Then $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_\lambda = \mathbf X^\top(\mathbf X\mathbf X^\top + \lambda I)^{-1}\mathbf y\to\mathbf X^\top(\mathbf X\mathbf X^\top)^{-1}\mathbf y = \hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_0$. (7) This is the pseudoinverse solution (1.4 lesson 87), and the interpolator gradient descent from zero converges to, which is why double descent's right-hand side (lesson 58) is about minimum-norm fits.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: infinitely many solutions (line 1). 1: $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_0$ solves the system (line 2). 3: minimum norm by orthogonality (lines 3, 4). 3: the limit through the push-through identity, with its motivation (lines 5, 6). 1: the link to the pseudoinverse or double descent (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* 50 candidate signals are estimated independently with standard error 1, so their $z$-scores are $\hat\theta_i\sim N(\theta_i, 1)$, and $\sum_i z_i^2 = 75$. (a) Compute the James-Stein shrinkage factor towards zero. (b) The best signal has $z = 3.2$. What does James-Stein estimate for it? (c) A colleague wants to trade only the top five signals at their raw $z$-scores. What do you say?
  *Answer.* (a) $1 - \frac{k - 2}{\sum z_i^2} = 1 - \frac{48}{75} = 0.36$. (b) $0.36\times 3.2 = 1.15$. (c) Decision point: the naive plan trusts the largest estimates most, but the largest are the ones most inflated by noise (lesson 144's winner's curse); James-Stein's total squared error beats the raw estimates for every configuration of true $\theta$ when $k\ge 3$. Shrink first (towards zero, or towards the group mean if the signals share a premium), size positions on the shrunk values, and expect next year's top five to regress.

### 3.3 Course CP (2.1)

**CP1 Logistic regression** (`set.classify.logistic`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the MLE under separation. Code drill `drl.ml.logistic-newton` (d4, `numpy`): `fit_logistic(X, y, max_iter=50, tol=1e-10)` by Newton's method with an intercept column, raising `SeparationError` when the coefficient norm passes $10^3$ or the Hessian's condition number passes $10^{12}$; `softmax(z)` computed stably. Hidden tests: a stored statsmodels `Logit` fixture matches to $10^{-8}$; an intercept-only fit returns $\ln\frac{\bar y}{1 - \bar y}$; separable data raise `SeparationError`; `softmax([1000, 999, 998])` returns $(0.665, 0.245, 0.090)$ to $10^{-3}$ without warnings. Known-wrong: `exp` without subtracting the maximum (fails test 4, returns `nan`); no separation check (fails test 3 by running to `max_iter` with exploding coefficients). Generators: `gen.ml.p-at-x`, `gen.ml.odds-ratio`, `gen.ml.log-loss`, `gen.ml.fit-by-hand`, `gen.ml.softmax`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Suppose some $\mathbf w_0$ separates the classes: $\mathbf x_i^\top\mathbf w_0 > 0$ whenever $y_i = 1$ and $< 0$ whenever $y_i = 0$. (a) Show the log-likelihood along $c\mathbf w_0$ rises towards 0 as $c\to\infty$, so no finite maximiser exists. (b) Show a ridge penalty restores a unique finite maximiser. (c) Say what a practitioner sees and should do.
  *Model answer.* (1) The log-likelihood is $\sum_i[y_i\ln\sigma(z_i) + (1 - y_i)\ln(1 - \sigma(z_i))]$ with $z_i = c\,\mathbf x_i^\top\mathbf w_0$. (2) For $y_i = 1$, $z_i\to+\infty$ and $\ln\sigma(z_i)\to 0$; for $y_i = 0$, $z_i\to-\infty$ and $\ln(1 - \sigma(z_i))\to 0$. Every term increases strictly with $c$. (3) So the log-likelihood increases towards its supremum 0, which no finite $\mathbf w$ attains, since every term is strictly negative at finite $\mathbf w$. The MLE does not exist (`mc.ml.separable-is-easy`). (4) With a penalty, maximise $\ell(\mathbf w) - \frac\lambda2\Vert\mathbf w\Vert^2$. The leap: the likelihood alone has no reason to stop; the penalty grows without bound along every ray while the likelihood is bounded above by 0, so the objective tends to $-\infty$ in every direction. (5) A continuous function that tends to $-\infty$ in every direction attains its maximum; it is strictly concave (the log-likelihood is concave, lesson 63, and $-\frac\lambda2\Vert\mathbf w\Vert^2$ strictly so), so the maximiser is unique. (6) In practice the optimiser's coefficients and standard errors explode, fitted probabilities hit 0 and 1, and the library warns. The remedy is a penalty (scikit-learn's default $L_2$, or the Firth correction), or reconsidering whether the separating feature is a leak (lesson 126).
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the log-likelihood along the ray (line 1). 2: the limits of each term (line 2). 2: non-existence with its reason (line 3). 2: the penalised objective and the motivation (line 4). 2: existence and uniqueness (line 5). 1: what to do, including the leak check (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A three-class model outputs logits $(1000, 999, 998)$ for one observation whose true class is the first. (a) What does a naive implementation of softmax return? (b) Compute the probabilities correctly. (c) Compute the cross-entropy loss and its gradient with respect to the logits. (d) What general rule does this illustrate?
  *Answer.* (a) $e^{1000}$ overflows to `inf` in double precision, so the naive ratio is $\frac{\infty}{\infty} = $ `nan` and the loss is `nan`. (b) Subtracting the maximum leaves the probabilities unchanged (lesson 65): logits $(0, -1, -2)$ give $(0.665, 0.245, 0.090)$. (c) Loss $-\ln 0.665 = 0.408$; gradient $\mathbf p - \mathbf e_1 = (-0.335, 0.245, 0.090)$. Computed as $\operatorname{logsumexp}(\mathbf z) - z_1 = 1000.408 - 1000$ it never forms a probability at all. (d) Decision point: the naive formula is mathematically right and numerically wrong. Work on the log scale (log-sum-exp) and use the invariance to shift; libraries fuse softmax and cross-entropy for this reason.

**CP2 Generative classifiers** (`set.classify.generative`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: LDA's linear discriminant. Code drill `drl.ml.lda` (d4, `numpy`): `fit_lda(X, y, priors=None)` with class means, pooled covariance (divisor $n - K$) and priors; `predict`. Hidden tests: predictions agree with a stored scikit-learn `LinearDiscriminantAnalysis(solver="lsqr")` fixture on 200 rows; one feature, two classes with means 0 and 2, variance 1 and priors $(0.8, 0.2)$ give the boundary 1.693 to $10^{-9}$; equal priors move it to 1. Known-wrong: divisor $n$ for the pooled covariance (fails test 1 on the fixture's unbalanced classes); priors ignored (fails test 2). Generators: `gen.ml.bayes-boundary`, `gen.ml.lda-boundary`, `gen.ml.param-count`, `gen.ml.naive-bayes-posterior`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Classes $k = 1, \dots, K$ have priors $\pi_k$ and densities $N(\boldsymbol\mu_k, \boldsymbol\Sigma)$ with a shared covariance. (a) Derive the discriminant $\delta_k(\mathbf x) = \mathbf x^\top\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1}\boldsymbol\mu_k - \frac12\boldsymbol\mu_k^\top\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1}\boldsymbol\mu_k + \ln\pi_k$ and show the boundaries are linear. (b) Give the one-dimensional boundary between two classes. (c) Say what changes when the covariances differ.
  *Model answer.* (1) The Bayes classifier picks the $k$ maximising $\pi_kf_k(\mathbf x)$, equivalently $\ln\pi_k + \ln f_k(\mathbf x)$ (lesson 66). (2) $\ln f_k(\mathbf x) = -\frac12(\mathbf x - \boldsymbol\mu_k)^\top\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1}(\mathbf x - \boldsymbol\mu_k) + c$, where $c$ holds $\ln\det\boldsymbol\Sigma$ and $\pi$ factors common to every class. (3) Expand: $-\frac12\mathbf x^\top\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf x + \mathbf x^\top\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1}\boldsymbol\mu_k - \frac12\boldsymbol\mu_k^\top\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1}\boldsymbol\mu_k$. (4) The leap: the quadratic term in $\mathbf x$ is the same for every class because $\boldsymbol\Sigma$ is shared, so it drops out of every comparison, leaving $\delta_k$ linear in $\mathbf x$. (5) The boundary between $k$ and $l$ is $\delta_k(\mathbf x) = \delta_l(\mathbf x)$, a linear equation: a hyperplane. (6) In one dimension with variance $\sigma^2$: $x\frac{\mu_k - \mu_l}{\sigma^2} = \frac{\mu_k^2 - \mu_l^2}{2\sigma^2} + \ln\frac{\pi_l}{\pi_k}$, so $x^* = \frac{\mu_k + \mu_l}{2} + \frac{\sigma^2}{\mu_k - \mu_l}\ln\frac{\pi_l}{\pi_k}$: the midpoint, moved away from the more likely class. (7) With class-specific $\boldsymbol\Sigma_k$ the quadratic terms and the $\ln\det\boldsymbol\Sigma_k$ terms differ by class and stay, so the boundaries are quadratic (QDA), at the cost of $K$ covariance matrices to estimate.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the Bayes rule on the log scale (line 1). 1: the log density (line 2). 2: the expansion (line 3). 2: the cancellation of the quadratic term and its reason (line 4). 1: linear boundaries (line 5). 2: the one-dimensional boundary with the prior shift (line 6). 1: the QDA case (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A headline classifier predicts whether a stock moves more than 2% today. Word A appears in 80% of moving headlines and 30% of the rest; word B always appears exactly when A does. The prior is $\frac12$. (a) The true probability of a move given both words? (b) Naive Bayes's probability? (c) Do the two rank headlines differently? (d) What would you use the model for?
  *Answer.* (a) B adds nothing to A: $\frac{0.8\times 0.5}{0.8\times 0.5 + 0.3\times 0.5} = 0.727$. (b) Naive Bayes multiplies the evidence twice: $\frac{0.8^2}{0.8^2 + 0.3^2} = \frac{0.64}{0.73} = 0.877$. (c) For these headlines, no: both are increasing in the same evidence, so naive Bayes ranks the same way while overstating confidence. (d) Decision point: the naive use sizes positions on 0.877. Use naive Bayes for ranking or screening, and recalibrate its probabilities on held-out data (lesson 79) before betting on them (lesson 80).

**CP3 Evaluating classifiers** (`set.classify.evaluation`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: AUC as a ranking probability. Code drill `drl.ml.metrics` (d3, `stdlib`): `confusion(y, yhat)`, `precision`, `recall`, `f1`, and `auc(scores, labels)` by pair counting with ties counted half, returning a `Fraction` when the scores are exact. Hidden tests: the hook's counts give precision $\frac25$, recall $\frac45$, $F_1 = \frac{8}{15}$; the hook's scores give AUC $\frac{11}{16}$; one positive and one negative with equal scores give $\frac12$; all scores equal give $\frac12$; no positives raise `ValueError`; precision with nothing flagged raises `ZeroDivisionError` with a message. Known-wrong: ties counted as wins (fails test 3); $F_1$ including true negatives (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.ml.counts-to-metrics`, `gen.ml.pair-auc`, `gen.ml.imbalance-precision`, `gen.ml.bayes-threshold`, `gen.ml.profit-threshold`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Define the ROC curve of a scoring rule and prove that the area under it equals $P(S^+ > S^-) + \frac12P(S^+ = S^-)$ for a random positive and an independent random negative. (b) Explain why AUC is unchanged by the base rate and by any increasing transform of the scores, and what that implies about using it to choose a threshold.
  *Model answer.* (1) At threshold $t$, $\text{TPR}(t) = P(S^+ > t)$ and $\text{FPR}(t) = P(S^- > t)$; the ROC curve plots TPR against FPR as $t$ falls from $+\infty$ to $-\infty$. (2) For finite samples with $n^+$ positives and $n^-$ negatives, lowering the threshold past one negative's score moves FPR right by $\frac{1}{n^-}$ while TPR stays at the fraction of positives scored above that negative. (3) The area of that vertical strip is $\frac{1}{n^-}\cdot\frac{\#\{\text{positives above it}\}}{n^+}$. (4) Summing over negatives counts, for each negative, the positives that outscore it: area $= \frac{1}{n^+n^-}\#\{(i, j): s_i^+ > s_j^-\}$. (5) A positive and a negative with equal scores move the curve diagonally, adding a triangle of half the strip: the $\frac12$ for ties. (6) The count is the probability that a randomly chosen positive outscores a randomly chosen negative, which is the Mann-Whitney statistic divided by $n^+n^-$. (7) TPR and FPR are each computed within one class, so the class proportions never enter: the base rate cannot change AUC. An increasing transform preserves every pairwise order, so it cannot change the count. (8) Hence AUC measures ranking only: it says nothing about calibration or about the threshold, which must come from costs and base rates (lessons 74, 75).
  *Rubric (10).* 1: definitions (line 1). 2: the strip argument (lines 2, 3). 2: the pair count (line 4). 1: ties (line 5). 1: the probability reading (line 6). 2: base-rate and transform invariance (line 7). 1: the implication for thresholds (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* A default model's scores are calibrated. A missed default costs £50 and a false alarm £5. The base rate is 2%. Two operating points: threshold 0.5 gives TPR 0.40 and FPR 0.01; threshold 0.09 gives TPR 0.85 and FPR 0.06. (a) What threshold does the cost rule suggest? (b) Expected cost per 1,000 applicants at each operating point? (c) Which do you choose, and what would accuracy have said?
  *Answer.* (a) $p^* = \frac{5}{5 + 50} = 0.091$. (b) Per 1,000: 20 defaulters and 980 others. At 0.5: 12 missed defaults cost £600 and 9.8 false alarms £49, £649 in all. At 0.09: 3 missed cost £150 and 58.8 false alarms £294, £444. (c) Decision point: accuracy prefers the 0.5 threshold (it makes $12 + 9.8 = 21.8$ errors against $3 + 58.8 = 61.8$), which is the naive choice; costs prefer 0.09, saving £205 per 1,000, as the cost rule predicted. Check the calibration before trusting $p^*$, because the rule needs honest probabilities.

**CP4 Probabilities you can bet on** (`set.classify.calibration`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the Brier score is proper; Murphy's decomposition. Code drill `drl.ml.calibration` (d3, `stdlib`): `brier(p, y)`, `log_loss(p, y, eps=1e-15)` with clipping, and `pav(values, weights=None)` (pool adjacent violators). Hidden tests: the hook's forecasts give Brier 0.142 and log loss 0.4297; a forecast of 0 for an event that happens gives log loss $-\ln 10^{-15} = 34.54$, not infinity; `pav([0.1, 0.3, 0.2, 0.4])` gives $[0.1, 0.25, 0.25, 0.4]$; PAV output is non-decreasing on 1,000 random inputs. Known-wrong: no clipping (fails test 2); PAV that pools only the first violation (fails test 4). Generators: `gen.ml.brier`, `gen.ml.read-diagram`, `gen.ml.kelly`, `gen.ml.ldp-size`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) For a binary event with true probability $p$, show the expected Brier score of forecast $q$ is minimised at $q = p$. (b) For forecasts grouped into bins $k$ with forecast $q_k$, observed frequency $\bar o_k$ and share $w_k$, and base rate $\bar o$, state Murphy's decomposition $\text{BS} = \sum_kw_k(q_k - \bar o_k)^2 - \sum_kw_k(\bar o_k - \bar o)^2 + \bar o(1 - \bar o)$ and interpret each term. (c) Why is accuracy not a proper score?
  *Model answer.* (1) $E[(q - Y)^2] = p(q - 1)^2 + (1 - p)q^2 = q^2 - 2pq + p$. (2) Its derivative $2q - 2p$ is zero at $q = p$ and the second derivative is 2, so the minimum is at the truth: honest forecasting is optimal, which defines a proper score. (3) Murphy's first term is reliability: the squared gap between what was said and what happened, bin by bin (0 for a calibrated forecaster). (4) The second, subtracted, is resolution: how far the bins' frequencies move from the base rate, so a forecaster who separates easy and hard cases scores better. (5) The third is uncertainty, $\bar o(1 - \bar o)$, a property of the event, not of the forecaster. (6) A forecaster who always says $\bar o$ has zero reliability error and zero resolution: calibrated and useless, scoring $\bar o(1 - \bar o)$. (7) Accuracy rewards only which side of $\frac12$ a forecast falls: a forecaster who believes $p = 0.6$ scores the same with $q = 0.51$ or $q = 0.99$, so it gives no incentive to report the probability, and it cannot be proper.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the expectation (line 1). 2: the minimum at $q = p$ with the second-order check (line 2). 3: the three terms interpreted (lines 3 to 5). 1: the base-rate forecaster (line 6). 2: why accuracy is not proper (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A model says a trade wins with probability 0.6 at even money; the truth is 0.55. Growth per bet is $g(f) = p\ln(1 + f) + (1 - p)\ln(1 - f)$. (a) The Kelly fraction the model recommends, and the true Kelly fraction? (b) The true growth per bet at each? (c) What sizing would you use, and what does it say about calibration?
  *Answer.* (a) Model: $2\times 0.6 - 1 = 0.2$. True: $2\times 0.55 - 1 = 0.1$. (b) At $f = 0.1$: $0.55\ln 1.1 + 0.45\ln 0.9 = 0.00501$. At $f = 0.2$: $0.55\ln 1.2 + 0.45\ln 0.8 = -0.00014$, slightly negative. (c) Decision point: the naive move bets the model's Kelly fraction. A five-point overstatement doubled the bet and turned positive growth into a slow loss, because betting twice the Kelly fraction grows at about zero. Recalibrate probabilities on held-out data (lesson 79), then bet a fraction of Kelly (half Kelly keeps three quarters of the growth at the true $p$ and is far safer if $p$ is overstated).

**CP5 Margins and kernels** (`set.classify.kernels`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the margin's width and why only support vectors matter. Code drill `drl.ml.linear-svm` (d4, `numpy`): `fit_linear_svm(X, y, lam, epochs, seed)` by the Pegasos subgradient method on hinge loss plus $\frac\lambda2\Vert\mathbf w\Vert^2$. Hidden tests: on a separable fixture with $\lambda = 10^{-4}$ every training margin $y_i\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x_i$ is at least $1 - 10^{-2}$; the direction is within 2 degrees of a stored scikit-learn `SVC(kernel="linear", C=1e4)` fixture; a fixed seed reproduces the stored weights to $10^{-12}$; labels must be $\pm 1$ (a 0 label raises `ValueError`). Known-wrong: labels in $\{0, 1\}$ silently accepted (fails test 4); subgradient taken for points with margin above 1 (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.ml.support-vectors`, `gen.ml.hinge`, `gen.ml.kernel-value`, `gen.ml.margin-width`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) For a separating hyperplane $\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x + b = 0$ scaled so that the closest points satisfy $y_i(\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x_i + b) = 1$, show the margin's width is $\frac{2}{\Vert\mathbf w\Vert}$ and write the hard-margin problem. (b) Using the KKT conditions, explain why only the support vectors determine the solution.
  *Model answer.* (1) The distance from $\mathbf x$ to the hyperplane is $\frac{\lvert\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x + b\rvert}{\Vert\mathbf w\Vert}$ (1.4's projection onto the normal $\frac{\mathbf w}{\Vert\mathbf w\Vert}$). (2) With the scaling, the closest points on each side are at distance $\frac{1}{\Vert\mathbf w\Vert}$, so the margin is $\frac{2}{\Vert\mathbf w\Vert}$; for $\mathbf w = (3, 4)$ it is 0.4. (3) Maximising the margin is minimising $\frac12\Vert\mathbf w\Vert^2$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x_i + b)\ge 1$ for all $i$. (4) The leap: a constrained problem with inequality constraints is solved through multipliers $\alpha_i\ge 0$ (1.3's Lagrange multipliers extended): stationarity gives $\mathbf w = \sum_i\alpha_iy_i\mathbf x_i$. (5) Complementary slackness: $\alpha_i\left[y_i(\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x_i + b) - 1\right] = 0$, so $\alpha_i > 0$ only for points exactly on the margin. (6) Hence $\mathbf w$ is a combination of the margin points alone, the support vectors; moving any other point, without crossing the margin, changes no $\alpha$ and no boundary. (7) The dual depends on the data only through inner products $\mathbf x_i^\top\mathbf x_j$, which is what lets a kernel replace them (lesson 84).
  *Rubric (10).* 1: point-to-plane distance (line 1). 2: the width (line 2). 1: the problem (line 3). 2: stationarity with the multipliers motivated (line 4). 2: complementary slackness (line 5). 1: the support-vector conclusion (line 6). 1: the inner-product form (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* (a) Show that $K(\mathbf x, \mathbf z) = (1 + \mathbf x^\top\mathbf z)^2$ on $\mathbb R^2$ equals $\phi(\mathbf x)^\top\phi(\mathbf z)$ for $\phi(\mathbf x) = (1, \sqrt2x_1, \sqrt2x_2, x_1^2, x_2^2, \sqrt2x_1x_2)$, and check it at $\mathbf x = (1, 2)$, $\mathbf z = (3, 1)$. (b) How many features does the degree-2 map have for 100 inputs? (c) When would you build the features explicitly instead?
  *Answer.* (a) $(1 + x_1z_1 + x_2z_2)^2 = 1 + 2x_1z_1 + 2x_2z_2 + x_1^2z_1^2 + x_2^2z_2^2 + 2x_1x_2z_1z_2$, term by term the inner product of the two maps. At the given points $\mathbf x^\top\mathbf z = 5$, so $K = 36$, and $\phi(\mathbf x) = (1, 1.414, 2.828, 1, 4, 2.828)$, $\phi(\mathbf z) = (1, 4.243, 1.414, 9, 1, 4.243)$ give $1 + 6 + 4 + 9 + 4 + 12 = 36$. (b) $\binom{102}{2} = 5{,}151$, the constant included. (c) Decision point: kernels cost $O(n^2)$ memory and $O(n^3)$ time in rows, explicit features cost in columns. With many rows and few inputs (a million ticks, five features) build the features (or random features) and fit a linear model; with few rows and many inputs, use the kernel.

### 3.4 Course TN (2.1)

**TN1 Decision trees** (`set.ensembles.trees`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: what Gini measures and why it prefers pure children. Code drill `drl.ml.best-split` (d3, `stdlib`): `best_split_gini(x, y)` and `best_split_rss(x, y)` on one feature, thresholds at midpoints between distinct sorted values, ties broken by the lowest threshold. Hidden tests: the hook's regression data give threshold 3.5 with RSS $\frac43$; ESL's tie (parent $(400, 400)$) prefers split B by Gini; a feature with one distinct value returns `None`; duplicated $x$ values never split between equal values. Known-wrong: thresholds at data values (fails test 1's threshold); misclassification in place of Gini (fails test 2). Generators: `gen.ml.gini-node`, `gen.ml.split-gain`, `gen.ml.best-threshold`, `gen.ml.prune-count`, `gen.ml.trace-path`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Show that the Gini impurity $G = 1 - \sum_k p_k^2$ of a node is the probability that two labels drawn at random with replacement from it differ. (b) Show that for any split the weighted Gini of the children is at most the parent's, with equality only when both children have the parent's class proportions. (c) Explain with ESL's example (parent $(400, 400)$; split A $(300, 100)$, $(100, 300)$; split B $(200, 400)$, $(200, 0)$) why misclassification error is a poor splitting criterion.
  *Model answer.* (1) Two independent draws agree with probability $\sum_k p_k^2$, so they differ with probability $1 - \sum_k p_k^2 = G$. (2) With children of weights $w$ and $1 - w$ and class proportions $\mathbf q$ and $\mathbf r$, the parent has $\mathbf p = w\mathbf q + (1 - w)\mathbf r$. (3) The leap: $G(\mathbf p) = 1 - \Vert\mathbf p\Vert^2$ is a strictly concave function of $\mathbf p$, so Jensen's inequality compares the parent with the weighted children directly. (4) Concavity gives $G(w\mathbf q + (1 - w)\mathbf r)\ge wG(\mathbf q) + (1 - w)G(\mathbf r)$, with equality only when $\mathbf q = \mathbf r$. (5) In ESL's example both splits misclassify 200 of 800, so misclassification scores them equal (0.25). (6) Weighted Gini is 0.375 for A and $\frac34\times\frac49 + \frac14\times 0 = 0.333$ for B, so Gini prefers B, which creates a pure node that needs no further splitting. (7) Misclassification error is linear in $\mathbf p$ within each region where the majority class is fixed, so it is not strictly concave: many splits leave it unchanged, and it gives a greedy grower no reason to make the pure nodes that pay off one split later. Entropy, also strictly concave, behaves like Gini (0.811 against 0.689 here).
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the two-draw interpretation (line 1). 1: the parent as a mixture (line 2). 3: concavity and Jensen with the equality case (lines 3, 4). 2: both criteria on ESL's example (lines 5, 6). 2: why misclassification fails, in terms of concavity (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A tree has root $t_1$ with children $t_2$ and $t_3$; $t_2$ splits into leaves $L_1, L_2$; $t_3$ splits into $t_4$ and leaf $L_3$; $t_4$ splits into leaves $L_4, L_5$. Training errors, as fractions of all rows, if each node were a leaf: $t_1$ 0.40, $t_2$ 0.15, $t_3$ 0.20, $t_4$ 0.10; leaves $L_1$ 0.05, $L_2$ 0.04, $L_3$ 0.06, $L_4$ 0.02, $L_5$ 0.03. Find the cost-complexity pruning sequence and the range of $\alpha$ for each tree.
  *Answer.* The weakest-link value of node $t$ is $g(t) = \frac{R(t) - R(T_t)}{\lvert T_t\rvert - 1}$. First pass: $g(t_4) = \frac{0.10 - 0.05}{1} = 0.05$; $g(t_3) = \frac{0.20 - 0.11}{2} = 0.045$; $g(t_2) = \frac{0.15 - 0.09}{1} = 0.06$; $g(t_1) = \frac{0.40 - 0.20}{4} = 0.05$. The smallest is $t_3$ at 0.045: collapse its whole subtree. Second pass on the 3-leaf tree (error 0.29): $g(t_2) = 0.06$ and $g(t_1) = \frac{0.40 - 0.29}{2} = 0.055$; prune at $t_1$. Sequence: the 5-leaf tree for $\alpha < 0.045$, the 3-leaf tree for $0.045\le\alpha < 0.055$, the root alone for $\alpha\ge 0.055$; $t_2$ is never pruned on its own. Decision point: the naive rule prunes the split with the smallest local gain ($t_4$'s 0.05 looks weakest by itself), but the weakest link is measured per leaf removed, and $t_3$'s subtree buys less per leaf.

**TN2 Bagging and random forests** (`set.ensembles.forests`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the variance of an average of correlated predictions. Code drill `drl.ml.oob` (d3, `numpy`): `bootstrap_indices(n, B, seed)`, `oob_masks(indices, n)` and `oob_predict(preds, masks)` averaging, for each row, only the trees that did not draw it. Hidden tests: a row drawn by a tree is never in that tree's OOB mask; with $B = 1$ the OOB set is exactly the undrawn rows; a row drawn by every tree gets `nan`; a fixed seed reproduces stored indices. Known-wrong: averaging all trees for every row (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.ml.variance-formula`, `gen.ml.oob-probability`, `gen.ml.rank-settings`, `gen.ml.mtry`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $Z_1, \dots, Z_B$ be identically distributed with variance $\sigma^2$ and pairwise correlation $\rho\ge 0$. (a) Prove $\operatorname{Var}\left(\frac1B\sum_bZ_b\right) = \rho\sigma^2 + \frac{1 - \rho}{B}\sigma^2$. (b) Interpret the two terms for bagged trees and random forests. (c) Explain why averaging leaves bias unchanged.
  *Model answer.* (1) $\operatorname{Var}\left(\sum_bZ_b\right) = \sum_b\operatorname{Var}(Z_b) + \sum_{b\ne c}\operatorname{Cov}(Z_b, Z_c) = B\sigma^2 + B(B - 1)\rho\sigma^2$. (2) Dividing by $B^2$: $\frac{\sigma^2}{B} + \frac{B - 1}{B}\rho\sigma^2 = \rho\sigma^2 + \frac{1 - \rho}{B}\sigma^2$. (3) The second term vanishes as $B$ grows; the first does not: correlation sets a floor. (4) Bagged trees grown on bootstrap samples of the same data are highly correlated (they tend to choose the same strong splits), so the floor $\rho\sigma^2$ dominates once $B$ is moderate. (5) A random forest's feature sampling forces trees to use different splits, lowering $\rho$ at the price of a larger $\sigma^2$ per tree; the forest wins when $\rho\sigma^2$ falls (lesson 94). (6) More trees only shrink the second term, which is why test error flattens rather than rises with $B$ (lesson 95). (7) $E\left[\frac1B\sum_bZ_b\right] = E[Z_1]$, so the average has each tree's mean and hence its bias; bagging helps unstable, low-bias models such as deep trees and does little for stable ones.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: variance of the sum with the covariance count (line 1). 2: the result (line 2). 1: the floor (line 3). 2: bagging and forests interpreted (lines 4, 5). 1: more trees (line 6). 2: bias unchanged, and which models benefit (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* With bagged deep trees each tree's prediction at a point has variance 1.0 and pairwise correlation 0.6. Sampling features at each split cuts the correlation to 0.2 but raises each tree's variance to 1.3. (a) The ensemble variance with 500 trees in each case? (b) The limit as $B\to\infty$? (c) If the trees' bias is 0.3 in both cases, the expected squared error at the point with noise variance 1? (d) Which do you deploy?
  *Answer.* (a) Bagging: $0.6 + \frac{0.4}{500} = 0.6008$. Forest: $0.2\times 1.3 + \frac{0.8\times 1.3}{500} = 0.2621$. (b) 0.6 and 0.26. (c) $1 + 0.09 + 0.6008 = 1.691$ against $1 + 0.09 + 0.262 = 1.352$. (d) Decision point: the naive view rejects the forest because each of its trees is worse (variance 1.3 against 1.0). The ensemble's variance depends on correlation far more than on single-tree variance, so the forest wins by 0.34 in expected squared error; check that feature sampling did not raise the bias, which here it did not.

**TN3 Boosting** (`set.ensembles.boosting`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: XGBoost's leaf weight and split gain from a second-order expansion. Code drill `drl.ml.l2-boost` (d4, `numpy`): `boost_stumps(x, y, n_rounds, nu)` for squared loss with depth-1 trees, returning the stumps and the training-loss path. Hidden tests: the training loss never rises; with $\nu = 1$ and one round the fit equals the best single stump; predictions equal the initial mean plus $\nu$ times the sum of stump outputs; a constant target leaves zero residuals after the first stage. Known-wrong: fitting each stump to $\mathbf y$ instead of the residual (fails test 3); omitting $\nu$ from the update (fails test 3 for $\nu < 1$). Generators: `gen.ml.next-stump`, `gen.ml.residual-after`, `gen.ml.pseudo-residual`, `gen.ml.xgb-gain`, `gen.ml.adaboost-weights`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* At a boosting round with current predictions $\hat y_i$, let $g_i = \frac{\partial\ell(y_i, \hat y_i)}{\partial\hat y_i}$ and $h_i = \frac{\partial^2\ell(y_i, \hat y_i)}{\partial\hat y_i^2}$. A new tree adds weight $w_j$ to every row in leaf $j$, with penalty $\gamma T + \frac\lambda2\sum_jw_j^2$ for $T$ leaves. (a) Derive the optimal leaf weight $w_j^* = -\frac{G_j}{H_j + \lambda}$, where $G_j$ and $H_j$ sum $g_i$ and $h_i$ over the leaf. (b) Derive the gain of splitting a leaf into L and R. (c) Give $g_i$ and $h_i$ for squared and log loss.
  *Model answer.* (1) The leap: the loss of an arbitrary tree is hard to optimise, but a second-order Taylor expansion in the new tree's output turns each leaf into a one-dimensional quadratic: $\ell(y_i, \hat y_i + w)\approx\ell(y_i, \hat y_i) + g_iw + \frac12h_iw^2$. (2) Summing over leaf $j$ and adding the penalty: $G_jw_j + \frac12(H_j + \lambda)w_j^2 + \gamma$ plus constants. (3) Minimising the quadratic: $w_j^* = -\frac{G_j}{H_j + \lambda}$, with minimum value $-\frac12\frac{G_j^2}{H_j + \lambda} + \gamma$. (4) Splitting one leaf into L and R replaces one such term by two and adds one $\gamma$: $\text{gain} = \frac12\left[\frac{G_L^2}{H_L + \lambda} + \frac{G_R^2}{H_R + \lambda} - \frac{(G_L + G_R)^2}{H_L + H_R + \lambda}\right] - \gamma$. (5) For squared loss $\frac12(y - \hat y)^2$: $g = \hat y - y$, $h = 1$, so $w^* = \frac{\sum(y - \hat y)}{n_j + \lambda}$, the mean residual shrunk by $\lambda$. (6) For log loss with $\hat y$ on the log-odds scale: $g = p - y$, $h = p(1 - p)$, so the leaf takes a Newton step, not the mean pseudo-residual (lesson 97's edge). (7) $\lambda$ shrinks leaf weights and stops a leaf with tiny $H$ (nearly pure under log loss) from getting a huge weight; $\gamma$ is a fixed charge per split, so splits whose gain is below it are not made.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the expansion with its motivation (line 1). 1: the per-leaf objective (line 2). 2: the weight and its minimum value (line 3). 2: the gain (line 4). 2: squared and log loss (lines 5, 6). 1: the roles of $\lambda$ and $\gamma$ (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* AdaBoost.M1 on 10 equally weighted points: the first stump misclassifies 2. (a) Its weighted error and its vote $\alpha_1 = \ln\frac{1 - \text{err}}{\text{err}}$ (ESL's convention)? (b) The new weights after multiplying misclassified points by $e^{\alpha_1}$ and normalising? (c) The first stump's weighted error under the new weights? (d) Why does that matter, and what is the danger with noisy labels?
  *Answer.* (a) $\text{err} = 0.2$, $\alpha_1 = \ln 4 = 1.386$. (b) Misclassified weights $0.1\times 4 = 0.4$, correct ones 0.1; the total is $0.8 + 0.8 = 1.6$, so each misclassified point gets 0.25 and each correct one 0.0625. (c) $2\times 0.25 = 0.5$ exactly. (d) Decision point: the naive reading is that AdaBoost "focuses on hard points". More precisely, the reweighting makes the last classifier no better than a coin on the new weights, so the next stump must find something new. With mislabelled points the same mechanism keeps doubling their weight, because no stump can fit them, which is why exponential loss is fragile to label noise and why gradient boosting with log loss is the usual choice in practice.

**TN4 Interpreting models** (`set.ensembles.interpret`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Shapley values and their properties. Code drill `drl.ml.shapley` (d3, `stdlib`): `shapley(players, v)` by enumerating coalitions, with `v` a dictionary from frozensets to values, returning `Fraction`s when the values are exact. Hidden tests: the hook's two-player game gives $(4, 2)$; the three-player game of example B gives $\left(\frac{19}{6}, \frac{19}{6}, \frac23\right)$; the values sum to $v(N) - v(\varnothing)$; a dummy player (adds 0 to every coalition) gets 0; two symmetric players get equal values; more than 12 players raise `ValueError`. Known-wrong: marginal contribution to the empty set only (fails test 1, giving $(3, 1)$); uniform weights over coalitions instead of $\frac{\lvert S\rvert!(n - \lvert S\rvert - 1)!}{n!}$ (fails test 2). Generators: `gen.ml.rank-by-method`, `gen.ml.id-trap`, `gen.ml.twin-effect`, `gen.ml.shapley-toy`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Define the Shapley value of player $i$ in a game $v$ on $n$ players, explain its weights as an average over orderings, and prove efficiency for $n = 2$. (b) Explain how a model's prediction becomes a game, and why interventional and conditional value functions differ when features correlate.
  *Model answer.* (1) $\phi_i = \sum_{S\subseteq N\setminus\{i\}}\frac{\lvert S\rvert!\,(n - \lvert S\rvert - 1)!}{n!}\left[v(S\cup\{i\}) - v(S)\right]$. (2) The leap: rather than assigning credit in one fixed order (which favours whoever comes last or first), average $i$'s marginal contribution over all $n!$ orders in which players could join; the weight counts the orders in which exactly the players of $S$ precede $i$: $\lvert S\rvert!$ arrangements before and $(n - \lvert S\rvert - 1)!$ after. (3) For $n = 2$: $\phi_1 = \frac12[v(\{1\}) - v(\varnothing)] + \frac12[v(\{1, 2\}) - v(\{2\})]$ and $\phi_2$ likewise. (4) Adding them: $\phi_1 + \phi_2 = \frac12[v(\{1\}) + v(\{1, 2\}) - v(\{2\}) - 2v(\varnothing) + v(\{2\}) + v(\{1, 2\}) - v(\{1\})] = v(\{1, 2\}) - v(\varnothing)$. (5) For a model $f$ and a row $\mathbf x$, set $v(S)$ to the expected prediction when the features in $S$ take the row's values and the rest are averaged over a background sample; efficiency then splits $f(\mathbf x) - E f$ exactly among the features. (6) The interventional version averages the other features independently of those in $S$; the conditional version averages them given $\mathbf x_S$. (7) With correlated features the interventional version evaluates the model on rows that never occur (high debt ratio with a high credit rating), while the conditional version spreads credit to a correlated feature the model never used. Neither is wrong; they answer different questions and must be named.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the formula (line 1). 2: the weights as orderings, with the motivation (line 2). 2: efficiency for two players (lines 3, 4). 2: the prediction as a game (line 5). 2: interventional against conditional, with the correlated-feature consequence (lines 6, 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A default model's prediction for one applicant, as a game on features 1 (debt ratio), 2 (income) and 3 (postcode): $v(\varnothing) = 0$, $v(\{1\}) = 2$, $v(\{2\}) = 2$, $v(\{3\}) = 0$, $v(\{1, 2\}) = 6$, $v(\{1, 3\}) = 3$, $v(\{2, 3\}) = 3$, $v(\{1, 2, 3\}) = 7$ (in percentage points of default probability above the average). (a) Compute the three Shapley values. (b) Check efficiency. (c) A regulator asks whether postcode "matters". What do you answer?
  *Answer.* (a) Feature 3's marginal contributions are $0$ (to $\varnothing$), $1$ (to $\{1\}$), $1$ (to $\{2\}$) and $1$ (to $\{1, 2\}$), with weights $\frac13, \frac16, \frac16, \frac13$: $\phi_3 = 0 + \frac16 + \frac16 + \frac13 = \frac23$. Features 1 and 2 are symmetric, so each gets $\frac{7 - \frac23}{2} = \frac{19}{6} = 3.17$. (b) $\frac{19}{6} + \frac{19}{6} + \frac23 = 7 = v(N) - v(\varnothing)$. (c) Decision point: the naive answer "postcode adds nothing on its own ($v(\{3\}) = 0$)" is wrong; it adds a point whenever another feature is present, and its Shapley value is $\frac23$ of a point. Whether that is acceptable is a legal question about proxies for protected characteristics, and the honest answer gives the attribution, how it was computed (interventional or conditional), and that it describes the model, not the world (lesson 106).

**TN5 Neural networks: representation** (`set.ensembles.nn-representation`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: linear layers collapse; ReLU networks are piecewise linear. Code drill `drl.ml.relu-forward` (d3, `stdlib`): `forward(x, layers)` for dense layers given as weight and bias lists, and `count_pieces_1d(layers, a, b)` counting the linear pieces of a one-input, one-output network on $[a, b]$ from its breakpoints. Hidden tests: the XOR weights give outputs 0, 1, 1, 0; the tent map composed $k$ times on $[0, 1]$ has $2^k$ pieces for $k = 1$ to 4; a network with only linear activations has 1 piece; a ReLU that is never active on $[a, b]$ adds no piece. Known-wrong: ReLU applied to the output layer as well (fails a stored network whose output is negative on part of the interval). Generators: `gen.ml.param-count`, `gen.ml.output-at-x`, `gen.ml.count-pieces`, `gen.ml.hand-set-xor`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Prove that a network of $L$ layers with identity activations computes an affine function, whatever $L$. (b) Prove that a one-hidden-layer ReLU network $f(x) = \sum_{j=1}^m a_j\max(0, w_jx + b_j) + c$ on $\mathbb R$ is continuous and piecewise linear with at most $m$ kinks. (c) Write $\lvert x\rvert$ and $\max(u, v)$ as small ReLU networks.
  *Model answer.* (1) One layer computes $\mathbf W_1\mathbf x + \mathbf b_1$. (2) Composing affine maps gives an affine map: $\mathbf W_2(\mathbf W_1\mathbf x + \mathbf b_1) + \mathbf b_2 = (\mathbf W_2\mathbf W_1)\mathbf x + (\mathbf W_2\mathbf b_1 + \mathbf b_2)$; by induction $L$ layers collapse to one with $\mathbf W = \mathbf W_L\cdots\mathbf W_1$. Depth adds nothing without a non-linearity, and XOR (lesson 108) stays unsolvable. (3) Each term $a_j\max(0, w_jx + b_j)$ is continuous and linear on each side of $x_j = -\frac{b_j}{w_j}$ (when $w_j\ne 0$), with one kink there. (4) A sum of continuous functions is continuous, and on any interval containing none of the $x_j$ every term is linear, so the sum is linear there. (5) Kinks can only occur at the $m$ points $x_j$ (fewer if some coincide or cancel), so there are at most $m$ kinks and $m + 1$ pieces. (6) $\lvert x\rvert = \max(0, x) + \max(0, -x)$: two units. (7) $\max(u, v) = v + \max(0, u - v)$, one unit on the input $u - v$; with $\max(0, v) - \max(0, -v)$ for the identity $v$, it is a three-unit network.
  *Rubric (10).* 3: the collapse with induction and its consequence (lines 1, 2). 4: piecewise linearity, continuity and the kink count (lines 3 to 5). 3: the two constructions (lines 6, 7).
- *Example B (d5).* The tent map $t(x) = \min(2x, 2 - 2x)$ on $[0, 1]$ is $2\operatorname{ReLU}(x) - 4\operatorname{ReLU}(x - \frac12)$ there, two hidden units. (a) How many linear pieces does $t$ composed with itself $k$ times have? (b) A network built by stacking $k$ such layers has how many hidden units? (c) How many units would a single hidden layer need to represent the same function? (d) What does this say about depth?
  *Answer.* (a) $2^k$: each composition maps every piece onto $[0, 1]$ once up and once down, doubling the pieces (the drill confirms 2, 4, 8, 16). (b) $2k$. (c) The function has $2^k - 1$ kinks, and lesson 109's bound says a one-hidden-layer network has at most one kink per unit, so it needs at least $2^k - 1$ units. (d) Decision point: the naive reading counts units as interchangeable. With 20 units, a 10-layer network can represent 1,024 pieces that a single layer would need 1,023 units for (Telgarsky 2016). Depth buys an exponential gain in representational power for some functions; whether training finds such weights is a separate question (lesson 113).

**TN6 Neural networks: training** (`set.ensembles.nn-training`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: backpropagation through a two-layer network in matrix form (canon-gap P10's exit item). Code drill `drl.ml.two-layer-backprop` (d4, `numpy`): `forward(X, params)` for $\mathbf X\to$ dense $\to$ ReLU $\to$ dense $\to$ softmax, `loss` (mean cross-entropy), and `backward` returning every gradient. Hidden tests: on a fixed $5\times 3$ input with 4 hidden units and 3 classes every gradient matches central differences with relative error below $10^{-6}$; gradient shapes equal parameter shapes; a hidden unit that is inactive on every row has zero gradient in its incoming weights; the squared-loss variant reproduces lesson 111's gradients ($\nabla_{\mathbf W_2} = (0, -3)$ and $\nabla_{\mathbf W_1}$ with rows $(0, 0)$ and $(2, 4)$). Known-wrong: the ReLU mask omitted from the backward pass (fails tests 1 and 3); the gradient not divided by the batch size while the loss is a mean (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.ml.fill-gradient`, `gen.ml.adam-step`, `gen.ml.init-variance`, `gen.ml.ewma-weight`, `gen.ml.name-the-filter`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For one example $\mathbf x\in\mathbb R^d$, hidden layer $\mathbf z_1 = \mathbf W_1\mathbf x + \mathbf b_1$, $\mathbf h = \operatorname{ReLU}(\mathbf z_1)$, logits $\mathbf z_2 = \mathbf W_2\mathbf h + \mathbf b_2$, probabilities $\mathbf p = \operatorname{softmax}(\mathbf z_2)$ and loss $L = -\ln p_y$, derive $\nabla_{\mathbf W_2}L$, $\nabla_{\mathbf b_2}L$, $\nabla_{\mathbf W_1}L$ and $\nabla_{\mathbf b_1}L$ in matrix form, and extend them to a batch.
  *Model answer.* (1) From lesson 21, $\boldsymbol\delta_2 = \frac{\partial L}{\partial\mathbf z_2} = \mathbf p - \mathbf e_y$. (2) $\mathbf z_2 = \mathbf W_2\mathbf h + \mathbf b_2$ gives $\frac{\partial z_{2,k}}{\partial W_{2,kj}} = h_j$, so $\nabla_{\mathbf W_2}L = \boldsymbol\delta_2\mathbf h^\top$ (an outer product with the shape of $\mathbf W_2$) and $\nabla_{\mathbf b_2}L = \boldsymbol\delta_2$. (3) The leap: rather than differentiating $L$ with respect to $\mathbf W_1$ through every path at once, pass the error backwards one layer at a time; at a fork the contributions add (`mc.ml.gradient-from-one-path`). (4) $\frac{\partial L}{\partial\mathbf h} = \mathbf W_2^\top\boldsymbol\delta_2$, summing over every logit $\mathbf h$ feeds. (5) Through the ReLU, whose derivative is $\mathbf 1[\mathbf z_1 > 0]$ elementwise: $\boldsymbol\delta_1 = (\mathbf W_2^\top\boldsymbol\delta_2)\odot\mathbf 1[\mathbf z_1 > 0]$. (6) As in line 2: $\nabla_{\mathbf W_1}L = \boldsymbol\delta_1\mathbf x^\top$ and $\nabla_{\mathbf b_1}L = \boldsymbol\delta_1$. (7) For a batch with rows stacked in $\mathbf X$ ($n\times d$) and mean loss, stack the errors as rows: $\boldsymbol\Delta_2 = \frac1n(\mathbf P - \mathbf Y)$, $\nabla_{\mathbf W_2} = \boldsymbol\Delta_2^\top\mathbf H$, $\boldsymbol\Delta_1 = (\boldsymbol\Delta_2\mathbf W_2)\odot\mathbf 1[\mathbf Z_1 > 0]$, $\nabla_{\mathbf W_1} = \boldsymbol\Delta_1^\top\mathbf X$, biases the column sums. (8) Check: every gradient has its parameter's shape, and a central difference on any one entry agrees to about $10^{-6}$ relative; the backward pass costs about twice the forward pass, which is why training is feasible.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: $\boldsymbol\delta_2$ (line 1). 2: the output-layer gradients (line 2). 1: the motivation of layer-by-layer propagation and the sum at forks (line 3). 2: the hidden error through $\mathbf W_2^\top$ and the ReLU mask (lines 4, 5). 1: the first-layer gradients (line 6). 2: the batch form (line 7). 1: the shape and finite-difference check (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* A 20-layer ReLU network has $n_{\text{in}} = 256$ units per layer, zero biases and independent zero-mean weights of variance $v$. (a) Show that the second moment of a pre-activation obeys $E[z_{l+1}^2] = n_{\text{in}}\,v\,\frac12E[z_l^2]$ for symmetric pre-activations. (b) With $v = \frac{1}{256}$, by what factor is $E[z^2]$ multiplied after 20 layers? (c) With $v = \frac{2}{256}$? (d) What else could fix a badly initialised deep network?
  *Answer.* (a) $z_{l+1} = \sum_iw_ih_i$ with independent zero-mean weights, so $E[z_{l+1}^2] = n_{\text{in}}vE[h^2]$; for a symmetric $z_l$, $E[\operatorname{ReLU}(z_l)^2] = \frac12E[z_l^2]$, because the ReLU keeps exactly the positive half. (b) Each layer multiplies by $256\times\frac{1}{256}\times\frac12 = \frac12$, so after 20 layers $0.5^{20} = 9.5\times 10^{-7}$: the signal, and with it the gradient, vanishes. (c) $v = \frac{2}{n_{\text{in}}}$ (He initialisation) gives a factor of 1 per layer. (d) Decision point: the naive fix is a larger learning rate, which cannot revive a signal that has vanished. The fixes are He initialisation, normalisation layers, or residual connections, which add the input back at every block so the identity path carries the signal regardless.

### 3.5 Course FD (2.1)

**FD1 Validation through time** (`set.finml.time-validation`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: why shuffled k-fold leaks, and the purge and embargo sets defined formally. Code drill `drl.ml.purged-kfold` (d4, `stdlib`): `purged_kfold(t0, t1, k, embargo)` for labels with closed spans $[t_{0,i}, t_{1,i}]$ in time order, yielding training and test index lists for $k$ contiguous test folds.
