Hidden tests: with $t_1 = t_0 + 10$ and a test fold of starts 500 to 599, the purged training set excludes exactly 490 to 499 and 600 to 609; no training span intersects any test span in any fold; with `embargo = 10` the next 10 labels after each purge are also excluded; with $t_1 = t_0$ (no overlap) and no embargo it equals plain contiguous k-fold. Known-wrong: purging only after the fold (fails test 1); half-open spans (fails test 1 at 490 and 609). Generators: `gen.ml.purge-set`, `gen.ml.embargo-set`, `gen.ml.train-rows`, `gen.ml.cpcv-count`, `gen.ml.assemble-path`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Daily labels are forward returns over $h$ days, label $i$ spanning $[i, i + h]$. (a) Explain why shuffled k-fold overstates out-of-sample accuracy for these labels even when the model is honest. (b) Define, for a test fold of labels with starts in $[a, b]$, the set of training labels to purge, and show it is $[a - h, a - 1]\cup[b + 1, b + h]$. (c) Explain why an embargo after the fold is still needed and why none is needed before it.
  *Model answer.* (1) Labels $i$ and $i + 1$ share $h - 1$ of their $h$ daily returns, so neighbouring labels are nearly copies. (2) In shuffled folds almost every test label has neighbours in training, so a flexible model that has memorised its training rows predicts the test rows well without having learned anything that transfers to new dates: the CV score measures memory, not skill (`mc.ml.shuffled-kfold-is-fine-for-time-series`). (3) The test labels jointly span $[a, b + h]$. (4) A training label $[i, i + h]$ intersects it exactly when $i + h\ge a$ and $i\le b + h$, that is $i\in[a - h, b + h]$. (5) Removing the test fold's own starts $[a, b]$ leaves the purge set $[a - h, a - 1]\cup[b + 1, b + h]$, $h$ labels on each side; for $h = 10$ and a fold of starts 500 to 599 it is 490 to 499 and 600 to 609. (6) The leap: purging removes shared labels, but features are often built from trailing windows that include the test period's returns (a 20-day volatility on day 615 contains days 596 to 615), so training rows just after the fold still carry test information through their features. The embargo drops a further band after the fold. (7) Rows before the fold have features computed from data before the fold and labels that, once purged, end before it, so they see nothing of the test period. (8) Under a return-sharing definition ($i$ uses returns $i + 1$ to $i + h$) the boundary labels 490 and 609 share only a price, not a return, and could be kept; the closed-span rule is the default because it is never too lenient.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: why shuffled folds score memory (lines 1, 2). 3: the purge set derived from interval intersection (lines 3 to 5). 2: the embargo and its motivation (line 6). 1: why none before (line 7). 2: the numbers and the boundary convention (lines 5, 8).
- *Example B (d5).* Combinatorial purged cross-validation splits 8 years of data into $N = 8$ groups and holds out $k = 2$ at a time. (a) How many splits? (b) In how many splits is each group in the test set? (c) How many complete backtest paths can be assembled, and how? (d) What fraction of the data trains each model, before purging? (e) What does CPCV's distribution of Sharpe ratios not tell you?
  *Answer.* (a) $\binom82 = 28$. (b) $\binom71 = 7$. (c) Each path needs one out-of-sample prediction for every group; each group has 7 out-of-sample versions, so $\varphi = \frac kN\binom Nk = \frac28\times 28 = 7$ paths, built by using each split's test predictions for one path per group. (d) 6 groups of 8, 75%, less the purged and embargoed rows around each test group. (e) Decision point: the naive reading treats 7 paths as 7 independent histories. They reuse the same returns in different combinations, so their dispersion understates the uncertainty of the one history we have; the same trial-count corrections (2.3 lessons 20 to 22) still apply to whatever configuration was chosen by looking at them.

**FD2 Leakage** (`set.finml.leakage`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: a written audit of a described study. Code drill `drl.ml.leak-free-pipeline` (d4, `sklearn`): `build_pipeline()` returns a scikit-learn `Pipeline` with imputation, scaling, univariate feature selection and logistic regression, and `honest_cv(X, y, pipeline, splitter)` scores it. Hidden tests: a spy transformer records the rows each fitted step saw, and no test row of any fold appears; on a pure-noise fixture (labels independent of 1,000 features) the CV accuracy lies in $[0.40, 0.60]$ for the stored seed (the reference gives 0.49, so the margin is proved in CI); a deliberately leaky reference scored the same way exceeds 0.85. Known-wrong: selection fitted before `cross_val_score` (fails test 2); scaler fitted on all rows (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.ml.leak-catalogue`, `gen.ml.pipeline-order`, `gen.ml.survivorship-bias`, `gen.ml.audit-study`, `gen.ml.known-at`.

- *Example A (long-form, written, d4).* Audit this study: "We predict next-day returns of the current S&P 500 members from 2005 to 2024. Features are z-scored over the full sample; missing fundamentals are filled with each stock's full-sample median; we keep the 50 features most correlated with returns; we include the day's closing VWAP and the analysts' consensus revised in the following week; 5-fold shuffled cross-validation gives 61% accuracy; we report the best of 200 model configurations."
  *Model answer.* (1) Survivorship: today's members exclude firms that failed or left the index, whose returns were often poor (lesson 127). Fix: a point-in-time universe with delisting returns. (2) Full-sample z-scores use future means and SDs (lesson 37). Fix: training-window or rolling statistics inside each fold. (3) Full-sample medians for imputation leak the same way, and the missingness itself may carry information (lesson 38). Fix: impute inside folds and add indicators. (4) Selecting 50 features on all rows before CV is lesson 28's leak. Fix: select inside each fold. (5) The day's closing VWAP is not known before the close, so it cannot drive a decision at the close (lesson 124). Fix: use values known at decision time with explicit timestamps. (6) A consensus revised the following week is future information (`mc.ml.restated-data-is-what-you-knew`). Fix: point-in-time vintages. (7) Shuffled 5-fold CV on daily returns leaks through neighbouring days and any overlapping features (lesson 117). Fix: purged, embargoed, time-ordered folds. (8) Reporting the best of 200 configurations inflates the score (lesson 144). Fix: a trial ledger, nested selection, a locked hold-out and the trial count reported. (9) Even 61% accuracy says nothing about profit without payoffs and costs (lesson 81).
  *Rubric (10).* One mark each for lines 1 to 8 (the flaw named and a correct fix), 2 marks for line 9 and for ordering the flaws by likely size (the future-dated features and selection first).
- *Example B (d5).* A broker-identity feature has 40 levels. For one broker the training labels are 1, 1, 0, 0, 1. (a) Its plain target encoding? (b) Its leave-one-out encodings for the 1-rows and the 0-rows? (c) What can a tree do with (b)? (d) What encoding is safe?
  *Answer.* (a) $\frac35 = 0.6$ for every row: each row's own label is inside its encoding. (b) A 1-row gets $\frac{3 - 1}{4} = 0.5$; a 0-row gets $\frac34 = 0.75$. (c) Within this broker, encoding 0.5 means label 1 and 0.75 means label 0, a perfect inverse code; a tree splitting on the encoding (with the broker's identity or count as a second feature) reads the training labels exactly and its training and in-fold scores soar. (d) Decision point: leave-one-out looks like the honest fix and is a leak in reverse. Safe encodings are computed out of fold (each row encoded from other folds' rows only, inside the CV loop) and shrunk towards the global mean for rare levels.

**FD3 Labels and sample weights** (`set.finml.labels`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: average uniqueness and the effective sample size of overlapping labels. Code drill `drl.ml.triple-barrier` (d4, `stdlib`): `first_touch(closes, i0, upper, lower, horizon)` returning `(t, label)` with label $+1$ for the upper barrier, $-1$ for the lower and the sign of the return (0 if exactly 0) at the vertical barrier, using closes only; `uniqueness(spans, T)`. Hidden tests: a path rising through the upper barrier on step 3 returns $(3, +1)$; a path through the lower barrier first returns $-1$ even if it later rises above the upper; a flat path returns the vertical barrier with label 0; the hook's spans give uniqueness $\left(\frac23, \frac12, \frac34\right)$ as `Fraction`s. Known-wrong: comparing only the final close with the barriers (fails test 2); using the vertical barrier's return without the earlier touches (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.ml.first-touch`, `gen.ml.label-value`, `gen.ml.uniqueness`, `gen.ml.bootstrap-prob`, `gen.ml.meta-precision`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Labels $i = 1, \dots, n$ span sets of days $T_i$, and $c_t$ is the number of labels covering day $t$. (a) Define the average uniqueness $\bar u_i = \frac{1}{\lvert T_i\rvert}\sum_{t\in T_i}\frac{1}{c_t}$ and compute it for A (days 1 to 3), B (2 to 4) and C (4 and 5). (b) Show that when every label spans $h$ days and labels start every day, $\bar u\approx\frac1h$, and explain why $\sum_i\bar u_i$ is a rough effective number of independent labels. (c) Say how López de Prado uses $\bar u$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Concurrency: day 1 has 1 label, days 2 to 4 have 2, day 5 has 1. (2) A: $\frac13\left(1 + \frac12 + \frac12\right) = \frac23$; B: $\frac13\left(\frac12 + \frac12 + \frac12\right) = \frac12$; C: $\frac12\left(\frac12 + 1\right) = \frac34$. (3) With $h$-day labels starting daily, away from the sample's ends every day is covered by $h$ labels, so $c_t = h$ and $\bar u_i = \frac1h$. (4) The leap: a label that shares each of its days with $c_t - 1$ others carries only a $\frac{1}{c_t}$ share of each day's new information; summing those shares over its days and averaging gives the fraction of the label that is its own. (5) So $\sum_i\bar u_i$ adds up the labels' own information, about $\frac nh$ for daily $h$-day labels: 2,500 daily 20-day labels are worth about 125 independent ones, which matches the count of non-overlapping 20-day windows. (6) López de Prado uses $\bar u$ to weight samples in training (unique labels count more), to set the bootstrap's sample size in bagging (draw about $\sum\bar u_i$ rows, not $n$), and as the basis of the sequential bootstrap, which favours draws that keep uniqueness high (lesson 133). (7) It also warns that standard errors computed as if $n$ labels were independent are too small by about $\sqrt h$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: concurrency (line 1). 2: the three uniquenesses (line 2). 2: the $\frac1h$ case (line 3). 2: the meaning of uniqueness and the effective count with its motivation (lines 4, 5). 2: the uses (line 6). 1: the standard-error consequence (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A mean-reversion rule produces 1,000 long signals labelled by a triple barrier at $\pm 2\%$: 550 hit the upper barrier first and 450 the lower. A meta-model keeps 400 signals, of which 260 hit the upper barrier. Costs are 0.1% per trade. (a) Precision, recall and $F_1$ of the meta-model with respect to the rule's winners. (b) Gross and net P&L, in percent of one position, before and after. (c) Which do you run?
  *Answer.* (a) Precision $\frac{260}{400} = 0.65$ (up from 0.55); recall $\frac{260}{550} = 0.473$; $F_1 = 0.547$. (b) Before: $550\times 2 - 450\times 2 = 200\%$ gross, $200 - 100 = 100\%$ net. After: $260\times 2 - 140\times 2 = 240\%$ gross, $240 - 40 = 200\%$ net. (c) Decision point: the naive reading says the meta-model "lost" 290 winners and must be worse. Each kept trade earns 0.6% gross against 0.2%, and fewer trades pay fewer costs, so net P&L doubles. Check that the meta-model's selection was validated with purged folds and that the gain holds on the hold-out, since precision gains are easy to overfit.

**FD4 Features from market data** (`set.finml.features`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the fractional-difference weights from the binomial series. Code drill `drl.ml.fracdiff` (d4, `numpy`): `fracdiff_weights(d, threshold)` keeping weights while $\lvert w_k\rvert\ge$ threshold, and `fracdiff(series, d, threshold)` with a fixed window. Hidden tests: $d = 0$ returns the series; $d = 1$ returns first differences; $d = 0.5$ gives $(1, -0.5, -0.125, -0.0625, -0.0390625)$ as the first five weights; at $d = 0.4$ with threshold $10^{-4}$ the window has 282 weights; the first window-minus-one outputs are `nan`. Known-wrong: the recursion $w_k = -w_{k-1}\frac{d - k}{k}$ (fails test 3); an expanding window (fails test 5). Generators: `gen.ml.build-bars`, `gen.ml.count-bars`, `gen.ml.weights`, `gen.ml.min-d`, `gen.ml.cusum-events`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Using the backshift operator $B$ ($BX_t = X_{t-1}$), expand $(1 - B)^d$ for real $d$ as a binomial series and derive the weights' recursion $w_0 = 1$, $w_k = -w_{k-1}\frac{d - k + 1}{k}$. (b) Show that $d = 1$ gives first differences and $d = 0$ the series itself. (c) Explain what $0 < d < 1$ buys, and how to choose $d$.
  *Model answer.* (1) The generalised binomial series gives $(1 - B)^d = \sum_{k\ge 0}\binom dk(-B)^k$ with $\binom dk = \frac{d(d - 1)\cdots(d - k + 1)}{k!}$. (2) So $\tilde X_t = \sum_k w_kX_{t-k}$ with $w_k = (-1)^k\binom dk$. (3) The ratio of consecutive weights is $\frac{w_k}{w_{k-1}} = -\frac{d - k + 1}{k}$, which gives the recursion. (4) For $d = 1$: $w_1 = -1$, and $w_2 = -w_1\frac{1 - 2 + 1}{2} = 0$, so every later weight is 0 and $\tilde X_t = X_t - X_{t-1}$. For $d = 0$: $w_1 = 0$ and all later weights vanish. (5) The leap: stationarity and memory look like a binary choice (prices keep memory and wander; returns are stationary and forget), but $d$ is a dial; for $0 < d < 1$ the weights decay slowly, so $\tilde X$ keeps a long memory of past levels while removing enough of the trend to be stationary. (6) Choose the smallest $d$ for which an ADF test (2.2 lesson 33) rejects a unit root on the training window, and check the correlation of $\tilde X$ with the original series, which shows how much memory survived (lesson 135; López de Prado reports $d$ well below 1 for many futures [recalled, unverified]). (7) Because the weights decay slowly, a fixed window with a weight threshold is used; at $d = 0.4$ and a threshold of $10^{-4}$ it needs 282 past observations, a warm-up that must not borrow from the test period.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the binomial series (line 1). 1: the weights (line 2). 2: the recursion (line 3). 2: the two special cases (line 4). 2: what fractional $d$ buys, with the motivation (line 5). 1: choosing $d$ and the window (lines 6, 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Ten trades (price in dollars, size in shares): (100.0, 200), (100.5, 150), (101.0, 300), (100.8, 100), (100.2, 250), (99.8, 400), (100.1, 120), (100.6, 180), (101.2, 260), (101.0, 90). (a) Build dollar bars of at least 50,000 dollars, closing a bar on the trade that reaches the threshold, and give each bar's open, high, low, close and volume. (b) How many tick bars of 4 trades? (c) Why might the dollar bars' returns be closer to normal than time bars' over a busy day?
  *Answer.* (a) Dollar values: 20,000, 15,075, 30,300, 10,080, 25,050, 39,920, 12,012, 18,108, 26,312, 9,090. Bar 1 closes on trade 3 (65,375): open 100.0, high 101.0, low 100.0, close 101.0, volume 650. Bar 2 closes on trade 6 (75,050): 100.8, 100.8, 99.8, 99.8, volume 750. Bar 3 closes on trade 9 (56,432): 100.1, 101.2, 100.1, 101.2, volume 560. Trade 10 (9,090) waits for the next bar. (b) Two complete bars, with trades 9 and 10 left over. (c) Decision point: the naive choice samples every minute. Clock time samples busy and quiet periods alike, so each time bar holds a different amount of trading and its returns mix small and large variances, which fattens the tails; dollar bars sample by traded value, so each bar carries a similar amount of information, and their returns are closer to identically distributed (López de Prado 2.3 [recalled, unverified as to his test results]).

**FD5 Unsupervised learning: structure in returns** (`set.finml.unsupervised`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: why Lloyd's algorithm never increases the k-means objective, and what that does not guarantee. Code drill `drl.ml.kmeans` (d3, `stdlib`): `lloyd(points, centroids, max_iter=100)` returning assignments, centroids and the objective after each iteration; an empty cluster keeps its previous centroid. Hidden tests: the hook's five points and two centroids give assignments $(0, 0, 1, 1, 0)$ and centroids $\left(\frac43, \frac43\right)$ and $(4.5, 4)$ after one step; the objective path never rises; an empty cluster keeps its centroid; ties in distance go to the lower-indexed centroid. Known-wrong: updating centroids before reassigning all points (fails test 2 on a stored case); dropping empty clusters (fails test 3). Generators: `gen.ml.assign-step`, `gen.ml.update-step`, `gen.ml.merge-order`, `gen.ml.corr-distance`, `gen.ml.responsibility`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Show that each step of Lloyd's algorithm (assign each point to its nearest centroid; move each centroid to its cluster's mean) cannot increase $J = \sum_i\Vert\mathbf x_i - \boldsymbol\mu_{c(i)}\Vert^2$. (b) Conclude that it stops after finitely many steps. (c) Explain why this does not make k-means find the best clustering, and two remedies.
  *Model answer.* (1) With centroids fixed, $J$ is a sum of separate terms, one per point; assigning each point to its nearest centroid minimises each term, so $J$ cannot rise. (2) With assignments fixed, $J$ splits into one sum per cluster, $\sum_{i\in C}\Vert\mathbf x_i - \boldsymbol\mu\Vert^2$, minimised by the cluster mean (1.6 lesson 3's movable centre, in several dimensions), so $J$ cannot rise. (3) The leap: an algorithm that alternates between two exact minimisations of the same objective is coordinate descent on that objective, so monotonicity is automatic. (4) There are finitely many assignments of $n$ points to $k$ clusters ($k^n$ at most), and with a fixed tie rule the algorithm cannot revisit an assignment once $J$ has strictly fallen, so it stops in finitely many steps. (5) It stops at a point where neither step helps, a local minimum of a non-convex problem: different starts give different answers (`mc.ml.kmeans-finds-the-true-clusters`). (6) Remedies: many random starts keeping the lowest $J$, and k-means++ seeding, which spreads the initial centroids and comes with an approximation guarantee [recalled]. (7) Even the global minimum depends on $k$ and on feature scaling, which are choices, not facts in the data (`mc.ml.elbow-is-objective`).
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the assignment step (line 1). 2: the update step (line 2). 1: the coordinate-descent view (line 3). 2: finite termination (line 4). 2: local minima and remedies (lines 5, 6). 1: the dependence on $k$ and scaling (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Four assets have correlations $\rho_{12} = 0.8$, $\rho_{13} = 0.3$, $\rho_{14} = 0.2$, $\rho_{23} = 0.4$, $\rho_{24} = 0.1$, $\rho_{34} = 0.7$. (a) Convert them to distances $d = \sqrt{\frac12(1 - \rho)}$. (b) Cluster by single linkage and by complete linkage, giving each merge's height. (c) Which linkage would you use for risk grouping, and why?
  *Answer.* (a) $d_{12} = 0.316$, $d_{13} = 0.592$, $d_{14} = 0.632$, $d_{23} = 0.548$, $d_{24} = 0.671$, $d_{34} = 0.387$. (b) Both merge $\{1, 2\}$ at 0.316 and $\{3, 4\}$ at 0.387. The final merge is at the closest cross pair under single linkage, $d_{23} = 0.548$, and at the farthest under complete linkage, $d_{24} = 0.671$ (average linkage gives 0.611). (c) Decision point: single linkage is the naive default and the usual one in López de Prado's HRP; it chains through one close pair, so a group can contain assets that are far apart. For risk grouping, where a cluster should hold only assets that move together, complete or average linkage is the safer choice; with noisy short-sample correlations, check the clusters' stability by resampling.

**FD6 Overfitting by search** (`set.finml.overfitting`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: why repeated hold-out use turns the hold-out into training data, and a protocol that prevents it. Code drill `drl.ml.expected-max` (d3, `stdlib`): `expected_max_normal(N)` by numerical integration of $E[\max] = \int z\,N\varphi(z)\Phi(z)^{N-1}\,dz$ with Simpson's rule on $[-10, 10]$, using `math.erf` for $\Phi$. Hidden tests: $N = 1$ gives 0 to $10^{-9}$; $N = 50$ gives 2.2491; $N = 100$ gives 2.5076; $N = 1{,}000$ gives 3.2414; the result increases with $N$. Known-wrong: using $\Phi^{-1}(1 - \frac1N)$ alone (fails test 2: 2.054). Generators: `gen.ml.emax`, `gen.ml.predict-oos`, `gen.ml.leaderboard`, `gen.ml.trust-region`.

- *Example A (long-form, written, d4).* A team tunes a return-prediction model with a fixed final hold-out year. After each of 15 rounds of changes they score the hold-out, keep the change if the hold-out improves, and finally report the hold-out score. (a) Explain why the reported score is biased, with a quantitative argument. (b) Describe a protocol that keeps a hold-out honest. (c) Describe the reusable hold-out idea and when it is worth the trouble.
  *Model answer.* (1) Each look selects among options using the hold-out's own noise: a change is kept when it improves the hold-out score, which happens for lucky changes as well as good ones. (2) After 15 adaptive looks the hold-out has been used like a validation set, and the final score is the best of many noisy evaluations: with standard error $s$ per evaluation and roughly independent changes, the selection inflates it by up to about $s\times E[\max_{15}Z] = 1.74s$ (lesson 144 and the drill; the value for 15 is 1.736). (3) The leap: the bias comes from adaptivity, not from the number of rows, so a bigger hold-out reduces it only by shrinking $s$. (4) Protocol: fix the hypothesis, target, universe, metric and decision rule first; tune only on training and validation folds (purged, lesson 120); log every trial; open the hold-out once, after the final model is frozen; report the trial count and the single hold-out score (lesson 147; 2.3 lesson 29). (5) If a second look is unavoidable, record it as a new trial and treat the old hold-out as validation data from then on (`mc.ml.holdout-peeking-is-harmless`). (6) The reusable hold-out (Dwork and others 2015) answers each query with noise added, and only reports the hold-out value when it differs from the training value by more than a threshold, so the hold-out's information leaks slowly and it survives many adaptive queries. (7) It is worth it when many adaptive decisions must be checked against fresh data and new data cannot be bought, as in finance; with plentiful data a fresh hold-out per decision is simpler.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the mechanism of selection on noise (line 1). 2: the quantitative inflation (line 2). 1: adaptivity, not size (line 3). 2: the protocol (line 4). 1: handling a second look (line 5). 2: the reusable hold-out and when to use it (lines 6, 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A public leaderboard scores submissions on 500 hidden labels (balanced classes); the private leaderboard uses different hidden labels. A competitor submits 100 sets of random predictions and keeps the best public score. (a) The expected best public accuracy? (b) Its expected private accuracy? (c) A rival's public score is 0.553 from one submission. Who do you believe? (d) What would make a leaderboard resistant to this?
  *Answer.* (a) Each random submission's accuracy has mean 0.5 and SD $\sqrt{0.25/500} = 0.0224$; the best of 100 is about $0.5 + 2.508\times 0.0224 = 0.556$. (b) 0.5: the predictions are random. (c) Decision point: the naive ranking puts the 0.556 first. The single submission at 0.553 is 2.4 standard errors above chance; the best of 100 random tries is 2.5, so only the rival's number is evidence, and weak evidence at that. (d) Limit submissions, report scores rounded or only when they improve by a margin (Blum and Hardt's Ladder), and judge by the private set, as Kaggle does.

**FD7 Capstone and exit** (`set.finml.capstone`). The capstone's milestone pack, not an ordinary set: 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5): the framing sheet (d3), the feature timeline with known-at times (d3), the purge and embargo plan for the chosen labels (d4), the `ml-workbench` audit with every predicate green (d4), the model comparison table with paired fold differences (d4), the trial ledger (d3), the results note (long-form, d5), and the exit code drill. Code drill `drl.ml.exit-pipeline` (d5, `sklearn`): the learner's exported script runs on a hidden variant of the capstone data set (`dataset-gen` tabular set, seeded per learner, with two planted leaks the audit should have removed). Hidden tests: the script uses a purged, embargoed time-ordered splitter (the spy records fold boundaries); no fitted step sees a test row; the planted leaky columns are not used (their importance in the final model is zero, or they are absent); the reported out-of-sample score is within the stored reference's margin (rule (c) of decision 3); the script writes the metrics file the results note cites. Known-wrong: the reference with shuffled `KFold` (fails test 1); with one planted leak left in (fails test 3, and its score fails test 4 by being too high). Generators: none (`ml-workbench` seeds vary each learner's data).

- *Example A (long-form, results note, d5).* Write the capstone's one-page results note for your own problem. *Model answer outline* (the model answer is a filled note for the reference capstone, "next-month volatility regime of a 200-stock universe", shipped with the item): (1) the question and the decision it serves; (2) data, universe and dates, with the point-in-time and survivorship treatment; (3) the target and labels, with horizon, barriers and uniqueness weights; (4) features with known-at times; (5) validation: purged, embargoed walk-forward folds and the locked hold-out, opened once; (6) models compared, with paired fold differences and standard errors; (7) results: hold-out score with an interval, against a stated benchmark (the reference note's numbers are filled by CI from the reference capstone's own run, so none is quoted here); (8) what failed, by name (for the reference: the network, and dollar bars, which did not help at a monthly horizon); (9) limitations and the trial count (the reference: 34 trials).
  *Rubric (10).* 1 each: question and decision; data and biases; labels; known-at times; validation scheme; honest comparison; result with interval and benchmark; failures named; limitations; trial count. The `results-note` widget's `sections-complete` and `metrics-consistent` predicates must pass before marking (`ASSESSMENT.md` 11.4).
- *Example B (d5).* Audit a peer's capstone summary: "Target: sign of next-day return. Features: 30, including the 20-day forward volatility and a sector dummy. Folds: walk-forward, no purge, because labels are one day. Best of 12 models: XGBoost, hold-out accuracy 0.57. The hold-out was used twice: once to choose between XGBoost and a forest." (a) List the problems. (b) What accuracy would you expect after fixing them, and what would convince you?
  *Answer.* (a) The 20-day forward volatility is a future-dated feature, a leak (lesson 124). One-day labels need no purge, but any feature built over trailing windows still needs an embargo after each test fold (lesson 121). Two looks at the hold-out make it validation data; the model choice should have been made on folds (lesson 146). The best of 12 models is inflated by about $\text{SE}\times 1.63$ (lesson 144; $E[\max_{12}Z] = 1.63$). (b) Decision point: the naive response "0.57 is good for daily returns" accepts a number built on a leak. Remove the forward volatility and expect accuracy near 0.50; what would convince is a single pre-registered hold-out score clearly above 0.5 (with its standard error, about 0.016 on 1,000 days), a costed P&L, and the trial count.

### 3.6 Course MP (3.1)

**MP1 How to read an empirical paper** (`set.momentum.reading`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: a structured summary of a paper from its abstract, method section and main table. The paper is our own fictional one, "Post-earnings drift in mid-cap stocks", written for the item so that no publisher's text is reproduced. Code drill `drl.papers.table-reader` (d3, `stdlib`): `read_cell(text, paren="t")` parses cells like `"0.42 (2.10)"` or `"-0.42*** (0.12)"` and returns coefficient, standard error, $t$ and the two-sided normal $p$-value; `paren="se"` reads the parenthesis as a standard error. Hidden tests: `"0.42 (2.10)"` gives SE 0.20 and $p = 0.0357$; `"0.42 (0.20)"` with `paren="se"` gives $t = 2.1$; a negative coefficient keeps its sign; stars are stripped and checked against the $p$-value (a mismatch raises `ValueError`); malformed text raises. Known-wrong: always reading parentheses as standard errors (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.papers.t-from-coef`, `gen.papers.map-questions`, `gen.papers.read-table`.

- *Example A (long-form, written, d4).* In at most 200 words, summarise the fictional paper: its claim, sample, method, identifying comparison, main number with its uncertainty, and the two threats you would test first.
  *Model answer.* (1) Claim: mid-cap stocks with the most positive earnings surprises outperform those with the most negative over the next 60 trading days. (2) Sample: the paper's 1,200 mid-caps, 2000 to 2019, quarterly announcements. (3) Method: sort announcements into surprise deciles each quarter; hold the top-minus-bottom portfolio for 60 days; report the mean return and a $t$-statistic. (4) Identification: the comparison is between high- and low-surprise firms announced in the same quarter, so market-wide moves cancel. (5) Main number: 2.1% over 60 days with $t = 3.4$, in parentheses as the table note states. (6) Threat one: the 60-day holding returns overlap across quarters for firms announcing near each other, and the table does not say whether its $t$ allows for that (lesson 16). (7) Threat two: the surprise uses analysts' consensus, which may be revised after the fact in the database (point-in-time data, lesson 7). (8) Further: costs, the universe's survivorship, and whether the effect holds after the sample.
  *Rubric (10).* 1 each: claim, sample, method, identification, number with uncertainty and its source in the table; 2 each for the two threats chosen well and tied to a check; 1 for staying within 200 words. A summary that copies the abstract's adjectives without the number earns at most 6.
- *Example B (d5).* A table reports a momentum strategy's mean monthly return as "0.42 (2.10)", and the note says "$t$-statistics in parentheses; returns are overlapping three-month holding-period returns sampled monthly". (a) The implied standard error and $p$-value as printed. (b) Correct the $t$-statistic for the overlap, assuming the monthly returns underneath are independent. (c) What do you conclude, and what would you ask the authors?
  *Answer.* (a) $\text{SE} = \frac{0.42}{2.10} = 0.20$, two-sided $p = 0.036$. (b) Overlapping three-month sums of independent monthly returns have autocorrelations $\frac23$ and $\frac13$ at lags 1 and 2, so the variance of their mean is $1 + 2\left(\frac23 + \frac13\right) = 3$ times the naive one; the corrected $t$ is $\frac{2.10}{\sqrt3} = 1.21$, $p = 0.23$. (c) Decision point: the naive reading stops at "significant at 5%". After the overlap correction the result is not significant; ask whether the $t$ used Hansen-Hodrick or Newey-West errors and at how many lags, or for JT-style cohort averaging, which avoids the overlap.

**MP2 The replication method** (`set.momentum.method`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: a replication ledger. Code drill `drl.papers.survival-ratio` (d3, `stdlib`): `survival(in_mean, in_se, out_mean, out_se, method="delta")` returns the ratio and a 95% interval by the delta method or by Fieller's method (`method="fieller"`), raising `ValueError` when the in-sample mean's $t$ is below 2 for `delta`. Hidden tests: lesson 10's numbers give $(0.40, \text{SE } 0.316)$; example B's give delta $(-0.19, 1.02)$ and Fieller $(-0.16, 1.26)$; an in-sample $t$ of 1.5 raises for `delta`; independence is assumed and stated in the docstring. Known-wrong: the variance of a ratio as the ratio of variances (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.papers.survival-ratio`, `gen.papers.unstated-choices`, `gen.papers.rehearsal-truth`.

- *Example A (long-form, written, d4).* A paper says: "We sort NYSE and AMEX stocks each month into deciles by their return over the past six months and hold equal-weighted decile portfolios for six months." Write the replication ledger: at least eight choices the sentence leaves open, the options for each, and the direction you expect each to push the winners-minus-losers return.
  *Model answer.* (1) Share types: ordinary common shares only, or ADRs, REITs and funds too; including non-common shares adds noise, direction unclear. (2) Price filter: exclude stocks below 5 dollars or not; excluding penny stocks usually lowers WML, which is strongest among small, illiquid names. (3) Skip: none, a week or a month between formation and holding; skipping removes short-term reversal and raises WML (lesson 12). (4) Formation return: compounded or summed, with or without dividends; small differences. (5) Minimum history: require a full six months of returns or not; requiring it drops new listings, direction small. (6) Delisting returns: included or dropped; dropping them biases loser returns upwards (failures are missing), lowering WML. (7) Weighting within deciles: equal (as stated) against value; value weights lower WML. (8) Decile breakpoints: from all stocks or NYSE only; NYSE breakpoints move small stocks into extreme deciles less often. (9) Overlap: one cohort per month averaged over $K$ cohorts (JT) or a single cohort; this changes the standard errors more than the mean (lesson 16). (10) Sample dates and data vendor: the exact first formation month and CRSP version.
  *Rubric (10).* 1 per choice for any eight of lines 1 to 10 with options stated; 2 for correct directions on at least four, including the skip and delisting returns. The ledger must mark which choices the paper states and which it leaves open.
- *Example B (d5).* A replication finds a mean WML of 1.2% a month in the paper's sample (SE 0.3%) and 0.5% after it (SE 0.35%); treat the two as independent. (a) The survival ratio, its delta-method standard error and 95% interval. (b) Fieller's 95% interval, which solves $(b - ra)^2 = z^2(s_b^2 + r^2s_a^2)$ for $r$. (c) What does the note say?
  *Answer.* (a) $r = \frac{0.5}{1.2} = 0.417$; $\text{SE}\approx\sqrt{\frac{0.35^2}{1.2^2} + \frac{0.5^2\times 0.3^2}{1.2^4}} = 0.310$; interval $(-0.19, 1.02)$. (b) The quadratic gives $(-0.16, 1.26)$, asymmetric because the ratio's uncertainty grows faster for small denominators. (c) Decision point: the naive note says "42% survived". The honest note says the point estimate is 42%, the data cannot rule out full survival or none (the interval spans 0 and 1), and states which interval was used and why.

**MP3 Momentum: the strategy** (`set.momentum.strategy`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: cohort averaging and the overlap problem. Code drill `drl.papers.momentum-sort` (d4, `pandas`): `momentum_returns(returns, J, skip, K, n_groups=10)` on a monthly returns frame (dates by stocks) returning the monthly WML series built from $K$ overlapping cohorts. Hidden tests: on a 6-stock, 24-month fixture with hand-computed values the WML series matches to $10^{-12}$; shuffling the returns after each formation date leaves every formation's ranks unchanged (no look-ahead); the first $K - 1$ months average fewer cohorts and say so in a `cohorts` column; stocks with missing formation returns are excluded from that cohort. Known-wrong: ranking on returns that include the holding month (fails test 2); averaging cohorts with equal weight before all $K$ exist without recording it (fails test 3). Generators: `gen.papers.wml-from-table`, `gen.papers.cohort-average`, `gen.papers.history-needed`, `gen.papers.top-decile`, `gen.papers.nw-se`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Explain how Jegadeesh and Titman turn a strategy with $K$-month holding periods into a series of non-overlapping monthly returns. (b) Show that if instead one samples each cohort's $K$-month return every month, the mean has a variance about $K$ times larger than the naive formula says, and what that does to the $t$-statistic. (c) Say which approach a replication should use and why.
  *Model answer.* (1) Each month a new cohort is formed and held for $K$ months, so at any time $K$ cohorts are alive. (2) The strategy's return in month $t$ is the equal-weighted average of the $K$ live cohorts' returns in month $t$: one monthly return per month, each computed from that month's returns only. (3) Successive monthly returns share no return periods, so the series has little autocorrelation by construction and ordinary standard errors are close to right. (4) If instead each cohort's $K$-month return is recorded every month, consecutive observations share $K - 1$ of their $K$ months; with independent monthly returns the autocorrelation at lag $j < K$ is $\frac{K - j}{K}$. (5) The variance of the mean of such a series is, for large $T$, about $\frac{\sigma^2}{T}\left(1 + 2\sum_{j=1}^{K-1}\frac{K - j}{K}\right) = \frac{\sigma^2}{T}K$. (6) So the naive $t$ is too large by about $\sqrt K$; Hansen-Hodrick errors with $K - 1$ lags fix it, and Newey-West's Bartlett weights at $K - 1$ lags only partly (homework example B). (7) The leap: the problem is not overlapping holdings (cohorts overlap in both methods) but overlapping return periods in the measured series; averaging across cohorts each month removes the second while keeping the first. (8) A replication should use the cohort method, as the paper does, so its standard errors are comparable; if it reports overlapping holding-period returns, it must use Hansen-Hodrick or state the lags.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the cohort construction (lines 1, 2). 1: why the series has little autocorrelation (line 3). 2: the overlap's autocorrelations (line 4). 2: the variance factor $K$ (line 5). 1: the correction (line 6). 1: the distinction between overlapping holdings and overlapping returns (line 7). 1: the recommendation (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* A deck reports a 12-month momentum strategy's $t = 5.0$, computed with plain standard errors from 12-month returns sampled monthly. Monthly returns underneath are independent. (a) The $t$ corrected by Hansen-Hodrick's uniform weights at 11 lags. (b) The $t$ corrected by Newey-West's Bartlett weights at 11 lags, $w_j = 1 - \frac{j}{12}$. (c) Why do they differ, and which is right here?
  *Answer.* (a) The true variance factor is $K = 12$, so $t = \frac{5}{\sqrt{12}} = 1.44$. (b) Bartlett's estimate of the factor is $1 + 2\sum_{j=1}^{11}\left(1 - \frac j{12}\right)\frac{12 - j}{12} = 1 + \frac{2\times 506}{144} = 8.03$, so $t = \frac{5}{\sqrt{8.03}} = 1.76$. (c) Decision point: Newey-West is the default most people reach for, and here it under-corrects, because its declining weights shrink the large, known autocorrelations of an overlap. When the overlap is known exactly, uniform weights up to $K - 1$ lags (Hansen-Hodrick) match it; Newey-West suits autocorrelation of unknown form. Either way the "$t = 5$" was not significant evidence of anything.

**MP4 What explains momentum** (`set.momentum.explanations`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: JT's profit decomposition. Code drill `drl.papers.event-time` (d4, `pandas`): `event_time_returns(returns, J, horizon=60)` returns the average cumulative return of winners minus losers for each month after formation. Hidden tests: on a fixture with a planted 12-month continuation followed by a reversal the curve rises then falls at the planted months; month 0 is excluded (formation); cohorts are aligned by formation date, not calendar date. Known-wrong: aligning by calendar month (fails test 3). Generators: `gen.papers.beta-crash`, `gen.papers.decomposition-terms`, `gen.papers.alpha-from-means`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Returns follow $r_{it} = \mu_i + b_if_t + e_{it}$, with $E[f_t] = 0$, idiosyncratic terms uncorrelated across stocks and with $f$, and $N$ stocks. The strategy holds weights $w_{it} = \frac1N(r_{i,t-1} - \bar r_{t-1})$. Derive $E[\pi_t] = \sigma_\mu^2 + \sigma_b^2\operatorname{Cov}(f_t, f_{t-1}) + \frac{N - 1}{N^2}\sum_i\operatorname{Cov}(e_{it}, e_{i,t-1})$, where $\sigma_\mu^2$ and $\sigma_b^2$ are cross-sectional variances, and interpret each term.
  *Model answer.* (1) $\pi_t = \sum_iw_{it}r_{it} = \frac1N\sum_ir_{i,t-1}r_{it} - \bar r_{t-1}\bar r_t$, because $\frac1N\sum_i\bar r_{t-1}r_{it} = \bar r_{t-1}\bar r_t$. (2) Under the model, $E[r_{i,t-1}r_{it}] = \mu_i^2 + b_i^2\operatorname{Cov}(f_t, f_{t-1}) + \operatorname{Cov}(e_{it}, e_{i,t-1})$; the cross terms vanish because $E f = 0$ and $e$ is uncorrelated with $f$. (3) $\bar r_t = \bar\mu + \bar bf_t + \bar e_t$, so $E[\bar r_{t-1}\bar r_t] = \bar\mu^2 + \bar b^2\operatorname{Cov}(f_t, f_{t-1}) + \frac{1}{N^2}\sum_i\operatorname{Cov}(e_{it}, e_{i,t-1})$, using no cross-stock correlation of $e$. (4) Subtract: $E\pi_t = \left(\frac1N\sum_i\mu_i^2 - \bar\mu^2\right) + \left(\frac1N\sum_ib_i^2 - \bar b^2\right)\operatorname{Cov}(f_t, f_{t-1}) + \left(\frac1N - \frac1{N^2}\right)\sum_i\operatorname{Cov}(e_{it}, e_{i,t-1})$. (5) The brackets are the cross-sectional variances $\sigma_\mu^2$ and $\sigma_b^2$, which gives the result. (6) The leap: the strategy's weights are last period's relative returns, so its expected profit is a cross-sectional covariance between consecutive returns, and the factor model splits that covariance into three sources. (7) $\sigma_\mu^2\ge 0$ earns even with no serial correlation at all: stocks with high expected returns tend to be past winners. (8) The factor term earns only if the factor itself trends and loadings differ; the idiosyncratic term earns if firm-specific news is underreacted to. JT argue the evidence points to the last [recalled, unverified], which is the behavioural reading lesson 19 tests.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the profit rewritten (line 1). 2: the expectations of the products (lines 2, 3). 2: the subtraction and the variances (lines 4, 5). 1: the motivation (line 6). 3: the three interpretations (lines 7, 8).
- *Example B (d5).* After a two-year bear market the loser decile's beta is 1.8 and the winner decile's 0.7. WML's alpha is 1% a month. (a) WML's return in a month when the market rebounds 15%. (b) A trader hedges WML with 1.1 units of the market. What does the hedged position earn that month? (c) What goes wrong with the hedge in practice?
  *Answer.* (a) WML's beta is $0.7 - 1.8 = -1.1$, so its return is about $1\% - 1.1\times 15\% = -15.5\%$. (b) The hedge earns $+1.1\times 15\% = 16.5\%$, so the hedged position earns the alpha, about 1%. (c) Decision point: the naive hedge uses a beta estimated over the past few years, when WML's beta was near zero; the dangerous beta is the current one, which swings after bear markets (Daniel and Moskowitz 2016). Estimate it from recent data or from the deciles' current betas, accept estimation error, and expect the hedge to be imperfect in the very months it matters; volatility scaling is the common alternative.

**MP5 Replicating Jegadeesh and Titman** (`set.momentum.replication`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5); the first rehearsal of the exit. Long-form: the momentum results note. Code drill `drl.papers.momentum-rehearsal` (d5, `pandas`): replicate the $J\times K$ table on a `panel-sim` panel with a planted 6/6 premium. Hidden tests: the 6/6 cell is within 4 standard errors of the planted premium; the cohort counts are right; the shuffle-the-future test passes; the output table has the stored shape and labels. Known-wrong: no skip on a panel with planted one-month reversal (fails test 1 by landing below the planted value). Generators: `gen.papers.rehearsal-truth`, `gen.papers.unstated-choices`, `gen.papers.survival-ratio`.

- *Example A (long-form, results note, d4).* Write the one-page note for the rehearsal replication. *Model answer outline:* question and the paper's claim; data (the rehearsal panel, its planted premium and its delisting rule); the ledger with every choice; the table against the planted truth, with standard errors; the extension (the panel's post-"publication" period) with the survival ratio and its interval; what failed (for the reference: the no-skip variant, which lost a third of the premium to the planted reversal); limitations (simulated data, no costs).
  *Rubric (10).* 1 each: claim; data; ledger; table with errors; comparison with the truth; extension; survival with interval; failures; limitations; length within a page. The `results-note` predicates `sections-complete`, `metrics-consistent` and `oos-section` must pass.
- *Example B (d5).* On real data your 6/6 WML is 1.0% a month with the paper's choices. Excluding stocks below 5 dollars gives 0.66%; value weights give 0.55%; both together give 0.40%. (a) Rank the choices by effect. (b) Which number goes in the replication's headline, and which in the extension? (c) What would a sceptical reader conclude about tradability?
  *Answer.* (a) Value weighting removes 45% of the premium, the price filter 34%, both together 60%. (b) Decision point: the naive move picks the largest number as "the" replication. The headline replicates the paper's own choices (1.0%), because replication means the method as published; the sensitivity table reports the other three; the pre-registered extension uses the same choices as the headline, so the survival ratio compares like with like. (c) Much of the premium lives in small, low-priced stocks where costs are highest, so the tradable premium is nearer 0.4% before costs; 2.3's cost lessons (BT3) then decide whether anything is left.

### 3.7 Course FF (3.1)

**FF1 The CAPM and its failure** (`set.factors.capm`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: errors in variables and why portfolios help. Code drill `drl.papers.fama-macbeth` (d4, `numpy`): `fama_macbeth(R, B, nw_lags=0, shanken_factor=None)` runs one cross-sectional regression per period of returns $R$ ($T\times N$) on betas $B$ ($N\times K$, or $T\times N\times K$ for rolling betas) and returns the average slopes, their standard errors (Newey-West if `nw_lags > 0`) and Shanken-corrected errors when given the factor moments. Hidden tests: a stored fixture's slopes and errors match a reference implementation to $10^{-10}$; with constant slopes the standard error is 0; the Shanken factor for a market premium of 0.6% and factor SD 4.5% is 1.0088. Known-wrong: pooled regression of all $T\times N$ observations (fails test 1's errors). Generators: `gen.papers.alpha-from-means`, `gen.papers.fm-slope`, `gen.papers.attenuation`, `gen.papers.alpha-se`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Average returns satisfy $\bar r_i = \gamma_0 + \gamma_1\beta_i + \eta_i$ across stocks, but the regression uses estimates $\hat\beta_i = \beta_i + u_i$ with estimation errors $u_i$ independent of $\beta_i$ and $\eta_i$. (a) Show that the least-squares slope of $\bar r_i$ on $\hat\beta_i$ tends to $\gamma_1\frac{\operatorname{Var}(\beta)}{\operatorname{Var}(\beta) + \operatorname{Var}(u)}$. (b) Explain how forming portfolios helps, and what it costs. (c) Name the correction applied to Fama-MacBeth standard errors and what it corrects.
  *Model answer.* (1) The slope's limit is $\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(\bar r, \hat\beta)}{\operatorname{Var}(\hat\beta)}$. (2) $\operatorname{Cov}(\gamma_0 + \gamma_1\beta + \eta, \beta + u) = \gamma_1\operatorname{Var}(\beta)$, because $u$ and $\eta$ are independent of everything else. (3) $\operatorname{Var}(\hat\beta) = \operatorname{Var}(\beta) + \operatorname{Var}(u)$. (4) So the slope tends to $\gamma_1\lambda$ with $\lambda = \frac{\operatorname{Var}(\beta)}{\operatorname{Var}(\beta) + \operatorname{Var}(u)} < 1$: estimated betas attenuate the premium towards zero, and the intercept rises to compensate, which flattens the security market line. (5) With $\operatorname{Var}(\beta) = 0.09$ and $\operatorname{Var}(u) = 0.09$, $\lambda = 0.5$. (6) The leap: the noise is in each stock's beta, not in the pricing relation, so averaging betas over many stocks shrinks it: a portfolio of 50 stocks with independent errors has error variance $\frac{0.09}{50}$, giving $\lambda = 0.98$. (7) The cost: grouping also shrinks the spread of true betas across test assets, so portfolios must be sorted on something that preserves it (Fama and MacBeth sort on pre-ranking betas), and fewer test assets mean less power. (8) Shanken's (1992) correction multiplies the Fama-MacBeth variance by $1 + \frac{\lambda^2}{\sigma_f^2}$ (premium over factor variance) to allow for the betas being estimated from the same data, which the period-by-period slopes' spread does not capture.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the slope's limit (line 1). 2: the covariance and variance (lines 2, 3). 2: attenuation and its effect on the line (line 4). 1: the number (line 5). 2: portfolios, with the motivation and the cost (lines 6, 7). 2: Shanken's correction (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* A portfolio's monthly excess returns average 0.8% over 360 months; its market beta is 1.2; the market's excess return averages 0.5% with SD 4.5%; the regression's residual SD is 3%. (a) The CAPM alpha. (b) Its standard error, $\sigma_e\sqrt{\frac1T\left(1 + \frac{\bar\mu_m^2}{\sigma_m^2}\right)}$, and $t$. (c) The manager says the alpha is "2.4% a year". What do you reply?
  *Answer.* (a) $0.8 - 1.2\times 0.5 = 0.2\%$ a month. (b) $3\sqrt{\frac{1}{360}(1 + 0.0123)} = 0.159\%$; $t = 1.26$. (c) Decision point: the naive reply accepts 2.4% a year because 30 years sounds like plenty. The alpha is 1.26 standard errors from zero; a 95% interval runs from about $-1.3\%$ to $6.1\%$ a year; and it is an alpha against one factor, which size and value loadings could explain (FF3). It is not evidence of skill.

**FF2 Building the factors** (`set.factors.construction`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the timing convention and the look-ahead it prevents. Code drill `drl.papers.smb-hml` (d4, `pandas`): `ff_factors(panel)` builds SMB and HML from a monthly panel with fiscal-year book equity, June size, December market equity, NYSE breakpoints at the size median and the 30th and 70th B/M percentiles, value weights and June rebalancing. Hidden tests: on a 60-stock fixture the six portfolios' returns match hand-computed values to $10^{-10}$; firms with negative book equity are excluded; breakpoints use only the rows flagged NYSE; a book-equity value dated after the formation June is not used; portfolios are unchanged from July to the next June. Known-wrong: breakpoints from all stocks (fails test 3); same-year book equity (fails test 4). Generators: `gen.papers.smb-hml`, `gen.papers.breakpoints`, `gen.papers.portfolio-return`, `gen.papers.timing`.

- *Example A (long-form, written, d4).* Explain Fama and French's timing convention for book-to-market (book equity for fiscal years ending in calendar $t - 1$, December $t - 1$ market equity, portfolios formed at the end of June $t$ and held to June $t + 1$), the look-ahead it prevents, and why size uses June market equity. State what a replication on a database without filing dates must do.
  *Model answer.* (1) Annual reports are published months after the fiscal year ends; a December year-end firm's book equity may not be public until March or April. (2) Forming in June $t$ with fiscal-year $t - 1$ book equity leaves at least six months for publication, so every input was public at formation (for December year-ends; later year-ends get less margin). (3) Using the latest book equity in a database would let June portfolios depend on numbers first published later: look-ahead that makes value look better if the late numbers correlate with later returns. (4) The leap: B/M divides book equity by market equity measured at the same date (December $t - 1$), so the ratio compares like with like and does not mix a stale book value with a fresh price whose recent move is itself a return signal. (5) Size uses June $t$ market equity because it is known at formation and is the most current measure of how big the firm is when the portfolio is formed. (6) Holding July to June means one rebalance a year, which keeps turnover low and matches the annual frequency of book data. (7) Without filing dates, a replication must impose the lag by rule (as here) and report it in the ledger; with point-in-time data it can use actual filing dates and say whether that changes the result.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: publication lags (line 1). 2: the six-month margin (line 2). 2: the look-ahead prevented (line 3). 1: same-date B/M with its motivation (line 4). 1: June size (line 5). 1: annual rebalancing (line 6). 1: what a replication must do (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Three months of returns (% a month) for the six portfolios SL, SM, SH, BL, BM, BH: month 1: 1.2, 1.0, 1.5, 0.8, 0.9, 1.1; month 2: $-2.0$, $-1.6$, $-1.0$, $-1.8$, $-1.5$, $-1.2$; month 3: 3.1, 2.5, 2.9, 2.4, 2.2, 2.6. (a) SMB and HML each month and their averages. (b) A colleague rebuilt the six with all-stock breakpoints and finds SMB doubled. Why might that be, and which do you keep?
  *Answer.* (a) SMB: 0.30, $-0.03$, 0.43; average 0.23. HML: 0.30, 0.80, 0.00; average 0.37. (b) Decision point: the naive move keeps the bigger premium. All-stock breakpoints put the size median among thousands of microcaps, so "small" becomes microcap and "big" absorbs mid-caps; SMB then measures microcaps against everything else, and its premium is concentrated in stocks that are expensive to trade. Keep NYSE breakpoints to match the paper and the library, and report the all-stock version as a sensitivity check.

**FF3 Time-series regressions and the GRS test** (`set.factors.grs`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: an alpha as the gain in squared Sharpe ratio. Code drill `drl.papers.grs` (d4, `scipy`): `grs(R, F)` for test-asset excess returns $R$ ($T\times N$) and factors $F$ ($T\times L$) returns the statistic, its $F(N, T - N - L)$ $p$-value, and the alphas, using residual covariance with divisor $T - L - 1$ and factor covariance with divisor $T - 1$ (stated, catalogue open question 9). Hidden tests: a stored fixture matches a reference implementation to $10^{-10}$; zero alphas by construction give a statistic of 0; doubling every alpha quadruples the statistic. Known-wrong: divisor $T$ for the residual covariance (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.papers.grs-from-pieces`, `gen.papers.read-grid`, `gen.papers.fit-loadings`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* One factor $f$ with mean $\mu_f$ and SD $\sigma_f$, and one test asset $r = \alpha + \beta f + e$ with $e$ uncorrelated with $f$ and SD $\sigma_e$ (all excess returns). Show that the best attainable squared Sharpe ratio from combining $f$ and $r$ is $\frac{\mu_f^2}{\sigma_f^2} + \frac{\alpha^2}{\sigma_e^2}$, and explain why this makes GRS a test of whether the test assets improve on the factors.
  *Model answer.* (1) Any portfolio of $f$ and $r$ is also a portfolio of $f$ and $r - \beta f = \alpha + e$, because the two pairs span the same set of returns. (2) The leap: rewrite the second asset so it is uncorrelated with the first; then the problem splits into two independent parts. (3) $\alpha + e$ has mean $\alpha$, SD $\sigma_e$ and zero correlation with $f$. (4) For two uncorrelated excess returns with Sharpe ratios $s_1$ and $s_2$, the best combination has squared Sharpe ratio $s_1^2 + s_2^2$: the maximum squared Sharpe ratio is $\boldsymbol\mu^\top\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1}\boldsymbol\mu$, and with a diagonal $\boldsymbol\Sigma$ it is $\frac{\mu_1^2}{\sigma_1^2} + \frac{\mu_2^2}{\sigma_2^2}$. (5) So the best squared Sharpe ratio is $\frac{\mu_f^2}{\sigma_f^2} + \frac{\alpha^2}{\sigma_e^2}$, and the gain from adding the test asset is exactly $\frac{\alpha^2}{\sigma_e^2}$, its squared appraisal ratio. (6) With $N$ assets the same argument gives $\boldsymbol\alpha^\top\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1}\boldsymbol\alpha = SR^2(\text{factors and assets}) - SR^2(\text{factors})$. (7) GRS scales this gain by $\frac{1}{1 + SR^2(\text{factors})}$ and by sample-size factors; it asks whether the test assets lift the factors' frontier by more than chance allows, which is what "the factors price the assets" means.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the spanning step (line 1). 1: the motivation (line 2). 2: the orthogonalised asset (line 3). 2: additivity for uncorrelated assets (line 4). 1: the result (line 5). 1: the $N$-asset form (line 6). 1: GRS's meaning (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* For 25 portfolios over $T = 360$ months and 3 factors, $\hat{\boldsymbol\alpha}^\top\hat{\boldsymbol\Sigma}^{-1}\hat{\boldsymbol\alpha} = 0.05$ and the factors' squared monthly Sharpe ratio is 0.04. (a) The GRS statistic, its 5% critical value and $p$-value. (b) Express the result as Sharpe ratios. (c) Three of the 25 alphas have $\lvert t\rvert > 2$. Does the model fail?
  *Answer.* (a) $\frac{360}{25}\cdot\frac{332}{356}\cdot\frac{0.05}{1.04} = 0.646$; the 5% critical value of $F(25, 332)$ is 1.54; $p = 0.91$. (b) The factors' best monthly Sharpe ratio is $\sqrt{0.04} = 0.20$; with the portfolios, $\sqrt{0.09} = 0.30$: in sample the gain looks large, yet with 25 estimated alphas it is well within chance. (c) Decision point: the naive count says three failures. With 25 tests at 5% about 1.25 are expected, and correlated portfolios make clusters of rejections likely; the joint test is the test, and it does not reject.

**FF4 Beyond three factors** (`set.factors.beyond`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: why a spanned factor adds nothing. Code drill `drl.papers.spanning` (d4, `statsmodels`): `spanning_test(f_new, F_old, nw_lags)` regresses a candidate factor on existing ones and returns the intercept, its Newey-West $t$ and the implied gain in squared Sharpe ratio. Hidden tests: a factor built as an exact combination of the old ones has intercept 0 and gain 0; the gain equals $SR^2(\text{all}) - SR^2(\text{old})$ computed directly, to $10^{-10}$; the lag argument is passed to statsmodels' HAC covariance. Known-wrong: judging by the new factor's own Sharpe ratio (fails test 2). Generators: `gen.papers.spanning`, `gen.papers.four-factor-alpha`, `gen.papers.max-sharpe-two`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Regress a candidate factor $g$ on existing factors $\mathbf f$: $g = a + \mathbf b^\top\mathbf f + e$. (a) Show that adding $g$ to $\mathbf f$ raises the best attainable squared Sharpe ratio by $\frac{a^2}{\sigma_e^2}$. (b) Conclude that $g$ is redundant when $a = 0$, however high its own Sharpe ratio, and say what test is used. (c) Apply it to Fama and French's (2015) finding about HML [recalled, unverified].
  *Model answer.* (1) Portfolios of $(\mathbf f, g)$ span the same returns as portfolios of $(\mathbf f, g - \mathbf b^\top\mathbf f) = (\mathbf f, a + e)$. (2) $a + e$ is uncorrelated with $\mathbf f$, so by additivity (FF3's long-form) the best squared Sharpe ratio is $SR^2(\mathbf f) + \frac{a^2}{\sigma_e^2}$. (3) If $a = 0$ the gain is 0: every return $g$ offers can be built from the existing factors, so $g$ is spanned. (4) The leap: a factor's own Sharpe ratio measures what it earns alone; what matters to an investor already holding the others is what it earns beyond them, its intercept. (5) The test is the intercept's $t$-statistic in the spanning regression, with Newey-West errors if returns are autocorrelated; for several candidates at once, a GRS-type joint test. (6) Fama and French report that HML's intercept on the other four factors of their five-factor model is close to zero, so HML is redundant in that model. (7) Redundancy is sample-specific: a factor spanned in one period may not be in another, so a replication should test it after the sample too.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: spanning (line 1). 2: the gain (line 2). 1: redundancy (line 3). 2: own Sharpe against intercept, with the motivation (line 4). 1: the test (line 5). 1: HML (line 6). 1: sample dependence (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Two factors each have an annual Sharpe ratio of 0.25 and correlate at 0.3. (a) The best Sharpe ratio of a combination. (b) Factor B's intercept on factor A, in Sharpe units, and the gain it implies. (c) A colleague wants to drop B because "it is no better than A". What do you say?
  *Answer.* (a) $SR^2 = \frac{0.0625 + 0.0625 - 2\times 0.3\times 0.0625}{1 - 0.09} = 0.0962$, so $SR = 0.310$. (b) $\frac{0.25 - 0.3\times 0.25}{\sqrt{0.91}} = 0.183$; its square, 0.0337, is exactly the gain $0.0962 - 0.0625$. (c) Decision point: the naive comparison ranks factors by their own Sharpe ratios and finds a tie. What matters is B's alpha on A: 0.183 in Sharpe units lifts the combination's Sharpe ratio from 0.25 to 0.31, a 24% improvement, so B earns its place as long as its intercept is significant and survives out of sample.

**FF5 Replicating Fama and French** (`set.factors.replication`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5); the default exit route's rehearsal. Long-form: the factor note. Code drill `drl.papers.ff-rehearsal` (d5, `statsmodels`): build SMB and HML on a `panel-sim` panel with planted size and value premia, run the 25 time-series regressions, compute GRS, and extend past the panel's "publication" date. Hidden tests: the planted premia are recovered within 4 standard errors; GRS on the planted three-factor world is not rejected at 1% for the stored seed (margin proved in CI); the post-publication premia match the planted decay within 4 standard errors. Known-wrong: same-year book equity (fails test 1 on a panel whose planted value premium appears only with the lag). Generators: `gen.papers.rehearsal-truth`, `gen.papers.survival-ratio`, `gen.papers.grs-from-pieces`.

- *Example A (long-form, results note, d4).* Write the note for the rehearsal replication of Fama and French. *Model answer outline:* the claim (three factors explain the 25 portfolios' average returns); data and construction with the ledger (NYSE breakpoints, June timing, value weights); loadings and $R^2$ patterns; the alpha grid and GRS against the planted truth; the extension with survival ratios for SMB and HML and intervals; what failed (for the reference: equal weights, which doubled SMB through microcaps); limitations.
  *Rubric (10).* As MP5's, with "loadings pattern" and "GRS" replacing "table with errors" and "comparison with the truth".
- *Example B (d5).* An illustrative extension (not the library's actual figures): HML averaged 0.45% a month in the paper's sample (SE 0.13%) and 0.15% after it (SE 0.17%). (a) The survival ratio and its delta-method interval. (b) What else must the note report before concluding value "died"? (c) What would a second out-of-sample test be?
  *Answer.* (a) $r = 0.33$, $\text{SE} = 0.39$, interval $(-0.43, 1.10)$. (b) Decision point: the naive conclusion reads a third survived as death. The interval spans both 0 and full survival; the note must report sub-periods, the sensitivity to the start date, and the post-sample mean's own interval ($0.15\pm 0.33$), and say plainly that the data cannot distinguish a smaller premium from none. (c) International data: the library's developed-market factors provide an independent sample (lesson 48).

### 3.8 Course PS (3.1)

**PS1 The distance method** (`set.pairs.distance`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: why minimum-SSD selection caps profits, and why the rule uses the formation SD. Code drill `drl.papers.pairs-formation` (d4, `numpy`): `normalise(prices, dividends)`, `ssd_matrix(norm)`, `top_pairs(ssd, n)` and `trades(norm_a, norm_b, sd, wait=1)` returning open and close indices and payoffs. Hidden tests: lesson 52's series give SSD 0.0006; 500 stocks give 124,750 candidate pairs; a fixture's trades open at the first day the gap exceeds $2\sigma$ and close at the next crossing; with `wait=1` every trade executes a day later; an open pair at the period's end is closed at the last price. Known-wrong: $\sigma$ from the trading period (fails the fixture's open day); raw prices instead of normalised (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.papers.ssd`, `gen.papers.pick-pairs`, `gen.papers.mark-trades`, `gen.papers.trade-return`, `gen.papers.capital-basis`.

- *Example A (long-form, written, d4).* (a) Explain why choosing pairs with the smallest sum of squared deviations favours pairs whose spread barely moves, and what that does to the profit per trade (Do and Faff 2010). (b) Explain why GGR open at two standard deviations of the formation-period spread rather than of the trading-period spread. (c) Propose one change to the selection that addresses (a), and its risk.
  *Model answer.* (1) SSD is $\sum_t(p_{A,t} - p_{B,t})^2$, which is small when the normalised spread stays near zero. (2) For a spread with mean near zero over the window, SSD is about $T$ times the spread's variance, so minimising SSD selects low-variance spreads. (3) The trigger is two formation SDs, so a low-variance pair opens on small gaps; its profit per trade, roughly the gap that closes, is small and can fall below trading costs. (4) The leap: the selection criterion and the trading rule interact; a pair that is "closest" in the formation window is not the pair with the most profitable mean reversion, which needs both a link and enough movement. (5) The formation SD is known at the start of trading; the trading-period SD uses returns not yet observed, so opening on it would be look-ahead. (6) A change: rank pairs by a mean-reversion measure (the formation spread's number of zero crossings, or an AR(1) coefficient's half-life) among pairs with SSD below a cutoff. (7) Its risk: a new criterion is a new trial; it must be chosen before the trading period and counted in the ledger.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: SSD and variance (lines 1, 2). 2: the effect on profit per trade (line 3). 1: the interaction (line 4). 2: formation SD and look-ahead (line 5). 2: a sensible change (line 6). 1: its risk (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A pair's formation SD of the normalised spread is 0.025. On day 10 of trading the normalised prices are A 1.12 and B 1.06; on day 11, A 1.11 and B 1.065; on day 25 both are 1.15. (a) Should the pair open on day 10, and in which direction? (b) The pair's payoff with same-day execution on one dollar long and one short. (c) The payoff with GGR's one-day wait. (d) Which number would you report?
  *Answer.* (a) The gap is $1.12 - 1.06 = 0.06 > 2\times 0.025 = 0.05$, so open: short A (the leader) and long B. (b) Short A: $-\left(\frac{1.15}{1.12} - 1\right) = -2.68\%$; long B: $\frac{1.15}{1.06} - 1 = 8.49\%$; payoff 5.81%. (c) Entering on day 11: $-\left(\frac{1.15}{1.11} - 1\right) + \left(\frac{1.15}{1.065} - 1\right) = -3.60\% + 7.98\% = 4.38\%$. (d) Decision point: the naive figure is 5.81%. Part of day 10's gap may be bid-ask bounce in the closing prices that reverses by itself; the one-day wait removes it, so report 4.38% as the strategy's return and the difference as an estimate of how much bounce the same-day figure contained (lesson 55).

**PS2 What the paper found, and why** (`set.pairs.findings`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: limits to arbitrage. Code drill `drl.papers.pairs-factors` (d3, `statsmodels`): regress a pairs portfolio's monthly excess returns on market, size, value, momentum and short-term reversal factors with Newey-West errors and report alpha. Hidden tests: on a fixture built as $0.5\times$ reversal plus noise plus a planted alpha, the reversal loading is within 4 standard errors of 0.5 and the alpha of the plant; the HAC lag argument is used. Known-wrong: plain standard errors (fails the stored $t$). Generators: `gen.papers.capital-basis`, `gen.papers.factor-alpha`, `gen.papers.decay-by-decade`.

- *Example A (long-form, written, d4).* A pairs fund's spreads widen sharply for two months after it opens most of its positions; investors withdraw a third of the capital at the low. Explain, with the limits-to-arbitrage argument, why a convergence trade with positive expected value can lose money and even fail, and list the protections a fund can buy and what each costs.
  *Model answer.* (1) Convergence is expected, not guaranteed, and its timing is unknown: the spread can widen before it narrows. (2) The fund's capital is borrowed against or provided by investors who judge it on interim marks; losses on paper become forced sales when margin is called or investors withdraw. (3) Forced selling at the widest point locks in the loss and can widen spreads further if others hold the same positions (crowding). (4) The leap: the risk is not in the pair, it is in the financing; a trade that would pay with patient capital loses with impatient capital, which is why arbitrage is limited exactly when mispricing is largest. (5) Short-sale constraints add cost: borrowing fees, recalls, and the risk that the short leg cannot be held. (6) Protections: lower gross exposure (less profit, more survival), longer investor lock-ups (harder to raise money), stop-losses at the pair level (which realise losses and can cut trades that would have converged, 2.1 lesson 130), diversification across uncorrelated pairs (limited if a common factor exists, lesson 57). (7) A pair can also simply break (a merger, a default), which no financing protects against; a cap on the time a pair may stay open bounds this.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: timing uncertainty (line 1). 2: financing and forced sales (lines 2, 3). 1: the motivation that the risk is in financing (line 4). 1: short-sale costs (line 5). 3: protections with costs (line 6). 1: broken pairs (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A portfolio of 50 pairs gets one dollar of committed capital per pair for a six-month trading period; 30 pairs open at some point, and their payoffs sum to 0.84 dollars. (a) The return on committed capital and on employed capital. (b) Annualise each by simple doubling. (c) Which would you compare with a fund's return, and why do GGR report both?
  *Answer.* (a) Committed: $\frac{0.84}{50} = 1.68\%$; employed: $\frac{0.84}{30} = 2.80\%$. (b) 3.36% and 5.60% a year. (c) Decision point: the naive figure is the larger one. A fund must hold capital for every pair it might open, so committed capital is the comparable basis; employed capital describes the trades' quality when open. GGR report both because each answers a different question, and the gap between them measures how often capital sits idle.

**PS3 Statistical arbitrage models** (`set.pairs.models`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: discretising the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process and recovering its parameters from an AR(1). Code drill `drl.papers.ou-fit` (d3, `numpy`): `fit_ou(spread, dt)` regresses $X_{t+1}$ on $X_t$ and returns $\kappa$, $\theta$, $\sigma$, the half-life and the equilibrium SD; `s_score(x, theta, sd_eq)`. Hidden tests: a simulated OU path with a fixed seed recovers the planted $\kappa$ within the reference's stored margin (decision 3 rule (c)); a coefficient $b = 0.95$ with $dt = \frac{1}{252}$ gives a half-life of 13.5 days; $b\ge 1$ raises `ValueError` ("no mean reversion"). Known-wrong: half-life $\frac{1}{1 - b}$ (fails test 2); $\kappa = 1 - b$ without the time scale (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.papers.half-life`, `gen.papers.ou-params`, `gen.papers.band-tradeoff`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* The spread follows $dX_t = \kappa(\theta - X_t)dt + \sigma dW_t$. (a) Show that sampled every $\Delta$ it is an AR(1): $X_{t+\Delta} = \theta(1 - e^{-\kappa\Delta}) + e^{-\kappa\Delta}X_t + \varepsilon$ with $\operatorname{Var}(\varepsilon) = \frac{\sigma^2}{2\kappa}(1 - e^{-2\kappa\Delta})$. (b) Recover $\kappa$, $\theta$ and the equilibrium SD from a fitted $X_{t+1} = a + bX_t + \varepsilon$. (c) Derive the half-life.
  *Model answer.* (1) The solution of the OU equation over an interval is $X_{t+\Delta} = \theta + (X_t - \theta)e^{-\kappa\Delta} + \sigma\int_t^{t+\Delta}e^{-\kappa(t + \Delta - s)}dW_s$ (2.2 lesson 40's discrete twin, derived with the integrating factor $e^{\kappa t}$ as in 1.3's first-order equations). (2) The stochastic integral is normal with mean 0 and variance $\sigma^2\int_0^\Delta e^{-2\kappa u}du = \frac{\sigma^2}{2\kappa}(1 - e^{-2\kappa\Delta})$ by Itô's isometry, stated. (3) Matching terms: $b = e^{-\kappa\Delta}$ and $a = \theta(1 - b)$, so $\kappa = -\frac{\ln b}{\Delta}$ and $\theta = \frac{a}{1 - b}$. (4) The stationary variance of the AR(1) is $\frac{\operatorname{Var}(\varepsilon)}{1 - b^2}$, which equals $\frac{\sigma^2}{2\kappa}$, so the equilibrium SD is $\frac{\sigma_\varepsilon}{\sqrt{1 - b^2}}$. (5) The leap: the half-life asks when the expected gap has halved; the expected gap decays like $b^h$ per step, which turns a stopping question into a power. (6) $b^h = \frac12$ gives $h = \frac{\ln\frac12}{\ln b}$ steps, or $\frac{\ln 2}{\kappa}$ in time units. (7) With $b$ near 1, $\ln b\approx b - 1$, so $h\approx\frac{0.69}{1 - b}$, not $\frac{1}{1 - b}$; and a small error in $b$ near 1 moves $h$ a lot, so report its interval.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the solution (line 1). 2: the noise variance (line 2). 2: the parameter mapping (line 3). 1: the equilibrium SD (line 4). 2: the half-life with its motivation (lines 5, 6). 1: the approximation and the sensitivity (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A daily spread fits $b = 0.98$ with residual SD 0.005. (a) $\kappa$ per year (252 days), the half-life in days, and the equilibrium SD. (b) The spread now sits 0.05 above $\theta$. Its s-score, and the expected gap in 34 trading days. (c) A trader opens at an s-score of 2 and plans to close in a week. What is wrong with the plan?
  *Answer.* (a) $\kappa = -252\ln 0.98 = 5.09$; half-life $\frac{\ln 0.5}{\ln 0.98} = 34.3$ days; equilibrium SD $\frac{0.005}{\sqrt{1 - 0.9604}} = 0.0251$. (b) s-score $\frac{0.05}{0.0251} = 1.99$; after 34 days the expected gap is about half, 0.025. (c) Decision point: the naive plan treats the s-score as a timer. With a 34-day half-life the expected gap after a week (5 days) has fallen only to $0.98^5 = 90\%$ of its start, so a one-week holding period captures about a tenth of the expected convergence while paying the full cost; size the holding period from the half-life, or trade spreads that revert faster.

**PS4 Replicating Gatev, Goetzmann and Rouwenhorst** (`set.pairs.replication`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the pairs note. Code drill `drl.papers.pairs-rehearsal` (d5, `pandas`): the full GGR pipeline (12-month formation, 6-month trading, staggered monthly, top 20 pairs, one-day wait, committed and employed capital) on a `panel-sim` panel with planted pairs. Hidden tests: the planted cointegrated pairs are among the top 20 in the stored seed's formation windows (margin proved in CI); the one-day wait is applied; staggered portfolios are averaged like JT's cohorts; no formation statistic uses trading-period data (shuffle-the-future test). Known-wrong: no wait (fails test 2 and inflates returns by the planted bounce). Generators: `gen.papers.rehearsal-truth`, `gen.papers.survival-ratio`, `gen.papers.survivorship-bias`.

- *Example A (long-form, results note, d4).* Write the rehearsal note for the pairs replication. *Model answer outline:* claim; data and the universe (with its survivorship treatment); the ledger (normalisation, SSD, top-$n$, trigger, wait, capital basis); results on both capital bases with errors; the extension and survival ratio; what failed (for the reference: the top-5 portfolio, too concentrated to beat costs); limitations, with the survivorship bias bounded.
  *Rubric (10).* As MP5's, with "both capital bases" and "survivorship bound" replacing "table with errors" and "comparison with the truth".
- *Example B (d5).* Your free-data replication uses today's 500 index members back 20 years. In the rehearsal panel, dropping delisted stocks raises the top-20 portfolio's excess return from 0.45% to 0.60% a month. (a) Which way does survivorship bias push a pairs strategy, and why? (b) How do you report the free-data result? (c) Could the bias go the other way?
  *Answer.* (a) Upwards: pairs that broke because one firm failed or was taken over are exactly the losing trades, and they disappear with the delisted firms. (b) Decision point: the naive report gives the free-data number. Report it with the rehearsal's estimate of the bias (0.15% a month, a quarter of the result) as a bound, say that the true figure is plausibly lower by about that much, and recommend a point-in-time universe. (c) In part: takeovers can make the target's leg jump towards the bidder's price, which can help a pair that was long the target; the net effect is an empirical question, which is why the rehearsal measures it rather than assumes it.

### 3.9 Course MM (3.1)

**MM1 The market maker's problem** (`set.making.problem`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the certainty equivalent under exponential utility. Code drill `drl.papers.fill-sim` (d3, `stdlib`): `simulate_fills(delta_a, delta_b, A, k, dt, steps, seed)` draws fills with probability $\min(\lambda(\delta)dt, 0.5)$ per side per step using the shipped `zqrandom`, returning fill counts and a `clamped` flag. Hidden tests: with $A = 140$, $k = 1.5$, $\delta = 0.75$ and $dt = 0.005$ the per-step probability is 0.2273; a fixed seed reproduces stored fill counts; a quote at $\delta = 0$ with large $A dt$ sets `clamped`; negative $\delta$ raises. Known-wrong: no clamp (fails test 3); one random draw shared by both sides (fails the stored counts). Generators: `gen.papers.best-delta`, `gen.papers.certainty-equivalent`, `gen.papers.inventory-variance`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) For wealth $W\sim N(\mu, s^2)$ and utility $u(W) = -e^{-\gamma W}$, show $E[u(W)] = -\exp\left(-\gamma\left(\mu - \frac\gamma2s^2\right)\right)$ and hence that the certainty equivalent is $\mu - \frac\gamma2s^2$. (b) Explain why the market maker's problem is a trade-off between the expected spread income and the variance of inventory value, and why exponential utility makes the solution independent of wealth.
  *Model answer.* (1) $E[e^{-\gamma W}]$ is the moment generating function of a normal at $-\gamma$: $E[e^{tW}] = e^{t\mu + \frac12t^2s^2}$ (1.5). (2) With $t = -\gamma$: $E[e^{-\gamma W}] = e^{-\gamma\mu + \frac12\gamma^2s^2} = e^{-\gamma(\mu - \frac\gamma2s^2)}$. (3) The certainty equivalent $c$ solves $-e^{-\gamma c} = E[u(W)]$, so $c = \mu - \frac\gamma2s^2$: a mean penalised by variance at price $\frac\gamma2$. (4) For the example in lesson 71, $1 - 0.05\times 4 = 0.8$. (5) The maker's terminal wealth is cash plus inventory times the final mid; quoting earns spread income (raising the mean) and accumulates inventory whose value varies with the mid (raising the variance by $q^2\sigma^2(T - t)$, lesson 72). (6) The leap: with exponential utility the problem's objective is a mean minus a variance, so every quote can be judged by how much it adds to expected income against how much inventory risk it brings, and the trade-off has a closed form. (7) Because $e^{-\gamma(x + W)} = e^{-\gamma x}e^{-\gamma W}$, current wealth $x$ factors out of every comparison, so the optimal quotes do not depend on how rich the maker is; the paper uses this to get quotes in closed form.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the MGF step (lines 1, 2). 2: the certainty equivalent (line 3). 1: the number (line 4). 2: the trade-off in the maker's problem (line 5). 1: the motivation (line 6). 2: wealth independence (line 7).
