- *Example B (d5).* One side of a book fills at rate $\lambda(\delta) = 100e^{-2\delta}$ per unit time when quoted $\delta$ from the mid. (a) The $\delta$ that maximises the expected profit rate $\delta\lambda(\delta)$ and the rate there. (b) Why does the maker not quote at that $\delta$ on both sides all session? (c) What happens to the best $\delta$ if the market becomes more competitive and $k$ rises?
  *Answer.* (a) $\frac{d}{d\delta}\left(\delta e^{-2\delta}\right) = e^{-2\delta}(1 - 2\delta) = 0$ at $\delta = 0.5$; the rate is $\frac{A}{ke} = \frac{100}{2e} = 18.4$ per unit time. (b) Decision point: the naive plan quotes symmetrically at the one-sided optimum. Fills on one side accumulate inventory whose value moves with the mid; the optimal quotes lean against inventory and widen with the time left and with volatility (lessons 74 and 76), trading some expected income for less variance. (c) $\delta^* = \frac1k$ falls: when fills drop off faster with distance, the maker must quote closer to earn anything, and the profit rate $\frac{A}{ke}$ falls too.

**MM2 The Avellaneda-Stoikov solution** (`set.making.solution`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the reservation price from the frozen-inventory value function. Code drill `drl.papers.as-quotes` (d3, `stdlib`): `reservation(s, q, gamma, sigma, tau)`, `spread(gamma, sigma, tau, k)` and `quotes(...)` returning bid and ask. Hidden tests: lesson 74's state gives 99.4; lesson 76's gives 1.4908; example B's state gives bid 99.5046 and ask 100.8954; as $\gamma\to 0$ (at $10^{-8}$) the spread is within $10^{-6}$ of $\frac2k$; $\tau = 0$ gives $r = s$. Known-wrong: $+q$ in the reservation price (fails test 1); $\frac1\gamma$ in place of $\frac2\gamma$ in the spread (fails test 2). Generators: `gen.papers.reservation-price`, `gen.papers.optimal-spread`, `gen.papers.sensitivity-order`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A maker holds $q$ shares and cash $x$, will not trade again, and values terminal wealth $x + qS_T$ by exponential utility, with $S_T\sim N(s, \sigma^2(T - t))$. (a) Show the value function is $v(x, s, q, t) = -e^{-\gamma x}e^{-\gamma qs}e^{\frac12\gamma^2q^2\sigma^2(T - t)}$. (b) Define the reservation bid $r^b$ by $v(x - r^b, s, q + 1, t) = v(x, s, q, t)$ and the reservation ask likewise, and derive them. (c) Derive $r = \frac{r^a + r^b}{2} = s - q\gamma\sigma^2(T - t)$ and interpret it.
  *Model answer.* (1) $x + qS_T$ is normal with mean $x + qs$ and variance $q^2\sigma^2(T - t)$, so by MM1's long-form $v = -\exp\left(-\gamma(x + qs) + \frac12\gamma^2q^2\sigma^2(T - t)\right)$. (2) The leap: the price the maker would pay for one more share is the cash amount that leaves utility unchanged, so the reservation price is an indifference condition, not a guess. (3) Buying one share at $r^b$: $-\gamma(x - r^b + (q + 1)s) + \frac12\gamma^2(q + 1)^2\sigma^2\tau = -\gamma(x + qs) + \frac12\gamma^2q^2\sigma^2\tau$, with $\tau = T - t$. (4) Simplifying: $\gamma r^b - \gamma s + \frac12\gamma^2(2q + 1)\sigma^2\tau = 0$, so $r^b = s - (1 + 2q)\frac{\gamma\sigma^2\tau}{2}$. (5) Selling one share at $r^a$ by the same steps gives $r^a = s + (1 - 2q)\frac{\gamma\sigma^2\tau}{2}$. (6) The average is $r = s - q\gamma\sigma^2\tau$. (7) Interpretation: a long maker ($q > 0$) values the stock below the mid because more of it adds risk, so it centres its quotes lower to sell; a short maker centres higher; the lean is larger with more risk aversion, more volatility and more time left, and vanishes at the close. (8) The paper's optimal quotes are centred on $r$, not on the mid (`mc.papers.quote-around-the-mid`).
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the value function (line 1). 1: indifference as the definition, with the motivation (line 2). 3: the reservation bid (lines 3, 4). 1: the ask (line 5). 1: the average (line 6). 2: the interpretation and the quote centre (lines 7, 8).
- *Example B (d5).* State: mid 100, inventory $-2$ (short), $\gamma = 0.1$, $\sigma = 2$, $k = 1.5$, a quarter of the session left. (a) The reservation price. (b) The optimal spread. (c) The bid and ask. (d) A colleague quotes symmetric 0.7 either side of the mid. Which side of their book fills more, and what happens to their inventory?
  *Answer.* (a) $r = 100 + 2\times 0.1\times 4\times 0.25 = 100.2$. (b) $0.1\times 4\times 0.25 + 20\ln\left(1 + \frac{0.1}{1.5}\right) = 0.1 + 1.2908 = 1.3908$. (c) Bid $100.2 - 0.6954 = 99.5046$; ask $100.2 + 0.6954 = 100.8954$. (d) Decision point: the symmetric quote ignores the short position. The AS quotes sit 0.2 higher, so the AS bid is closer to the mid (0.495 against 0.7) and buys back more often while its ask is farther (0.895) and sells less, pulling inventory towards zero; the colleague's symmetric book keeps a short position it is paid nothing extra to hold, with the variance growing in the remaining time.

**MM3 Simulating the paper** (`set.making.simulation`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: why the inventory strategy narrows the profit distribution. Code drill `drl.papers.as-sim` (d5, `numpy`): `simulate(strategy, params, n_runs, seed)` implements the paper's scheme (the mid moves $\pm\sigma\sqrt{dt}$ each step; each side fills with probability $\lambda(\delta)dt$) for the inventory strategy and a symmetric strategy with the same average spread, returning final profit and inventory per run. Hidden tests: a fixed seed reproduces stored means and SDs to $10^{-10}$; the inventory strategy's SD of final inventory is below the symmetric strategy's on the stored seed (margin proved in CI); profit equals cash plus inventory at the final mid in every run; spread capture plus inventory P&L equals profit. Known-wrong: quotes centred on the mid for the inventory strategy (fails test 2). Generators: `gen.papers.predict-batch`, `gen.papers.pnl-attribution`, `gen.papers.fill-probability`.

- *Example A (long-form, written, d4).* Explain, without simulation, why the Avellaneda-Stoikov inventory strategy should have a narrower distribution of final profit than a symmetric strategy with the same average spread, why its mean need not be higher, and which comparison the paper's table makes [recalled, unverified as to the table's figures].
  *Model answer.* (1) Final profit is spread capture plus inventory P&L, $\sum_tq_t\Delta s_t$ (lesson 83). (2) With a martingale mid, inventory P&L has mean zero for any strategy that does not know the future, so strategies differ in mean only through spread capture. (3) Its variance is $\sigma^2\sum_tq_t^2dt$, so it grows with the squared inventory held over time. (4) The leap: the inventory strategy's skew is a feedback loop on $q$: a long position lowers both quotes, making sells likelier and buys rarer, which pulls $q$ back to zero, much as mean reversion bounds a random walk. (5) A symmetric strategy has no such pull, so its inventory wanders like a random walk and $\sum q_t^2$ grows roughly with the square of the number of steps. (6) The inventory strategy pays for the pull with some spread income: skewed quotes fill the less favourable side more often, so its mean can be slightly lower. (7) A risk-averse maker prefers it because it gives up a little mean for much less variance; the paper's table reports mean and SD of profit and final inventory for both strategies to show exactly this trade.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the decomposition (line 1). 2: zero-mean inventory P&L (line 2). 1: its variance (line 3). 2: the feedback, with its motivation (line 4). 1: the symmetric random walk (line 5). 2: the cost to the mean (line 6). 1: the comparison the table makes (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A four-step session: the mid goes 100, 100.5, 99.5, 100, 101. The maker buys one share at 99.3 at step 0 (mid 100), sells one at 101.2 at step 1 (mid 100.5), buys one at 98.9 at step 2 (mid 99.5), and holds to the end. (a) Spread capture. (b) Inventory P&L by $\sum_tq_t\Delta s_t$. (c) Total profit from cash and the final mark, and the check. (d) Which component would a longer session's variance be dominated by?
  *Answer.* (a) $0.7 + 0.7 + 0.6 = 2.0$. (b) Inventory after each step: 1, 0, 1, 1; mid changes $+0.5$, $-1.0$, $+0.5$, $+1.0$; so $1\times 0.5 + 0\times(-1.0) + 1\times 0.5 + 1\times 1.0 = 2.0$. (c) Cash $-99.3 + 101.2 - 98.9 = -97.0$; inventory 1 at 101; total 4.0, which equals $2.0 + 2.0$. (d) Decision point: the naive reading credits the whole 4.0 to market making. Half came from holding a long position into a rising mid, which is directional risk, not spread capture; over many sessions inventory P&L averages to about zero and dominates the variance, which is the risk the AS skew is designed to cut.

**MM4 Beyond the paper** (`set.making.beyond`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Glosten-Milgrom's bid and ask. Code drill `drl.papers.gm-update` (d3, `stdlib`): `gm_quotes(p_high, mu, v_high, v_low)` returns the ask and bid, and `update(p_high, trade, mu)` the posterior after a buy or a sell, using `Fraction`s when the inputs are exact. Hidden tests: lesson 85's state gives ask 102 and bid 98; with $\mu = 0.4$ the ask is 104; at $\mu = 0.4$, after two buys the probability of the high value is $\frac{49}{58}$ and the next ask $\frac{4016}{37} = 108.54$; $\mu = 0$ gives bid = ask = the prior mean. Known-wrong: quoting the prior mean plus a fixed half-spread (fails test 3). Generators: `gen.papers.gm-quotes`, `gen.papers.kyle-lambda`, `gen.papers.calibrate-fills`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Value $V$ is $V_H$ or $V_L$ with probability $p$ and $1 - p$. A fraction $\mu$ of traders know $V$ and buy when it is high and sell when it is low; the rest buy or sell with equal chance. A competitive, risk-neutral maker sets the ask to $E[V\mid\text{buy}]$ and the bid to $E[V\mid\text{sell}]$. (a) Derive both. (b) Show the maker breaks even on average. (c) Explain why the spread widens with $\mu$ and what happens after a sequence of buys.
  *Model answer.* (1) $P(\text{buy}\mid V_H) = \mu + \frac{1 - \mu}{2}$ and $P(\text{buy}\mid V_L) = \frac{1 - \mu}{2}$. (2) By Bayes' rule, $P(V_H\mid\text{buy}) = \frac{p\left(\mu + \frac{1 - \mu}2\right)}{p\left(\mu + \frac{1 - \mu}2\right) + (1 - p)\frac{1 - \mu}2}$, and the ask is $V_L + (V_H - V_L)P(V_H\mid\text{buy})$; the bid is the same with a sell. (3) For $p = \frac12$, $V$ of 90 or 110 and $\mu = 0.2$: $P(V_H\mid\text{buy}) = 0.6$, ask 102, bid 98. (4) The leap: the maker cannot tell informed from uninformed traders, but a buy is more likely to come from an informed trader when the value is high, so the trade itself is information, and pricing on it is Bayes' rule. (5) Break-even: at the ask the maker sells at $E[V\mid\text{buy}]$, so its expected profit on a buy is zero; the uninformed traders' losses pay exactly for the informed traders' gains. (6) A larger $\mu$ makes each trade more informative, moving the conditional expectations apart and widening the spread; with $\mu = 0.4$ the ask is 104 and the bid 96. (7) After a buy the maker updates $p$ upwards and quotes from the new prior; repeated buys push $p$ towards 1 and both quotes towards $V_H$, so prices learn the value from order flow.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the conditional trade probabilities (line 1). 2: Bayes and the quotes (line 2). 1: the numbers (line 3). 1: the motivation (line 4). 2: break-even and who pays (line 5). 1: the effect of $\mu$ (line 6). 1: learning (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* In the same model with $\mu = 0.4$ and prior $\frac12$: (a) the first ask and bid; (b) the probability of the high value after two buys, and the next ask; (c) a maker quotes 102 and 98 regardless (the $\mu = 0.2$ quotes). Who trades with them, and what happens?
  *Answer.* (a) $P(\text{buy}\mid V_H) = 0.7$, so $P(V_H\mid\text{buy}) = 0.7$; ask $90 + 20\times 0.7 = 104$, bid 96. (b) $\frac{0.7^2}{0.7^2 + 0.3^2} = 0.845$; the next buy's posterior is $\frac{0.845\times 0.7}{0.845\times 0.7 + 0.155\times 0.3} = 0.927$, so the ask is $90 + 20\times 0.927 = 108.54$. (c) Decision point: the naive maker keeps last week's spread. With more informed traders, a 4-point spread no longer covers adverse selection: informed traders buy at 102 when the value is 110 and sell at 98 when it is 90, and the maker loses on average. The spread must move with the share of informed flow, which is one reason spreads widen around news.

### 3.10 Course WS (3.1)

**WS1 The factor zoo** (`set.survival.zoo`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: why the hurdle must rise with the number of tests, and what HLZ's $t > 3$ does and does not mean. Code drill `drl.papers.mtc` (d4, `stdlib`): `adjust(pvalues, method, alpha=0.05)` for `bonferroni`, `holm`, `bh` and `by` returning reject flags and adjusted $p$-values. Hidden tests: example B's ten $p$-values match statsmodels' `multipletests` (stored fixture) to $10^{-12}$ for all four methods; adjusted $p$-values are capped at 1 and monotone in the sorted order; ties are handled as statsmodels does; an empty list returns empty. Known-wrong: BH without the step-up (stopping at the first failure from the top; fails test 1 for `bh`); BY's constant omitted (fails test 1 for `by`). Generators: `gen.papers.hurdle-at-m`, `gen.papers.bhy-set`, `gen.papers.expected-false`, `gen.papers.haircut`.

- *Example A (long-form, written, d4).* (a) Derive the Bonferroni $t$-hurdle for $M$ two-sided tests at family-wise level 5% and compute it for $M = 1$, 10, 100 and 300. (b) Explain why correlation between factors makes Bonferroni conservative, and what an effective number of tests is. (c) Explain what HLZ's recommendation of $t > 3$ is, and three reasons a particular new factor might deserve a lower or higher bar.
  *Model answer.* (1) Bonferroni bounds the chance of any false rejection by $\sum_m P(\text{reject } m) = M\alpha'$, so each test uses $\alpha' = \frac{0.05}{M}$. (2) Two-sided, the hurdle is $\Phi^{-1}\left(1 - \frac{0.025}{M}\right)$: 1.96, 2.81, 3.48 and 3.76. (3) The union bound is tight only when rejections rarely coincide; correlated factors reject together, so the true family-wise rate is below $M\alpha'$ and the hurdle is higher than needed. (4) An effective number of tests $M_{\text{eff}} < M$, estimated for example from the eigenvalues of the factors' correlation matrix or by bootstrap, gives a less conservative hurdle. (5) HLZ's $t > 3$ is a rounded hurdle from their multiple-testing adjustments (including BHY's FDR control and an allowance for unpublished tests) given the number of factors tested by the early 2010s [recalled, unverified]. (6) The leap: a $t$-statistic answers "how surprising is this if the factor were null?", but the probability the factor is real also depends on how many nulls were tried and on prior plausibility; the hurdle is a crude way to put the second part into the first. (7) A lower bar may suit a factor predicted by theory before the data were seen, or replicated in independent data; a higher bar suits a factor found by search, with no mechanism, or one that needs many free choices (lesson 99).
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the Bonferroni argument (line 1). 2: the four hurdles (line 2). 2: correlation and the effective number (lines 3, 4). 1: what $t > 3$ is (line 5). 1: the motivation (line 6). 2: three reasons for a different bar (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Ten candidate factors have $t$-statistics 4.1, 3.6, 3.3, 3.0, 2.8, 2.5, 2.2, 2.0, 1.7 and 1.2 (two-sided normal $p$-values). At 5%, which survive (a) Bonferroni, (b) Holm, (c) BH, (d) BHY? (e) Which list would you take to the investment committee?
  *Answer.* The $p$-values are 0.00004, 0.00032, 0.00097, 0.0027, 0.0051, 0.0124, 0.0278, 0.0455, 0.0891, 0.2301. (a) Bonferroni (threshold 0.005): the first 4. (b) Holm adds the fifth ($0.0051\le\frac{0.05}{6}$): 5. (c) BH (step-up, $\frac{i}{10}\times 0.05$): the first 7. (d) BHY divides BH's thresholds by $c(10) = 2.93$: 5. (e) Decision point: the naive list counts every $t > 2$ (8 factors). For a committee that will deploy capital on each factor, control the family-wise error or BHY's false discovery rate under dependence (5 factors); BH's 7 suits a research screen whose survivors will be tested again out of sample.

**WS2 The replication evidence** (`set.survival.evidence`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: a critique of a decay study. Code drill `drl.papers.decay-regression` (d4, `statsmodels`): regress monthly long-short returns of many anomalies on dummies for "after the sample" and "after publication", with standard errors clustered by month, and return the decay estimates. Hidden tests: on a `panel-sim` panel with planted decay of 25% after the sample and 56% after publication, the estimates fall within 4 clustered standard errors of the plants; the clustering argument is used (the errors differ from plain ones on the fixture); anomalies with no post-publication months are excluded. Known-wrong: plain standard errors (fails test 2). Generators: `gen.papers.decay-pct`, `gen.papers.weighting-effect`, `gen.papers.spec-count`.

- *Example A (long-form, written, d4).* Critique this claim: "Anomaly returns fall by about half after publication, so half of all anomalies were false discoveries." Explain at least three other reasons returns fall, how a study could tell them apart, and what each implies for a researcher replicating one anomaly.
  *Model answer.* (1) Statistical bias: in-sample returns of published anomalies are selected for being large, so even a real effect regresses after the sample (the winner's curse, 2.1 lesson 144). (2) Arbitrage: once published, investors trade the anomaly, prices adjust sooner, and returns shrink; the effect was real and is now partly priced. (3) Costs and capacity: trading pressure raises the cost of implementing it, which shows up in net returns. (4) Changing markets: lower costs, decimalisation and faster information shrink some effects regardless of publication. (5) The leap: the decline is a single number with several causes, and the way to separate them is to find a comparison where only one cause operates. (6) McLean and Pontiff's design separates the post-sample but pre-publication period (statistical bias only) from the post-publication period (bias plus arbitrage), and tests whether decay is larger for anomalies that are cheaper to arbitrage [recalled, unverified]. (7) Data mining predicts decay already after the sample's end; arbitrage predicts extra decay after publication, concentrated in liquid stocks. (8) For one replication: report the survival ratio with its interval (lesson 106), say which period it covers, and do not infer "false discovery" from a smaller post-publication return alone.
  *Rubric (10).* 1 each for three alternative causes from lines 1 to 4 (3), 1 for the motivation (line 5), 2 for the design that separates them (line 6), 2 for the distinguishing predictions (line 7), 2 for the implication for one replication (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* A planted anomaly in the rehearsal panel earns 0.80% a month in sample, 0.60% after the sample but before publication, and 0.35% after publication. (a) The decay after the sample and after publication, as percentages of the in-sample return. (b) What does each measure, in McLean and Pontiff's reading? (c) The post-publication return's standard error is 0.25%. What would you report?
  *Answer.* (a) After the sample: $1 - \frac{0.60}{0.80} = 25\%$. After publication: $1 - \frac{0.35}{0.80} = 56\%$. (b) The 25% is the drop with no publication effect, so it estimates statistical bias; the further 31 points after publication estimate the effect of investors learning about it. (c) Decision point: the naive report is "56% decay, so mostly gone". The post-publication mean is 1.4 standard errors from zero, so the data cannot say whether a small premium remains; report the survival ratio $\frac{0.35}{0.80} = 0.44$ with its interval, and the two decay estimates with theirs.

**WS3 Machine learning meets the cross-section** (`set.survival.ml`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: a referee report on a described ML finance paper. Code drill `drl.papers.oos-r2` (d3, `numpy`): `r2_oos(y, yhat, benchmark="zero")` and with `benchmark="expanding-mean"` (the mean of past $y$ only). Hidden tests: forecasts equal to zero give 0 against zero; perfect forecasts give 1; the expanding-mean benchmark at time $t$ uses only $y_1, \dots, y_{t-1}$ (the first value is skipped); a constant forecast equal to the full-sample mean scores above 0 against zero but is flagged as look-ahead when `benchmark="expanding-mean"` is requested with `check=True`. Known-wrong: the benchmark mean over the whole sample (fails test 3). Generators: `gen.papers.oos-r2`, `gen.papers.sharpe-from-r2`, `gen.papers.audit-ml-paper`.

- *Example A (long-form, referee report, d5).* Write a referee report on this fictional paper: "We train a gradient-boosted model on 200 firm characteristics to predict monthly returns of all US stocks from 1990 to 2023. Hyperparameters are chosen to maximise the long-short Sharpe ratio over 2015 to 2023, which is also the test period. The decile spread earns a Sharpe ratio of 2.4. Characteristics are taken from the latest database vintage. We conclude that machine learning uncovers large, unexploited mispricing."
  *Model answer.* (1) Summary in two sentences, neutral. (2) Main concern 1: hyperparameters are tuned on the test period, so the reported Sharpe ratio is in sample; ask for a separate validation period before 2015 and a single evaluation on 2015 to 2023 (2.1 lessons 29 and 146). (3) Main concern 2: the latest database vintage includes restated and backfilled characteristics; ask for point-in-time data or a lag of at least six months on accounting variables (2.1 lesson 124). (4) Main concern 3: "all US stocks" with equal-weighted deciles is dominated by microcaps; ask for value-weighted results and NYSE breakpoints (lesson 96). (5) Main concern 4: costs and turnover are absent; ask for turnover and net returns (2.3 AR). (6) Further requests: the number of model configurations tried (2.1 lesson 144), the out-of-sample $R^2$ against zero (lesson 101), comparison with a linear benchmark on the same features (Gu, Kelly and Xiu 2020). (7) Minor points last. (8) Recommendation: major revision; the conclusion "unexploited mispricing" is not supported until concerns 1 to 4 are addressed.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: neutral summary. 2 each for the tuning-on-test and vintage concerns with a fixable request. 1 each for the microcap and cost concerns. 1: further requests. 1: ordering (main before minor) and a recommendation tied to the concerns. A report that leads with typos or rejects without saying what would change the verdict earns at most 6.
- *Example B (d5).* A market-timing model's monthly out-of-sample $R^2$ is 1% against a zero forecast. The market's monthly Sharpe ratio is 0.12. (a) By Campbell and Thompson's approximation $SR^{*2} = \frac{SR^2 + R^2}{1 - R^2}$, the timing strategy's Sharpe ratio, monthly and annual. (b) A colleague dismisses a 1% $R^2$ as noise. What do you say? (c) What must be checked before believing it?
  *Answer.* (a) $SR^{*2} = \frac{0.0144 + 0.01}{0.99} = 0.0246$, so $SR^* = 0.157$ monthly, 0.54 a year against the market's 0.42. (b) Decision point: the naive dismissal compares 1% with the $R^2$s of other fields. For returns, whose variance is almost all noise, a 1% $R^2$ that is real lifts a mean-variance investor's Sharpe ratio by about 30%. (c) That it is out of sample with no tuning on the evaluation period, its standard error (over 30 years of months an $R^2$ of 1% is about 1.9 standard errors of a correlation from zero, using $\text{SE}(\rho)\approx\frac1{\sqrt T}$ and $\sqrt{0.01} = 0.1$ against $\frac{1}{\sqrt{360}} = 0.053$), and how many models were tried.

**WS4 The exit: your replication** (`set.survival.exit`). The exit project's milestone pack: 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5): the paper and route choice with its biases (d3), the replication `PLAN.md` with ledger and pre-registration (d4), the data manifest with hashes (d3), the main-table replication with its comparison (d4), the extension with the survival ratio (d4), the test summary (`pytest -q` paste-verify, d3), the results note (long-form, d5), and the defence transcript from `viva` (d5, self-marked against the rubric with Bertie's draft). Code drill: the learner's own repository (paste-verify), with the rehearsal drill of the chosen paper as the in-app check. Generators: none (the project is the learner's).

- *Example A (long-form, results note, d5).* Write the replication's results note, including the section on how much of the effect survived. *Model answer outline* (shipped as a filled note for the default route, Fama and French from Ken French's library): (1) claim and the table replicated; (2) data route, files, dates and their hashes; (3) the ledger, marking every unstated choice and the one taken; (4) the replicated table beside the published one, cell differences explained; (5) the pre-registered extension; (6) how much survived: survival ratios of SMB and HML with intervals, the post-sample alphas of the 25 portfolios, and the GRS statistic after 1993; (7) what failed or did not match; (8) limitations (the library's revised data, no costs, value weights only), with their likely direction; (9) next steps. The figures in the shipped model note are computed by CI from the library files at a pinned download date and are not quoted here.
  *Rubric (10).* 1 each for lines 1 to 9, and 1 for plainness (no claim without a number; within two pages). The exit's `oos-section` and `survival-ratio` predicates must pass (decision R9).
- *Example B (d5).* Critique a peer's replication summary: "We replicated Gatev, Goetzmann and Rouwenhorst on today's S&P 500 constituents from 2003 to 2024. Our top-20 portfolio earns 1.1% a month with same-day execution, more than the paper. We tried the top 5, 10, 20 and 50 and report the best. The effect fully survived." (a) List the problems. (b) Rewrite the last sentence honestly.
  *Answer.* (a) Today's constituents bias pairs returns upwards (lesson 66); same-day execution includes bid-ask bounce (lesson 55); choosing the best of four portfolio sizes is selection, so the reported return is inflated (2.1 lesson 144); no interval is given; and "more than the paper" on a different, survivor-only sample is not a comparison of like with like. (b) Decision point: the naive sentence claims full survival. Honest: "With survivor-only data, same-day execution and the best of four portfolio sizes, the top-20 portfolio earned 1.1% a month (95% interval to be computed); after a one-day wait and with the bias measured in the rehearsal panel, we expect a figure materially lower, and we cannot yet say how much of GGR's effect survived."

### 3.11 Drills, labs and projects

**The ML Lab** (2.1, 16 drills of about 30 minutes, in the projects block; each a `drl.ml.lab-*` folder). They are the code companion to ISLP's labs and Géron's chapters, written from the methods, never copied.

| # | Drill | Needs | What the learner builds | Hidden tests (summary) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `drl.ml.lab-polyfit` | `numpy` | Sample lesson 1's table: training and test MSE by degree on the fixed data | Every entry of section 7's table to $10^{-3}$ |
| 2 | `drl.ml.lab-knn` | `stdlib` | k-NN regression and classification with a tie rule | Hook values; tie rule |
| 3 | `drl.ml.lab-bias-variance` | `numpy` | Bias and variance by simulation against the closed form | Simulation within the stored margin of the formula (decision 3 rule (c)) |
| 4 | `drl.ml.lab-gd-logistic` | `numpy` | Logistic regression by gradient descent and by Newton's method | Both reach the stored optimum; Newton in under 10 steps |
| 5 | `drl.ml.lab-cv` | `numpy` | k-fold, LOOCV (with the shortcut) and the bootstrap's 0.632 | Lesson 25's shortcut; fold partition |
| 6 | `drl.ml.lab-paths` | `sklearn` | Ridge and lasso paths, $\lambda_{\min}$ and $\lambda_{1\text{se}}$ | Paths match the stored fixture; one-SE choice |
| 7 | `drl.ml.lab-splines` | `numpy` | Regression and natural splines for the intraday volume U | Basis shapes; CV-chosen knots on the stored seed |
| 8 | `drl.ml.lab-classifiers` | `sklearn` | Logistic, LDA, QDA, naive Bayes and k-NN on four data shapes | The ISLP 4.5 ranking on the stored data within margins |
| 9 | `drl.ml.lab-roc-pr` | `numpy` | ROC, AUC, PR curves and a cost-based threshold | Exact AUC; threshold from costs |
| 10 | `drl.ml.lab-calibration` | `sklearn` | Platt and isotonic recalibration on a held-out split | Reliability bins; Brier improves on the stored split |
| 11 | `drl.ml.lab-cart` | `stdlib` | A CART regressor and classifier from scratch with cost-complexity pruning | Identical structure to `ml-core` on tie-free fixtures |
| 12 | `drl.ml.lab-ensembles` | `sklearn` | Random forest and gradient boosting with early stopping on a low-signal panel | Test error at the stored margin; OOB against CV |
| 13 | `drl.ml.lab-micrograd` | `stdlib` | A scalar autograd engine and a two-layer network trained on XOR (after Karpathy's micrograd) | Gradients against finite differences; XOR solved |
| 14 | `drl.ml.lab-torch-mlp` | `local-torch` | The same network in PyTorch on the learner's machine (Géron chapters 9 and 10 [searched: chapter titles]) | Paste-back of the test summary and the final loss |
| 15 | `drl.ml.lab-purged-cv` | `sklearn` | A `PurgedKFold` splitter compatible with `cross_val_score`, with embargo | Lesson 120's purge set; scikit-learn accepts it |
| 16 | `drl.ml.lab-labels` | `pandas` | Triple-barrier labels, meta-labels and uniqueness weights on a price series | Lesson 132's uniqueness; stored first touches |

**The Replication Lab** (3.1, five rehearsals of about 1.5 hours, in the projects block): `drl.papers.momentum-rehearsal` (MP5), `drl.papers.ff-rehearsal` (FF5), `drl.papers.pairs-rehearsal` (PS4), `drl.papers.as-sim` (MM3, the paper's simulation in full) and `drl.papers.decay-rehearsal` (`statsmodels`; WS2: a McLean and Pontiff design on 50 planted anomalies). Each runs on `panel-sim` or `as-sim` with known truth and is graded against that truth (decision R10).

**Exit project 2.1: `prj.ml.exit`** (kind `ml-capstone`, 32 hours). One tabular prediction problem, end to end, with time-ordered cross-validation, run first in `ml-workbench` on the learner's seeded data set and then exported to the learner's repository. Milestones (each with its evidence kind): framing sheet (self); feature timeline and labels (self, checked by the workbench audit); purged and embargoed folds (workbench predicate); model comparison with paired fold differences (workbench predicate); locked hold-out opened once (workbench lock record); `drl.ml.exit-pipeline` passing (drill); repository with tests (paste-verify of `pytest -q`); results note at 7 of 10 with `sections-complete` and `metrics-consistent` passing; `viva` rehearsal (2.1 lesson 152). The learner chooses the problem; the default is the volatility-regime problem of FD7's model note, and the researcher track's suggestion is a cross-sectional return-rank model on Ken French's portfolios or free prices with the survivorship bias stated.

**Exit project 3.1: `prj.papers.replication`** (kind `replication`, 41 hours). One of the five papers (default: Fama and French from Ken French's library), replicated and extended past publication, in the learner's repository under project-forge's three phases. Milestones: paper and route (self); `PLAN.md` with ledger and pre-registration (self, reviewed against the WS4 rubric); data manifest with hashes (paste-verify); the main table replicated beside the published one (self, with the rehearsal drill passing in-app); the extension and survival ratio (the `survival-ratio` predicate); tests (paste-verify); results note at 7 of 10 with `oos-section` and `survival-ratio` passing; defence (`viva`, 3.1 lesson 108).

## 4. Topic exam blueprint and the exit tests

### 4.0 Rules for both papers

- **Kind** `topic` (`ASSESSMENT.md` 3.1): timed, one sitting, no hints, Bertie away app-wide (`AI-TUTOR.md` 2.12), resits after the cool-down of `ASSESSMENT.md` 3.8, pass at 70% of marks.
- **Forms.** At least 3 forms with equal total marks; each form's difficulty mix within 5 percentage points of the declared mix (by marks); every skill of the topic in some form; at least 50% of each form's marks from generator slots; an exam reserve of at least $6\times\text{forms}\times\text{count}$ admissible points per generator slot (`ASSESSMENT.md` 3.2).
- **Aids.** None. The 2.1 exit says "without notes"; 3.1's paper follows it so the two items are examined alike. Calculator: the in-app scientific calculator. Code: predict-the-output items (T) only; code that needs scikit-learn lives in the drills.
- **Long-form items** are marked out of 10 against their rubric, with Bertie's draft once the paper is submitted and the learner confirming (`AI-TUTOR.md` 2.7), then scaled to the slot's marks. A `slotMin` bar of 0.7 means 7 of 10 on the rubric.
- **Runs on** both targets: no item needs Python beyond the in-app calculator.

### 4.2 The three exit slots in full (specimen versions)

**Q18 (specimen): leakage.** "Explain what data leakage is and why it makes a model look better than it is. Give three distinct ways leakage enters a pipeline that predicts asset returns from features, and for each say how to prevent it and how you would detect it. Use one concrete example."

*Model answer.* (1) Leakage is any information available during training or evaluation that would not be available at the moment the model is used, so the evaluation measures a model the deployment cannot have. (2) It inflates scores because the model, or the procedure that chose it, has seen part of the answer: the test score then estimates the error of a different, better-informed procedure. (3) Mechanism one, temporal: a feature dated after the decision (a closing price used for a decision at the close, a restated accounting figure). Prevent with explicit known-at timestamps and point-in-time data; detect with a feature-time audit and a "shift every feature one more day" check that should barely change an honest score. (4) Mechanism two, preprocessing or selection on test rows: a scaler, imputer or feature screen fitted on all rows before cross-validation. Prevent with a pipeline refitted inside each fold; detect with a shuffled-label run, which should score at chance and does not when selection leaks (lesson 28's 2% error on noise). (5) Mechanism three, overlapping labels and shuffled folds: 20-day forward returns share 19 days with their neighbours, so shuffled folds put near-copies of test rows in training. Prevent with purged, embargoed, time-ordered folds; detect by comparing shuffled and blocked CV, where a large gap is the signature. (6) Others count too: target encoding with the row's own label, survivorship in the universe, repeated looks at a hold-out. (7) Example [illustrative]: a capstone feature "20-day realised volatility" computed on a centred window used tomorrow's returns; blocked CV fell from AUC 0.61 to 0.53 once it was made trailing. (8) The general test: for every number the model touches, ask "could I have known this, at this time, in production?"
*Rubric (10).* 2: definition in terms of availability at use time (line 1). 1: why scores inflate (line 2). 2 each for three distinct mechanisms, each with a prevention and a detection (lines 3 to 5; a mechanism without both earns 1). 1: a concrete example (line 7). Line 6 or line 8 can replace a missing half-mark in a mechanism. Common losses: naming only target leakage; "use a hold-out" as the only prevention; no detection method.

**Q19 (specimen): regularisation.** "Explain what regularisation is and why it can lower test error even though it raises training error. Contrast ridge and the lasso, using either their geometry or their effect on a single coefficient. Say how the penalty is chosen and give one form regularisation takes in a non-linear model."

*Model answer.* (1) Regularisation restricts or penalises a model's flexibility, here by adding a penalty on coefficient size to the training loss: minimise RSS $+\lambda\cdot$penalty. (2) It raises training error and bias, and lowers variance; when the variance saved exceeds the squared bias added, test error falls (the bias-variance trade-off, lesson 7). (3) In finance, where signal is weak and estimates noisy, the variance saving is usually large, which is why shrinkage helps. (4) Ridge penalises $\sum_j\beta_j^2$: in an orthonormal design it scales each coefficient by $\frac{1}{1 + \lambda}$, and in general it shrinks most along the data's low-variance directions (the SVD form), never to exactly zero. (5) The lasso penalises $\sum_j\lvert\beta_j\rvert$: in an orthonormal design it soft-thresholds, $\operatorname{sign}(z)(\lvert z\rvert - \lambda)_+$ in the $\frac12$RSS scaling, so small coefficients become exactly zero and the model selects features; geometrically the $L_1$ ball's corners lie on the axes. (6) With correlated features the lasso picks one of a group arbitrarily, and the elastic net mixes the two penalties to keep groups together. (7) $\lambda$ is chosen by cross-validation (time-ordered and purged for returns), often by the one-standard-error rule, after standardising features, because the penalty depends on units; never on the test set. (8) Non-linear forms: tree depth and minimum leaf size, shrinkage and early stopping in boosting, weight decay (which is ridge) and dropout in networks. (9) A Bayesian reading: ridge is the posterior mode under a Gaussian prior with $\lambda = \sigma^2/\tau^2$, the lasso under a Laplace prior.
*Rubric (10).* 1: definition (line 1). 2: why test error can fall, through bias and variance (line 2). 2: ridge's effect (line 4). 2: the lasso's effect and why it gives zeros (line 5). 2: choosing $\lambda$ honestly, with standardisation (line 7). 1: a non-linear form (line 8). Lines 3, 6 and 9 can each replace one missing mark. Common losses: "the lasso shrinks proportionally"; $\lambda$ chosen on the test set; no reason why test error falls.

**Q20 (specimen): the bias-variance trade-off.** "For a new observation $Y_0 = f(x_0) + \varepsilon_0$, derive $E\left[(Y_0 - \hat f(x_0))^2\right] = \sigma^2 + \text{bias}^2 + \text{variance}$, stating each assumption where it is used. Then use k-nearest neighbours to explain why test error is U-shaped in flexibility, and say what changes when the signal-to-noise ratio is very low, as in return prediction."

*Model answer.* (1) to (7) as homework LD2's example A: the add-and-subtract split, the expectation over the new noise and the training set, the three vanishing cross terms (one using $E\varepsilon_0 = 0$, one using the independence of $\varepsilon_0$ from the training data, one because $E[\hat f - E\hat f] = 0$), and the result. (8) For k-NN on a fixed design, the variance is $\frac{\sigma^2}{k}$ and the bias is $f(x_0)$ minus the average of $f$ over the $k$ neighbours. (9) Small $k$ (flexible): small bias, because the neighbours are close, and large variance, because few points are averaged. Large $k$ (rigid): the reverse, as distant neighbours pull the average away from $f(x_0)$. (10) The sum of a falling variance and a rising squared bias, above the constant floor $\sigma^2$, is U-shaped in flexibility; training error, by contrast, keeps falling (at $k = 1$ it is 0). (11) With very low signal-to-noise the noise floor dominates and the variance term is large relative to any achievable reduction in bias, so the optimum moves strongly towards rigid, heavily regularised models; the best possible out-of-sample $R^2$ is small, and a flexible model's apparent fit is mostly variance.
*Rubric (10).* 1: the split with its motivation. 3: the three cross terms, each with its assumption named. 1: the result. 2: k-NN's bias and variance. 2: the U-shape against training error. 1: the low-signal consequence. Common losses: unsquared bias; independence never stated; the U-shape asserted without the two opposing terms.

# exit part data (ASSESSMENT.md 2.10 schema)

The `time-ordered-validation` note predicate is new: the results note's validation section must name the splitter, the purge and embargo lengths, and the hold-out dates, and they must match the workbench's record. **Runs on:** the paper on both targets; the drill wherever scikit-learn ships, otherwise the web app with a result code (`ASSESSMENT.md` 2.5). `viva` (2.1 lesson 152) is rehearsal, not a gate, because Bertie is away during exams.

Generator slots carry 49 of 72 marks (68%). Q17's rubric is WS3 example A's; Q18's is below.

**Q18 (specimen).** "An anomaly earned 1.2% a month in its paper's sample (SE 0.3%) and 0.5% since (SE 0.35%). Give the survival ratio with delta-method and Fieller intervals, say which you would report and why, state what the data can and cannot tell you, and name two analyses that would change your verdict."
*Model answer.* (1) $r = 0.417$; delta $\text{SE} = 0.310$, interval $(-0.19, 1.02)$ (homework MP2). (2) Fieller: $(-0.16, 1.26)$. (3) Report Fieller, or both, because the ratio's sampling distribution is skewed when the denominator is uncertain; the delta method is symmetric by construction. (4) The data cannot distinguish full survival from none: both 0 and 1 lie inside either interval. (5) They do say the post-sample mean is 1.4 standard errors above zero, weak evidence that something remains. (6) Analyses that would change the verdict: a longer or independent out-of-sample period (another country, the library's international files); a pooled test across many related anomalies (the McLean and Pontiff design); costs, which could take the net ratio below zero. (7) Verdict in a sentence: "point estimate 42% survived; the data allow anything from none to all".
*Rubric (10).* 2: the ratio and delta interval. 2: Fieller. 1: which to report and why. 2: what the data can and cannot say. 2: two analyses. 1: a plain one-sentence verdict.

## 5. Reading and resource map per level

### 5.0 How to use this map

- **Spine first, one resource per topic** (curriculum rule 1): ISLP for 2.1, the papers themselves for 3.1. Lectures and stretch reading are optional parallels; the hours of section 1.6 and 1.11 count the spine, the named lectures and the problems.
- **Read after the level's lessons, before its homework.** Lessons give the picture; the reading gives the full treatment and the exercises.
- **Problems over reading** (rule 2): every row names exercises or a lab to do, not only pages to read.
- **Citation roles** follow `canon-gap.md` 0.4: spine `basis`, lectures `parallel-reading`, problems `problem-inspired`, stretch `further`.
- **Free sources** (URLs as of 6 October 2026): ISLP at https://www.statlearning.com (free PDF and Python labs); ESL at https://hastie.su.domains/ElemStatLearn/ [check path]; CS229 Autumn 2018 lectures on Stanford Online's YouTube channel [searched] with notes at https://cs229.stanford.edu; Caltech Learning From Data at https://work.caltech.edu/lectures.html [searched]; MIT 15.097 at https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/15-097-prediction-machine-learning-and-statistics-spring-2012 [searched]; MIT 6.036 (Fall 2020) at https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-036-introduction-to-machine-learning-fall-2020 [searched] (the Open Learning Library hosts an earlier, 2019 run); MIT 18.S096 Matrix Calculus for Machine Learning and Beyond (IAP 2023) at https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-s096-matrix-calculus-for-machine-learning-and-beyond-january-iap-2023 [searched]; Andrew Ng's Machine Learning Specialization (DeepLearning.AI and Stanford Online, on Coursera), the curriculum's English video route, free to audit [check audit terms]; StatQuest on YouTube; 3Blue1Brown's neural-network series at https://www.3blue1brown.com; Ken French's data library at https://mba.tuck.dartmouth.edu/pages/faculty/ken.french/data_library.html; Open Source Asset Pricing at https://www.openassetpricing.com; SSRN at https://www.ssrn.com; arXiv quantitative finance at https://arxiv.org/archive/q-fin. Paid: Géron (O'Reilly 2025), López de Prado (Wiley 2018), Bali, Engle and Murray (Wiley 2016), Cochrane (Princeton 2005), Cartea, Jaimungal and Penalva (CUP 2015).
- **Section numbers** for ISLP and ESL follow the editions named (ISLP 1st ed 2023, ESL 2nd ed); they are recalled and marked [check] once per course rather than on every cell. Lecture numbers for CS229 and Caltech are [searched].

## 6. Verification approach

Every graded answer is computed twice, by two implementations that share no code (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 6.2): the item's generator or widget in TypeScript, and the Python verifier `zqverify` run in CI on CPython with the pinned stack. Disagreement fails the build. The families below say what each implementation is and what else CI checks.

| Family | Items in this file | First implementation | Second implementation and checks |
|---|---|---|---|
| Closed-form numbers | Optimism, the noise $R^2$ benchmark, k-NN bias and variance, entropies and KL, gradient-descent factors, ridge degrees of freedom and shrinkage, soft thresholds, odds and probabilities, Bayes and LDA boundaries, cost thresholds, Kelly growth, CAPM alphas and their errors, GRS, spanning gains, OU half-lives, reservation prices and spreads, Glosten-Milgrom quotes, Kyle's $\lambda$, Bonferroni hurdles, haircuts, survival ratios (delta and Fieller), Campbell-Thompson | Generator in TypeScript on `dmath` (deterministic elementary functions, catalogue requirement 1) | `zqverify` with `fractions.Fraction` where the answer is rational and NumPy or SciPy (`scipy.stats`, `scipy.integrate`) where it is not; tolerances stated per item and replayed through the grader with every distractor (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 6.5, 6.6) |
| Exact counting | Gini and entropy of nodes, split gains, precision and recall, AUC by pairs (ties half), purge and embargo sets, CPCV splits and paths, concurrency and uniqueness, sequential-bootstrap probabilities, Shapley values, linkage merge orders, bar counts, cohort counts, BH and BHY decisions | Generator with the shared `rational` service (catalogue 9, rule 4) | Brute-force enumeration in Python over every pair, coalition, label span or ordering; BH and BHY against statsmodels' `multipletests` |
| Derivations and identities | Every `derive` screen and long-form model answer: the bias-variance decomposition, optimism, KL $\ge 0$, gradients and Hessians, the LOOCV shortcut, the soft threshold, LDA's discriminant, the variance of an average, XGBoost's gain, backpropagation, the minimum-norm limit, the fractional-difference recursion, JT's decomposition, GRS geometry, spanning, the OU discretisation, the reservation price, Glosten-Milgrom | Authored lines in the derivation widget | SymPy checks that each line follows from the last (difference simplifies to 0, or the stated substitution holds), and NumPy spot-checks every identity at 1,000 random points; matrix gradients are also checked by central differences at relative error $10^{-6}$ (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 6.8) |
| Model numbers on screens | Fits, residuals, trees, paths, forests, boosting rounds, network activations, decision fields | `ml-core` in a worker | Parity with scikit-learn fixtures generated by the CPython pipeline (catalogue 2.12: closed-form models to $10^{-10}$ relative, iterative convex models to $10^{-6}$, trees identical in structure on tie-free data, forests and networks by distribution across 20 seeds and by cross-browser determinism) |
| Predictions before a run | "Which curve wins", "sketch the error curves", "which scheme reports higher accuracy", "which strategy has the narrower distribution" | The prediction is graded, not the run (decision 3 (a)) | CI proves the qualitative outcome holds on every generated instance (the margin rule, catalogue 9 rule 2); a generator whose instance could flip is rejected |
| Deterministic runs with margins | Drills and screens that train a model and compare a score with a threshold (nested CV, leak-free pipelines, the exit pipeline, OU fits, AS simulations, rehearsal replications) | The learner's code on the item's fixed seed | CI shows the reference clears the threshold by the stated margin and every `wrong/*.py` fails its named test, on CPython 3.11 and on Pyodide's CPython (decision 3 (c)) |
| Simulation statements in prose | "2% over 50 runs" (lesson 28), "0.302 over 2,000 fits" (lesson 5), the Monte Carlo values quoted in hooks | Never graded | Regenerated by the fixture pipeline with a stored seed; CI warns if a regenerated value drifts more than 4 standard errors from the printed one |
| Code drills | Every `drl.ml.*` and `drl.papers.*` | The learner's code | `zqtest` suites against `reference.py`; known-wrong solutions must fail named tests; `stdlib` drills also run under MicroPython; `sklearn`, `scipy` and `statsmodels` drills are pinned to Pyodide's versions; `local-torch` drills verify the pasted summary's format and the loss against a range stated in the drill |
| Research rehearsals | `panel-sim` and `as-sim` items, the Replication Lab | The rehearsal's planted truth | CI checks the reference sorts and regressions recover the planted premia within 4 standard errors at large $N$ and $T$, with golden hashes per seed (catalogue 2.12) |
| Published figures | Every [recalled, unverified] number about a paper | Context only | A lint forbids any check, accepted answer or distractor from referencing a figure tagged unverified; the content team verifies against the paper and flips the tag (decision R10) |
| Long-form items | One per homework set; Q18 to Q20 of `exm.ml`; Q17 and Q18 of `exm.papers` | The rubric (4 to 10 points, 10 marks, each point naming model-answer lines) | The model answer's mathematics passes the derivation family and its numbers the closed-form family; Bertie's marks need evidence quotes found in the learner's text (`AI-TUTOR.md` 2.7); the learner confirms |
| Results notes | Capstone and replication notes | `results-note` predicates (`sections-complete`, `metrics-consistent`, `oos-section`, and the new `survival-ratio` and `time-ordered-validation`) | Each predicate has unit tests on passing and failing notes; `metrics-consistent` compares every number in the note with the metrics file |
| Citations | Sections 1, 2 and 5 | `sources.yaml` entries with `checked: false` | A person with the book or paper confirms each [check] item; CI lists every unchecked citation that content cites |
| Copy | Every learner-facing string | Authoring | Lints: British spelling, no em dashes, the banned-word list (with the technical-term allow-list of open item 6), sentence length, the tutor-field leak check (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.10) |

**Verified while writing this file** [verified]: every numeric hook, homework answer, exam specimen and sample-lesson value, with Python 3.11.15, NumPy 2.4.6, SciPy 1.17.1, statsmodels 0.15.0, scikit-learn 1.9.1, SymPy 1.14.0 and pandas 3.0.6, including the sample lesson's fits (the degree-9 curve also in exact rational arithmetic by Lagrange interpolation), the null $R^2$ simulation (0.302 against $\frac{30}{99} = 0.303$; 0.336 against $\frac{40}{119}$), the wrong-way CV simulation (error 0.016 against 0.516 the right way, 50 runs), $E[\max]$ of $N$ standard normals by numerical integration ($N = 12, 15, 20, 30, 50, 100, 1000$: 1.629, 1.736, 1.867, 2.043, 2.249, 2.508, 3.241), the pruning sequence, the tent map's $2^k$ pieces, the purge sets under both span conventions, the BH and BHY decisions against statsmodels, Runge's errors (1.916, 0.109, 0.022), and the GRS critical value ($F_{0.95}(25, 332) = 1.539$). The scripts live in the session scratchpad, not in the repository; the fixture pipeline regenerates every value in CI.
